Reflections on the Revolution in France

A letter to a young Frenchman, written while the Revolution was still being cheered in London

by Edmund Burke

Section One: The Letter, and the Club That Congratulated a Revolution

Dear Sir,

You are good enough to ask me again, and with some insistence, for my thoughts on the recent proceedings in France. I will not let you imagine that I think my opinions valuable enough to want to be asked for them. They are of too little consequence for me to be very anxious either to give them or to withhold them. It was out of regard for you, and for you only, that I hesitated when you first asked to have them. In the first letter I had the honour of writing to you, which I am at last sending, I wrote neither for nor on behalf of any set of men, and I shall not do so in this one either. My errors, if I have made any, are my own. My reputation alone has to answer for them.

You will see from the long letter I have sent you that although I heartily wish France may be animated by a spirit of rational liberty, and although I think you are bound, by any honest statecraft, to provide a permanent body for that spirit to live in and an effective instrument for it to act through, I have the misfortune to entertain grave doubts about several important points in your recent proceedings.

When you last wrote, you supposed I might be counted among those who approve of certain proceedings in France, because of the solemn public seal of approval those proceedings have received from two clubs of gentlemen in London, called the Constitutional Society and the Revolution Society.

I do have the honour of belonging to more than one club in which the constitution of this kingdom and the principles of the glorious Revolution of 1688 are held in high reverence, and I count myself among the most forward in my zeal for maintaining that constitution and those principles in their utmost purity and vigour. It is because I do that I think it necessary there should be no mistake. Those who cultivate the memory of our Revolution, and those who are attached to the constitution of this kingdom, will take good care how they are mixed up with people who, on the pretext of zeal for the Revolution and the constitution, too often wander from their true principles, and are ready on every occasion to depart from the firm but cautious and deliberate spirit that produced the one and presides over the other. Before I go on to answer the more substantial particulars in your letter, let me give you what information I have been able to get about the two clubs that have thought fit, as bodies, to interfere in the affairs of France — first assuring you that I am not, and never have been, a member of either.

The first, which calls itself the Constitutional Society, or the Society for Constitutional Information, or something of that kind, has been going, I believe, for seven or eight years. Its purpose appears to be charitable, and so far laudable: it was intended to circulate, at the members' expense, a great many books that few other people would go to the expense of buying, and which might otherwise sit in the booksellers' hands to the great loss of a useful body of men. Whether the books so charitably circulated were ever as charitably read is more than I know. Possibly several of them have been exported to France and, like goods for which there is no demand here, may have found a market with you. I have heard a great deal of talk about the light to be drawn from books sent over from here. What improvement they may have undergone on the voyage — some liquors, they say, are the better for crossing the sea — I cannot tell; but I have never heard a man of common judgment, or with the least degree of information, say a word in praise of most of the publications that society circulates, and nobody except some of themselves has ever thought their proceedings of any serious consequence.

Your National Assembly seems to hold much the same opinion of this poor charitable club as I do. As a nation you reserved your whole stock of eloquent acknowledgments for the Revolution Society, when their fellows in the Constitutional Society were in fairness entitled to some share of it. Since you have singled out the Revolution Society as the great object of your national thanks and praise, you will think me excusable for making its recent conduct the subject of my remarks. The National Assembly of France has given these gentlemen importance by adopting them, and they return the favour by acting as a committee in England for spreading the principles of the National Assembly. From now on we must regard them as a kind of privileged persons, as no inconsiderable members of the diplomatic body. This is one of the revolutions that have given splendour to obscurity and distinction to merit nobody had noticed. Until very lately I do not recall ever hearing of this club. I am quite sure it never occupied a moment of my thoughts, nor, I believe, of anybody's outside their own set. I find on inquiry that on the anniversary of the Revolution of 1688 a club of Dissenters — of what denomination I do not know — have long had the custom of hearing a sermon in one of their meeting houses, and afterwards spending the day cheerfully at the tavern, as other clubs do. But I never heard that any public measure or political system, much less the merits of the constitution of a foreign nation, had ever been the subject of a formal proceeding at their festivities, until, to my inexpressible surprise, I found them in a sort of public capacity, sending a congratulatory address that gave an authoritative sanction to the proceedings of the National Assembly in France.

In the club's older principles and conduct, so far at least as they were ever declared, I see nothing I could take exception to. I think it very likely that for some purpose new members may have joined them, and that some truly Christian politicians, who love to dispense benefits but are careful to hide the hand that distributes the dole, may have made them the instruments of their pious designs. Whatever I may have reason to suspect about private management, I shall state nothing as certain except what is public.

For my own part, I should be sorry to be thought concerned, directly or indirectly, in their proceedings. I certainly take my full share, along with the rest of the world, in my private and individual capacity, in theorising about what has been done, or is being done, on the public stage anywhere, ancient or modern — in the republic of Rome or the republic of Paris. But having no general apostolic mission, being the citizen of a particular state, and being bound to a considerable degree by its public will, I should think it at the least improper and irregular for me to open a formal public correspondence with the actual government of a foreign nation without the express authority of the government I live under.

I should be still more unwilling to enter into such a correspondence under anything like an ambiguous description, which to many people unfamiliar with our usages might make the address I had joined in look like the act of persons in some sort of chartered capacity, recognised by the laws of this kingdom and authorised to speak the mind of some part of it. It is because of the ambiguity and uncertainty of unauthorised general descriptions, and of the deceit that can be practised under them — and not from mere formality — that the House of Commons would reject the most cringing petition on the most trifling subject if it came signed in the manner for which you have thrown open the folding doors of your presence chamber, ushering it into your National Assembly with as much ceremony and parade, and as great a bustle of applause, as if you had been visited by the whole representative majesty of the English nation. If what this society thought fit to send out had been a piece of argument, it would have mattered little whose argument it was. It would be neither more nor less convincing for the party it came from. But this is only a vote and a resolution. It stands solely on authority, and in this case on the mere authority of individuals, few of whom appear. Their signatures ought, in my opinion, to have been attached to their document. The world would then have the means of knowing how many they are, who they are, and what their opinions may be worth, judging by their personal abilities, their knowledge, their experience, or their standing and authority in this state. To me, who am only a plain man, the proceeding looks a little too refined and too ingenious. It has too much the air of a political stratagem, adopted in order to give, under a high-sounding name, an importance to this club's public declarations which, when the matter came to be closely inspected, they did not altogether deserve. It is a policy with very much the complexion of a fraud.

I flatter myself that I love a manly, moral, regulated liberty as well as any gentleman of that society, whoever he may be; and perhaps I have given as good proofs of my attachment to that cause in the whole course of my public life. I think I begrudge liberty to other nations as little as they do. But I cannot come forward and give praise or blame to anything concerning human actions and human affairs on a bare view of the object, stripped of every relation, in all the nakedness and solitude of metaphysical abstraction. Circumstances — which with some gentlemen count for nothing — are what in reality give every political principle its distinguishing colour and its particular effect. Circumstances are what make every civil and political scheme beneficial or harmful to mankind. Speaking abstractly, government is a good thing, and so is liberty; and yet could I in common sense have congratulated France ten years ago on her enjoyment of a government — for she had a government then — without inquiring what the nature of that government was, or how it was administered? Can I now congratulate the same nation on its freedom? Am I seriously to congratulate a madman who has escaped from the protective restraint and wholesome darkness of his cell on his restoration to the enjoyment of light and liberty, merely because liberty in the abstract may be classed among the blessings of mankind? Am I to congratulate a highwayman and murderer who has broken out of prison on the recovery of his natural rights? That would be to play out all over again the scene of the criminals condemned to the galleys and their heroic deliverer, the metaphysical Knight of the Sorrowful Countenance.

When I see the spirit of liberty in action, I see a strong principle at work; and for a while that is all I can possibly know about it. The wild gas has plainly broken loose. But we ought to suspend our judgment until the first effervescence has subsided a little, until the liquid has cleared, and until we can see something deeper than the agitation of a troubled and frothy surface. Before I venture to congratulate men publicly on a blessing, I must be tolerably sure they have really received one. Flattery corrupts the receiver and the giver alike, and adulation does the people no more good than it does kings. I should therefore hold back my congratulations on the new liberty of France until I was told how it had been combined with government, with public force, with the discipline and obedience of armies, with the collection of an effective and well-distributed revenue, with morality and religion, with solidity and property, with peace and order, with civil and social manners. All of these are good things too, in their way; and without them liberty is not a benefit while it lasts, and is unlikely to last long. The effect of liberty on individuals is that they may do as they please; we ought to see what it will please them to do before we risk congratulations that may soon have to be turned into complaints. Prudence would say as much in the case of separate, isolated private men. But liberty, when men act in bodies, is power. Considering people, before they declare themselves, will watch what use is made of power — and particularly of so trying a thing as new power in new hands, whose principles, tempers and dispositions they have little or no experience of, and in situations where those who appear to be most active on the scene may possibly not be the real movers.

All these considerations, however, were beneath the transcendent dignity of the Revolution Society. While I remained in the country, from where I had the honour of writing to you, I had only an imperfect idea of what they had been doing. On coming to town I sent for an account of their proceedings, published by their own authority, containing a sermon by Dr Price along with letters from the Duc de La Rochefoucauld and the Archbishop of Aix and several other documents. The whole publication, with its manifest design of connecting the affairs of France with those of England by drawing us into an imitation of the conduct of the National Assembly, gave me a considerable degree of uneasiness. The effect of that conduct on the power, credit, prosperity and tranquillity of France was becoming more evident every day. The shape of the constitution to be settled for their future government was becoming clearer. We are now in a position to make out with tolerable exactness the true nature of the thing we are being held up to imitate. If the prudence of reserve and decorum dictates silence in some circumstances, in others a prudence of a higher order may justify us in speaking our minds. The beginnings of confusion here in England are feeble enough at present; but in your country we have seen an infancy still more feeble growing by the minute into a strength that can heap mountains on mountains and make war on Heaven itself. Whenever the neighbour's house is on fire, it cannot be amiss for the engines to play a little on our own. Better to be despised for being too anxious than ruined by being too confident.

Concerned chiefly for the peace of my own country, but by no means unconcerned for yours, I want to set out at greater length what was at first intended only for your private satisfaction. I shall still keep your affairs in view and go on addressing myself to you. Allowing myself the freedom of writing a letter, I shall throw out my thoughts and express my feelings just as they arise in my mind, with very little attention to formal method. I begin with the proceedings of the Revolution Society, but I shall not confine myself to them. How could I? It looks to me as though I were in a great crisis, not of the affairs of France alone, but of all Europe, and perhaps of more than Europe. Taking all the circumstances together, the French Revolution is the most astonishing thing that has yet happened in the world. In many instances the most wonderful things are brought about by the most absurd and ridiculous means, in the most ridiculous ways, and apparently by the most contemptible instruments. Everything seems out of nature in this strange chaos of levity and ferocity, of every sort of crime jumbled together with every sort of folly. Watching this monstrous tragicomic scene, the most opposite passions necessarily follow one another in the mind, and sometimes mix: contempt and indignation by turns, laughter and tears by turns, scorn and horror by turns.

It cannot be denied, however, that to some people this strange scene appeared in quite another light. In them it inspired nothing but exultation and rapture. They saw in what has been done in France nothing but a firm and temperate exercise of freedom, so consistent on the whole with morality and with piety as to deserve not only the secular applause of dashing Machiavellian politicians but to make it a fit subject for all the devout outpourings of sacred eloquence.

Section Two: Dr Price's Sermon, and Whether We Choose Our Kings

On the morning of the fourth of November last, Dr Richard Price, an eminent Nonconformist minister, preached at the Dissenting meeting house of the Old Jewry, to his club or society, a very extraordinary miscellaneous sermon, in which some good moral and religious sentiments, not badly expressed, are mixed into a sort of porridge of assorted political opinions and reflections — but the Revolution in France is the chief ingredient in the cauldron. I regard the address the Revolution Society sent to the National Assembly through Earl Stanhope as originating in the principles of that sermon, and as a corollary of them. It was proposed by the preacher of the sermon. It was passed by men who came straight from the sermon still steaming, with no censure or qualification, stated or implied. If, however, any of the gentlemen concerned should wish to separate the sermon from the resolution, they know how to acknowledge the one and disavow the other. They may do it. I cannot.

For my part, I looked on that sermon as the public declaration of a man closely connected with literary intriguers and plotting philosophers, with political theologians and theological politicians, at home and abroad. I know they set him up as a kind of oracle, because, with the best intentions in the world, he naturally philippizes — speaks as the interested party would have him speak — and chants his prophetic song in exact unison with their designs.

That sermon is in a style which I believe has not been heard in this kingdom, from any pulpit that is tolerated or encouraged in it, since 1648, when a predecessor of Dr Price, the Reverend Hugh Peters, made the vault of the king's own chapel at St James's ring with the honour and privilege of the saints, who, with the "high praises of God in their mouths, and a two-edged sword in their hands, were to execute judgment on the heathen, and punishments upon the people; to bind their kings with chains, and their nobles with fetters of iron." Few harangues from the pulpit, except in the days of your League in France or of our Solemn League and Covenant in England, have ever breathed less of the spirit of moderation than this lecture in the Old Jewry. But even supposing something like moderation were visible in this political sermon, politics and the pulpit are terms that have little in common. No sound ought to be heard in church but the healing voice of Christian charity. The cause of civil liberty and civil government gains as little as the cause of religion by this confusion of duties. Those who abandon their proper character to assume one that does not belong to them are for the most part ignorant both of the character they leave and of the one they take up. Wholly unacquainted with the world they are so fond of meddling in, and inexperienced in all the affairs they pronounce on with so much confidence, they have nothing of politics but the passions politics excites. Surely the church is a place where one day's truce ought to be allowed to the quarrels and animosities of mankind.

Footnote: Psalm 149.

This pulpit style, revived after being so long out of use, struck me as a novelty, and a novelty not entirely without danger. I do not charge every part of the discourse equally with that danger. The hint dropped to a noble and reverend lay divine, supposed to hold high office in one of our universities, and to other lay divines "of rank and literature", may be proper and timely, if somewhat new. If these noble Seekers should find nothing to satisfy their pious fancies in the old stock of the national Church, or in all the rich variety to be had in the well-stocked warehouses of the Dissenting congregations, Dr Price advises them to improve on Nonconformity and each set up a separate meeting house on his own particular principles. It is somewhat remarkable that this reverend divine should be so eager to have new churches set up and so perfectly indifferent about the doctrine that may be taught in them. His zeal is of a curious kind. It is not for spreading his own opinions but for spreading any opinions. It is not for the diffusion of truth but for the propagation of contradiction. Let the noble teachers only dissent; it does not matter from whom or from what. That great point once secured, it is taken for granted their religion will be rational and manly.

I doubt whether religion would reap all the benefits our calculating divine reckons up from this "great company of great preachers". It would certainly add a valuable set of unclassifiable specimens to the ample collection of known classes, genera and species that at present adorn the hortus siccus, the dried-flower album, of Dissent. A sermon from a noble duke, or a noble marquess, or a noble earl, or a bold baron would certainly increase and vary the amusements of this town, which is beginning to grow tired of the unchanging round of its insipid dissipations. I should only stipulate that these new Mess-Johns in robes and coronets should keep some sort of bounds in the democratic and levelling principles expected from their titled pulpits. The new evangelists will, I dare say, disappoint the hopes formed of them. They will not become polemical divines in the literal as well as the figurative sense, or be inclined to drill their congregations so that they may, as in former blessed times, preach their doctrines to regiments of dragoons and to corps of infantry and artillery. Such arrangements, however favourable to the cause of compulsory freedom, civil and religious, may not be equally conducive to national tranquillity. These few restrictions are, I hope, no great stretches of intolerance, no very violent exercises of despotism.

Footnote: Discourse on the Love of our Country, Nov. 4, 1789, by Dr Richard Price, 3rd edition, pp. 17 and 18.

Footnote: "Those who dislike that mode of worship which is prescribed by public authority ought, if they can find no worship out of the Church which they approve, to set up a separate worship for themselves; and by doing this, and giving an example of a rational and manly worship, men of weight from their rank and literature may do the greatest service to society and the world." — P. 18, Dr Price's Sermon.

But of our preacher I may say, "Utinam nugis tota illa dedisset et tempora sævitiæ" — would that he had given all those hours to trifles instead of to cruelty. Not everything in this thundering bull of his is so harmless in its tendency. His doctrines strike at our constitution in its vital parts. He tells the Revolution Society, in this political sermon, that His Majesty "is almost the only lawful king in the world, because the only one who owes his crown to the choice of his people." As for the kings of the world, every one of whom except this one is swept by this arch-pontiff of the rights of men into a single clause of ban and anathema — with all the fullness and more than the boldness of the papal deposing power at its twelfth-century height — and proclaimed a usurper by circles of longitude and latitude across the whole globe, it is for them to consider how they admit into their territories these apostolic missionaries who are to tell their subjects they are not lawful kings. That is their concern. Ours, as a domestic matter of some importance, is to consider seriously how solid the only principle is on which these gentlemen acknowledge a king of Great Britain to be entitled to their allegiance.

This doctrine, as applied to the prince now on the British throne, is either nonsense, and therefore neither true nor false, or else it asserts a most unfounded, dangerous, illegal and unconstitutional position. According to this spiritual doctor of politics, if His Majesty does not owe his crown to the choice of his people, he is no lawful king. Now nothing could be less true than that His Majesty holds the crown of this kingdom in that way. Therefore, if you follow their rule, the king of Great Britain, who most certainly does not owe his high office to any form of popular election, is in no respect better than the rest of the gang of usurpers who reign — or rather rob — all over the face of this miserable world, without any sort of right or title to their people's allegiance. The policy behind this general doctrine, so qualified, is plain enough. The propagators of this political gospel hope that their abstract principle — that a popular choice is necessary to the lawful existence of the sovereign power — will pass unnoticed while the king of Great Britain is exempted from it. Meanwhile their congregations' ears would gradually be habituated to it, as though it were a first principle admitted without dispute. For the present it would operate only as a theory, pickled in the preserving juices of pulpit eloquence and laid by for future use. Condo et compono quæ mox depromere passim: I store and stow away what I may soon bring out. By this policy, while our government is soothed with an exemption in its favour that it has no claim to, the security it has in common with all governments, so far as opinion is security, is taken away from it.

So these politicians proceed, while little notice is taken of their doctrines. But when they come to be examined on the plain meaning of their words and the direct tendency of what they teach, then the equivocations and the slippery constructions come into play. When they say the king owes his crown to the choice of his people, and is therefore the only lawful sovereign in the world, they will perhaps tell us they mean no more than that some of the king's predecessors were called to the throne by some sort of choice, and that he therefore owes his crown to the choice of his people. By this miserable subterfuge they hope to make their proposition safe by making it meaningless. They are welcome to the refuge they seek from their offence, since they take shelter in their own folly. For if you allow that interpretation, how does their idea of election differ from our idea of inheritance? And how does settling the crown on the Brunswick line, derived from James the First, make our monarchy any more legal than that of any of the neighbouring countries? At some time or other, to be sure, all the founders of dynasties were chosen by those who called them to govern. There is ground enough for the opinion that all the kingdoms of Europe were at some remote period elective, with more or fewer limits on who could be chosen. But whatever kings may have been here or elsewhere a thousand years ago, and in whatever way the ruling dynasties of England or France may have begun, the king of Great Britain is today king by a fixed rule of succession, according to the laws of his country. And while the legal conditions of the compact of sovereignty are performed by him — as they are — he holds his crown in contempt of the choice of the Revolution Society, who have not a single vote for a king among them, individually or collectively, though I have no doubt they would soon set themselves up as an electoral college if things were ripe enough to give effect to their claim. His Majesty's heirs and successors, each in his time and order, will come to the crown with the same contempt for their choice with which His Majesty succeeded to the one he wears.

Whatever success their evasion may have in explaining away the gross error of fact, which supposes that His Majesty owes his crown to the choice of his people — though he does hold it with their goodwill — nothing can evade their full and explicit declaration about the principle of a right in the people to choose, which is directly maintained and tenaciously held to. All the oblique hints about election rest on this proposition and refer back to it. In case the foundation of the king's exclusive legal title should be taken for a mere flourish of flattering freedom, the political divine goes on to assert dogmatically that by the principles of the Revolution the people of England have acquired three fundamental rights, all of which, for him, make up one system and lie together in one short sentence: that we have acquired a right

Footnote: P. 34, Discourse on the Love of our Country, by Dr Price.

1. "To choose our own governors."

2. "To cashier them for misconduct."

3. "To frame a government for ourselves."

This new and hitherto unheard-of bill of rights, though made in the name of the whole people, belongs to those gentlemen and their faction alone. The body of the people of England have no share in it. They utterly disown it. They will resist any practical assertion of it with their lives and fortunes. They are bound to do so by the laws of their country, made at the time of that very Revolution which is being appealed to in favour of the fictitious rights claimed by the society that abuses its name.

These gentlemen of the Old Jewry, in all their reasoning about the Revolution of 1688, have another revolution — the one that happened in England about forty years before it — and the recent French Revolution so much before their eyes and in their hearts that they are constantly confusing all three together. We must separate what they confuse. We must recall their wandering fancies to the acts of the Revolution we revere, in order to discover its true principles. If the principles of the Revolution of 1688 are to be found anywhere, they are in the statute called the Declaration of Right. In that most wise, sober and considered declaration, drawn up by great lawyers and great statesmen and not by warm and inexperienced enthusiasts, not one word is said, and not one hint given, of a general right "to choose our own governors, to cashier them for misconduct, and to form a government for ourselves."

This Declaration of Right — the act of the 1st of William and Mary, session 2, chapter 2 — is the cornerstone of our constitution, reinforced, explained, improved, and settled for ever in its fundamental principles. It is called "An act for declaring the rights and liberties of the subject, and for settling the succession of the crown." You will notice that these rights and this succession are declared in one body and bound indissolubly together.

A few years later a second opportunity arose for asserting a right of election to the crown. With the prospect of a total failure of issue from King William and from the princess who afterwards became Queen Anne, the settlement of the crown, and a further security for the liberties of the people, came before the legislature again. Did they this second time make any provision for legalising the crown on the spurious Revolution principles of the Old Jewry? No. They followed the principles that prevailed in the Declaration of Right, indicating with more precision who was to inherit in the Protestant line. This act too, by the same policy, incorporated our liberties and a hereditary succession in one and the same act. Instead of a right to choose our own governors, they declared that the succession in that line — the Protestant line drawn from James the First — was absolutely necessary "for the peace, quiet, and security of the realm", and that it was equally pressing on them "to maintain a certainty in the succession thereof, to which the subjects may safely have recourse for their protection." Both these acts, in which we hear the unerring, unambiguous oracles of Revolution policy, instead of countenancing the delusive fortune-teller's prediction of a "right to choose our governors", prove conclusively how utterly opposed the wisdom of the nation was to turning a case of necessity into a rule of law.

Unquestionably there was at the Revolution, in the person of King William, a small and temporary deviation from the strict order of a regular hereditary succession. But it is against all genuine principles of jurisprudence to draw a principle from a law made in a special case and concerning an individual person. Privilegium non transit in exemplum: a privilege does not become a precedent. If ever there was a time favourable for establishing the principle that a king of popular choice was the only legal king, it was without doubt the Revolution. That it was not done then is proof that the nation held it ought never to be done at all. Nobody is so completely ignorant of our history as not to know that the majority in Parliament, of both parties, were so little inclined to anything resembling that principle that at first they were determined to place the vacant crown not on the head of the Prince of Orange but on that of his wife Mary, King James's daughter, the eldest of the children of that king, whom they acknowledged as undoubtedly his. It would be to repeat a very trite story to recall to your memory all the circumstances that showed their accepting King William was not properly a choice. For everyone who did not wish in effect to recall King James, or to drown their country in blood, and to bring their religion, laws and liberties back into the danger they had just escaped, it was an act of necessity, in the strictest moral sense the word can bear.

In the very act by which Parliament departed, for a time and in a single case, from the strict order of inheritance, in favour of a prince who, though not next, was very near in the line of succession, it is curious to see how Lord Somers, who drew the bill called the Declaration of Right, conducted himself on that delicate occasion. It is curious to see with what address this temporary break in continuity is kept out of sight, while everything in the act of necessity that could support the idea of a hereditary succession is brought forward, nursed, and made the most of by that great man and by the legislature that followed him. Leaving the dry, imperative style of an act of Parliament, he has the Lords and Commons break into a pious legislative ejaculation and declare that they consider it "as a marvellous providence, and merciful goodness of God to this nation, to preserve their said Majesties' royal persons most happily to reign over us on the throne of their ancestors, for which, from the bottom of their hearts, they return their humblest thanks and praises." The legislature plainly had in view the Act of Recognition of the first year of Queen Elizabeth, chapter 3, and that of James the First, chapter 1, both strongly declaring the inheritable nature of the crown; and in many places they follow, with nearly literal precision, the words and even the form of thanksgiving found in those old declaratory statutes.

The two Houses, in King William's act, did not thank God that they had found a fair opportunity to assert a right to choose their own governors, much less to make election the only lawful title to the crown. That they had been in a position to avoid even the appearance of it, as far as possible, was regarded by them as a providential escape. They threw a politic, well-worked veil over every circumstance tending to weaken the rights they meant to perpetuate in the improved order of succession, or that might provide a precedent for any future departure from what they had then settled for ever. Accordingly, so as not to slacken the sinews of their monarchy, and to keep close to the practice of their ancestors as it appeared in the declaratory statutes of Queen Mary and Queen Elizabeth, in the next clause they vest, by recognition, in their Majesties all the legal prerogatives of the crown, declaring "that in them they are most fully, rightfully, and entirely invested, incorporated, united, and annexed." In the clause that follows, to prevent questions arising from any pretended titles to the crown, they declare — observing here too the traditional language along with the traditional policy of the nation, and repeating as from a rubric the language of the preceding acts of Elizabeth and James — that on preserving "a certainty in the SUCCESSION thereof the unity, peace, and tranquillity of this nation doth, under God, wholly depend."

Footnote: 1st Mary, session 3, chapter 1.

They knew that a doubtful title of succession would look far too much like an election, and that an election would be utterly destructive of the "unity, peace, and tranquillity of this nation", which they thought considerations of some weight. To provide for these things, and so to shut out for ever the Old Jewry doctrine of "a right to choose our own governors", they follow with a clause containing the most solemn pledge, taken from the earlier act of Queen Elizabeth — as solemn a pledge as ever was or can be given in favour of a hereditary succession, and as solemn a renunciation as could be made of the principles this society imputes to them: "The Lords Spiritual and Temporal, and Commons, do, in the name of all the people aforesaid, most humbly and faithfully submit themselves, their heirs, and posterities forever; and do faithfully promise that they will stand to, maintain, and defend their said Majesties, and also the limitation of the crown, herein specified and contained, to the utmost of their powers," and so on.

So far is it from being true that we acquired a right at the Revolution to elect our kings, that if we had possessed such a right before, the English nation at that moment most solemnly renounced and abdicated it, for themselves and for all their posterity for ever. These gentlemen may value themselves as much as they please on their Whig principles; but I have no wish to be thought a better Whig than Lord Somers, or to understand the principles of the Revolution better than the men who brought it about, or to read into the Declaration of Right mysteries unknown to those whose penetrating style engraved the words and the spirit of that immortal law on our statute book and on our hearts.

It is true that, aided by the powers that came from force and opportunity, the nation was at that time in some sense free to take whatever course it pleased for filling the throne — but only free to do so on the same grounds on which it might have abolished the monarchy altogether, and every other part of its constitution. They did not, however, think such bold changes within their commission. It is indeed difficult, perhaps impossible, to set limits to the mere abstract competence of the supreme power, such as Parliament exercised at that time. But the limits of a moral competence, which subjects even the most indisputably sovereign powers, and subjects momentary will to permanent reason and to the steady maxims of faith, justice and settled fundamental policy, are perfectly intelligible and perfectly binding on everyone who exercises any authority in the state, under any name or any title. The House of Lords, for instance, is not morally competent to dissolve the House of Commons — nor even to dissolve itself, nor to give up, if it wished to, its share in the legislature of the kingdom. A king may abdicate for his own person, but he cannot abdicate for the monarchy. By as strong a reason, or a stronger, the House of Commons cannot renounce its share of authority. The engagement and pact of society, which generally goes by the name of the constitution, forbids such an invasion and such a surrender. The constituent parts of a state are as much obliged to keep public faith with one another, and with everyone who has any serious interest depending on their engagements, as the whole state is bound to keep faith with separate communities. Otherwise competence and power would soon be confounded, and no law would be left but the will of whatever force prevailed. On this principle the succession of the crown has always been what it now is, a hereditary succession by law: in the old line a succession by common law, in the new by statute law operating on the principles of the common law, not changing the substance but regulating the manner and naming the persons. Both kinds of law have the same force and are derived from an equal authority, flowing from the common agreement and original compact of the state, communi sponsione reipublicæ, and as such are equally binding on king and people alike, as long as the terms are observed and they continue the same body politic.

It is far from impossible, if we do not let ourselves be tangled in the mazes of metaphysical sophistry, to reconcile the use of a fixed rule with an occasional deviation from it — the sacredness of a hereditary principle of succession in our government with a power of changing its application in cases of extreme emergency. Even in that extremity, if we take the measure of our rights from the way we exercised them at the Revolution, the change is to be confined to the diseased part only, to the part that made the deviation necessary; and even then it is to be done without decomposing the whole civil and political mass in order to originate a new civil order out of the first elements of society.

A state without the means of some change is without the means of its conservation. Without such means it might even risk losing the very part of the constitution it most religiously wished to preserve. The two principles of conservation and correction operated strongly at the two critical periods of the Restoration and the Revolution, when England found itself without a king. At both those moments the nation had lost the binding element in its ancient building; and yet it did not pull the whole fabric down. On the contrary, in both cases they regenerated the defective part of the old constitution through the parts that were not damaged. They kept the old parts exactly as they were, so that the recovered part could be fitted to them. They acted through the ancient organised estates in the shape of their old organisation, and not through the loose moleculæ of a disbanded people. Perhaps at no time did the sovereign legislature show a more tender regard for that fundamental principle of British constitutional policy than at the Revolution, when it deviated from the direct line of hereditary succession. The crown was carried a little way out of the line it had moved in before; but the new line was drawn from the same stock. It was still a line of hereditary descent, still a hereditary descent in the same blood, though a hereditary descent qualified by Protestantism. In altering the direction while keeping the principle, the legislature showed that it held the principle inviolable.

On this principle the law of inheritance had been amended before, in the old days and long before the era of the Revolution. Some time after the Conquest great questions arose about the legal principles of hereditary descent. It became doubtful whether the heir per capita or the heir per stirpes was to succeed; but whether the heir per capita gave way when heirship per stirpes took effect, or the Catholic heir when the Protestant was preferred, the inheritable principle survived with a kind of immortality through all its transmigrations —

	Multosque per annos
	Stat fortuna domûs, et avi numerantur avorum.

This is the spirit of our constitution, not only in its settled course but in all its revolutions. Whoever came in, and however he came in, whether he got the crown by law or by force, the hereditary succession was either continued or adopted.

The gentlemen of the Society for Revolutions see nothing in the Revolution of 1688 but the deviation from the constitution, and they take the deviation from the principle for the principle itself. They pay little attention to the obvious consequences of their doctrine, though they might notice that it leaves positive authority in very few of the positive institutions of this country. Once such an indefensible maxim is established — that no throne is lawful but an elective one — not one act of the princes who reigned before this era of fictitious election can be valid. Do these theorists mean to imitate certain of their predecessors, who dragged the bodies of our ancient sovereigns out of the quiet of their tombs? Do they mean to attaint and disqualify, retrospectively, every king who reigned before the Revolution, and so to stain the throne of England with the blot of a continual usurpation? Do they mean to invalidate, annul or call into question, along with the titles of the whole line of our kings, that great body of statute law that was passed under the men they treat as usurpers? To annul laws of inestimable value to our liberties, of at least as great value as any that have been passed at or since the Revolution? If kings who did not owe their crown to the choice of their people had no title to make laws, what becomes of the statute De tallagio non concedendo? Of the Petition of Right? Of the Habeas Corpus Act? Do these new doctors of the rights of men presume to assert that King James the Second, who came to the crown as next of blood, by the rules of a then unqualified succession, was not to all intents and purposes a lawful king of England before he had done any of the acts that were justly construed as an abdication of his crown? If he was not, a great deal of trouble in Parliament might have been saved at the very period these gentlemen commemorate. But King James was a bad king with a good title, not a usurper. The princes who succeeded under the act of Parliament that settled the crown on the Electress Sophia and her Protestant descendants came in as much by a title of inheritance as King James did. He came in according to the law as it stood at his accession, and the princes of the House of Brunswick came to the inheritance of the crown not by election but by the law as it stood at their several accessions, by Protestant descent and inheritance, as I hope I have sufficiently shown.

The law by which this royal family is specifically destined to the succession is the act of the 12th and 13th of King William. Its terms bind "us, and our heirs, and our posterity, to them, their heirs, and their posterity", being Protestants, to the end of time, in the same words in which the Declaration of Right bound us to the heirs of King William and Queen Mary. It therefore secures both a hereditary crown and a hereditary allegiance. On what ground, except the constitutional policy of building an establishment that would secure the kind of succession which shuts out a choice of the people for ever, could the legislature have fastidiously rejected the fair and abundant choice our own country offered them, and searched in foreign lands for a foreign princess, from whose womb the line of our future rulers was to derive its title to govern millions of men through a series of ages?

The Princess Sophia was named in the Act of Settlement of the 12th and 13th of King William as a stock and root of inheritance for our kings, and not for her merits as a temporary administrator of a power she might never exercise, and in fact never did. She was adopted for one reason and one only: because, says the act, "the most excellent Princess Sophia, Electress and Duchess Dowager of Hanover, is daughter of the most excellent Princess Elizabeth, late Queen of Bohemia, daughter of our late sovereign lord King James the First, of happy memory, and is hereby declared to be the next in succession in the Protestant line", and so on; "and the crown shall continue to the heirs of her body, being Protestants." Parliament made this limitation so that through the Princess Sophia an inheritable line would not only be continued in future, but — what they thought very material — would be connected through her with the old stock of inheritance in King James the First, so that the monarchy might keep an unbroken unity through all ages, and might be preserved, with safety to our religion, in the old approved way of descent, in which, if our liberties had once been endangered, they had often been preserved through all the storms and struggles of prerogative and privilege. They did well. No experience has taught us that our liberties can be regularly perpetuated and kept as sacred as our hereditary right by any course or method other than a hereditary crown. An irregular, convulsive movement may be necessary to throw off an irregular, convulsive disease. But the course of succession is the healthy habit of the British constitution. Was it that the legislature, in the act limiting the crown to the Hanoverian line drawn through the female descendants of James the First, lacked a due sense of the inconveniences of having two or three, or possibly more, foreigners in succession to the British throne? No. They had a due sense of the evils that might come from such foreign rule, and more than a due sense of them. But no more decisive proof could be given of the full conviction of the British nation that the principles of the Revolution did not authorise them to elect kings at their pleasure, without any attention to the ancient fundamental principles of our government, than that they went on adopting a plan of hereditary Protestant succession in the old line with all the dangers and inconveniences of a foreign line full in their view, and pressing on their minds with the utmost force.

A few years ago I should have been ashamed to burden a matter so well able to support itself with the then unnecessary support of any argument. But this seditious, unconstitutional doctrine is now publicly taught, avowed and printed. My dislike of revolutions, whose signals have so often been given from pulpits; the spirit of change that has gone abroad; the total contempt that prevails with you, and may come to prevail with us, for all ancient institutions when they are set against a present sense of convenience or the bent of a present inclination — all these considerations make it advisable, in my opinion, to call our attention back to the true principles of our own domestic laws, so that you, my French friend, may begin to know them, and that we may go on cherishing them. We ought not, on either side of the water, to let ourselves be imposed on by the counterfeit goods that certain people, by a double fraud, export to you in illicit bottoms as raw commodities of British growth, though they are wholly alien to our soil, in order afterwards to smuggle them back into this country manufactured in the newest Paris fashion of an improved liberty.

The people of England will not ape fashions they have never tried, or go back to ones they have found mischievous on trial. They look on the legal hereditary succession of their crown as one of their rights, not one of their wrongs — as a benefit, not a grievance; as a security for their liberty, not a badge of servitude. They look on the frame of their commonwealth, such as it stands, as of inestimable value, and they consider the undisturbed succession of the crown a pledge of the stability and permanence of every other member of our constitution.

Before I go any further, let me take notice of some paltry tricks that the champions of election as the only lawful title to the crown are ready to use, in order to make defending the just principles of our constitution a somewhat invidious task. These sophists substitute a fictitious cause and imaginary persons, whom they suppose you to be defending, whenever you defend the inheritable nature of the crown. It is common with them to argue as if they were in conflict with some of those exploded fanatics of slavery who used to maintain — what I believe no creature maintains now — "that the crown is held by divine, hereditary, and indefeasible right". Those old fanatics of single arbitrary power laid it down as dogma that hereditary royalty was the only lawful government in the world, just as our new fanatics of popular arbitrary power maintain that a popular election is the sole lawful source of authority. The old prerogative enthusiasts did indeed speculate foolishly, and perhaps impiously too, as if monarchy had more of a divine sanction than any other form of government, and as if a right to govern by inheritance were strictly indefeasible in every person found in the succession to a throne, and in every circumstance — which no civil or political right can be. But an absurd opinion about the king's hereditary right to the crown does not damage a rational one, founded on solid principles of law and policy. If all the absurd theories of lawyers and divines were to spoil the subjects they deal with, we should have no law and no religion left in the world. But an absurd theory on one side of a question is no justification for alleging a false fact, or promulgating mischievous maxims, on the other.

Section Three: The Right to Cashier a King for Misconduct

The second claim of the Revolution Society is "a right of cashiering their governors for misconduct". Perhaps our ancestors' fear of setting a precedent for "cashiering for misconduct" is why the declaration of the act that implied King James's abdication was, if it had any fault, rather too guarded and too circumstantial. But all this guarding, and all this piling up of circumstances, serves to show the spirit of caution that prevailed in the national councils at a moment when men irritated by oppression, and elated by a triumph over it, are apt to give themselves up to violent and extreme courses. It shows how anxious the great men who directed affairs at that great event were to make the Revolution the parent of a settlement and not the nursery of future revolutions.

Footnote: "That King James the Second, having endeavored to subvert the Constitution of the kingdom, by breaking the original contract between king and people, and, by the advice of Jesuits and other wicked persons, having violated the fundamental laws, and having withdrawn himself out of the kingdom, hath abdicated the government, and the throne is thereby vacant."

No government could stand for a moment if it could be blown down by anything as loose and indefinite as an opinion of "misconduct". The men who led at the Revolution grounded their virtual abdication of King James on no such light and uncertain principle. They charged him with nothing less than a design, confirmed by a multitude of illegal overt acts, to subvert the Protestant Church and State and their fundamental, unquestionable laws and liberties; they charged him with having broken the original contract between king and people. This was more than misconduct. A grave and overruling necessity obliged them to take the step they took, and they took it with infinite reluctance, as under the most rigorous of all laws. Their trust for preserving the constitution in future was not placed in future revolutions. The grand policy behind all their regulations was to make it almost impossible for any future sovereign to force the estates of the kingdom to resort to those violent remedies again. They left the crown what in the eye and estimation of law it had always been: perfectly irresponsible. And in order to lighten the crown still further, they loaded responsibility onto ministers of state. By the statute of the 1st of King William, session 2, called "the act for declaring the rights and liberties of the subject, and for settling the succession of the crown", they enacted that ministers should serve the crown on the terms of that declaration. Soon afterwards they secured the frequent meetings of Parliament, by which the whole government would be under the constant inspection and active control of the popular representatives and of the great men of the kingdom. In the next great constitutional act, that of the 12th and 13th of King William, for the further limitation of the crown and for better securing the rights and liberties of the subject, they provided "that no pardon under the great seal of England should be pleadable to an impeachment by the Commons in Parliament". The rule laid down for government in the Declaration of Right, the constant inspection of Parliament, and the practical claim of impeachment they thought an infinitely better security, not only for their constitutional liberty but against the vices of an administration, than reserving a right so difficult in practice, so uncertain in its outcome, and often so mischievous in its consequences, as this "cashiering of their governors".

Dr Price in his sermon very properly condemns the practice of addressing kings in grossly flattering terms. Instead of that fulsome style, he proposes that His Majesty should be told, on occasions of congratulation, that "he is to consider himself as more properly the servant than the sovereign of his people". As a compliment, this new form of address does not seem very soothing. Those who are servants in name as well as in fact do not like to be reminded of their situation, their duty and their obligations. The slave in the old play tells his master, "Hæc commemoratio est quasi exprobratio" — to be reminded of a favour is as good as to be reproached with it. It is not pleasant as a compliment; it is not wholesome as instruction. And after all, if the king were to bring himself to echo this new kind of address, to adopt it in so many words, and even to take the title Servant of the People as his royal style, I cannot imagine how either he or we would be much the better for it. I have seen very high-handed letters signed "Your most obedient, humble servant". The proudest domination ever endured on earth took a title of even greater humility than the one now proposed for sovereigns by the Apostle of Liberty. Kings and nations were trampled under the foot of a man calling himself "The Servant of Servants", and orders for deposing sovereigns were sealed with the signet of "The Fisherman".

Footnote: Pp. 23, 23, 24.

I should have thought all this no more than a sort of flippant, empty talk, in which, as in a stale vapour, several people let the spirit of liberty evaporate — if it were not plainly in support of the idea, and part of the scheme, of "cashiering kings for misconduct". Seen in that light it deserves some remark.

Kings are undoubtedly the servants of the people in one sense, because their power has no other rational end than the general advantage. But it is not true that they are servants in the ordinary sense, at least under our constitution: the essence of a servant's position is to obey somebody else's commands and to be removable at pleasure. The king of Great Britain obeys nobody. All other persons, individually and collectively, are under him and owe him a legal obedience. The law, which knows neither how to flatter nor how to insult, calls this high magistrate not our servant, as this humble divine calls him, but "our sovereign lord the king"; and we for our part have learned to speak only the original language of the law, and not the confused jargon of their Babylonian pulpits.

Since he is not to obey us, but we are to obey the law in him, our constitution has made no provision of any kind for making him, as a servant, responsible in any degree. Our constitution knows nothing of a magistrate like the Justicia of Aragon, nor of any legally appointed court, nor of any legally settled process, for subjecting the king to the responsibility that belongs to all servants. In this he is no different from the Commons and the Lords, who in their several public capacities can never be called to account for their conduct — although the Revolution Society chooses to assert, in direct opposition to one of the wisest and most beautiful parts of our constitution, that "a king is no more than the first servant of the public, created by it, and responsible to it".

Our ancestors at the Revolution would have deserved their fame for wisdom very ill if they had found no security for their freedom except in making their government feeble in its operation and precarious in its tenure — if they had been able to contrive no better remedy against arbitrary power than civil confusion. Let these gentlemen state who this representative public is to whom they will say the king, as a servant, is responsible. It will then be time enough for me to produce the positive statute law that says he is not.

The ceremony of cashiering kings, which these gentlemen talk about so easily, can rarely if ever be performed without force. It then becomes a case of war and not of constitution. Laws are ordered to hold their tongues among weapons, and tribunals fall to the ground along with the peace they can no longer uphold. The Revolution of 1688 was obtained by a just war, in the only case in which any war, and much more a civil war, can be just: "Justa bella quibus NECESSARIA" — those wars are just which are necessary. The question of dethroning a king, or, if these gentlemen prefer the phrase, of "cashiering" him, will always be, as it always has been, an extraordinary question of state and wholly outside the law: a question, like every other question of state, of dispositions and of means and of probable consequences, rather than of positive rights. As it was not made for common abuses, so it is not to be debated by common minds. The theoretical line of demarcation, where obedience ought to end and resistance must begin, is faint, obscure and not easily defined. It is not a single act or a single event that determines it. Governments must be abused and deranged indeed before it can even be thought of, and the prospect of the future must be as bad as the experience of the past. When things are in that lamentable condition, the nature of the disease indicates the remedy to those whom nature has qualified to administer, in extremities, this critical, ambiguous, bitter medicine to a diseased state. Times and occasions and provocations will teach their own lessons. The wise will decide from the gravity of the case; the irritable from their sensitivity to oppression; the high-minded from disdain and indignation at abusive power in unworthy hands; the brave and bold from love of honourable danger in a generous cause. But with or without right, a revolution will be the very last resort of the thinking and the good.

Section Four: The Right to Frame a Government for Ourselves

The third head of right asserted from the pulpit of the Old Jewry — the "right to form a government for ourselves" — has at least as little support from anything done at the Revolution, in precedent or in principle, as the first two of their claims. The Revolution was made to preserve our ancient indisputable laws and liberties, and that ancient constitution of government which is our only security for law and liberty. If you want to know the spirit of our constitution, and the policy that prevailed in that great period which has secured it to this hour, look for both in our histories, in our records, in our acts of Parliament and journals of Parliament, and not in the sermons of the Old Jewry and the after-dinner toasts of the Revolution Society. In the first you will find other ideas and another language. Such a claim is as ill suited to our temper and our wishes as it is unsupported by any appearance of authority. The very idea of manufacturing a new government is enough to fill us with disgust and horror. At the time of the Revolution we wished, and we still wish, to derive everything we possess as an inheritance from our forefathers. On that body and stock of inheritance we have taken care not to graft any shoot alien to the nature of the original plant. All the reforms we have made so far have proceeded on the principle of referring back to antiquity, and I hope — indeed I am persuaded — that any that may be made hereafter will be carefully framed on precedent, authority and example by analogy.

Our oldest reformation is Magna Carta. You will find that Sir Edward Coke, that great oracle of our law, and indeed all the great men who follow him down to Blackstone, are industrious in proving the pedigree of our liberties. They set out to prove that the ancient charter, the Magna Carta of King John, was connected with another positive charter from Henry the First, and that both were nothing more than a re-affirmation of the still more ancient standing law of the kingdom. As a matter of fact these authors appear to be right for the most part, though perhaps not always. But if the lawyers are mistaken in some particulars, that proves my point all the more strongly, because it shows the powerful predisposition towards antiquity with which the minds of all our lawyers and legislators, and of all the people they wish to influence, have always been filled, and the settled policy of this kingdom in regarding its most sacred rights and franchises as an inheritance.

Footnote: See Blackstone's Magna Carta, printed at Oxford, 1759.

In the famous law of the 3rd of Charles the First, called the Petition of Right, Parliament says to the king, "Your subjects have inherited this freedom" — claiming their franchises not on abstract principles, "as the rights of men", but as the rights of Englishmen, and as a patrimony handed down from their forefathers. Selden and the other profoundly learned men who drew up this Petition of Right were at least as well acquainted with all the general theories about the "rights of men" as anyone who discourses in our pulpits or on your tribune — quite as well as Dr Price, or as the Abbé Sieyès. But for reasons worthy of the practical wisdom that displaced their theoretical science, they preferred this positive, recorded, hereditary title to everything that can be dear to a man and a citizen, over that vague speculative right which would have exposed their secure inheritance to be scrambled for and torn to pieces by every wild and litigious spirit.

The same policy runs through all the laws that have been made since for the preservation of our liberties. In the 1st of William and Mary, in the famous statute called the Declaration of Right, the two Houses do not utter a syllable about "a right to frame a government for themselves". You will see that their whole care was to secure the religion, laws and liberties that had long been possessed and had lately been endangered. "Taking into their most serious consideration the best means for making such an establishment that their religion, laws, and liberties might not be in danger of being again subverted," they open all their proceedings by naming among those best means, "in the first place", to do "as their ancestors in like cases have usually done for vindicating their ancient rights and liberties, to declare"; and then they pray the king and queen "that it may be declared and enacted that all and singular the rights and liberties asserted and declared are the true ancient and indubitable rights and liberties of the people of this kingdom."

Footnote: 1 William and Mary.

You will notice that from Magna Carta to the Declaration of Right it has been the uniform policy of our constitution to claim and assert our liberties as an entailed inheritance, handed down to us from our forefathers and to be handed on to our posterity — as an estate belonging specifically to the people of this kingdom, without any reference whatever to any more general or prior right. By this means our constitution preserves a unity in so great a diversity of parts. We have an inheritable crown, an inheritable peerage, and a House of Commons and a people inheriting privileges, franchises and liberties from a long line of ancestors.

This policy seems to me the result of profound reflection — or rather the happy effect of following nature, which is wisdom without reflection, and above it. A spirit of innovation is generally the product of a selfish temper and narrow views. People who never look back to their ancestors will not look forward to their posterity either. Besides, the people of England know very well that the idea of inheritance supplies a sure principle of conservation and a sure principle of transmission, without in the least excluding a principle of improvement. It leaves acquisition free, but it secures what it acquires. Whatever advantages a state gains by proceeding on these maxims are locked up fast, as in a sort of family settlement, held as in a kind of perpetual trust. By a constitutional policy that works on the pattern of nature, we receive, we hold and we transmit our government and our privileges in the same way that we enjoy and transmit our property and our lives. The institutions of statecraft, the goods of fortune, the gifts of Providence come down to us, and pass on from us, in the same course and order. Our political system is placed in a just correspondence and symmetry with the order of the world, and with the mode of existence decreed to a permanent body made up of transitory parts — in which, by the disposition of a stupendous wisdom that moulds together the great mysterious incorporation of the human race, the whole at any one moment is never old, or middle-aged, or young, but, in a condition of unchangeable constancy, moves on through the varied course of perpetual decay, fall, renewal and progress. So, by keeping to the method of nature in the conduct of the state, in what we improve we are never wholly new, and in what we retain we are never wholly obsolete. By sticking to our forefathers in this way and on these principles, we are guided not by the superstition of antiquarians but by the spirit of philosophic analogy. In this choice of inheritance we have given our frame of government the image of a relation in blood: binding up the constitution of our country with our dearest domestic ties, adopting our fundamental laws into the bosom of our family affections, and keeping inseparable, and cherishing with the warmth of all their combined and mutually reflected affections, our state, our hearths, our graves and our altars.

By this same plan of conforming to nature in our artificial institutions, and by calling in the aid of her unerring and powerful instincts to fortify the fallible and feeble contrivances of our reason, we have derived several other benefits, and no small ones, from regarding our liberties as an inheritance. Always acting as though in the presence of canonised forefathers, the spirit of freedom, which left to itself leads to misrule and excess, is tempered with an awful gravity. This idea of a liberal descent inspires us with a sense of habitual inborn dignity, which prevents that upstart insolence almost inevitably clinging to, and disgracing, those who are the first acquirers of any distinction. By this means our liberty becomes a noble freedom. It has an imposing and majestic aspect. It has a pedigree and illustrious ancestors. It has its bearings and its heraldic arms. It has its gallery of portraits, its monumental inscriptions, its records, its evidences and its titles. We win reverence for our civil institutions on the same principle by which nature teaches us to revere individual men: on account of their age, and on account of those they are descended from. All your sophists cannot produce anything better adapted to preserving a rational and manly freedom than the course we have followed, who have chosen our nature rather than our theories, our hearts rather than our inventions, as the great storehouses and magazines of our rights and privileges.

Section Five: What France Might Have Done Instead

You might, if you had chosen, have profited by our example and given your recovered freedom a matching dignity. Your privileges, though they had lapsed, were not lost to memory. Your constitution, it is true, had suffered waste and dilapidation while you were out of possession of it; but you still had the walls of a noble and venerable castle in some parts, and its foundations everywhere. You might have repaired those walls; you might have built on those old foundations. Your constitution was suspended before it was perfected, but you had the elements of a constitution very nearly as good as anyone could wish. In your old estates you had that variety of parts corresponding to the various descriptions of people your community was happily composed of. You had all that combination and all that opposition of interests, that action and counteraction, which in the natural world and in the political world alike draws the harmony of the universe out of the reciprocal struggle of discordant powers. These opposed and conflicting interests, which you regarded as such a great blemish in your old constitution and in our present one, interpose a healthy check on all hasty resolutions. They make deliberation a matter not of choice but of necessity. They make every change a subject of compromise, which naturally breeds moderation. They produce temperaments that prevent the grievous evil of harsh, crude, unqualified reformations and make all the headlong exertions of arbitrary power, whether by the few or by the many, for ever impracticable. Through that diversity of members and interests, general liberty had as many securities as there were separate points of view in the several orders; while by pressing the whole down with the weight of a real monarchy, the separate parts would have been kept from warping and springing out of their allotted places.

You had all these advantages in your ancient estates, and yet you chose to act as though you had never been formed into a civil society and had everything to begin over again. You began badly, because you began by despising everything that belonged to you. You set up in trade without any capital. If the last few generations of your countrymen seemed to you to have little lustre, you might have passed them over and drawn your claims from an earlier race of ancestors. Under a pious preference for those ancestors your imagination would have made them into a standard of virtue and wisdom above the common practice of the moment, and you would have risen towards the example you aspired to imitate. In respecting your forefathers you would have been taught to respect yourselves. You would not have chosen to regard the French as a people of yesterday, a nation of low-born, servile wretches until the emancipating year of 1789. You would not have been content, in order to furnish your apologists over here with an excuse for several of your enormities at the expense of your own honour, to be represented as a gang of Maroons suddenly broken loose from the house of bondage, and therefore to be forgiven for abusing a liberty you were unused to and ill fitted for.

Would it not have been wiser, my worthy friend, to be thought what I for one have always thought you: a generous and gallant nation, long misled to its own disadvantage by its high and romantic feelings of fidelity, honour and loyalty; that events had gone against you, but that you were not enslaved through any mean or servile disposition; that even in your most devoted submission you were moved by a principle of public spirit, and that it was your country you worshipped, in the person of your king? If you had given it to be understood that in the delusion of this amiable error you had gone further than your wise ancestors, and that you were resolved to resume your ancient privileges while keeping the spirit of your ancient and your recent loyalty and honour — or if, distrusting yourselves and not clearly making out the almost obliterated constitution of your ancestors, you had looked across to your neighbours in this country, who have kept alive the ancient principles and models of the old common law of Europe, improved and adapted to its present state — then by following wise examples you would have given new examples of wisdom to the world. You would have made the cause of liberty venerable in the eyes of every worthy mind in every nation. You would have shamed despotism off the earth by showing that freedom is not only reconcilable with law but, when it is well disciplined, an ally of it. You would have had a revenue that was productive without being oppressive. You would have had a flourishing commerce to feed it. You would have had a free constitution, a powerful monarchy, a disciplined army, a reformed and respected clergy; a nobility with its edges taken off but its spirit intact, to lead your virtue and not to smother it; a liberal order of commons to rival that nobility and to recruit it; and a protected, contented, industrious and obedient people, taught to look for and to recognise the happiness that virtue can find in every condition of life. That is where the true moral equality of mankind lies — not in that monstrous fiction which, by putting false ideas and vain expectations into the heads of men destined to travel the obscure path of laborious life, serves only to aggravate and embitter the real inequality it can never remove, and which the order of civil life establishes as much for the benefit of those it must leave in a humble state as of those it is able to raise to a more splendid condition, though not a happier one. You had a smooth and easy career of happiness and glory laid open before you, beyond anything recorded in the history of the world; but you have shown that difficulty is good for man.

Add up your gains. See what has been got by those extravagant and presumptuous theories that taught your leaders to despise all their predecessors and all their contemporaries, and even to despise themselves, until the moment when they became genuinely despicable. By following those false lights France has bought undisguised calamities at a higher price than any nation ever paid for the most unequivocal blessings. France has bought poverty with crime. France has not sacrificed her virtue to her interest; she has abandoned her interest in order to prostitute her virtue. Every other nation has begun the building of a new government, or the reform of an old one, by establishing some rite of religion or other, or by enforcing it more exactly. Every other people has laid the foundations of civil freedom in stricter manners and a more austere and masculine system of morality. France, when she slackened the reins of royal authority, doubled the licence of a ferocious dissoluteness in manners and an insolent irreligion in opinion and practice, and has spread through every rank of life, as if she were sharing out a privilege or opening up some benefit that had been kept back, all the unhappy corruptions that used to be the diseases of wealth and power. This is one of the new principles of equality in France.

France, through the treachery of her leaders, has utterly disgraced the tone of mild counsel in the cabinets of princes and stripped it of its most powerful arguments. She has sanctified the dark, suspicious maxims of tyrannical distrust, and taught kings to tremble at what will hereafter be called the delusive plausibilities of moral politicians. Sovereigns will now regard anyone who advises them to place unlimited confidence in their people as subverters of their thrones, as traitors aiming at their destruction by leading their easy good nature, under specious pretexts, into admitting combinations of bold and faithless men to a share of their power. This alone, if there were nothing else, is an irreparable calamity to you and to mankind. Remember that your Parliament of Paris told your king that in calling the estates together he had nothing to fear but the extravagant excess of their zeal in providing for the support of the throne. It is right that these men should hide their heads. It is right that they should bear their part in the ruin their counsel has brought on their sovereign and their country. Such sanguine assurances tend to lull authority to sleep, to encourage it to plunge rashly into perilous adventures of untried policy, to neglect the provisions, preparations and precautions that distinguish benevolence from imbecility, and without which no man can answer for the beneficial effect of any abstract plan of government or of freedom. For want of these, they have seen the medicine of the state corrupted into its poison.
