They have seen the French rebel against a mild and lawful monarch with more fury, outrage and insult than any people has ever been known to rise against the most illegal usurper or the most bloodthirsty tyrant. Their resistance was made to concession; their revolt was from protection; their blow was aimed at a hand held out with favours, graces and immunities in it.

This was unnatural. The rest follows in order. They have found their punishment in their success. Laws overturned; courts subverted; industry without vigour; commerce dying; the revenue unpaid and yet the people impoverished; a church plundered and a state no better off for it; civil and military anarchy made into the constitution of the kingdom; everything human and divine sacrificed to the idol of public credit, and national bankruptcy the consequence. And to crown it all, the paper securities of a new, precarious, tottering power — the discredited paper securities of impoverished fraud and beggared plunder — held out as a currency to support an empire, in place of the two great recognised metals that stand for the lasting conventional credit of mankind, and which vanished and hid themselves in the earth they came from as soon as the principle of property, whose creatures and representatives they are, was systematically subverted.

Were all these dreadful things necessary? Were they the inevitable results of a desperate struggle by determined patriots, forced to wade through blood and tumult to the quiet shore of a tranquil and prosperous liberty? No. Nothing of the kind. The fresh ruins of France, which shock our feelings wherever we turn our eyes, are not the devastation of a civil war. They are the sad but instructive monuments of rash and ignorant counsel in a time of profound peace. They are the display of an authority that is inconsiderate and presumptuous because it has been unresisted and irresistible. The men who have squandered away the precious treasure of their crimes in this manner, who have made this prodigal and wild waste of public evils — the last stake reserved for the ultimate ransom of a state — met on their way with little opposition, or rather with none at all. Their whole march was more like a triumphal procession than the progress of a war. Their pioneers went before them and demolished everything and laid it level at their feet. Not one drop of their blood have they shed for the country they have ruined. They have sacrificed nothing to their projects of any more consequence than their shoe buckles, while they were imprisoning their king, murdering their fellow citizens, and plunging thousands of worthy men and worthy families into tears and poverty and distress. Their cruelty has not even been the base product of fear. It has been the effect of their sense of perfect safety, in authorising treasons, robberies, rapes, assassinations, massacres and burnings throughout their harassed land. But the cause of it all was plain from the beginning.

Section Six: Who the Men of the National Assembly Actually Are

This unforced choice, this fond preference for evil, would seem perfectly unaccountable if we did not consider what the National Assembly is made of — I do not mean its formal constitution, objectionable enough as that now stands, but the materials it is largely composed of, which matter ten thousand times more than all the formalities in the world. If we knew nothing of this assembly but its title and its function, no colours could paint anything more venerable to the imagination. Seen in that light, the mind of an inquirer, overawed by so solemn an image as the virtue and wisdom of a whole people gathered into one focus, would pause and hesitate before condemning things even of the very worst appearance. Instead of blameable they would seem merely mysterious. But no name, no power, no function, no artificial institution whatever can make the men who compose any system of authority anything other than God and nature and education and their habits of life have made them. The people have no capacities beyond these to give. Virtue and wisdom may be what they aim at in their choice, but their choice confers neither on the men they lay their ordaining hands upon. They have no undertaking from nature and no promise from revelation for any such powers.

After I had read through the list of the persons and descriptions elected to the Tiers État, nothing they afterwards did could seem astonishing. Among them, it is true, I saw some men of known rank and some of shining talents; but not one man of any practical experience in government was to be found. The best of them were only men of theory. But whatever the distinguished few may have been, it is the substance and mass of a body that gives it its character and must in the end determine its direction. In all bodies, those who mean to lead must also, to a considerable degree, follow. They have to fit their proposals to the taste and the talent and the disposition of the men they wish to conduct. So if an assembly is viciously or feebly composed in a very large part of it, nothing but such a supreme degree of virtue as very rarely appears in the world — and which for that reason cannot be counted on — will prevent the men of talent scattered through it from becoming merely the expert instruments of absurd projects. If, as is more likely, instead of that unusual degree of virtue they are driven by sinister ambition and a lust for showy glory, then the feeble part of the assembly, which they conform to at first, becomes in its turn the dupe and the instrument of their designs. In this political trade the leaders will have to bow to the ignorance of their followers, and the followers to become subservient to the worst designs of their leaders.

To secure any degree of sobriety in the proposals made by the leaders of a public assembly, they ought to respect, and perhaps to some degree to fear, the men they are conducting. To be led in any way but blindly, the followers must be qualified, if not to be actors, at least to be judges — and judges of natural weight and authority. Nothing can secure a steady and moderate conduct in such assemblies but that the body of them should be respectably composed in point of station in life, of permanent property, of education, and of the habits that enlarge and liberalise the understanding.

In the calling of the States-General of France, the first thing that struck me was a great departure from the ancient course. I found the representation of the third estate composed of six hundred persons. They were equal in number to the representatives of both the other orders together. If the orders were to act separately, the number would not, beyond the question of expense, matter much. But once it became clear that the three orders were to be melted down into one, the policy and the necessary effect of this numerous representation became obvious. A very small desertion from either of the other two orders must throw the power of both into the hands of the third. In fact, the whole power of the state was soon resolved into that body. What it was made of therefore became infinitely more important.

Judge my surprise, Sir, when I found that a very large proportion of the Assembly — a majority, I believe, of the members who actually attended — consisted of practitioners of the law. And not of distinguished judges, who had given their country pledges of their learning, prudence and integrity; not of leading advocates, the glory of the bar; not of renowned university professors; but for far the greater part, as with such numbers it had to be, of the inferior, unlearned, mechanical, merely instrumental members of the profession. There were distinguished exceptions, but the general composition was of obscure provincial advocates, of stewards of petty local jurisdictions, country attorneys, notaries, and the whole train of the ministers of municipal litigation, the stirrers-up and conductors of the petty war of village vexation. From the moment I read the list I saw distinctly, and very nearly as it has turned out, everything that was to follow.

The degree of esteem in which any profession is held becomes the measure of the esteem in which its members hold themselves. Whatever the personal merits of many individual lawyers may have been — and in many they were undoubtedly very considerable — in that military kingdom no part of the profession had been much regarded except the very highest, who often joined great family splendour to their professional offices and were invested with great power and authority. Those were certainly highly respected, and with no small degree of awe. The next rank was not much esteemed, and the mechanical part of the profession stood in very low repute.

Whenever supreme authority is vested in a body composed like this, it must plainly produce the consequences of supreme authority placed in the hands of men not brought up in the habit of respecting themselves, who had no previous fortune of character at stake, and who could not be expected to bear with moderation, or to manage with discretion, a power that they themselves must be more surprised than anyone to find in their hands. Who could flatter himself that these men, snatched suddenly and as if by enchantment out of the humblest rank of subordination, would not be intoxicated by a greatness they had not been prepared for? Who could imagine that men habitually meddling, daring, subtle, busy, of litigious dispositions and restless minds, would fall easily back into their old condition of obscure squabbling and laborious, low, unprofitable trickery? Who could doubt that, at any cost to a state they understood nothing about, they must pursue their private interests, which they understood only too well? This was not an event that depended on chance or contingency. It was inevitable; it was necessary; it was planted in the nature of things. They must join in — if their capacity did not allow them to lead — any project that could procure them a litigious constitution, and lay open to them the innumerable lucrative jobs that follow in the train of all great convulsions and revolutions in a state, and particularly of all great and violent transfers of property. Was it to be expected that they would attend to the stability of property, when their own livelihood had always depended on whatever made property questionable, ambiguous and insecure? Their objects would grow larger with their elevation; but their disposition, their habits and their way of accomplishing their designs must stay the same.

Well, but these men were to be tempered and restrained by other sorts of men, of soberer minds and larger understandings. Were they to be overawed, then, by the pre-eminent authority and solemn dignity of a handful of country bumpkins who have seats in that assembly, some of whom are said to be unable to read and write — and by no greater a number of tradesmen who, though somewhat better instructed and more conspicuous in the order of society, had never known anything beyond their counting house? No. Both these sorts were far better formed to be overborne and swayed by the intrigues and artifices of lawyers than to be a counterweight to them. With so dangerous a disproportion, the whole must inevitably be governed by them.

To the faculty of law was joined a fairly considerable proportion of the faculty of medicine. That faculty had not, any more than the law, enjoyed its proper esteem in France. Its members must therefore have the qualities of men not accustomed to feelings of dignity. But supposing they had ranked as they ought to do, and as with us they actually do, the bedsides of the sick are not the academies for forming statesmen and legislators. Then came the dealers in stocks and funds, who must be eager at any cost to change their imaginary paper wealth for the more solid substance of land. To these were joined men of other descriptions, from whom as little knowledge of or attention to the interests of a great state was to be expected, and as little regard for the stability of any institution — men formed to be instruments, not controls. Such, in general, was the composition of the Tiers État in the National Assembly, in which hardly the slightest trace was to be seen of what we call the natural landed interest of the country.

We know that the British House of Commons, without shutting its doors to merit in any class, is, by the sure operation of adequate causes, filled with everything illustrious in rank, in descent, in inherited and in acquired wealth, in cultivated talent, and in military, civil, naval and political distinction, that the country can supply. But supposing — what can hardly be supposed as a case at all — that the House of Commons were composed in the same way as the Tiers État in France: would this dominion of pettifogging be borne with patience, or even imagined without horror? God forbid that I should insinuate anything derogatory to a profession that is another priesthood, administering the rites of sacred justice. But while I revere men in the functions that belong to them, and would do as much as one man can to prevent their being excluded from any, I cannot flatter them by giving the lie to nature. They are good and useful in the mixture; they must be mischievous if they preponderate so far as to become virtually the whole. Their very excellence in their own particular functions may be far from a qualification for others. It cannot escape notice that when men are too much confined to professional and technical habits, and become as it were ingrained by constant employment in that narrow circle, they are disabled rather than qualified for anything depending on a knowledge of mankind, on experience in mixed affairs, on a comprehensive and connected view of the various, complicated, external and internal interests that go to form that many-sided thing called a state.

After all, even if the House of Commons were of a wholly professional and technical composition, what is the power of the House of Commons — circumscribed and hemmed in by the immovable barriers of laws, usages and positive rules of doctrine and practice, counterpoised by the House of Lords, and existing at every moment at the discretion of the crown to continue, prorogue or dissolve us? The power of the House of Commons, direct and indirect, is indeed great, and long may it be able to preserve its greatness and the spirit that belongs to true greatness in full. And it will do so as long as it can keep the breakers of law in India from becoming the makers of law for England. But the power of the House of Commons, at its least diminished, is a drop of water in the ocean compared with the power residing in a settled majority of your National Assembly. Since the destruction of the orders, that assembly has no fundamental law, no strict convention, no respected usage to restrain it. Instead of finding themselves obliged to conform to a fixed constitution, they have the power to make a constitution that will conform to their designs. Nothing in heaven or on earth can serve as a check on them. What must be the heads, the hearts, the dispositions of men who are qualified — or who dare — not merely to make laws under a fixed constitution, but at one heat to strike out an entirely new constitution for a great kingdom, and in every part of it, from the monarch on the throne down to the vestry of a parish? But

"Fools rush in where angels fear to tread."

In such a state of unbounded power, for undefined and indefinable purposes, the evil of a moral and almost physical unfitness of the man for the function must be the greatest we can conceive happening in the management of human affairs.

Having considered the composition of the third estate as it originally stood, I looked at the representatives of the clergy. There too it appeared that quite as little regard had been paid, in the principles of their election, to the general security of property or to the fitness of the deputies for their public purposes. That election was so contrived as to send a very large proportion of mere country curates to the great and arduous work of remodelling a state: men who had never seen the state so much as in a picture; men who knew nothing of the world beyond the bounds of an obscure village; who, sunk in hopeless poverty, could regard all property, secular or ecclesiastical, with no eye but envy; among whom there must be many who for the smallest hope of the meanest share in the plunder would readily join any attempt on a body of wealth in which they could scarcely expect any share except in a general scramble. Instead of balancing the power of the active pettifoggers in the other assembly, these curates must necessarily become the active helpers, or at best the passive instruments, of the very men who had habitually guided them in their petty village concerns. Nor could they be the most conscientious of their kind, who, presuming on their inadequate understanding, could intrigue for a trust that took them away from their natural relation to their flocks and their natural sphere of action, to undertake the regeneration of kingdoms. This preponderating weight, added to the force of the body of pettifoggers in the Tiers État, completed that momentum of ignorance, rashness, presumption and lust for plunder that nothing has been able to resist.

To observant men it must have been clear from the beginning that the majority of the third estate, joined with such a deputation from the clergy as I have described, would, while it pursued the destruction of the nobility, inevitably become subservient to the worst designs of individuals in that class. In the spoil and humiliation of their own order those individuals would have a sure fund to pay their new followers with. Squandering away the things that made their fellows happy would be no sacrifice to them at all. Turbulent, discontented men of quality, in proportion as they are puffed up with personal pride and arrogance, generally despise their own order. One of the first symptoms they show of a selfish and mischievous ambition is a profligate disregard for a dignity they share with others. To be attached to the subdivision, to love the little platoon we belong to in society, is the first principle — the germ, as it were — of public affection. It is the first link in the chain by which we go on to a love of our country and of mankind. The interest of that portion of the social arrangement is a trust in the hands of everyone who composes it; and as none but bad men would justify abusing it, none but traitors would barter it away for their own personal advantage.

In the time of our civil troubles in England there were several people — I do not know whether you have any such in your Assembly in France — like the Earl of Holland of that day, who by themselves or their families had brought odium on the throne by its prodigal dispensation of favours to them, and who afterwards joined the rebellions that arose out of the discontents they had themselves caused: men who helped to subvert the very throne to which some of them owed their existence and others all the power they used to ruin their benefactor. If any bounds are set to the rapacious demands of that sort of person, or if others are allowed to share in the objects they would monopolise, revenge and envy soon fill up the craving void left in their avarice. Bewildered by the complication of their diseased passions, their reason is disturbed; their views become vast and confused, inexplicable to others and uncertain to themselves. On every side they find their unprincipled ambition running up against limits in any fixed order of things; but in the fog and haze of confusion everything is enlarged and appears to have no limit at all.

When men of rank sacrifice every idea of dignity to an ambition with no distinct object, and work with low instruments for low ends, the whole composition becomes low and base. Does not something like this appear in France now? Does it not produce something ignoble and inglorious — a kind of meanness in all the prevailing policy, a tendency in everything that is done to lower, along with individuals, all the dignity and importance of the state? Other revolutions have been conducted by men who, while they attempted or affected changes in the commonwealth, sanctified their ambition by raising the dignity of the people whose peace they disturbed. They had long views. They aimed at ruling their country, not at destroying it. They were men of great civil and great military talents, and if they were the terror of their age they were also its ornament. They were not like Jew brokers contending with one another over who could best patch up, with fraudulent circulation and depreciated paper, the wretchedness and ruin their degenerate counsels had brought on their country. The compliment paid to one of the great bad men of the old stamp — Cromwell — by his kinsman, a favourite poet of that time, shows what it was he proposed, and what indeed to a great degree he achieved in the success of his ambition:

	"Still as you rise, the state, exalted too,
	Finds no distemper whilst 't is changed by you;
	Changed like the world's great scene, when without noise
	The rising sun night's vulgar lights destroys."

These disturbers were not so much men usurping power as men asserting their natural place in society. Their rising was to light up and beautify the world. They conquered their competitors by outshining them. The hand that smote the country like a destroying angel also communicated to it the force and energy under which it suffered. I do not say — God forbid — that the virtues of such men are to be set as a balance against their crimes; but they were some corrective to their effects. Such, as I said, was our Cromwell. Such was your whole race of Guises, Condés and Colignys. Such were the Richelieus, who in quieter times acted in the spirit of a civil war. Such, as better men and in a less doubtful cause, were your Henry the Fourth and your Sully, though they were nursed in civil confusions and not wholly free of their taint. It is a thing to wonder at, how very soon France, when she had a moment to breathe, recovered and emerged from the longest and most dreadful civil war ever known in any nation. Why? Because among all their massacres they had not killed the mind of their country. A conscious dignity, a noble pride, a generous sense of glory and emulation had not been extinguished. On the contrary, it had been kindled and inflamed. The organs of the state, however battered, still existed. All the prizes of honour and virtue, all the rewards, all the distinctions, remained. But your present confusion, like a paralysis, has struck at the fountain of life itself. Everyone in your country who is in a position to be moved by a principle of honour is disgraced and degraded, and can feel no sensation of life except a mortified and humiliated indignation. But this generation will quickly pass away. The next generation of the nobility will resemble the artisans and clowns and money-jobbers, usurers and Jews who will always be their fellows and sometimes their masters. Believe me, Sir, those who attempt to level never equalise. In every society made up of various descriptions of citizens, some description must be uppermost. The levellers therefore only change and pervert the natural order of things: they overload the building of society by setting up in the air what the solidity of the structure requires to be on the ground. The associations of tailors and carpenters that the republic — of Paris, for instance — is composed of cannot be equal to the situation into which, by the worst of usurpations, a usurpation on the prerogatives of nature, you are attempting to force them.

The Chancellor of France, at the opening of the States-General, said, in a flourish of oratory, that all occupations were honourable. If he meant only that no honest employment is disgraceful, he would not have gone beyond the truth. But in asserting that anything is honourable, we imply some distinction in its favour. The occupation of a hairdresser, or of a working tallow-chandler, cannot be a matter of honour to anyone, to say nothing of a number of other more servile employments. Such men ought not to suffer oppression from the state; but the state suffers oppression if men like them, individually or collectively, are allowed to rule. In this you think you are combating prejudice, but you are at war with nature.

Footnote: Ecclesiasticus, chapter 38, verses 24, 25. "The wisdom of a learned man cometh by opportunity of leisure: and he that hath little business shall become wise. How can he get wisdom that holdeth the plough, and that glorieth in the goad; that driveth oxen, and is occupied in their labors, and whose talk is of bullocks?" Verse 27: "So every carpenter and workmaster, that laboreth night and day," etc. Verse 33: "They shall not be sought for in public counsel, nor sit high in the congregation: they shall not sit on the judge's seat, nor understand the sentence of judgment: they cannot declare justice and judgment, and they shall not be found where parables are spoken." Verse 34: "But they will maintain the state of the world." I do not determine whether this book is canonical, as the Gallican Church until lately considered it, or apocryphal, as it is taken to be here. I am sure it contains a great deal of sense and truth.

I do not take you, my dear Sir, to be of that sophistical, quibbling spirit, or of that ungenerous dullness, which demands for every general observation or sentiment an explicit list of the correctives and exceptions that reason will presume to be included in all the general propositions of reasonable men. You do not imagine that I wish to confine power, authority and distinction to blood and names and titles. No, Sir. There is no qualification for government but virtue and wisdom, actual or presumed. Wherever they are actually found, they carry, in whatever state, condition, profession or trade, Heaven's own passport to human place and honour. Woe to the country that would madly and impiously reject the service of the talents and virtues, civil, military or religious, that are given to grace and to serve it, and would condemn to obscurity everything formed to shed lustre and glory round a state. And woe to the country too that, going to the opposite extreme, considers a low education, a mean and narrow view of things, and a sordid, mercenary occupation a preferable title to command. Everything ought to be open — but not indifferently to every man. No rotation, no appointment by lot, no method of election operating in the spirit of drawing lots or taking turns can be generally good in a government concerned with extensive objects, because none of them has any tendency, direct or indirect, to select the man with a view to the duty, or to fit the one to the other. I do not hesitate to say that the road to eminence and power from an obscure condition ought not to be made too easy, or too much a matter of course. If rare merit is the rarest of all rare things, it ought to pass through some sort of probation. The temple of honour ought to be set on a height. If it is entered through virtue, let it be remembered too that virtue is never tested except by some difficulty and some struggle.

Nothing is a due and adequate representation of a state that does not represent its ability as well as its property. But since ability is a vigorous and active principle, and property is sluggish, inert and timid, property can never be safe from the invasions of ability unless it is, out of all proportion, predominant in the representation. It must be represented too in great masses of accumulation, or it is not properly protected. The characteristic essence of property, formed out of the combined principles of acquiring it and conserving it, is to be unequal. The great masses, therefore, which excite envy and tempt rapacity, must be put beyond the possibility of danger. They then form a natural rampart round the lesser properties in all their gradations. The same quantity of property, divided among many in the natural course of things, does not work the same way. Its defensive power is weakened as it is spread out. In that diffusion each man's portion is less than what, in the eagerness of his desires, he may flatter himself he could get by dissipating other people's accumulations. The plunder of the few would in fact give an inconceivably small share to each in the distribution among the many. But the many are not capable of making this calculation, and those who lead them to plunder never intend any such distribution.

The power of perpetuating our property in our families is one of the most valuable and interesting things about it, and the thing that most tends to the perpetuation of society itself. It makes our weakness serve our virtue; it grafts benevolence even onto avarice. The possessors of family wealth, and of the distinction that goes with hereditary possession, being the most concerned in it, are the natural securities for this transmission. With us the House of Peers is formed on this principle. It is composed wholly of hereditary property and hereditary distinction, and is made for that reason the third branch of the legislature, and, in the last resort, the sole judge of all property in all its subdivisions. The House of Commons too, though not necessarily, is always so composed in fact for far the greater part. Let those large proprietors be what they will — and they have their chance of being among the best — at the very worst they are the ballast in the vessel of the commonwealth. For though hereditary wealth and the rank that goes with it are too much idolised by creeping sycophants and by the blind, abject admirers of power, they are too rashly slighted in the shallow speculations of the petulant, presuming, short-sighted coxcombs of philosophy. Some decent, regulated pre-eminence, some preference — not exclusive appropriation — given to birth, is neither unnatural nor unjust nor impolitic.

It is said that twenty-four millions ought to prevail over two hundred thousand. True, if the constitution of a kingdom is a problem in arithmetic. This sort of talk does well enough with a lamp-post as its second; to men who may reason calmly it is ridiculous. The will of the many and their interest must very often differ, and the difference will be great when they make an evil choice. A government of five hundred country attorneys and obscure curates is not good for twenty-four million men, even if it were chosen by forty-eight million; nor is it any the better for being guided by a dozen persons of quality who have betrayed their trust in order to get that power. At present you seem to have strayed off the high road of nature in everything. The property of France does not govern it. Of course property is being destroyed, and rational liberty has no existence. All you have got for the present is a paper circulation and a stock-jobbing constitution. And as for the future, do you seriously think that the territory of France, on the republican system of eighty-three independent municipalities — to say nothing of the parts they are made up of — can ever be governed as one body, or ever be set in motion by the impulse of one mind? When the National Assembly has completed its work, it will have accomplished its own ruin. These commonwealths will not long bear being subject to the republic of Paris. They will not bear having that one body monopolise the captivity of the king and the dominion over the assembly that calls itself national. Each will keep its own portion of the spoil of the Church for itself, and will not allow that spoil, or the juster fruits of its own industry, or the natural produce of its own soil, to be sent off to swell the insolence or pamper the luxury of the tradesmen of Paris. In that they will see none of the equality on the pretext of which they were tempted to throw off their allegiance to their sovereign, and the ancient constitution of their country with it. There can be no capital city in such a constitution as they have lately made. They have forgotten that in framing democratic governments they have virtually dismembered their country. The person they persist in calling king has not a hundredth part of the power left him that would be needed to hold this collection of republics together. The republic of Paris will indeed try to complete the corruption of the army, and to perpetuate the Assembly illegally, without any recourse to its constituents, as the means of continuing its despotism. It will make efforts, by becoming the heart of a boundless paper circulation, to draw everything to itself. But in vain. All this policy will in the end prove as feeble as it is now violent.

Section Seven: The Rights of Men, and What They Are Not

If this is your actual situation, compared with the situation you were called to as if by the voice of God and man, I cannot find it in my heart to congratulate you on the choice you have made, or on the success that has attended your efforts. As little can I recommend to any other nation a course founded on such principles and productive of such effects. That I must leave to those who can see further into your affairs than I can, and who know best how far your actions serve their designs. The gentlemen of the Revolution Society, who were so early with their congratulations, appear to be strongly of the opinion that there is some political scheme relating to this country in which your proceedings may somehow be useful. For your Dr Price, who seems to have theorised himself into no small degree of fervour on the subject, addresses his hearers in these very remarkable words: "I cannot conclude without recalling particularly to your recollection a consideration which I have more than once alluded to, and which probably your thoughts have been all along anticipating; a consideration with which my mind is impressed more than can express: I mean the consideration of the favorableness of the present times to all exertions in the cause of liberty."

It is plain that the mind of this political preacher was at the time big with some extraordinary design, and it is very likely that the thoughts of his audience, who understood him better than I do, ran ahead of him all along in his reflection and through the whole train of consequences it led to.

Before I read that sermon I really thought I lived in a free country, and it was an error I cherished, because it made me like the country I lived in the better. I was of course aware that a jealous, ever-waking vigilance, to guard the treasure of our liberty not only from invasion but from decay and corruption, was our best wisdom and our first duty. But I regarded that treasure as a possession to be secured rather than a prize to be fought for. I did not see how the present time came to be so very favourable to all exertions in the cause of freedom. The present time differs from any other only in the circumstance of what is being done in France. If the example of that nation is to have an influence here, I can easily see why some of their proceedings, which have an unpleasant look and are not entirely reconcilable with humanity, generosity, good faith and justice, are excused with such milky good nature towards the perpetrators and borne with such heroic fortitude on behalf of the sufferers. It is certainly not prudent to discredit the authority of an example you mean to follow.

But granting that, we are led to a very natural question. What is this cause of liberty, and what are these exertions in its favour, for which the example of France is so singularly encouraging? Is our monarchy to be annihilated, along with all the laws, all the courts and all the ancient chartered bodies of the kingdom? Is every landmark in the country to be abolished in favour of a geometrical and arithmetical constitution? Is the House of Lords to be voted useless? Is episcopacy to be abolished? Are the Church lands to be sold to Jews and speculators, or given away to bribe newly invented municipal republics into a share of the sacrilege? Are all taxes to be voted grievances, and the revenue reduced to a patriotic contribution, or patriotic presents? Are silver shoe buckles to be substituted for the land tax and the malt tax to support the naval strength of this kingdom? Are all orders, ranks and distinctions to be jumbled together, so that out of universal anarchy joined to national bankruptcy three or four thousand democracies may be formed into eighty-three, and so that they may all, by some unknown power of attraction, be organised into one? Is it for this great end that the army is to be seduced away from its discipline and its loyalty, first by every kind of debauchery and then by the terrible precedent of a bounty in the form of increased pay? Are the curates to be seduced away from their bishops by holding out the delusive hope of a share in the spoils of their own order? Are the citizens of London to be drawn away from their allegiance by being fed at the expense of their fellow subjects? Is a compulsory paper currency to be substituted for the legal coin of this kingdom? Is what remains of the plundered stock of the public revenue to be spent on the wild project of maintaining two armies to watch each other and to fight each other? If these are the ends and the means of the Revolution Society, I admit they are well matched, and France can furnish them with precedents for both.

I see that your example is held up to shame us. I know we are supposed to be a dull, sluggish race, made passive by finding our situation tolerable, and prevented by a mediocrity of freedom from ever attaining freedom in its full perfection. Your leaders in France began by professing to admire, almost to adore, the British constitution; but as they went on they came to look on it with sovereign contempt. The friends of your National Assembly among us have quite as mean an opinion of what used to be thought the glory of their country. The Revolution Society has discovered that the English nation is not free. They are convinced that the inequality in our representation is a "defect in our Constitution so gross and palpable as to make it excellent chiefly in form and theory"; that representation in the legislature of a kingdom is not only the basis of all constitutional liberty in it but of "all legitimate government; that without it a government is nothing but an usurpation"; that "when the representation is partial, the kingdom possesses liberty only partially; and if extremely partial, it gives only a semblance; and if not only extremely partial, but corruptly chosen, it becomes a nuisance." Dr Price considers this inadequacy of representation our fundamental grievance; and though as to the corruption of this semblance of representation he hopes it has not yet reached its full perfection of depravity, he fears that "nothing will be done towards gaining for us this essential blessing, until some great abuse of power again provokes our resentment, or some great calamity again alarms our fears, or perhaps till the acquisition of a pure and equal representation by other countries, whilst we are mocked with the shadow, kindles our shame." To this he adds a note in these words: "A representation chosen chiefly by the Treasury, and a few thousands of the dregs of the people, who are generally paid for their votes."

Footnote: Discourse on the Love of our Country, 3rd edition, p. 39.

You will smile here at the consistency of these democrats, who, when they are off their guard, treat the humbler part of the community with the greatest contempt, while at the same time claiming to make them the depositories of all power. It would take a long discourse to point out to you the many fallacies lurking in the general and equivocal phrase "inadequate representation". I shall only say here, in justice to that old-fashioned constitution under which we have long prospered, that our representation has been found perfectly adequate to every purpose for which a representation of the people can be wanted or devised. I defy the enemies of our constitution to show otherwise. To set out the particulars in which it is found to serve its ends so well would require a treatise on our practical constitution. I state the doctrine of the revolutionists here only so that you and others may see what opinion these gentlemen hold of the constitution of their own country, and why they seem to think that some great abuse of power, or some great calamity, would be much softened to their feelings by giving a chance for a constitution according to their ideas. You see why they are so enamoured of your fair and equal representation, which, once obtained, might produce the same effects here. You see that they regard our House of Commons as only "a semblance", "a form", "a theory", "a shadow", "a mockery", and perhaps "a nuisance".

These gentlemen pride themselves on being systematic, and not without reason. They must therefore look on this gross and palpable defect of representation, this fundamental grievance as they call it, as a thing not merely vicious in itself but as making our whole government absolutely illegitimate and no better than a downright usurpation. Another revolution, to get rid of this illegitimate and usurped government, would of course be perfectly justifiable, if not absolutely necessary. Indeed their principle, if you consider it at all carefully, goes much further than altering the election of the House of Commons. For if popular representation, or choice, is necessary to the legitimacy of all government, the House of Lords is at one stroke made a bastard and corrupted in its blood. That House is no representative of the people at all, even in "semblance" or "in form". The case of the crown is quite as bad. In vain may the crown try to shelter itself from these gentlemen behind the authority of the settlement made at the Revolution. The Revolution, which is appealed to for a title, wants a title itself on their system. On their theory the Revolution rests on no more solid a basis than our present formalities, since it was made by a House of Lords representing nobody but themselves and by a House of Commons exactly like the present one — that is, as they call it, by a mere "shadow and mockery" of representation.

They must destroy something, or they seem to themselves to exist for no purpose. One set is for destroying the civil power through the ecclesiastical; another for demolishing the ecclesiastical through the civil. They are aware that the worst consequences might follow for the public from accomplishing this double ruin of Church and State; but they are so heated with their theories that they give more than hints that this ruin, with all the mischiefs that must lead to it and accompany it, and which appear quite certain to them, would not be unwelcome, or very far from their wishes. A man among them of great authority, and certainly of great talents, speaking of a supposed alliance between Church and State, says: "Perhaps we must wait for the fall of the civil powers, before this most unnatural alliance be broken. Calamitous, no doubt, will that time be. But what convulsion in the political world ought to be a subject of lamentation, if it be attended with so desirable an effect?" You see with what a steady eye these gentlemen are prepared to contemplate the greatest calamities that could befall their country.

It is no wonder, then, that holding these ideas of everything in their constitution and government at home, in Church and State alike, as illegitimate and usurped, or at best a hollow mockery, they look abroad with an eager and passionate enthusiasm. While they are possessed by such notions, it is useless to talk to them of the practice of their ancestors, the fundamental laws of their country, or the fixed form of a constitution whose merits are confirmed by the solid test of long experience and of growing public strength and national prosperity. They despise experience as the wisdom of unlettered men; and as for the rest, they have worked a mine underground that will blow up, in one grand explosion, all the examples of antiquity, all precedents, charters and acts of Parliament. They have "the rights of men". Against these there can be no prescription; against these no argument is binding; these admit of no tempering and no compromise; anything withheld from their full demand is so much fraud and injustice. Against these rights of men of theirs, let no government look for security in the length of its existence or in the justice and mildness of its administration. If its forms do not square with their theories, the objections of these theorists are as valid against an old and beneficent government as against the most violent tyranny or the newest usurpation. They are always at issue with governments, not on a question of abuse but on a question of competence and a question of title. I have nothing to say to the clumsy subtlety of their political metaphysics. Let it be their amusement in the schools.

	Illa se jactet in aula
	Æolus, et clauso ventorum carcere regnet.

But do not let them break out of prison to burst on us like a Levanter, sweeping the earth with their hurricane and breaking up the fountains of the great deep to overwhelm us.

I am far from denying in theory, and my heart is quite as far from withholding in practice — if it were in my power to give or withhold — the real rights of men. In denying their false claims of right, I do not mean to injure the rights that are real, and that their pretended rights would utterly destroy. If civil society is made for the advantage of man, then all the advantages it is made for become his right. It is an institution of beneficence, and law itself is only beneficence acting by a rule. Men have a right to live by that rule. They have a right to justice as between themselves, whether their fellows hold political office or follow an ordinary occupation. They have a right to the fruits of their industry, and to the means of making their industry fruitful. They have a right to what their parents acquired, to the nourishment and improvement of their children, to instruction in life and consolation in death. Whatever each man can do separately, without trespassing on others, he has a right to do for himself; and he has a right to a fair portion of everything that society, with all its combinations of skill and force, can do in his favour. In this partnership all men have equal rights, but not to equal things. A man with only five shillings in the partnership has as good a right to it as the man with five hundred pounds has to his larger share; but he has no right to an equal dividend out of the product of the joint stock. And as for the share of power, authority and direction that each individual ought to have in the management of the state, that I must deny to be among the direct original rights of man in civil society; for I have in mind the civil social man and no other. It is a thing to be settled by convention.

If civil society is the offspring of convention, that convention must be its law. That convention must limit and modify every kind of constitution formed under it. Every sort of legislative, judicial or executive power is its creature. They can have no existence in any other state of things; and how can any man claim, under the conventions of civil society, rights that do not even presuppose its existence — rights that are absolutely repugnant to it? One of the first motives to civil society, and one that becomes one of its fundamental rules, is that no man should be judge in his own cause. By this each person has at once divested himself of the first fundamental right of uncovenanted man, which is to judge for himself and assert his own cause. He abdicates all right to be his own governor. He gives up, largely, even the right of self-defence, the first law of nature. Men cannot enjoy the rights of an uncivil and of a civil state together. So that he may obtain justice, he gives up his right of determining what justice is in the points that matter most to him. So that he may secure some liberty, he surrenders the whole of it in trust.

Government is not made in virtue of natural rights, which may and do exist entirely independently of it, and exist in much greater clarity and in a much greater degree of abstract perfection; but their abstract perfection is their practical defect. By having a right to everything they lack everything. Government is a contrivance of human wisdom to provide for human wants. Men have a right that these wants should be provided for by that wisdom. Among these wants is to be counted the want, outside civil society, of a sufficient restraint on their passions. Society requires not only that the passions of individuals be subdued, but that even in the mass and body, as well as in individuals, the inclinations of men should frequently be thwarted, their will controlled, and their passions brought into subjection. This can only be done by a power out of themselves, one that is not, in the exercise of its function, subject to the very will and the very passions it is its office to bridle and subdue. In this sense the restraints on men, as well as their liberties, are to be counted among their rights. But since liberties and restrictions vary with times and circumstances, and admit of infinite modification, they cannot be settled by any abstract rule, and nothing is so foolish as to discuss them on that principle.

The moment you take anything away from the full right of every man to govern himself, and allow any artificial, positive limitation on those rights, from that moment the whole organisation of government becomes a matter of convenience. That is what makes the constitution of a state, and the due distribution of its powers, a matter of the most delicate and complicated skill. It requires a deep knowledge of human nature and human necessities, and of the things that help or hinder the various ends the machinery of civil institutions is meant to pursue. The state must have recruits for its strength and remedies for its diseases. What is the use of debating a man's abstract right to food or to medicine? The question is how to procure them and how to administer them. In that deliberation I shall always advise calling in the farmer and the physician rather than the professor of metaphysics.

The science of constructing a commonwealth, or renovating it, or reforming it, is like every other experimental science: it cannot be taught a priori, in advance of the experiment. Nor is it a short experience that can instruct us in that practical science, because the real effects of moral causes are not always immediate, and what is harmful in the first instance may be excellent in its remoter operation — its excellence may even arise from the ill effects it produces at the beginning. The reverse happens too: very plausible schemes with very pleasing beginnings have often had shameful and lamentable conclusions. In states there are often obscure and almost hidden causes, things that appear at first sight of little importance, on which a very great part of their prosperity or adversity may most essentially depend. Since the science of government is therefore so practical in itself, and intended for such practical purposes, and requires experience — more experience, indeed, than any one person can acquire in a whole lifetime, however shrewd and observant he may be — it is with infinite caution that any man ought to venture on pulling down a building that has answered the common purposes of society in any tolerable degree for ages, or on building it up again without having models and patterns of proven usefulness in front of him.

These metaphysical rights, entering common life like rays of light passing into a dense medium, are by the laws of nature refracted out of their straight line. Indeed, in the gross and complicated mass of human passions and concerns, the primitive rights of men undergo such a variety of refractions and reflections that it becomes absurd to talk of them as if they continued in the simplicity of their original direction. The nature of man is intricate; the objects of society are of the greatest possible complexity; and therefore no simple disposition or direction of power can suit either man's nature or the quality of his affairs. When I hear simplicity of contrivance aimed at and boasted of in any new political constitution, I am at no loss to decide that the builders are either grossly ignorant of their trade or totally negligent of their duty. The simple governments are fundamentally defective, to say no worse of them. If you were to contemplate society from only one point of view, all these simple forms of government are infinitely captivating. In effect each would answer its single end far more perfectly than the more complex kind can attain all its complex purposes. But it is better that the whole should be answered imperfectly and irregularly than that, while some parts are provided for with great exactness, others should be totally neglected, or perhaps materially damaged, by excessive care for a favourite member.

The pretended rights of these theorists are all extremes, and in proportion as they are metaphysically true they are morally and politically false. The rights of men lie in a sort of middle, incapable of definition but not impossible to discern. The rights of men in governments are their advantages, and these are often balances between different goods, compromises sometimes between good and evil and sometimes between evil and evil. Political reason is a computing principle: adding, subtracting, multiplying and dividing morally true denominations, not metaphysically or mathematically true ones.

These theorists almost always confound the right of the people with their power by a piece of sophistry. The body of the community, whenever it can come to act, can meet with no effective resistance; but until power and right are the same thing, the whole body of them has no right that is inconsistent with virtue, and with the first of all the virtues, prudence. Men have no right to what is unreasonable and not for their benefit. A pleasant writer did say "Liceat perire poetis" — let poets be allowed to perish — when one of them was said to have leapt in cold blood into the flames of a volcanic revolution, "ardentem frigidus Ætnam insiluit"; but I consider such a frolic an unjustifiable poetic licence rather than one of the franchises of Parnassus. And whether the man who chose to exercise this kind of right were poet, divine or politician, I think wiser and therefore more charitable thoughts would urge me to save him rather than to preserve his brass slippers as monuments of his folly.

Section Eight: The Sixth of October, and the Age of Chivalry

The kind of anniversary sermon that a great part of what I am writing refers to will, unless men are shamed out of their present course, cheat many of them out of the principles and deprive them of the benefits of the very Revolution they are commemorating. I confess to you, Sir, that I have never liked this continual talk of resistance and revolution, or the practice of making the extreme medicine of the constitution into its daily bread. It makes the habit of society dangerously sickly; it is like taking periodic doses of corrosive sublimate, and swallowing repeated stimulants to whip up our love of liberty.

This disease of the remedy, once it becomes habitual, relaxes and wears out, by common and prostituted use, the spring of the spirit that ought to be exerted on great occasions. It was in the most patient period of Roman servitude that themes of tyrannicide were the ordinary exercise of schoolboys — cum perimit sævos classis numerosa tyrannos, when a numerous class destroys its savage tyrants. In the ordinary state of things it produces the worst effects in a country like ours, even on the cause of the very liberty it abuses with the dissoluteness of an extravagant theory. Almost all the high-bred republicans of my time have, after a short interval, become the most decided and thoroughgoing courtiers. They soon left the business of a tedious, moderate but practical resistance to those of us whom, in the pride and intoxication of their theories, they had dismissed as little better than Tories. Hypocrisy naturally delights in the most sublime speculations, for since it never intends to go beyond speculation, it costs nothing to make them magnificent. But even where levity rather than fraud was to be suspected in these ranting theories, the outcome has been much the same. These professors, finding their extreme principles do not apply to cases that call only for a qualified — one might say a civil and legal — resistance, offer no resistance at all in such cases. With them it is a war, or a revolution, or it is nothing. Finding their political schemes unsuited to the state of the world they live in, they often come to think lightly of all public principle, and are ready for their part to abandon for a very trivial interest what they have decided is of very trivial value. Some, indeed, are of a steadier and more persevering nature; but these are eager politicians outside Parliament, who have little temptation to give up their favourite projects. They always have some change in the Church or the State, or both, in view. When that is the case they are always bad citizens and thoroughly unreliable connections. For, regarding their theoretical designs as of infinite value and the actual arrangement of the state as of none, they are at best indifferent to it. They see no merit in the good management of public affairs and no fault in the bad; they rather rejoice in the bad, as more favourable to a revolution. They see no merit or demerit in any man, or any action, or any political principle, except so far as it advances or retards their design of change. So they take up one day the most violent and strained ideas of prerogative and another the wildest democratic ideas of freedom, and pass from the one to the other without any regard to cause, or person, or party.

In France you are now in the crisis of a revolution, in transit from one form of government to another; you cannot see that kind of man in quite the same situation in which we see him in this country. With us he is militant, with you he is triumphant, and you know how he can act when his power matches his will. I would not be supposed to confine these observations to any one description of men, or to include all men of any description in them — far from it. I am as incapable of that injustice as I am of keeping terms with those who profess principles of extremes, and who under the name of religion teach little else than wild and dangerous politics. The worst of these politics of revolution is this: they temper and harden the breast in order to prepare it for the desperate strokes that are sometimes needed on extreme occasions. But since those occasions may never arrive, the mind takes a taint for nothing, and the moral feelings suffer not a little when no political purpose at all is served by the corruption. This sort of people are so taken up with their theories about the rights of man that they have entirely forgotten his nature. Without opening a single new avenue to the understanding, they have succeeded in blocking up the ones that lead to the heart. They have perverted, in themselves and in those who listen to them, all the well-placed sympathies of the human breast.

This famous sermon of the Old Jewry breathes nothing but this spirit through the whole of its political part. Plots, massacres, assassinations seem to some people a trivial price for obtaining a revolution. A cheap, bloodless reformation, a guiltless liberty, seems flat and insipid to their taste. There must be a great change of scene; there must be a magnificent stage effect; there must be a grand spectacle to rouse an imagination grown torpid with the lazy enjoyment of sixty years of security and the still less stimulating repose of public prosperity. The preacher found all of it in the French Revolution. It sends a youthful warmth through his whole frame. His enthusiasm kindles as he goes on, and by the time he reaches his peroration it is in a full blaze. Then, surveying from the Pisgah of his pulpit the free, moral, happy, flourishing and glorious state of France, as though it were a bird's-eye landscape of a promised land, he breaks out into this rapture:

"What an eventful period is this! I am thankful that I have lived to it; I could almost say, Lord, now lettest thou thy servant depart in peace, for mine eyes have seen thy salvation. — I have lived to see a diffusion of knowledge which has undermined superstition and error. — I have lived to see the rights of men better understood than ever, and nations panting for liberty which seemed to have lost the idea of it. — I have lived to see thirty millions of people, indignant and resolute, spurning at slavery, and demanding liberty with an irresistible voice; their king led in triumph, and an arbitrary monarch surrendering himself to his subjects."

Footnote: Another of these reverend gentlemen, who witnessed some of the spectacles Paris has lately exhibited, expresses himself thus: "A king dragged in submissive triumph by his conquering subjects is one of those appearances of grandeur which seldom rise in the prospect of human affairs, and which, during the remainder of my life, I shall think of with wonder and gratification." These gentlemen agree marvellously in their feelings.

Before I go further, I should remark that Dr Price seems rather to overrate the great acquisitions of light he has obtained and spread abroad in this age. The last century seems to me to have been quite as enlightened. It had, though in another place, a triumph as memorable as Dr Price's, and some of the great preachers of that period shared in it as eagerly as he has shared in the triumph of France. At the trial of the Reverend Hugh Peters for high treason, evidence was given that when King Charles was brought to London for trial, the Apostle of Liberty of that day conducted the triumph. "I saw," says the witness, "his Majesty in the coach with six horses, and Peters riding before the king triumphing." Dr Price, when he talks as if he had made a discovery, is only following a precedent. For after the king's trial began, this forerunner, the same Dr Peters, ended a long prayer in the royal chapel at Whitehall — he had chosen his place very triumphantly — by saying: "I have prayed and preached these twenty years; and now I may say with old Simeon, Lord, now lettest thou thy servant depart in peace, for mine eyes have seen thy salvation." Peters did not get the fruits of his prayer, for he neither departed as soon as he wished nor in peace. He became — and I heartily hope none of his followers in this country may become the same — a sacrifice himself to the triumph he had led as high priest. At the Restoration they dealt with this poor good man perhaps too hard. But we owe it to his memory and his sufferings to say that he had as much illumination and as much zeal, and had as effectively undermined all the superstition and error that might impede the great business he was engaged in, as any who follow him and repeat after him in this age, which would claim for itself an exclusive title to the knowledge of the rights of men and all the glorious consequences of that knowledge.

Footnote: State Trials, Vol. II, pp. 360, 363.

After this sally by the preacher of the Old Jewry, which differs only in place and time from the rapture of 1648 and agrees perfectly with its spirit and its letter, the Revolution Society — the fabricators of governments, the heroic band of cashierers of monarchs, electors of sovereigns and leaders of kings in triumph — strutting in the proud consciousness of that diffusion of knowledge of which every member had received so large a share in the handout, were in haste to make a generous diffusion of the knowledge they had received for nothing. To make this bountiful communication they adjourned from the church in the Old Jewry to the London Tavern, where the same Dr Price, in whom the fumes of his oracular tripod had not entirely evaporated, moved and carried the resolution, or address of congratulation, that Lord Stanhope forwarded to the National Assembly of France.

Here I find a preacher of the Gospel profaning the beautiful and prophetic ejaculation commonly called the Nunc dimittis, uttered at the first presentation of our Saviour in the temple, and applying it, with an inhuman and unnatural rapture, to perhaps the most horrid, atrocious and afflicting spectacle ever exhibited to the pity and indignation of mankind. This "leading in triumph", a thing unmanly and irreligious even in its best form, which fills our preacher with such unhallowed transports, must, I believe, shock the moral taste of every well-born mind. Several Englishmen were the stupefied and indignant spectators of that triumph. Unless we have been strangely deceived, it was a spectacle far more like a procession of American savages entering Onondaga after some of their murders — called victories — and leading their captives into huts hung round with scalps, overwhelmed by the jeers and blows of women as ferocious as themselves, than it was like the triumphal pomp of a civilised martial nation. If indeed a civilised nation, or any men with a sense of generosity, could be capable of a personal triumph over the fallen and the afflicted.

This, my dear Sir, was not the triumph of France. I must believe that as a nation it overwhelmed you with shame and horror. I must believe that the National Assembly find themselves in a state of the greatest humiliation at not being able to punish the authors of this triumph or the actors in it, and that they are in a situation in which any inquiry they may make into it must be destitute even of the appearance of liberty or impartiality. Their apology lies in their situation; but when we approve of what they must bear, that is in us the degenerate choice of a corrupted mind.

With a forced appearance of deliberation, they vote under the dominion of a stern necessity. They sit, as it were, in the heart of a foreign republic. They reside in a city whose constitution has come neither from their king's charter nor from their own legislative power. There they are surrounded by an army raised neither by the authority of their crown nor by their own command, and which, if they ordered it to dissolve itself, would instantly dissolve them. There they sit, after a gang of assassins has driven away some hundreds of the members, while those who hold the same moderate principles, with more patience or better hope, remain exposed every day to outrageous insult and murderous threats. There a majority — sometimes real, sometimes pretended, and itself a captive — compels a captive king to issue as royal edicts, at third hand, the polluted nonsense of their most licentious and giddy coffee houses. It is notorious that all their measures are decided before they are debated. It is beyond doubt that under the terror of the bayonet, the lamp-post and the torch at their houses, they are obliged to adopt all the crude and desperate measures suggested by clubs made up of a monstrous medley of every condition, tongue and nation. Among them are men in comparison with whom Catiline would be thought scrupulous and Cethegus a man of sobriety and moderation. Nor is it only in these clubs that public measures are deformed into monsters. They undergo a preliminary distortion in academies set up as so many seminaries for the clubs in every place of public resort. In these meetings of every kind, every counsel is taken for the mark of superior genius in proportion as it is daring and violent and treacherous. Humanity and compassion are ridiculed as the fruits of superstition and ignorance. Tenderness towards individuals is regarded as treason against the public. Liberty is always to be reckoned perfect in proportion as property is made insecure. Amid assassination, massacre and confiscation, committed or intended, they are drawing up plans for the good order of future society. Embracing the carcasses of base criminals and promoting their relations on the strength of their crimes, they drive hundreds of virtuous people to the same end by forcing them to live either by beggary or by crime.

The Assembly, which is their instrument, plays the farce of deliberation before them with as little decency as liberty. They act like the comedians at a fair before a riotous audience. They act amid the tumultuous shouting of a mixed mob of ferocious men and of women lost to shame, who, as their insolent fancy takes them, direct, control, applaud and hiss them off, and sometimes mingle with them and take seats among them, domineering over them with a strange mixture of servile impudence and proud, presumptuous authority. Having inverted order in everything, they have put the gallery in the place of the House. This assembly, which overthrows kings and kingdoms, has not even the face and appearance of a grave legislative body — nec color imperii, nec frons erat ulla senatûs: no colour of empire, no countenance of a senate.

They have been given a power like that of the Evil Principle, to subvert and destroy, but none to construct, except such machines as may be fitted for further subversion and further destruction. Who is there that admires and is attached from the heart to national representative assemblies who must not turn with horror and disgust from such a profane burlesque and abominable perversion of that sacred institution? Lovers of monarchy and lovers of republics must alike abhor it. The members of your Assembly must themselves groan under a tyranny of which they have all the shame, none of the direction and little of the profit. I am sure many of the members who make up even the majority of that body must feel as I do, notwithstanding the applause of the Revolution Society.

Miserable king! Miserable assembly! How silently scandalised that assembly must be by the members among them who could call a day that seemed to blot the sun out of heaven "un beau jour"! How inwardly indignant at hearing others think fit to declare to them that "the vessel of the state would fly forward in her course towards regeneration with more speed than ever", driven by the stiff gale of treason and murder that preceded our preacher's triumph! What must they have felt when, with outward patience and inward indignation, they heard of the slaughter of innocent gentlemen in their own houses that "the blood spilled was not the most pure"! What must they have felt when, besieged by complaints of disorders that shook their country to its foundations, they were compelled to tell the complainants coolly that they were under the protection of the law, and that they would petition the king — the captive king — to have the laws enforced for their protection, at a time when that captive king's enslaved ministers had formally notified them that there was neither law nor authority nor power left to protect anybody! What must they have felt at being obliged, by way of new year's congratulation, to ask their captive king to forget the stormy period of the last year, on account of the great good he was likely to produce for his people — the practical demonstrations of their loyalty being adjourned until that good should be completely attained, while they assured him of their obedience for a time when he would no longer have any authority to command!

Footnote: 6th of October, 1789.

This address was made with much good nature and affection, no doubt. But among the revolutions in France we must count a considerable revolution in their ideas of politeness. In England we are said to learn our manners at second hand from your side of the water, and to dress our behaviour in French frippery. If so, we are still in the old cut, and have not yet conformed so far to the new Parisian mode of good breeding as to think it quite the most refined strain of delicate compliment — whether of condolence or congratulation — to tell the most humiliated creature that crawls on the earth that great public benefits have been derived from the murder of his servants, the attempted assassination of himself and of his wife, and the mortification, disgrace and degradation he has personally suffered. It is a topic of consolation that our prison chaplain at Newgate would be too humane to use to a criminal at the foot of the gallows. I should have thought the hangman of Paris, now that he is liberalised by a vote of the National Assembly and allowed his rank and his coat of arms in the Herald's College of the rights of men, would be too generous a man, too gallant, too full of a sense of his new dignity, to employ that cutting consolation on any of the persons whom the crime of lèze-nation might bring under the administration of his executive powers.

A man has fallen indeed when he is flattered like this. The soothing draught of oblivion, drugged in this way, is well calculated to preserve a galling wakefulness and to feed the living ulcer of a corroding memory. To administer the opiate of amnesty powdered with all the ingredients of scorn and contempt is to hold to his lips, instead of "the balm of hurt minds", the cup of human misery filled to the brim, and to force him to drink it to the dregs.

Yielding to reasons at least as forcible as those so delicately urged in the new year compliment, the king of France will probably try to forget these events, and that compliment along with them. But history, which keeps a lasting record of all our acts and exercises her awful censure over the proceedings of sovereigns of every sort, will forget neither those events nor the era of this liberal refinement in the dealings of mankind. History will record that on the morning of the sixth of October, 1789, the king and queen of France, after a day of confusion, alarm, dismay and slaughter, lay down under the pledged security of public faith to give nature a few hours of respite and troubled, melancholy sleep. From that sleep the queen was first startled by the voice of the sentinel at her door, crying out to her to save herself by flight — that this was the last proof of fidelity he could give — that they were upon him, and he was a dead man. He was cut down instantly. A band of cruel ruffians and assassins, reeking with his blood, rushed into the queen's chamber and pierced with a hundred strokes of bayonet and dagger the bed from which this persecuted woman had only just had time to fly, almost naked, and had escaped by ways unknown to the murderers, to seek refuge at the feet of a king and husband who was not himself secure of his life for a moment.

This king, to say no more of him, and this queen, and their infant children — who would once have been the pride and hope of a great and generous people — were then forced to abandon the sanctuary of the most splendid palace in the world, which they left swimming in blood, polluted by massacre and strewn with scattered limbs and mutilated bodies. From there they were conducted to the capital of their kingdom. Two men had been picked out of the unprovoked, unresisted, indiscriminate slaughter that had been made of the gentlemen of birth and family who composed the king's bodyguard. These two gentlemen were cruelly and publicly dragged to the block, with all the parade of an execution of justice, and beheaded in the great court of the palace. Their heads were stuck on spears and led the procession, while the royal captives who followed in the train were moved slowly along amid the horrid yells, the shrilling screams, the frantic dancing, the infamous insults and all the unutterable abominations of the furies of hell in the abused shape of the vilest of women. After they had been made to taste, drop by drop, more than the bitterness of death, in the slow torture of a twelve-mile journey drawn out over six hours, they were lodged, under a guard made up of the very soldiers who had conducted them through this famous triumph, in one of the old palaces of Paris, now converted into a Bastille for kings.

Is this a triumph to be consecrated at altars, to be commemorated with grateful thanksgiving, to be offered up to the Divine Humanity with fervent prayer and enthusiastic ejaculation? These Theban and Thracian orgies, acted in France and applauded only in the Old Jewry, kindle prophetic enthusiasm, I assure you, in the minds of very few people in this kingdom — although a saint and apostle who may have revelations of his own, and who has so completely vanquished all the mean superstitions of the heart, may be inclined to think it pious and decorous to compare it with the entrance into the world of the Prince of Peace, proclaimed in a holy temple by a venerable sage, and not long before announced no worse by the voice of angels to the quiet innocence of shepherds.

At first I was at a loss to account for this fit of unguarded transport. I knew, of course, that the sufferings of monarchs are a delicious repast to certain palates. There were reflections that might have kept this appetite within some bounds of temperance. But when I took one circumstance into account, I was obliged to admit that a good deal of allowance ought to be made for the society, and that the temptation was too strong for ordinary discretion. I mean the circumstance of the victory-shout of the triumph, the animating cry calling for "all the BISHOPS to be hanged on the lamp-posts", which might well have brought on a burst of enthusiasm at the foreseen consequences of this happy day. To such enthusiasm I allow a little deviation from prudence. I allow this prophet to break out into hymns of joy and thanksgiving at an event that looks like the forerunner of the Millennium and of the projected Fifth Monarchy, in the destruction of all Church establishments. There was, however, as there is in all human affairs, something in the middle of this joy to exercise the patience of these worthy gentlemen and to try the long-suffering of their faith. The actual murder of the king and queen and their child was still wanting to the other auspicious circumstances of this "beautiful day". The actual murder of the bishops, though called for by so many holy ejaculations, was wanting too. A group of regicide and sacrilegious slaughter was indeed boldly sketched, but it was only sketched. Unhappily it was left unfinished, in this great history-piece of the massacre of the innocents. What hardy brush from the school of the rights of men will finish it remains to be seen. The age has not yet the complete benefit of that diffusion of knowledge which has undermined superstition and error; and the king of France needs another object or two to consign to oblivion, in consideration of all the good that is to come out of his own sufferings and the patriotic crimes of an enlightened age.

Footnote: "Tous les Évêques à la lanterne!"

Footnote: It is proper here to refer to a letter written on this subject by an eyewitness. That eyewitness was one of the most honest, intelligent and eloquent members of the National Assembly, and one of the most active and zealous reformers of the state.
