He was obliged to withdraw from the Assembly, and afterwards became a voluntary exile, on account of the horrors of this pious triumph and the dispositions of the men who, profiting by crimes if not causing them, have taken the lead in public affairs. Extract of M. de Lally Tollendal's Second Letter to a Friend. "Parlons du parti que j'ai pris; il est bien justifé dans ma conscience.—Ni cette ville coupable, ni cette assemblée plus coupable encore, ne méritoient que je me justifie; mais j'ai à cœur que vous, et les personnes qui pensent comme vous, ne me condamnent pas.—Ma santé, je vous jure, me rendoit mes fonctions impossibles; mais même en les mettant de côté il a été au-dessus de mes forces de supporter plus longtems l'horreur que me causoit ce sang,—ces têtes,—cette reine presque egorgée,—ce roi, amené esclave, entrant à Paris au milieu de ses assassins, et précédé des têtes de ses malheureux gardes,—ces perfides janissaires, ces assassins, ces femmes cannibales,—ce cri de TOUS LES ÉVÊQUES À LA LANTERNE, dans le moment où le roi entre sa capitale avec deux évêques de son conseil dans sa voiture,—un coup de fusil, que j'ai vu tirer dans un des carrosses de la reine,—M. Bailly appellant cela un beau jour,—l'assemblée ayant déclaré froidement le matin, qu'il n'étoit pas de sa dignité d'aller toute entière environner le roi,—M. Mirabeau disant impunément dans cette assemblée, que le vaisseau de l'état, loin d'être arrêté dans sa course, s'élanceroit avec plus de rapidité que jamais vers sa régénération,—M. Barnave, riant avec lui, quand des flots de sang couloient autour de nous,—le vertueux Mounier échappant par miracle à vingt assassins, qui avoient voulu faire de sa tête un trophée de plus: Voilà ce qui me fit jurer de ne plus mettre le pied dans cette caverne d'Antropophages [the National Assembly], où je n'avois plus de force d'élever la voix, où depuis six semaines je l'avois élevée en vain. Moi, Mounier, et tous les honnêtes gens, ont pensé que le dernier effort à faire pour le bien étoit d'en sortir. Aucune idée de crainte ne s'est approchée de moi. Je rougirois de m'en défendre. J'avois encore reçû sur la route de la part de ce peuple, moins coupable que ceux qui l'ont enivré de fureur, des acclamations, et des applaudissements, dont d'autres auroient été flattés, et qui m'ont fait frémir. C'est à l'indignation, c'est à l'horreur, c'est aux convulsions physiques, que le seul aspect du sang me fait éprouver que j'ai cédé. On brave une seule mort; on la brave plusieurs fois, quand elle peut être utile. Mais aucune puissance sous le ciel, mais aucune opinion publique ou privée n'ont le droit de me condamner à souffrir inutilement mille supplices par minute, et à périr de désespoir, de rage, au milieu des triomphes, du crime que je n'ai pu arrêter. Ils me proscriront, ils confisqueront mes biens. Je labourerai la terre, et je ne les verrai plus. Voilà ma justification. Vous pourrez la lire, la montrer, la laisser copier; tant pis pour ceux qui ne la comprendront pas; ce ne sera alors moi qui auroit eu tort de la leur donner." In English: "Let me speak of the course I have taken; it is fully justified in my own conscience. Neither that guilty city nor that still guiltier assembly deserved that I should justify myself; but I care that you, and those who think as you do, should not condemn me. My health, I swear to you, made my duties impossible; but even setting that aside, it was beyond my strength to endure any longer the horror that this blood caused me — those heads — that queen with her throat all but cut — that king brought in as a slave, entering Paris among his assassins and preceded by the heads of his unhappy guards — those treacherous janissaries, those assassins, those cannibal women — that cry of ALL THE BISHOPS TO THE LAMP-POST at the very moment when the king enters his capital with two bishops of his council in his carriage — a musket shot that I saw fired into one of the queen's coaches — M. Bailly calling this a beautiful day — the Assembly having coldly declared that morning that it was not consistent with its dignity to go in a body and surround the king — M. Mirabeau saying with impunity in that assembly that the ship of state, far from being checked in her course, would drive on towards her regeneration faster than ever — M. Barnave laughing along with him while floods of blood ran round about us — the virtuous Mounier escaping by a miracle from twenty assassins who wanted to make one more trophy of his head. That is what made me swear never again to set foot in that cavern of cannibals, where I no longer had the strength to raise my voice, and where for six weeks I had raised it in vain. Mounier and I, and all honest men, judged that the last effort we could make for good was to get out of it. No idea of fear came near me. I should blush to defend myself against the charge. On the road I had even received, from that people who are less guilty than the men who made them drunk with fury, acclamations and applause that would have flattered others and that made me shudder. It is to indignation, to horror, to the physical convulsions that the mere sight of blood brings on in me, that I have given way. A man faces a single death; he faces it many times over, when it can be of use. But no power under heaven, no opinion public or private, has the right to condemn me to suffer a thousand torments a minute to no purpose, and to perish of despair and rage in the middle of the triumphs of a crime I could not stop. They will proscribe me, they will confiscate my goods. I shall plough the earth, and I shall not see them again. There is my justification. You may read it, show it, let it be copied; so much the worse for those who will not understand it; it will not then be I who was in the wrong to give it to them." This military man had not such good nerves as the peaceable gentlemen of the Old Jewry. See M. Mounier's narrative of these transactions: also a man of honour and virtue and talent, and therefore a fugitive. (Note: M. Mounier was then speaker of the National Assembly. He has since been obliged to live in exile, though one of the firmest defenders of liberty.)

Although this work of our new light and knowledge did not go as far as in all probability it was meant to go, I must still think that such treatment of any human creature must be shocking to anyone not made for accomplishing revolutions. But I cannot stop there. Moved by the inborn feelings of my nature, and not illuminated by a single ray of this newly sprung modern light, I confess to you, Sir, that the exalted rank of the sufferers — and particularly the sex, the beauty and the amiable qualities of the descendant of so many kings and emperors, together with the tender age of royal infants, unaware only through infancy and innocence of the cruel outrages their parents were exposed to — instead of being a subject for exultation, adds not a little to what I feel about that most melancholy occasion.

I hear that the august person who was the principal object of our preacher's triumph, though he bore himself well, felt that shameful occasion deeply. As a man it became him to feel for his wife and his children, and for the faithful guards of his person who were massacred in cold blood around him. As a prince it became him to feel for the strange and frightful transformation of his civilised subjects, and to grieve more for them than to be anxious for himself. This takes little from his fortitude, while it adds infinitely to the honour of his humanity. I am very sorry to say — very sorry indeed — that such persons are in a situation in which it is not unbecoming in us to praise the virtues of the great.

I hear, and I rejoice to hear, that the great lady, the other object of the triumph, bore that day as she bears all the days since — one is anxious that beings made for suffering should suffer well — and that she bears the imprisonment of her husband, and her own captivity, and the exile of her friends, and the insulting flattery of formal addresses, and the whole weight of her accumulated wrongs, with a serene patience, in a manner suited to her rank and race and worthy of the daughter of a sovereign distinguished for her piety and her courage; that like her mother she has lofty feelings; that she feels with the dignity of a Roman matron; that in the last extremity she will save herself from the last disgrace; and that if she must fall, she will fall by no ignoble hand.

It is now sixteen or seventeen years since I saw the queen of France, then the Dauphiness, at Versailles; and surely there never lighted on this orb, which she hardly seemed to touch, a more delightful vision. I saw her just above the horizon, decorating and cheering the elevated sphere she had just begun to move in, glittering like the morning star, full of life and splendour and joy. Oh, what a revolution! And what a heart I must have, to contemplate that elevation and that fall without emotion! Little did I dream, when she added titles of veneration to those of enthusiastic, distant, respectful love, that she should ever be obliged to carry the sharp antidote against disgrace concealed in that bosom. Little did I dream that I should have lived to see such disasters fall on her in a nation of gallant men, in a nation of men of honour and of cavaliers. I thought ten thousand swords must have leapt from their scabbards to avenge even a look that threatened her with insult.

But the age of chivalry is gone. That of sophisters, economists and calculators has succeeded, and the glory of Europe is extinguished for ever. Never, never more shall we behold that generous loyalty to rank and sex, that proud submission, that dignified obedience, that subordination of the heart, which kept alive, even in servitude itself, the spirit of an exalted freedom. The unbought grace of life, the cheap defence of nations, the nurse of manly sentiment and heroic enterprise, is gone. It is gone, that sensibility of principle, that chastity of honour, which felt a stain like a wound, which inspired courage while it softened ferocity, which ennobled whatever it touched, and under which vice itself lost half its evil by losing all its grossness.

This mixed system of opinion and sentiment had its origin in the ancient chivalry; and the principle, though varied in appearance by the varying state of human affairs, lived on and had its influence through a long succession of generations, right down to the time we live in. If it should ever be totally extinguished, the loss, I fear, will be great. It is this that has given modern Europe its character. It is this that has distinguished Europe under all its forms of government, and distinguished it to its advantage, from the states of Asia, and possibly from the states that flourished in the most brilliant periods of the ancient world. It was this that, without confounding ranks, produced a noble equality and handed it down through all the gradations of social life. It was this opinion that softened kings into companions and raised private men to be the fellows of kings. Without force and without opposition, it subdued the fierceness of pride and power. It obliged sovereigns to submit to the soft collar of social esteem, compelled stern authority to submit to elegance, and gave a domination that vanquishes laws to be itself subdued by manners.

But now all this is to be changed. All the pleasing illusions that made power gentle and obedience liberal, that harmonised the different shades of life, and that by a bland assimilation brought into politics the feelings which beautify and soften private society, are to be dissolved by this new conquering empire of light and reason. All the decent drapery of life is to be rudely torn off. All the added ideas, furnished out of the wardrobe of a moral imagination, which the heart owns and the understanding ratifies as necessary to cover the defects of our naked, shivering nature and to raise it to dignity in our own estimation, are to be exploded as a ridiculous, absurd and antiquated fashion.

On this scheme of things, a king is only a man, a queen is only a woman, a woman is only an animal — and an animal not of the highest order. All homage paid to the sex in general as such, without any distinct view to the individual, is to be regarded as romance and folly. Regicide and parricide and sacrilege are only fictions of superstition, corrupting jurisprudence by destroying its simplicity. The murder of a king, or a queen, or a bishop, or a father is only common homicide — and if the people happen in any way to be gainers by it, a sort of homicide much the most pardonable, and one we ought not to inquire into too severely.

On the scheme of this barbarous philosophy, which is the offspring of cold hearts and muddy understandings, and which is as void of solid wisdom as it is destitute of all taste and elegance, laws are to be supported only by their own terrors, and by whatever concern each individual may find in them from his own private theorising, or can spare for them from his own private interests. In the groves of their academy, at the end of every avenue, you see nothing but the gallows. Nothing is left to engage the affections on behalf of the commonwealth. On the principles of this mechanical philosophy, our institutions can never be embodied, if I may use the expression, in persons, so as to create in us love, veneration, admiration or attachment. But the sort of reason that banishes the affections is incapable of taking their place. These public affections, combined with manners, are needed sometimes as supplements, sometimes as correctives, and always as aids to law. The rule laid down by a wise man, and a great critic too, for the construction of poems, is equally true of states: "Non satis est pulchra esse poemata, dulcia sunto" — it is not enough for poems to be beautiful; they must be sweet. There ought to be a system of manners in every nation that a well-formed mind would relish. To make us love our country, our country ought to be lovely.

But power of some kind or other will survive the shock in which manners and opinions perish, and it will find other and worse means of supporting itself. The usurpation that has destroyed ancient principles in order to subvert ancient institutions will hold power by arts similar to those by which it acquired it. When the old feudal and chivalrous spirit of fealty, which by freeing kings from fear freed both kings and subjects from the precautions of tyranny, is extinct in men's minds, plots and assassinations will be forestalled by preventive murder and preventive confiscation, and by that long roll of grim and bloody maxims which make up the political code of every power that does not rest on its own honour and the honour of those who are to obey it. Kings will be tyrants from policy, when subjects are rebels from principle.

When ancient opinions and rules of life are taken away, the loss cannot possibly be measured. From that moment we have no compass to steer by, nor can we tell distinctly for what port we are steering. Europe taken as a whole was undoubtedly in a flourishing condition on the day your Revolution was completed. How much of that prosperous state was owing to the spirit of our old manners and opinions is not easy to say; but as such causes cannot be without effect, we must presume that on the whole their effect was beneficial.

We are all too apt to consider things in the state we find them in, without sufficiently attending to the causes that produced them and may be holding them up. Nothing is more certain than that our manners, our civilisation, and all the good things connected with manners and civilisation, have in this European world of ours depended for ages on two principles, and were indeed the result of both combined: I mean the spirit of a gentleman and the spirit of religion. The nobility and the clergy, the one by profession and the other by patronage, kept learning in existence even amid arms and confusion, and while governments were still in their causes rather than formed. Learning paid back what it had received to nobility and priesthood, and paid it with interest, by enlarging their ideas and furnishing their minds. Happy, if they had all gone on knowing their indissoluble union and their proper places! Happy, if learning, not corrupted by ambition, had been content to remain the instructor and had not aspired to be the master! Along with its natural protectors and guardians, learning will be cast into the mire and trodden down under the hooves of a swinish multitude.

Footnote: See the fate of Bailly and Condorcet, supposed to be particularly alluded to here. Compare the circumstances of the trial and execution of the first of them with this prediction.

If, as I suspect, modern letters owe more than they are always willing to admit to ancient manners, so do other interests that we value quite as much as they are worth. Even commerce and trade and manufacture, the gods of our economical politicians, are themselves perhaps only creatures, only effects, which we choose to worship as first causes. They certainly grew up in the same shade in which learning flourished. They too may decay along with the principles that naturally protect them. With you, for the present at least, they all threaten to disappear together. Where a people lacks trade and manufactures, and the spirit of nobility and religion remains, sentiment supplies their place, and not always badly; but if commerce and the arts should be lost in an experiment to see how well a state can stand without these old fundamental principles, what sort of thing must a nation be — gross, stupid, ferocious, and at the same time poor and sordid barbarians, destitute of religion, honour or manly pride, possessing nothing at present and hoping for nothing hereafter?

I hope you are not going fast, and by the shortest road, to that horrible and disgusting condition. Already a poverty of conception, a coarseness and vulgarity, appears in all the proceedings of the Assembly and of all their instructors. Their liberty is not liberal. Their science is presumptuous ignorance. Their humanity is savage and brutal.

It is not clear whether in England we learned those grand and decorous principles and manners, of which considerable traces still remain, from you, or whether you took them from us. But I think we trace them best to you. You seem to me to be gentis incunabula nostræ, the cradle of our race. France has always influenced manners in England, more or less; and when your fountain is choked up and polluted, the stream will not run long or run clear with us, or perhaps with any nation. This gives all Europe, in my opinion, only too close and connected an interest in what is being done in France. Excuse me, then, if I have dwelt too long on the atrocious spectacle of the sixth of October, 1789, or have given too much scope to the reflections that have risen in my mind on the occasion of the most important of all revolutions, which may be dated from that day: I mean a revolution in sentiments, manners and moral opinions. As things now stand, with everything respectable destroyed outside us, and an attempt being made to destroy every principle of respect within us, one is almost forced to apologise for harbouring the common feelings of men.

Section Nine: Why I Feel Differently, and a Defence of Prejudice

Why do I feel so differently from the Reverend Dr Price, and from those of his lay flock who choose to adopt the sentiments of his discourse? For this plain reason: because it is natural that I should. Because we are so made as to be affected at such spectacles with melancholy thoughts about the unstable condition of mortal prosperity and the terrible uncertainty of human greatness. Because in those natural feelings we learn great lessons. Because in events like these our passions instruct our reason. Because when kings are hurled from their thrones by the Supreme Director of this great drama, and become objects of insult to the base and of pity to the good, we look on such disasters in the moral order as we should look on a miracle in the physical one. We are alarmed into reflection; our minds, as was observed long ago, are purified by terror and pity; our weak, unthinking pride is humbled under the workings of a mysterious wisdom. Some tears might be drawn from me if such a spectacle were shown on the stage. I should be truly ashamed to find in myself that superficial, theatrical sense of painted distress, while I could exult over the real thing in life. With a mind so perverted I could never venture to show my face at a tragedy. People would think that the tears Garrick used to extort from me, or Siddons not long since, were the tears of hypocrisy; I should know them to be the tears of folly.

Indeed, the theatre is a better school of moral sentiment than churches where the feelings of humanity are outraged like this. Poets, who have to deal with an audience not yet graduated from the school of the rights of men, and who must address themselves to the moral constitution of the heart, would not dare present such a triumph as a matter for exultation. There, where men follow their natural impulses, they would not tolerate the odious maxims of a Machiavellian policy, whether applied to gaining monarchical or democratic tyranny. They would reject them on the modern stage as they once did on the ancient, where an audience could not bear even the hypothetical proposal of such wickedness in the mouth of a tyrant being played, however suited it was to the character he sustained. No theatre audience in Athens would sit through what has been borne in the middle of the real tragedy of this triumphal day: a leading actor weighing, as though in scales hung up in a shop of horrors, so much actual crime against so much possible advantage — and after putting weights in and taking them out, announcing that the balance came down on the side of the advantages. They would not bear to see the crimes of the new democracy posted up as in a ledger against the crimes of the old despotism, and the book-keepers of politics finding democracy still in debt, but by no means unable or unwilling to pay the balance. In the theatre, the first instinctive glance, without any elaborate process of reasoning, would show that this method of political accounting would justify crime to any extent whatever. They would see that on these principles, even where the very worst acts were not committed, that was owing to the luck of the conspirators rather than to any thrift on their part in spending treachery and blood. They would soon see that criminal means, once tolerated, are soon preferred. They offer a shorter road to the object than the highway of the moral virtues. Once perfidy and murder are justified for the public benefit, the public benefit soon becomes the pretext and perfidy and murder the end — until rapacity, malice, revenge and a fear more dreadful than revenge have glutted their insatiable appetites. Such must be the consequence of losing, in the splendour of these triumphs of the rights of men, all natural sense of right and wrong.

But the reverend pastor exults in this "leading in triumph" because, if you please, Louis the Sixteenth was "an arbitrary monarch" — that is to say, in other words, neither more nor less than because he was Louis the Sixteenth, and because he had the misfortune to be born king of France, with prerogatives that a long line of ancestors, and a long acquiescence of the people, had put him in possession of without any act of his own. It has indeed turned out a misfortune to him that he was born king of France. But misfortune is not crime, nor is indiscretion always the greatest guilt. I shall never think that a prince whose whole reign was a series of concessions to his subjects, who was willing to relax his authority, to give up his prerogatives, to call his people to a share of freedom their ancestors had never known and perhaps never wanted — such a prince, though he may be subject to the common frailties of men and of princes, though he once thought it necessary to provide force against the desperate designs plainly being carried on against his person and the remains of his authority — even taking all that into account, I shall be brought only with great difficulty to think he deserves the cruel and insulting triumph of Paris and of Dr Price. I tremble for the cause of liberty, at such an example set before kings. I tremble for the cause of humanity, at the unpunished outrages of the most wicked of mankind. But there are people of that low and degenerate cast of mind who look up with a sort of complacent awe and admiration at kings who know how to keep firm in their seat, to hold a strict hand over their subjects, to assert their prerogative, and by the wide-awake vigilance of a severe despotism to guard against the very first approaches of freedom. Against such men as those they never raise their voices. Deserters from principle, enlisted with fortune, they never see any good in suffering virtue nor any crime in prosperous usurpation.

If it could be made clear to me that the king and queen of France — those, I mean, who were such before the triumph — were inexorable and cruel tyrants, and that they had formed a deliberate scheme for massacring the National Assembly (I think I have seen something like the latter insinuated in certain publications), I should think their captivity just. If that is true, much more ought to have been done — but done, in my opinion, in another manner. The punishment of real tyrants is a noble and awful act of justice, and it has truly been called a consolation to the human mind. But if I were to punish a wicked king I should have regard to the dignity of avenging the crime. Justice is grave and decorous, and in its punishments seems rather to submit to a necessity than to make a choice. Had the subject been Nero, or Agrippina, or Louis the Eleventh, or Charles the Ninth — if Charles the Twelfth of Sweden after the murder of Patkul, or his predecessor Christina after the murder of Monaldeschi, had fallen into your hands, Sir, or into mine, I am sure our conduct would have been different.

If the French king, or the king of the French — or whatever name he goes by in the new vocabulary of your constitution — has really, in his own person and in his queen's, deserved these unacknowledged but unavenged attempts at murder, and those frequent indignities that are crueller than murder, then such a person would ill deserve even the subordinate executive trust that I understand is to be placed in him; nor is he fit to be called chief of a nation he has outraged and oppressed. A worse choice for such an office in a new commonwealth than a deposed tyrant could not possibly be made. But to degrade and insult a man as the worst of criminals, and afterwards to trust him with your highest concerns as a faithful, honest and zealous servant, is not consistent in reasoning, nor prudent in policy, nor safe in practice. Those who could make such an appointment would be guilty of a more flagrant breach of trust than any they have committed against the people yet. Since this is the only crime in which your leading politicians could have acted inconsistently, I conclude that there is no sort of ground for these horrid insinuations. I think no better of all the other calumnies.

In England we give them no credit. We are generous enemies; we are faithful allies. We spurn from us with disgust and indignation the slanders of men who bring us their anecdotes with the fleur-de-lis branded on their shoulder. We have Lord George Gordon fast in Newgate; and neither his being a public convert to Judaism, nor his having in his zeal against Catholic priests and clergy of every sort raised a mob — excuse the term, it is still in use here — that pulled down all our prisons, has preserved him a liberty he did not make himself worthy of by any virtuous use of it. We have rebuilt Newgate and tenanted the mansion. We have prisons almost as strong as the Bastille for those who dare to libel the queens of France. In that spiritual retreat let the noble libeller remain. Let him meditate there on his Talmud until he learns a conduct more becoming to his birth and his parts, and less disgraceful to the ancient religion he has converted to — or until some persons from your side of the water, to please your new Hebrew brethren, ransom him. He may then be able to purchase, with the old hoards of the synagogue and a very small percentage on the long compound interest of the thirty pieces of silver (Dr Price has shown us what miracles compound interest will perform in 1790 years), the lands that have lately been discovered to have been usurped by the Gallican Church. Send us your Popish Archbishop of Paris, and we will send you our Protestant rabbi. We shall treat the person you send us in exchange like a gentleman and an honest man, as he is; but pray let him bring the fund of his hospitality, bounty and charity with him, and depend upon it we shall never confiscate a shilling of that honourable and pious fund, nor think of enriching the Treasury with the spoils of the poor-box.

To tell you the truth, my dear Sir, I think the honour of our nation is somewhat concerned in disowning the proceedings of this society of the Old Jewry and the London Tavern. I hold no man's proxy. I speak only for myself when I disclaim, as I do with all possible earnestness, all communion with the actors in that triumph or with its admirers. When I assert anything else about the people of England, I speak from observation and not from authority; but I speak from the experience I have had in a fairly wide and mixed acquaintance with the inhabitants of this kingdom, of every description and rank, and after a course of attentive observation begun early in life and continued for nearly forty years. Considering that we are divided from you by a slender ditch of about twenty-four miles, and that traffic between the two countries has lately been very great, I have often been astonished to find how little you seem to know of us. I suspect this is because you form your judgment of this nation from certain publications which represent the opinions and dispositions generally prevalent in England very erroneously, if they represent them at all. The vanity, restlessness, petulance and spirit of intrigue of several petty cabals, who try to hide their total unimportance in bustle and noise and puffing and quoting one another, makes you imagine that our contemptuous neglect of their abilities is a general sign that we agree with their opinions. No such thing, I assure you. Because half a dozen grasshoppers under a fern make the field ring with their importunate chirping, while thousands of great cattle repose beneath the shadow of the British oak and chew the cud and are silent, pray do not imagine that the ones making the noise are the only inhabitants of the field, or that they are therefore many in number, or that they are anything after all but the little, shrivelled, meagre, hopping, though loud and troublesome insects of the hour.

I will almost venture to say that not one in a hundred among us shares in the "triumph" of the Revolution Society. If the king and queen of France and their children were to fall into our hands by the chance of war, in the bitterest of all hostilities — I deprecate such an event, I deprecate such hostility — they would be treated to another sort of triumphal entry into London. We have had a king of France in that situation before now; you have read how he was treated by the victor in the field, and in what manner he was afterwards received in England. Four hundred years have passed over us, but I do not believe we are materially changed since then. Thanks to our sullen resistance to innovation, thanks to the cold sluggishness of our national character, we still bear the stamp of our forefathers. We have not, as I conceive, lost the generosity and dignity of thinking of the fourteenth century; nor have we yet refined ourselves into savages. We are not the converts of Rousseau; we are not the disciples of Voltaire; Helvétius has made no progress among us. Atheists are not our preachers; madmen are not our lawgivers. We know that we have made no discoveries, and we think none are to be made, in morality — nor many in the great principles of government, nor in the ideas of liberty, which were understood long before we were born quite as well as they will be after the grave has heaped its earth on our presumption and the silent tomb has imposed its law on our pert loquacity. In England we have not yet been completely gutted of our natural entrails. We still feel within us, and we cherish and cultivate, those inbred sentiments which are the faithful guardians and active monitors of our duty and the true supporters of every liberal and manly morality. We have not been drawn and trussed so that we might be stuffed, like birds in a museum, with chaff and rags and paltry blurred shreds of paper about the rights of man. We keep the whole of our feelings still native and entire, unspoiled by pedantry and unbelief. We have real hearts of flesh and blood beating in our breasts. We fear God; we look up with awe to kings, with affection to Parliaments, with duty to magistrates, with reverence to priests and with respect to nobility. Why? Because when such ideas are brought before our minds it is natural to be affected in that way; because all other feelings are false and spurious, and tend to corrupt our minds, to vitiate our primary morals, to make us unfit for rational liberty, and, by teaching us a servile, licentious and abandoned insolence, to make us the cheap sport of a few holidays and perfectly fit for — and justly deserving of — slavery for the whole course of our lives.

Footnote: The English are, I think, misrepresented in a letter published in one of the papers by a gentleman thought to be a Dissenting minister. Writing to Dr Price about the spirit prevailing in Paris, he says: "The spirit of the people in this place has abolished all the proud distinctions which the king and nobles had usurped in their minds: whether they talk of the king, the noble, or the priest, their whole language is that of the most enlightened and liberal amongst the English." If this gentleman means to confine the terms enlightened and liberal to one set of men in England, it may be true. It is not generally so.

You see, Sir, that in this enlightened age I am bold enough to confess that we are generally men of untaught feelings; that instead of throwing away all our old prejudices we cherish them to a very considerable degree; and, to take more shame to ourselves, we cherish them because they are prejudices, and the longer they have lasted and the more generally they have prevailed, the more we cherish them. We are afraid to set men up to live and trade each on his own private stock of reason, because we suspect that the stock in each man is small, and that the individuals would do better to draw on the general bank and capital of nations and of ages. Many of our men of speculation, instead of exploding a general prejudice, employ their sagacity to discover the latent wisdom in it. If they find what they are looking for — and they seldom fail — they think it wiser to keep the prejudice with the reason wrapped inside it than to throw away the coat of prejudice and leave nothing but the naked reason; because prejudice, with its reason, carries a motive to put that reason into action and an affection that will make it last. Prejudice is ready to hand in an emergency. It engages the mind beforehand in a steady course of wisdom and virtue, and does not leave a man hesitating at the moment of decision, sceptical, puzzled and unresolved. Prejudice makes a man's virtue his habit, and not a series of unconnected acts. Through just prejudice his duty becomes a part of his nature.

Your literary men and your politicians, and the whole clan of the enlightened among us with them, differ from us fundamentally on these points. They have no respect for the wisdom of others, but they make up for it by a very full measure of confidence in their own. With them it is a sufficient reason to destroy an old scheme of things that it is an old one. As to the new, they feel no sort of fear about how long a building run up in haste will last, because duration is no object to men who think that little or nothing was done before their own time and who place all their hopes in discovery. They conceive, very systematically, that everything which gives permanence is mischievous, and so they are at inexpiable war with all establishments. They think government may be varied like fashions in dress, and with as little ill effect; that no principle of attachment to any constitution of the state is needed beyond a sense of present convenience. They always speak as though they held that there is a peculiar sort of contract between them and their magistrates which binds the magistrate but has nothing reciprocal in it, and that the majesty of the people has a right to dissolve it for no reason but its own will. Their attachment even to their own country goes only so far as it agrees with some of their passing projects; it begins and ends with whatever scheme of politics happens to suit their opinion of the moment.

These doctrines, or rather these sentiments, seem to prevail with your new statesmen. But they are wholly different from those we have always acted on in this country.

I hear that it is sometimes given out in France that what is being done among you follows the example of England. Allow me to say that hardly anything done with you has originated in the practice or the prevailing opinions of this people, either in the act or in the spirit of the proceeding. Let me add that we are as unwilling to learn these lessons from France as we are sure that we never taught them to that nation. The cabals here who take a sort of share in your transactions consist as yet of only a handful of people. If, unluckily, by their intrigues and their sermons and their publications, and by a confidence drawn from an expected union with the counsels and forces of the French nation, they should draw considerable numbers into their faction, and should then seriously attempt anything here in imitation of what has been done with you, the outcome, I will venture to prophesy, will be that with some trouble to their country they will soon accomplish their own destruction. This people refused to change their law in remote ages out of respect for the infallibility of popes, and they will not alter it now out of a pious implicit faith in the dogmatism of philosophers — though the first was armed with the anathema and the crusade, and though the second should act with the libel and the lamp-post.

Formerly your affairs were your own concern only. We felt for them as men, but we kept aloof from them, because we were not citizens of France. But when we see the model held up for ourselves, we must feel as Englishmen, and, feeling, we must provide as Englishmen. Your affairs, in spite of us, are made part of our interest — so far at least as to keep your panacea, or your plague, at a distance. If it is a panacea, we do not want it: we know the consequences of unnecessary medicine. If it is a plague, it is such a plague that the precautions of the strictest quarantine ought to be set up against it.

I hear on all sides that a cabal calling itself philosophic takes the glory of many of these late proceedings, and that its opinions and systems are the true animating spirit of the whole of them. I have never heard of any party in England, literary or political, known at any time by such a description. It is not composed of those men, is it, whom ordinary people in their blunt, homely style commonly call atheists and infidels? If it is, I admit that we too have had writers of that description, who made some noise in their day. At present they repose in lasting oblivion. Who born within the last forty years has read one word of Collins, and Toland, and Tindal, and Chubb, and Morgan, and that whole race who called themselves freethinkers? Who reads Bolingbroke now? Who ever read him through? Ask the booksellers of London what has become of all these lights of the world. In as few years their few successors will go to the family vault of "all the Capulets". But whatever they were, or are, with us they were and are wholly unconnected individuals. With us they kept the common nature of their kind and were not gregarious. They never acted in a body, nor were they known as a faction in the state, nor did they presume to influence any of our public concerns in that name or character or for the purposes of such a faction. Whether they ought to exist so, and be permitted to act so, is another question. As no such cabals have existed in England, so neither has the spirit of them had any influence in establishing the original frame of our constitution, or in any one of the several repairs and improvements it has undergone. The whole has been done under the auspices, and is confirmed by the sanction, of religion and piety. The whole has come out of the simplicity of our national character, and from a sort of native plainness and directness of understanding which for a long time marked the men who have successively held authority among us. That disposition remains still — at least in the great body of the people.

We know, and, what is better, we feel inwardly, that religion is the basis of civil society and the source of all good and all comfort. In England we are so convinced of this that there is no rust of superstition, with which the accumulated absurdity of the human mind might have crusted it over in the course of ages, that ninety-nine in a hundred of the people of England would not prefer to impiety. We shall never be such fools as to call in an enemy of the substance of any system to remove its corruptions, to supply its defects, or to perfect its construction. If our religious tenets should ever need further explaining, we shall not call on atheism to explain them. We shall not light up our temple from that unhallowed fire. It will be illuminated with other lights. It will be perfumed with other incense than the infectious stuff imported by the smugglers of adulterated metaphysics. If our church establishment should need revision, it is not avarice or rapacity, public or private, that we shall employ to audit or receive or apply its consecrated revenue. Violently condemning neither the Greek nor the Armenian, nor, now that the heats have subsided, the Roman system of religion, we prefer the Protestant — not because we think it has less of the Christian religion in it, but because in our judgment it has more. We are Protestants not from indifference but from zeal.

Footnote: Sit igitur hoc ab initio persuasum civibus, dominos esse omnium rerum ac moderatores deos; eaque, quæ gerantur, eorum geri vi, ditione, ac numine; eosdemque optime de genere hominum mereri; et qualis quisque sit, quid agat, quid in se admittat, qua mente, qua pietate colat religiones intueri: piorum et impiorum habere rationem. His enim rebus imbutæ mentes haud sane abhorrebunt ab utili et a vera sententia. — Cicero, On the Laws, book 2. (Let the citizens be persuaded from the first that the gods are the lords and rulers of all things, that what is done is done by their power and authority, that they are the great benefactors of the human race, and that they observe what each man is, what he does and what he takes upon himself, with what mind and with what piety he keeps the observances of religion — and that they take account of the pious and the impious. Minds steeped in these things will hardly be averse to a useful and a true opinion.)

We know, and it is our pride to know, that man is by his constitution a religious animal; that atheism is against not only our reason but our instincts, and that it cannot prevail for long. But if, in a moment of riot, in a drunken delirium from the hot spirit drawn out of the alembic of hell which is now boiling so furiously in France, we should uncover our nakedness by throwing off the Christian religion, which has been our boast and comfort till now and one great source of civilisation among us and among many other nations, then we are apprehensive — being well aware that the mind will not endure a vacuum — that some uncouth, pernicious and degrading superstition might take its place.

For that reason, before we take away the natural, human means of respect from our establishment and give it up to contempt, as you have done, and in doing it have incurred the penalties you richly deserve to suffer, we ask that something else be presented to us in its place. We shall then form our judgment.

On these ideas, instead of quarrelling with establishments, as some do who have made a philosophy and a religion out of their hostility to such institutions, we cleave closely to them. We are resolved to keep an established church, an established monarchy, an established aristocracy and an established democracy, each in the degree in which it exists, and in no greater. I shall show you presently how much of each of these we possess.

It has been the misfortune of this age — not, as these gentlemen think it, its glory — that everything is to be argued about, as if the constitution of our country were always to be a subject of altercation rather than of enjoyment. For that reason, and also for the satisfaction of any among you who may wish to profit by examples, I venture to trouble you with a few thoughts on each of these establishments. I do not think they were unwise in ancient Rome, who, when they wanted to remodel their laws, sent commissioners to examine the best-constituted republics within their reach.

Section Ten: The Church Establishment, and the Seizure of Its Property

First let me speak of our church establishment, which is the first of our prejudices — not a prejudice destitute of reason, but one with profound and far-reaching wisdom wrapped up in it. I speak of it first. It is first and last and midmost in our minds. For taking our stand on the religious system we are now in possession of, we go on acting on the sense of mankind, early received and uniformly continued. That sense has not only, like a wise architect, built up the august fabric of states; like a provident owner, to preserve the structure from profanation and ruin as a sacred temple purged of all the impurities of fraud and violence and injustice and tyranny, it has solemnly and for ever consecrated the commonwealth and everyone who officiates in it. This consecration is made so that all who administer the government of men — in which they stand in the person of God Himself — should have high and worthy notions of their function and their destination; so that their hope should be full of immortality; so that they should look not to the paltry pelf of the moment, nor to the temporary and passing praise of ordinary people, but to a solid, permanent existence in the permanent part of their nature, and to a permanent fame and glory in the example they leave as a rich inheritance to the world.

Such sublime principles ought to be infused into persons in exalted situations, and religious establishments provided that will continually revive and enforce them. Every sort of moral, every sort of civil, every sort of political institution that aids the rational and natural ties connecting the human understanding and affections to the divine is no more than necessary to build up that wonderful structure, Man — whose prerogative it is to be to a great degree a creature of his own making, and who, when he is made as he ought to be made, is destined to hold no trivial place in the creation. But whenever a man is set over men, as the better nature ought always to preside, in that case above all he should be brought as near to his perfection as possible.

The consecration of the state by a state religious establishment is also necessary in order to work a wholesome awe upon free citizens; because to secure their freedom they must enjoy some definite portion of power. To them, therefore, a religion connected with the state and with their duty towards it becomes even more necessary than it is in societies where the people, by the terms of their subjection, are confined to private sentiments and the management of their own family concerns. Everyone possessing any portion of power ought to be strongly and awfully impressed with the idea that they act in trust, and that they must account for their conduct in that trust to the one great Master, Author and Founder of society.

This principle ought to be impressed even more strongly on the minds of those who make up the collective sovereignty than on the minds of single princes. Without instruments those princes can do nothing. Whoever uses instruments, in finding helps, finds impediments as well. Their power is therefore by no means complete, nor are they safe in extreme abuse of it. Such persons, however elevated by flattery and arrogance and self-conceit, must be aware that in one way or another, whether positive law covers them or not, they are accountable even here for abusing their trust. If they are not cut off by a rebellion of their people, they may be strangled by the very janissaries kept for their security against every other rebellion. So we have seen the king of France sold by his soldiers for an increase of pay. But where popular authority is absolute and unrestrained, the people have an infinitely greater confidence in their own power, because a far better founded one. They are themselves in great measure their own instruments. They are nearer to their objects. Besides, they are less answerable to one of the greatest controlling powers on earth, the sense of fame and reputation. The share of infamy likely to fall to each individual in a public act is small indeed: the force of opinion works in inverse ratio to the number of those who abuse the power. Their own approval of their own acts has to them the appearance of a public verdict in their favour. A perfect democracy is therefore the most shameless thing in the world. Being the most shameless, it is also the most fearless. No man there fears that he can personally be made subject to punishment. Certainly the people at large never ought to be: for as all punishments are examples made for the preservation of the people at large, the people at large can never become the subject of punishment by any human hand. It is therefore of infinite importance that they should not be allowed to imagine that their will, any more than a king's, is the standard of right and wrong. They ought to be persuaded that they are quite as little entitled, and far less qualified, to use arbitrary power of any kind with safety to themselves; and that therefore they are not, under a false show of liberty, really to exercise an unnatural, inverted domination, tyrannically exacting from those who serve the state not an entire devotion to their interest, which is their right, but an abject submission to their momentary will — extinguishing by that means, in everyone who serves them, all moral principle, all sense of dignity, all use of judgment and all consistency of character, while by the very same process they hand themselves over as a proper, a suitable, but a most contemptible prey to the servile ambition of popular sycophants or courtly flatterers.

Footnote: Quicquid multis peccatur inultum. (Whatever is done wrong by many goes unpunished. — Lucan.)

When the people have emptied themselves of all the appetite of selfish will — which without religion it is utterly impossible they ever should — and when they are conscious that they exercise, and exercise perhaps at a higher link in the chain of delegation, a power which to be legitimate must accord with that eternal, unchangeable law in which will and reason are the same thing, they will be more careful how they place power in base and incapable hands. In naming men to office they will not appoint to the exercise of authority as to a pitiful job, but as to a holy function; not according to their sordid, selfish interest, nor their wanton caprice, nor their arbitrary will. They will confer that power — which any man may well tremble to give or to receive — only on those in whom they can discern that predominant proportion of active virtue and wisdom, taken together and fitted to the charge, which is to be found in the great and unavoidable mixed mass of human imperfections and infirmities.

When they are habitually convinced that no evil can be acceptable, either in the doing or the permitting, to Him whose essence is good, they will be better able to root out of the minds of all magistrates — civil, ecclesiastical or military — anything that bears the least resemblance to a proud and lawless domination.

But one of the first and most leading principles on which the commonwealth and the laws are consecrated is this: that the temporary possessors and life-tenants in it, forgetting what they have received from their ancestors or what is owed to their posterity, should not act as if they were the outright masters; that they should not think it among their rights to cut off the entail or commit waste on the inheritance, destroying at their pleasure the whole original fabric of their society, and risking leaving those who come after them a ruin instead of a habitation — teaching those successors to respect their contrivances as little as they had themselves respected the institutions of their forefathers. By this unprincipled readiness to change the state as often and as much and in as many ways as there are floating fancies or fashions, the whole chain and continuity of the commonwealth would be broken; no one generation could link with another; men would become little better than the flies of a summer.

And first of all, the science of jurisprudence, the pride of the human intellect, which with all its defects and redundancies and errors is the collected reason of ages, combining the principles of original justice with the infinite variety of human concerns, would be studied no longer, being treated as a heap of old exploded errors. Personal self-sufficiency and arrogance — the certain attendants on everyone who has never met with a wisdom greater than his own — would usurp the tribunal. Of course there would then be no settled laws establishing invariable grounds of hope and fear to keep men's actions in a certain course or direct them to a certain end. Nothing stable in the ways of holding property or exercising office could give a solid ground for any parent to calculate on in educating his children, or in choosing how they were to be established in the world later. No principles would be worked early into their habits. As soon as the ablest instructor had finished his laborious course of teaching, instead of sending out his pupil accomplished in a virtuous discipline that would win him attention and respect in his place in society, he would find everything altered, and that he had turned out a poor creature for the contempt and derision of the world, ignorant of the true grounds on which anyone is now valued. Who could guarantee that a tender and delicate sense of honour would beat almost with the first pulses of the heart, when no man could know what the test of honour would be in a nation continually varying the standard of its coinage? No part of life would keep what it had gained. Barbarism in science and literature, unskilfulness in the arts and manufactures, would infallibly follow from the want of a steady education and a settled principle; and so the commonwealth itself would in a few generations crumble away, be disconnected into the dust and powder of individuality, and at last be scattered to all the winds of heaven.

To avoid, then, the evils of inconstancy and changeableness, ten thousand times worse than those of obstinacy and the blindest prejudice, we have consecrated the state, so that no man should approach to look into its defects or corruptions except with due caution; so that he should never dream of beginning its reformation by its subversion; so that he should come to the faults of the state as to the wounds of a father, with pious awe and trembling concern. By this wise prejudice we are taught to look with horror on those children of their country who are ready to hack that aged parent rashly in pieces and put him in the magicians' kettle, in hopes that by their poisonous weeds and wild incantations they may regenerate the paternal constitution and renew their father's life.

Society is indeed a contract. Subordinate contracts for objects of merely occasional interest may be dissolved at pleasure; but the state ought not to be considered as nothing better than a partnership agreement in a trade of pepper and coffee, calico or tobacco, or some other such low concern, to be taken up for a little temporary interest and dissolved at the whim of the parties. It is to be looked on with another sort of reverence, because it is not a partnership in things that serve only the gross animal existence of a temporary and perishable nature. It is a partnership in all science, a partnership in all art, a partnership in every virtue and in all perfection. As the ends of such a partnership cannot be reached in many generations, it becomes a partnership not only between those who are living, but between those who are living, those who are dead, and those who are to be born. Each contract of each particular state is only a clause in the great primeval contract of eternal society, linking the lower natures with the higher, connecting the visible and the invisible world, according to a fixed compact sanctioned by the inviolable oath that holds all physical and all moral natures each in its appointed place. This law is not subject to the will of those who, by an obligation above them and infinitely superior to them, are bound to submit their own will to it. The municipal corporations of that universal kingdom are not morally at liberty, at their pleasure and on their speculations about some possible improvement, to separate and tear apart wholesale the bonds of their subordinate community and dissolve it into an unsocial, uncivil, unconnected chaos of elementary principles. Only the first and supreme necessity — a necessity that is not chosen but chooses, a necessity paramount to deliberation, which admits no discussion and demands no evidence — can justify a resort to anarchy. This necessity is no exception to the rule, because the necessity is itself also a part of that moral and physical disposition of things which man must obey by consent or by force. But if what is only submission to necessity is made an object of choice, the law is broken, nature is disobeyed, and the rebels are outlawed, cast out and exiled from this world of reason and order and peace and virtue and fruitful penitence into the opposite world of madness, discord, vice, confusion and unavailing sorrow.

These, my dear Sir, are, and were, and I think long will be, the sentiments of not the least learned and reflecting part of this kingdom. Those included in that description form their opinions on such grounds as such persons ought to form them on. The less inquiring receive them from an authority which men whom Providence dooms to live on trust need not be ashamed to rely on. These two sorts of men move in the same direction, though not in the same place. They both move with the order of the universe. They all know or feel this great ancient truth: "Quod illi principi et præpotenti Deo qui omnem hunc mundum regit nihil eorum quæ quidem fiant in terris acceptius quam concilia et coetus hominum jure sociati quæ civitates appellantur" — nothing that is done on earth is more acceptable to that supreme and all-powerful God who rules this whole world than those assemblies and gatherings of men associated under law which are called states. They take this tenet of the head and heart not from the great name it immediately bears, nor from the greater name it is derived from, but from the only thing that can give true weight and sanction to any learned opinion: the common nature and common relation of men. Persuaded that all things ought to be done with reference to a point, and referring everything to the point of reference all things should be directed to, they think themselves bound to renew the memory of their high origin and lineage not only as individuals in the sanctuary of the heart, or gathered together in that personal capacity, but also in their corporate character, to perform their national homage to the Institutor and Author and Protector of civil society — without which civil society man could not possibly reach the perfection his nature is capable of, nor even make a remote and faint approach to it. They conceive that He who gave us our nature to be perfected by our virtue also willed the necessary means of its perfection: He willed, therefore, the state; He willed its connection with the source and original archetype of all perfection. Those who are convinced of this His will, which is the law of laws and the sovereign of sovereigns, cannot think it blameworthy that this corporate fealty and homage of ours, this recognition of a paramount lordship — I had almost said this offering up of the state itself as a worthy oblation on the high altar of universal praise — should be performed, as all public, solemn acts are performed, in buildings, in music, in decoration, in speech, in the dignity of persons, according to the customs of mankind as their nature has taught them: that is, with modest splendour, with unassuming state, with mild majesty and sober pomp. For those purposes they think some part of the wealth of the country is as usefully employed as it could be in feeding the luxury of individuals. It is the public ornament. It is the public consolation. It nourishes the public hope. The poorest man finds his own importance and dignity in it, whereas the wealth and pride of individuals at every moment makes the man of humble rank and fortune feel his inferiority, and degrades and cheapens his condition. It is for the man in humble life, to raise his nature and to remind him of a state in which the privileges of wealth will cease, where he will be equal by nature and may be more than equal by virtue, that this portion of his country's general wealth is employed and sanctified.

I assure you I do not aim at singularity. I am giving you opinions that have been accepted among us from very early times to this moment, with a continued and general approval, and which are indeed so worked into my mind that I cannot distinguish what I have learned from others from the results of my own reflection.

It is on some such principles that the majority of the people of England, far from thinking a national religious establishment unlawful, hardly think it lawful to be without one. In France you are wholly mistaken if you do not believe us attached to it above all other things and beyond all other nations; and where this people has acted unwisely and unjustifiably in its favour, as in some instances they most certainly have, you will at least discover their zeal in their very errors.

This principle runs through their whole system of politics. They do not regard their church establishment as convenient but as essential to their state — not as something foreign and separable, added on for convenience, which they may either keep up or lay aside according to their notions of the moment. They regard it as the foundation of their whole constitution, with which, and with every part of which, it holds an indissoluble union. Church and state are inseparable ideas in their minds, and the one is scarcely ever mentioned without the other.

Our education is so shaped as to confirm and fix this impression. Our education is in a manner wholly in the hands of clergymen, at every stage from infancy to manhood. Even when our young men leave schools and universities and enter that most important period of life when experience and study begin to link together, and when with that in view they visit other countries, three quarters of those who go abroad with our young nobility and gentlemen are clergymen — not as austere masters, nor as mere attendants, but as friends and companions of a graver cast, and not seldom men as well born as themselves. Instead of the old family servants we have seen acting as governors to leading men from other parts of Europe, we send these; and with them, as with relations, our young men commonly keep up a close connection through life. By this connection we conceive that we attach our gentlemen to the church, and that we liberalise the church by its intercourse with the leading characters of the country.

We are so tenacious of the old ecclesiastical forms and fashions of instruction that very little alteration has been made in them since the fourteenth or fifteenth century, adhering in this as in everything else to our old settled maxim, never to depart from antiquity entirely or all at once. We found these old institutions on the whole favourable to morality and discipline, and we thought they could be amended without altering the ground they stood on. We thought they were capable of receiving and improving, and above all of preserving, the additions of science and literature as the order of Providence should successively produce them. And after all, with this Gothic and monkish education — for such it is in its groundwork — we may put in our claim to as ample and as early a share in all the improvements in science, in the arts and in literature that have illuminated and adorned the modern world as any other nation in Europe: and we think one main cause of that improvement was our not despising the patrimony of knowledge our forefathers left us.

It is because of our attachment to a church establishment that the English nation did not think it wise to entrust that great fundamental interest of the whole to what they entrust no part of their civil or military public service to — that is, to the unsteady and precarious contributions of individuals. They go further. They certainly never have allowed, and never will allow, the fixed estate of the church to be converted into a pension, dependent on the Treasury, to be delayed, withheld, or perhaps extinguished by fiscal difficulties — difficulties that may sometimes be pretended for political purposes, and are in fact often brought on by the extravagance, negligence and rapacity of politicians. The people of England think they have constitutional motives, as well as religious ones, against any scheme for turning their independent clergy into ecclesiastical pensioners of the state. They tremble for their liberty at the thought of the influence of a clergy dependent on the crown; they tremble for the public peace at the thought of the disorders of a factious clergy, if it were made to depend on anything other than the crown. They therefore made their church, like their king and their nobility, independent.

Out of these united considerations of religion and constitutional policy, and out of their sense of a duty to make sure provision for the consolation of the feeble and the instruction of the ignorant, they have incorporated and identified the estate of the church with the mass of private property, of which the state is not the owner, either for use or for dominion, but only the guardian and the regulator. They have provided that the endowment of this establishment should be as stable as the earth it stands on, and should not fluctuate with the tides of stocks and shares.

The men of England — I mean the men of light and leading in England, whose wisdom, if they have any, is open and direct — would be ashamed, as of a silly and deceitful trick, to profess a religion in name that by their proceedings they plainly despise. If by their conduct, the only language that rarely lies, they seemed to regard the great ruling principle of the moral and natural world as a mere invention for keeping ordinary people in obedience, they think they would defeat the political purpose they had in view by such conduct. They would find it difficult to make others believe in a system they visibly gave no credit to themselves. The Christian statesmen of this land would indeed provide first for the multitude, because it is the multitude, and is therefore as such the first object of the ecclesiastical institution and of all institutions. They have been taught that the fact of the Gospel's being preached to the poor was one of the great tests of its true mission. They think, therefore, that those who do not take care that it should be preached to the poor do not believe it. But since they know that charity is not confined to any one description of people but ought to apply itself to all who have wants, they are not deprived of a due and anxious feeling of pity for the distresses of the miserable great. They are not repelled by a fastidious delicacy, by the stench of their arrogance and presumption, from giving a medicinal attention to their mental blotches and running sores. They are aware that religious instruction is of more consequence to those people than to any others: from the greatness of the temptation they are exposed to; from the important consequences that attend their faults; from the contagion of their bad example; from the necessity of bending the stubborn neck of their pride and ambition to the yoke of moderation and virtue; and from a consideration of the fat stupidity and gross ignorance about what it most concerns men to know which prevails at courts, and at the head of armies, and in senates, quite as much as at the loom and in the field.

The English people are satisfied that to the great the consolations of religion are as necessary as its instructions. They too are among the unhappy. They feel personal pain and domestic sorrow. In these they have no privilege, but must pay their full share of the levy imposed on mortality. They want this sovereign balm under their gnawing cares and anxieties, which, being less taken up with the limited wants of animal life, range without limit and are varied by infinite combinations in the wild and unbounded regions of the imagination. Some charitable dole is needed by these, our often very unhappy brethren, to fill the gloomy void that reigns in minds with nothing on earth to hope or fear; something to relieve the killing languor and overworked lassitude of people who have nothing to do; something to excite an appetite for existence in the jaded satiety that attends all pleasures which can be bought, where nature is not left to her own process, where even desire is anticipated and so enjoyment is defeated by studied schemes and contrivances of delight, and no interval and no obstacle is put between the wish and its accomplishment.

The people of England know how little influence the teachers of religion are likely to have with the wealthy and powerful of long standing, and how much less with the newly fortunate, if they appear in a style in no way matched to those they must associate with, and over whom they must in some cases exercise something like an authority. What are people to think of a body of teachers if they see no part of it above the level of their own household servants? If the poverty were voluntary there might be some difference. Strong instances of self-denial work powerfully on our minds, and a man who has no wants has gained great freedom and firmness and even dignity. But since the mass of any description of men are only men, and their poverty cannot be voluntary, the disrespect that attends all lay poverty will not spare the ecclesiastical kind. Our provident constitution has therefore taken care that those who are to instruct presumptuous ignorance, and to be censors over insolent vice, should neither incur their contempt nor live on their alms; nor will it tempt the rich to neglect the true medicine of their minds. For these reasons, while we provide first for the poor, and with a parental solicitude, we have not banished religion, like something we were ashamed to be seen with, to obscure municipalities or rustic villages. No: we will have her raise her mitred head in courts and parliaments. We will have her mixed through the whole mass of life and blended with all the classes of society. The people of England will show the haughty potentates of the world and their talking sophisters that a free, a generous, an informed nation honours the high magistrates of its church; that it will not allow the insolence of wealth and titles, or any other species of proud pretension, to look down with scorn on what they look up to with reverence, nor presume to trample on that acquired personal nobility which they intend always to be, and which often is, the fruit — not the reward, for what could be the reward? — of learning, piety and virtue. They can see an archbishop walk before a duke without pain or grudging. They can see a bishop of Durham or a bishop of Winchester in possession of ten thousand pounds a year, and cannot conceive why it is in worse hands than estates of the same value in the hands of this earl or that squire; although it may be true that the bishop does not keep so many dogs and horses, fed with the food that ought to nourish the children of the people. It is true that the whole church revenue is not always employed, down to the last shilling, in charity, nor perhaps ought it to be; but some of it generally is. It is better to cherish virtue and humanity by leaving much to free will, even at some loss to the object, than to try to make men mere machines and instruments of a political benevolence. The world on the whole will gain by a liberty without which virtue cannot exist.

Once the commonwealth has established the estates of the church as property, it can consistently hear nothing about more or less. Too much and too little are alike treason against property. What evil can arise from the quantity in any one hand, while the supreme authority has full sovereign oversight of this, as of any property, to prevent every species of abuse — and, whenever it notably goes astray, to give it a direction agreeable to the purposes it was instituted for?

In England most of us think it is envy and malignity towards men who are often the founders of their own fortune, and not any love of the self-denial and mortification of the ancient church, that makes some people look askance at the distinctions and honours and revenues which, taken from nobody, are set apart for virtue. The ears of the people of England can tell the difference. They hear these men speak broad. Their tongue betrays them. Their language is the patois of fraud, the cant and gibberish of hypocrisy. The people of England must think so when these praters pretend to carry the clergy back to that primitive evangelical poverty which in spirit ought always to exist in them — and in us too, however little we may like it — but which in the fact must vary when the relation of that body to the state is altered, when manners, when modes of life, when indeed the whole order of human affairs has undergone a total revolution. We shall believe these reformers to be honest enthusiasts, instead of thinking them, as we now do, cheats and deceivers, when we see them throwing their own goods into the common stock and submitting their own persons to the austere discipline of the early church.

With these ideas rooted in their minds, the Commons of Great Britain will never in a national emergency look for their resource in confiscating the estates of the church and the poor. Sacrilege and proscription are not among the ways and means of our Committee of Supply. The Jews in Change Alley have not yet dared to hint at their hopes of a mortgage on the revenues belonging to the see of Canterbury. I am not afraid of being disavowed when I assure you that there is not one public man in this kingdom whom you would care to quote — no, not one, of any party or description — who does not condemn the dishonest, perfidious and cruel confiscation the National Assembly has been driven to make of that property which it was their first duty to protect.

It is with the exultation of a little national pride that I tell you those among us who wished to pledge the societies of Paris in the cup of their abominations have been disappointed. The robbery of your church has proved a security to the possessions of ours. It has roused the people. They see that enormous and shameless act of proscription with horror and alarm. It has opened their eyes, and will open them more and more, to the selfish enlargement of mind and the narrow liberality of sentiment of insidious men, who, beginning in close hypocrisy and fraud, have ended in open violence and plunder. At home we see similar beginnings. We are on our guard against similar conclusions.

I hope we shall never be so utterly lost to all sense of the duties the law of social union imposes on us as to confiscate the goods of a single unoffending citizen on any pretext of public service. Who but a tyrant — a name that expresses everything which can vitiate and degrade human nature — could think of seizing the property of men unaccused, unheard, untried, by whole descriptions, by hundreds and thousands together? Who that had not lost every trace of humanity could think of casting men of exalted rank and sacred function, some of them of an age to call at once for reverence and compassion, down from the highest situation in the commonwealth, where they were maintained by their own landed property, into a state of poverty, depression and contempt?

The confiscators have indeed made their victims some allowance out of the scraps and fragments of their own tables, from which they have been so harshly driven, and which have been spread so bountifully as a feast for the harpies of usury. But to drive men from independence to living on alms is itself a great cruelty. What might be a tolerable condition for men in one state of life, unaccustomed to anything else, may, when all those circumstances are altered, be a dreadful revolution — one that a virtuous mind would feel pain in condemning any guilt to, except a guilt that would demand the offender's life. But to many minds this punishment of degradation and infamy is worse than death. Undoubtedly it is an infinite aggravation of this cruel suffering that the persons who were taught a double prejudice in favour of religion, by their education and by the place they held in administering its functions, are to receive the remnants of their property as alms from the profane and impious hands of the men who plundered them of all the rest — to receive, if they are to receive at all, the maintenance of religion not from the charitable contributions of the faithful but from the insolent tenderness of known and avowed atheism, measured out to them on the scale of the contempt in which religion is held, and for the purpose of rendering those who take the allowance vile and of no account in the eyes of mankind.

But this act of seizing property, it seems, is a judgment in law and not a confiscation. They have discovered, it seems, in the academies of the Palais Royal and the Jacobins, that certain men had no right to the possessions they held under law, usage, the decisions of courts and the accumulated prescription of a thousand years.
