They say that clergymen are fictitious persons, creatures of the state, whom the state may destroy at pleasure and of course limit and modify in every particular; that the goods they possess are not properly theirs but belong to the state that created the fiction; and that we need not therefore trouble ourselves about what they may suffer in their natural feelings and their natural persons on account of what is done to them in this constructed character of theirs. What does it matter under what names you injure men and deprive them of the just earnings of a profession that the state not only permitted but encouraged them to enter, and on the supposed certainty of those earnings had made the plan of their lives, contracted debts, and led multitudes into entire dependence on them?

You do not imagine, Sir, that I am going to compliment this miserable distinction of persons with any long discussion. The arguments of tyranny are as contemptible as its force is dreadful. Had your confiscators not obtained, by their early crimes, a power that secures them indemnity for every crime they have since been guilty of or may yet commit, it is not the logician's syllogism but the executioner's lash that would have refuted a sophistry which makes itself an accomplice of theft and murder. The sophistical tyrants of Paris are loud in their declamations against the dead royal tyrants who vexed the world in former ages. They are bold in that because they are safe from the dungeons and iron cages of their old masters. Shall we be more tender towards the tyrants of our own time, when we see them acting worse tragedies before our eyes? Shall we not take the same liberty they take, when we can take it with the same safety, and when speaking honest truth requires nothing but a contempt for the opinions of men whose actions we abhor?

This outrage on all the rights of property was at first covered by what, on their system of conduct, was the most astonishing of all pretexts — a regard for the national honour. The enemies of property at first pretended a most tender, delicate and scrupulous anxiety about keeping the king's engagements with the public creditor. These professors of the rights of men are so busy teaching others that they have no leisure to learn anything themselves; otherwise they would have known that the first and original faith of civil society is pledged to the property of the citizen, not to the demands of the creditor of the state. The citizen's claim is prior in time, paramount in title, superior in equity. The fortunes of individuals, whether held by purchase or by descent or by a share in the goods of some community, were no part of the creditor's security, expressed or implied. They never so much as entered his head when he made his bargain. He knew perfectly well that the public, whether represented by a monarch or by a senate, can pledge nothing but the public estate, and that it can have no public estate except what it derives from a just and proportioned levy on the citizens at large. That was pledged to the public creditor, and nothing else could be. No man can mortgage his injustice as a pledge for his good faith.

It is impossible to avoid remarking on the contradictions produced by the extreme rigour and the extreme laxity of this new public faith, which operated in this transaction not according to the nature of the obligation but according to the description of the persons it was owed to. No acts of the old government of the kings of France are held valid in the National Assembly except its money engagements — of all its acts the most doubtful in legality. The rest of that royal government's acts are regarded in so odious a light that to have a claim under its authority is looked on as a sort of crime. A pension given as a reward for service to the state is surely as good a ground of property as any security for money advanced to the state. It is better, for money is paid, and well paid, to obtain that service. Yet we have seen multitudes of people of that description in France, who had never been deprived of their allowances by the most arbitrary ministers in the most arbitrary times, robbed without mercy by this assembly of the rights of men. They were told, in answer to their claim to the bread earned with their blood, that their services had not been rendered to the country that now exists.

This laxity of public faith is not confined to those unfortunate people. The Assembly, with perfect consistency it must be owned, is engaged in a respectable deliberation as to how far it is bound by the treaties made with other nations under the former government; and its committee is to report which of them they ought to ratify and which not. By this means they have put the external good faith of this virgin state on a level with its internal.

It is not easy to conceive on what rational principle the royal government should not, of the two, rather have possessed the power of rewarding service and making treaties by virtue of its prerogative than the power of pledging the revenue of the state, actual and possible, to creditors. Of all things, the treasure of the nation has been the least allowed to the prerogative of the king of France, or to the prerogative of any king in Europe. To mortgage the public revenue implies sovereign dominion over the public purse in the fullest sense. It goes far beyond the trust even of a temporary and occasional taxation. Yet the acts of that dangerous power — the distinctive mark of a boundless despotism — are the only ones held sacred. How did a democratic assembly come to give this preference to a body of property that derives its title from the most critical and objectionable of all the exertions of monarchical authority? Reason can furnish nothing to reconcile an inconsistency, nor can partial favour be accounted for on equitable principles. But a contradiction and a partiality that admit no justification are not for that reason without an adequate cause, and I do not think that cause is difficult to discover.

Through the vast debt of France a great moneyed interest has imperceptibly grown up, and with it a great power. Under the ancient usages that prevailed in that kingdom, the general circulation of property, and in particular the mutual convertibility of land into money and money into land, had always been difficult. Family settlements, rather more general and more strict than they are in England; the jus retractûs; the great mass of landed property held by the crown and, by a maxim of French law, held inalienably; the vast estates of the ecclesiastical corporations — all these had kept the landed and the moneyed interests more separate in France, less mixable, and had left the owners of the two distinct kinds of property less well disposed towards one another than they are in this country.

Moneyed property was long looked on with rather an evil eye by the people. They saw it connected with their distresses and aggravating them. It was no less envied by the old landed interests — partly for the same reasons that made it obnoxious to the people, but much more because it eclipsed, by the splendour of an ostentatious luxury, the unendowed pedigrees and bare titles of several of the nobility. Even when the nobility, who represented the more permanent landed interest, allied themselves by marriage with the other sort, as sometimes happened, the wealth that saved the family from ruin was supposed to contaminate and degrade it. So the enmities and heartburnings of these parties were increased even by the usual means through which discord is made to cease and quarrels are turned into friendships. Meanwhile the pride of wealthy men who were not noble, or newly noble, grew along with its cause. They resented an inferiority whose grounds they did not acknowledge. There was no measure they were not willing to lend themselves to in order to be revenged for the outrages of this rival pride and to raise their wealth to what they considered its natural rank and estimation. They struck at the nobility through the crown and the church. They attacked them particularly on the side where they thought them most vulnerable — that is, the possessions of the church, which through the patronage of the crown generally fell to the nobility. The bishoprics and the great commendatory abbeys were held, with few exceptions, by that order.

In this state of real though not always perceived warfare between the old noble landed interest and the new moneyed interest, the greatest strength — because the most usable — was in the hands of the latter. The moneyed interest is by its nature readier for any adventure, and its possessors more disposed to new enterprises of every kind. Being recently acquired, it falls in more naturally with novelties. It is therefore the kind of wealth that everyone who wants change will turn to.

Along with the moneyed interest, a new description of men had grown up with whom that interest soon formed a close and marked union: I mean the political men of letters. Men of letters, being fond of distinguishing themselves, are rarely averse to innovation. Since the decline of the life and greatness of Louis the Fourteenth they had not been so much cultivated by him, or by the Regent, or by his successors on the throne; nor were they attached to the court by favours and salaries as systematically as they had been during the splendid period of that ostentatious and not impolitic reign. What they lost in the old court protection they tried to make up by joining in a sort of incorporation of their own, to which the two academies of France, and afterwards the vast undertaking of the Encyclopédie, carried on by a society of these gentlemen, contributed not a little.

The literary cabal had some years ago formed something like a regular plan for the destruction of the Christian religion. They pursued this object with a zeal that had until then been found only in the propagators of some system of piety. They were possessed by a spirit of proselytism to the most fanatical degree — and from that, by an easy progress, by the spirit of persecution so far as their means allowed. What could not be done towards their great end by any direct and immediate act might be worked out by a longer process through the medium of opinion. To command opinion, the first step is to establish a dominion over those who direct it. They contrived to possess themselves, with great method and perseverance, of all the avenues to literary fame. Many of them did indeed stand high in the ranks of literature and science. The world had done them justice, and in favour of their general talents forgave the evil tendency of their peculiar principles. That was true liberality — which they repaid by trying to confine the reputation for sense, learning and taste to themselves and their followers. I will venture to say that this narrow, exclusive spirit has been no less damaging to literature and taste than to morals and true philosophy. These atheistical fathers have a bigotry of their own, and they have learned to talk against monks with the spirit of a monk. But in some things they are men of the world. The resources of intrigue are called in to supply the defects of argument and wit. To this system of literary monopoly was joined an unremitting industry to blacken and discredit, in every way and by every means, everyone who did not belong to their faction. To anyone who has observed the spirit of their conduct it has long been clear that nothing was wanting but the power to carry the intolerance of the tongue and the pen into a persecution that would strike at property, liberty and life.

Footnote: This — down to the end of the first sentence in the next paragraph — and some other parts here and there were inserted, on his reading the manuscript, by my lost son.

The desultory and feeble persecution carried on against them, which came more from a compliance with form and decency than from any serious resentment, neither weakened their strength nor slackened their efforts. The upshot of the whole was that, what with opposition and what with success, a violent and malignant zeal of a kind till then unknown in the world had taken entire possession of their minds and made their whole conversation, which would otherwise have been pleasing and instructive, perfectly disgusting. A spirit of cabal, intrigue and proselytism pervaded all their thoughts, words and actions. And since controversial zeal soon turns its thoughts to force, they began to insinuate themselves into a correspondence with foreign princes, in hopes that through their authority, which at first they flattered, they might bring about the changes they had in view. It was all one to them whether those changes were to be accomplished by the thunderbolt of despotism or by the earthquake of popular commotion. The correspondence between this cabal and the late king of Prussia will throw no small light on the spirit of all their proceedings. For the same purpose for which they intrigued with princes, they cultivated the moneyed interest of France in a marked manner; and partly through the means furnished by men whose particular offices gave them the most extensive and certain channels of communication, they carefully occupied all the avenues to opinion.

Footnote: I do not choose to shock the feelings of the moral reader with any quotation of their vulgar, base and profane language.

Writers, especially when they act as a body and in one direction, have great influence on the public mind; the alliance of these writers with the moneyed interest therefore had no small effect in removing the popular odium and envy that attended that species of wealth. These writers, like the propagators of all novelties, pretended to a great zeal for the poor and the lower orders, while in their satires they made the faults of courts, of the nobility and of the priesthood hateful by every kind of exaggeration. They became a sort of demagogues. They served as a link uniting obnoxious wealth with restless and desperate poverty in favour of one object.

Footnote: Their connection with Turgot and almost all the people of the finance.

As these two sorts of men appear as the principal leaders in all the recent transactions, their junction and their politics will account — not on any principles of law or of policy, but as a cause — for the general fury with which all the landed property of ecclesiastical corporations has been attacked, and for the great care that has been taken, contrary to their professed principles, of a moneyed interest originating in the authority of the crown. All the envy against wealth and power was artificially directed against other kinds of riches. On what other principle than the one I have stated can we account for anything so extraordinary and unnatural as the ecclesiastical possessions, which had stood through so many successions of ages and shocks of civil violence, and were guarded at once by justice and by prejudice, being applied to the payment of debts that are comparatively recent, invidious, and contracted by a decried and overthrown government?

Was the public estate a sufficient stake for the public debts? Suppose it was not, and that a loss must be incurred somewhere. When the only estate lawfully possessed, and the only one the contracting parties had in view at the time the bargain was made, happens to fail, who according to the principles of natural and legal equity ought to be the sufferer? Certainly it ought to be either the party who trusted, or the party who persuaded him to trust, or both — and not third parties who had nothing to do with the transaction. In any insolvency those ought to suffer who were weak enough to lend on bad security, or those who fraudulently held out a security that was not valid. The law knows no other rules of decision. But by the new institute of the rights of men, the only persons who in equity ought to suffer are the only persons who are to be held harmless: those are to answer the debt who were neither lenders nor borrowers, neither mortgagors nor mortgagees.

What had the clergy to do with these transactions? What had they to do with any public engagement beyond the extent of their own debt? To that, to be sure, their estates were bound to the last acre. Nothing leads more directly to the true spirit of the Assembly, which sits for public confiscation with its new equity and its new morality, than attending to their proceedings with regard to this debt of the clergy. The body of confiscators, true to that moneyed interest for which they were false to every other, have found the clergy competent to incur a legal debt. Of course by doing so they declared them legally entitled to the property which their power of incurring the debt and mortgaging the estate implied — recognising the rights of those persecuted citizens in the very act by which they so grossly violated them.

If, as I said, anyone besides the public at large is to make good the deficiencies to the public creditor, it must be those who managed the agreement. Why, then, are the estates of all the comptrollers-general not confiscated? Why not those of the long succession of ministers, financiers and bankers who have been enriched while the nation was impoverished by their dealings and their counsels? Why is the estate of M. Laborde not declared forfeit, rather than that of the Archbishop of Paris, who has had nothing to do with the creation or the jobbing of the public funds? Or if you must confiscate old landed estates in favour of the money-jobbers, why is the penalty confined to one description of men? I do not know whether the Duke de Choiseul's expenses have left anything of the infinite sums he drew from his master's bounty during the transactions of a reign that contributed largely, by every species of prodigality in war and peace, to the present debt of France. If any of it remains, why is that not confiscated? I remember being in Paris during the time of the old government. I was there just after the Duke d'Aiguillon had been snatched, as it was generally thought, from the block by the hand of a protecting despotism. He was a minister, and had some part in the affairs of that prodigal period. Why do I not see his estate handed over to the municipalities it lies in? The noble family of Noailles have long been servants — meritorious servants, I admit — to the crown of France, and have of course had some share in its bounties. Why do I hear nothing of applying their estates to the public debt? Why is the estate of the Duke de la Rochefoucauld more sacred than that of the Cardinal de la Rochefoucauld? The former is, I do not doubt, a worthy person; and, if it were not a sort of profanity to talk of the use of property as affecting the title to it, he makes a good use of his revenues. But it is no disrespect to him to say what authentic information well warrants me in saying: that the use his brother, the Cardinal Archbishop of Rouen, made of a property equally valid was far more laudable and far more public-spirited. Can anyone hear of the proscription of such persons and the confiscation of their effects without indignation and horror? A man who does not feel such emotions on such occasions is not a man. He does not deserve the name of a free man who will not express them.

Footnote: All have been confiscated in their turn.

Footnote: Not his brother, nor any near relation; but this mistake does not affect the argument.

Few barbarous conquerors have ever made so terrible a revolution in property. None of the heads of the Roman factions, when they set up crudelem illam hastam — that cruel spear which marked a public auction of plunder — in all their auctions of rapine, ever put up for sale the goods of the conquered citizen to such an enormous amount. It must be allowed in favour of those tyrants of antiquity that what they did could hardly be said to be done in cold blood. Their passions were inflamed, their tempers soured, their understandings confused with the spirit of revenge, with innumerable recent and reciprocal inflictions and retaliations of blood and rapine. They were driven beyond all bounds of moderation by the fear that power would return along with property to the families they had injured beyond all hope of forgiveness.

These Roman confiscators, who were still only in the elements of tyranny and had not been instructed in the rights of men so as to practise every sort of cruelty on one another without provocation, thought it necessary to spread a sort of colour over their injustice. They considered the vanquished party as made up of traitors who had borne arms or otherwise acted with hostility against the commonwealth. They regarded them as persons who had forfeited their property by their crimes. With you, in your improved state of the human mind, there was no such formality. You seized five million pounds sterling of annual rent and turned forty or fifty thousand human creatures out of their houses, because "such was your pleasure". The tyrant Henry the Eighth of England, not being better enlightened than the Roman Mariuses and Sullas, and not having studied in your new schools, did not know what an effective instrument of despotism was to be found in that grand arsenal of offensive weapons, the rights of men. When he resolved to rob the abbeys, as the club of the Jacobins has robbed all the clergy, he began by setting up a commission to examine the crimes and abuses prevailing in those communities. As might be expected, his commission reported truths, exaggerations and falsehoods. But truly or falsely, it reported abuses and offences. However, since abuses might be corrected, since a crime committed by persons does not always mean a forfeiture for the community, and since in that dark age property had not yet been discovered to be a creature of prejudice, all those abuses — and there were plenty of them — were hardly thought sufficient ground for the sort of confiscation it suited his purposes to make. He therefore procured a formal surrender of these estates. All these laborious proceedings were adopted by one of the most decided tyrants in the rolls of history as necessary preliminaries, before he could venture — by bribing the members of his two servile Houses with a share of the spoil, and holding out to them an eternal immunity from taxation — to demand a confirmation of his iniquitous proceedings by an act of Parliament. Had fate reserved him for our times, four technical terms would have done his business and saved him all this trouble; he would have needed nothing more than one short form of incantation: "Philosophy, Light, Liberality, the Rights of Men."

I can say nothing in praise of those acts of tyranny, which no voice has ever yet commended under any of their false colours; and yet in those false colours despotism paid a homage to justice. The power that was above all fear and all remorse was not set above all shame. While shame keeps watch, virtue is not wholly extinguished in the heart, nor will moderation be utterly banished from the minds of tyrants.

I believe every honest man shares the reflections of our political poet on that occasion, and will pray to avert the omen whenever these acts of rapacious despotism present themselves to his view or his imagination:

	                  "May no such storm
	Fall on our times, where ruin must reform!
	Tell me, my Muse, what monstrous, dire offence,
	What crime could any Christian king incense
	To such a rage? Was't luxury, or lust?
	Was he so temperate, so chaste, so just?
	Were these their crimes? They were his own much more:
	But wealth is crime enough to him that's poor."

Footnote: The rest of the passage is this: "Who, having spent the treasures of his crown, Condemns their luxury to feed his own. And yet this act, to varnish o'er the shame Of sacrilege, must bear Devotion's name. No crime so bold, but would be understood A Real, or at least a seeming good. Who fears not to do ill, yet fears the name, And free from conscience, is a slave to fame. Thus he the Church at once protects and spoils: But princes' swords are sharper than their styles. And thus to th' ages past he makes amends, Their charity destroys, their faith defends. Then did Religion in a lazy cell, In empty, airy contemplations, dwell; And like the block, unmovèd lay: but ours, As much too active, like the stork devours. Is there no temperate region can be known Betwixt their frigid and our torrid zone? Could we not wake from that lethargic dream, But to be restless in a worse extreme? And for that lethargy was there no care, But to be cast into a calenture? Can knowledge have no bound, but must advance So far, to make us wish for ignorance, And rather in the dark to grope our way, Than, led by a false guide, to err by day? Who sees these dismal heaps, but would demand What barbarous invader sack'd the land? But when he hears no Goth, no Turk did bring This desolation, but a Christian king, When nothing but the name of zeal appears 'Twixt our best actions and the worst of theirs, What does he think our sacrilege would spare, When such th' effects of our devotions are?" Cooper's Hill, by Sir John Denham.

This same wealth, which is at all times treason and lèze-nation to a needy and rapacious despotism under every form of government, was your temptation to violate property, law and religion, all united in one object. But was the state of France so wretched and undone that no resource but plunder remained to preserve its existence? On this point I should like some information. When the States met, was the condition of the finances of France such that, after economising on principles of justice and mercy through every department, no fair distribution of the burdens over all the orders could possibly have restored them? If such an equal levy would have been sufficient, you know very well that it might easily have been made. M. Necker, in the budget he laid before the orders assembled at Versailles, gave a detailed account of the state of the French nation.

Footnote: Rapport de Mons. le Directeur-Général des Finances, fait par Ordre du Roi à Versailles. Mai 5, 1789. (Report of the Director-General of Finance, made by the King's order at Versailles, 5 May 1789.)

If we credit him, there was no need to resort to any new impositions at all to bring the receipts of France into balance with its expenses. He put the permanent charges of every description, including the interest on a new loan of four hundred millions, at 531,444,000 livres, and the fixed revenue at 475,294,000, making the deficiency 56,150,000, or a little under £2,200,000 sterling. But to balance it he brought forward savings and improvements of revenue, considered entirely certain, to rather more than the amount of that deficiency; and he concludes with these emphatic words, on page 39: "Quel pays, Messieurs, que celui, où, sans impôts et avec de simples objets inaperçus, on peut faire disparoître un déficit qui a fait tant de bruit en Europe!" — what a country, gentlemen, where without new taxes, and by simple items nobody had noticed, a deficit that has made so much noise in Europe can be made to disappear! As to the repayment, the sinking of the debt and the other great objects of public credit and political arrangement indicated in M. Necker's speech, there could be no doubt that a very moderate and proportioned assessment on the citizens without distinction would have provided for all of them to the fullest extent of their demand.

If this representation of M. Necker's was false, then the Assembly is culpable in the highest degree for having forced the king to accept him as his minister, and, since the king's deposition, for having employed as their minister a man capable of so notoriously abusing his master's confidence and their own — and in a matter of the highest moment, belonging directly to his particular office. But if the representation was exact, as, having always shared your high respect for M. Necker, I have no doubt it was, then what can be said for men who, instead of a moderate, reasonable and general contribution, have in cold blood and driven by no necessity resorted to a partial and cruel confiscation?

Was that contribution refused on a pretext of privilege, either by the clergy or by the nobility? Certainly not. As for the clergy, they even ran ahead of the wishes of the third order. Before the States met, they had in all their instructions expressly directed their deputies to renounce every immunity that put them on a footing distinct from their fellow subjects. The clergy were even more explicit in this renunciation than the nobility.

But let us suppose the deficiency had remained at the fifty-six millions, or £2,200,000 sterling, as first stated by M. Necker. Let us grant that all the resources he set against that deficiency were impudent and groundless fictions, and that the Assembly, or its lords of articles at the Jacobins, were thereby justified in laying the whole burden of that deficiency on the clergy. Even granting all that, a necessity of £2,200,000 sterling will not support a confiscation to the amount of five millions. Imposing £2,200,000 on the clergy would, as a partial measure, have been oppressive and unjust, but it would not have been altogether ruinous to those it was imposed on; and therefore it would not have answered the managers' real purpose.

Footnote: In the constitution of Scotland during the Stuart reigns a committee sat to prepare bills, and none could pass but those they had previously approved. This committee was called the Lords of Articles.

Perhaps people unacquainted with the state of France, on hearing that the clergy and the nobility were privileged in the matter of taxation, may imagine that before the Revolution these bodies contributed nothing to the state. That is a great mistake. They certainly did not contribute equally with each other, nor either of them equally with the commons. Both, however, contributed largely. Neither nobility nor clergy enjoyed any exemption from the excise on consumable goods, from customs duties, or from any of the other numerous indirect taxes which in France, as here, make up so very large a proportion of all payments to the public. The nobility paid the poll tax. They also paid a land tax, called the twentieth penny, sometimes as high as three and sometimes four shillings in the pound — both of them direct taxes, of no light kind and no trivial yield. The clergy of the provinces annexed to France by conquest, which make about an eighth part of the whole in extent but a much larger proportion in wealth, likewise paid the poll tax and the twentieth penny at the rate the nobility paid. The clergy in the old provinces did not pay the poll tax, but they had bought themselves out of it at a cost of about twenty-four millions, a little more than a million sterling. They were exempt from the twentieths; but then they made free gifts, they contracted debts for the state, and they were subject to some other charges, the whole reckoned at about a thirteenth part of their clear income. They ought to have paid about forty thousand pounds a year more to put them on a par with the contribution of the nobility.

When the terrors of this tremendous proscription hung over the clergy, they made an offer of a contribution, through the Archbishop of Aix, which for its extravagance ought not to have been accepted. But it was evidently and obviously more advantageous to the public creditor than anything the confiscation could rationally promise. Why was it not accepted? The reason is plain. There was no desire that the church should be brought to serve the state. The service of the state was made a pretext for destroying the church. On their way to destroying the church they would not scruple to destroy their country — and they have destroyed it. One great end of the project would have been defeated if a plan of extortion had been adopted instead of the scheme of confiscation. The new landed interest connected with the new republic, and connected with it for its very existence, could not have been created. This was among the reasons why that extravagant ransom was not accepted.

The madness of the confiscation project, on the plan first pretended, soon became apparent. To bring this unwieldy mass of landed property, enlarged by the confiscation of the whole vast landed domain of the crown, onto the market all at once was obviously to defeat the profits the confiscation was meant to produce, by depreciating the value of those lands and indeed of all the landed estates throughout France. Such a sudden diversion of all the country's circulating money from trade into land must be a further mischief. What step was taken? Did the Assembly, on becoming aware of the inevitable ill effects of their projected sale, go back to the offers of the clergy? No distress could oblige them to travel by a road disgraced by any appearance of justice. Giving up all hope of a general immediate sale, another project seems to have succeeded. They proposed to take stock in exchange for the church lands. Great difficulties arose in that project over equalising the things to be exchanged. Other obstacles presented themselves too, which threw them back on some scheme of sale. The municipalities had taken alarm. They would not hear of transferring the whole plunder of the kingdom to the stockholders in Paris. Many of those municipalities had been reduced, and reduced by system, to the most deplorable poverty. Money was nowhere to be seen. They were therefore brought to the point that was so ardently desired. They panted for a currency of any kind that might revive their perishing industry. The municipalities were then to be admitted to a share of the spoil, which plainly made the first scheme, if it had ever been seriously entertained, altogether impracticable. Public needs pressed on every side. The Minister of Finance repeated his call for supply in a most urgent, anxious and foreboding voice. Pressed on all sides in this way, instead of the first plan of converting their bankers into bishops and abbots, and instead of paying the old debt, they contracted a new one at three per cent, creating a new paper currency founded on an eventual sale of the church lands. They issued this paper currency to satisfy, in the first instance, chiefly the demands made on them by the bank of discount, the great machine or paper-mill of their fictitious wealth.

The spoil of the church had now become the only resource for all their operations in finance, the vital principle of all their politics, the sole security for the existence of their power. It was necessary, by every means including the most violent, to put every individual on the same footing and to bind the nation together in one guilty interest to uphold this act and the authority of the men who did it. In order to force the most reluctant into a share of their pillage, they made their paper circulation compulsory in all payments. Anyone who considers the general tendency of their schemes towards this one object as a centre, and as a centre from which all their later measures radiate, will not think I dwell too long on this part of the National Assembly's proceedings.

To cut off all appearance of connection between the crown and public justice, and to bring the whole under implicit obedience to the dictators in Paris, the old independent judicature of the Parlements, with all its merits and all its faults, was wholly abolished. While the Parlements existed it was clear that the people might one day resort to them and rally under the standard of their ancient laws. It became a matter for consideration, however, that the magistrates and officers of the courts now abolished had purchased their places at a very high price, for which — as well as for the duty they performed — they received only a very low return of interest. Simple confiscation is a favour reserved for the clergy; towards the lawyers some appearances of equity are to be kept up, and they are to receive compensation to an immense amount. Their compensation becomes part of the national debt, for the liquidation of which there is the one inexhaustible fund. The lawyers are to have their compensation in the new church paper, which is to march in step with the new principles of judicature and legislature. The dismissed magistrates are to take their share of martyrdom with the clergy, or else to receive their own property out of such a fund and in such a manner as everyone seasoned in the ancient principles of jurisprudence, and sworn to guard property, must look on with horror. Even the clergy are to receive their miserable allowance out of the depreciated paper, stamped with the indelible character of sacrilege and with the symbols of their own ruin — or else they must starve. So violent an outrage on credit, property and liberty as this compulsory paper currency has seldom been exhibited by the alliance of bankruptcy and tyranny in any age or any nation.

In the course of all these operations the grand arcanum at last comes out: that in reality, and in any fair sense, the lands of the church, so far as anything certain can be gathered from their proceedings, are not to be sold at all. By the late resolutions of the National Assembly they are indeed to be delivered to the highest bidder. But it is to be observed that a certain portion only of the purchase-money is to be laid down. A period of twelve years is to be allowed for paying the rest. The philosophic purchasers are therefore to be put instantly into possession of the estate on payment of a sort of fine. It becomes in some respects a kind of gift to them, to be held on the feudal tenure of zeal for the new establishment. This project is plainly meant to let in a body of purchasers who have no money. The consequence will be that these purchasers, or rather grantees, will pay not only out of the rents as they fall due, which the state might just as well receive itself, but out of the spoil of building materials, out of waste committed in the woods, and out of whatever money hands habituated to the grip of usury can wring from the miserable peasant. He is to be handed over to the mercenary and arbitrary discretion of men who will be spurred to every species of extortion by the growing demands on the growing profits of an estate held under the precarious settlement of a new political system.

When all the frauds, impostures, violences, robberies, burnings, murders, confiscations, compulsory paper currencies and every description of tyranny and cruelty used to bring about and uphold this Revolution have their natural effect — that is, to shock the moral feelings of all virtuous and sober minds — the supporters of this philosophic system immediately strain their throats declaiming against the old monarchical government of France. When they have made that deposed power sufficiently black, they then argue as though everyone who disapproves of their new abuses must of course be a partisan of the old, and as though those who condemn their crude and violent schemes of liberty ought to be treated as advocates of servitude. I admit that their necessities do compel them to this base and contemptible fraud. Nothing can reconcile men to their proceedings and projects but the supposition that there is no third option between them and some tyranny as odious as anything the records of history or the invention of poets can supply. This prattle of theirs hardly deserves the name of sophistry. It is nothing but plain impudence. Have these gentlemen never heard, in the whole circle of the worlds of theory and practice, of anything between the despotism of a monarch and the despotism of a multitude? Have they never heard of a monarchy directed by laws, controlled and balanced by the great hereditary wealth and hereditary dignity of a nation, and both of these controlled again by a judicious check from the reason and feeling of the people at large, acting through a suitable and permanent organ? Is it impossible, then, that a man might be found who, without criminal ill intention or pitiable absurdity, prefers such a mixed and tempered government to either extreme — and who may think that nation destitute of all wisdom and all virtue which, having it in its power to obtain such a government easily, or rather to confirm it when actually possessed, chose instead to commit a thousand crimes and to subject their country to a thousand evils in order to avoid it? Is it a truth so universally acknowledged that a pure democracy is the only tolerable form human society can be cast in, that a man is not allowed to hesitate about its merits without being suspected of being a friend to tyranny — that is, an enemy of mankind?

I do not know what to call the present ruling authority in France. It professes to be a pure democracy, though I think it is in a direct line to become shortly a mischievous and ignoble oligarchy. But for the present I will grant it to be a contrivance of the nature and effect it pretends to. I condemn no form of government on merely abstract principles. There may be situations in which the purely democratic form becomes necessary. There may be some — very few, and very particularly circumstanced — where it would be clearly desirable. I do not take this to be the case of France, or of any other great country. Until now we have seen no examples of considerable democracies. The ancients knew them better. Not being wholly unread in the authors who saw most of those constitutions and understood them best, I cannot help agreeing with their opinion that an absolute democracy, no more than an absolute monarchy, is to be reckoned among the legitimate forms of government. They think it rather the corruption and degeneracy of a republic than its sound constitution. If I remember rightly, Aristotle observes that a democracy has many striking points of resemblance to a tyranny. Of this I am certain: that in a democracy the majority of the citizens is capable of exercising the cruellest oppression on the minority, whenever strong divisions prevail in that kind of polity, as they often must — and that the oppression of the minority will extend to far greater numbers, and will be carried on with much greater fury, than can almost ever be feared from the dominion of a single sceptre. In such a popular persecution, individual sufferers are in a far more deplorable condition than in any other. Under a cruel prince they have the balmy compassion of mankind to soothe the smart of their wounds, and the applause of the people to encourage their generous constancy under their sufferings; but those who are wronged by multitudes are deprived of all outside consolation. They seem deserted by mankind, overpowered by a conspiracy of their whole species.

Footnote: When I wrote this I quoted from memory, many years after reading the passage. A learned friend has found it, and it is as follows: τὸ ἠ̂θος τὸ αὐτό, καὶ ἄμφω δεσποτικὰ τω̂ν βελτιόνων, καὶ τὰ ψηφίσματα ὥσπερ ἐκει̂ τὰ ἐπιτάγματα, καὶ ὁ δημαγωγὸς καὶ ὁ κόλαξ οἱ αὐτοὶ καὶ ἀνάλογον. καὶ μάλιστα δ' ἑκάτεροι παρ' ἑκατέροις ἰσχύουσιν, οἱ μὲν κόλακες παρὰ τοι̂ς τυράννοις, οἱ δὲ δημαγωγοὶ παρὰ τοι̂ς δήμοις τοι̂ς τοιούτοις. "The ethical character is the same: both exercise despotism over the better class of citizens; and decrees are in the one what ordinances and arrêts are in the other. The demagogue, too, and the court favourite are not infrequently the same men, and always bear a close analogy; and these have the principal power, each in their respective forms of government — favourites with the absolute monarch, and demagogues with a people such as I have described." — Aristotle, Politics, book 4, chapter 4.

But suppose democracy does not have that inevitable tendency to party tyranny which I take it to have, and suppose it possesses as much good when unmixed as I am sure it possesses when compounded with other forms. Does monarchy, on its side, contain nothing at all to recommend it? I do not often quote Bolingbroke, nor have his works in general left any lasting impression on my mind. He is a presumptuous and a superficial writer. But he has one observation that is in my opinion not without depth and solidity. He says he prefers a monarchy to other governments because you can graft any description of republic on a monarchy better than you can graft anything of monarchy on the republican forms. I think he is perfectly right. The fact is so historically, and it agrees well with the theory.

I know how easy a subject it is to dwell on the faults of departed greatness. By a revolution in the state, yesterday's fawning sycophant is converted into the austere critic of the present hour. But steady, independent minds, when they have something of such serious concern to mankind as government under consideration, will disdain to play the satirist and the declaimer. They will judge human institutions as they judge human characters. They will sort the good from the evil, which is mixed in mortal institutions as it is in mortal men.

Your government in France, though usually and I think justly reputed the best of the unqualified or ill-qualified monarchies, was still full of abuses. Those abuses accumulated over a length of time, as they must accumulate in every monarchy that is not under the constant inspection of a popular representative body. I am no stranger to the faults and defects of the overthrown government of France, and I do not think I am inclined either by nature or by policy to write a panegyric on anything that is a just and natural object of censure. But the question now is not about the vices of that monarchy but about its existence. Is it true, then, that the French government was such as to be incapable of reform or undeserving of it, so that it was of absolute necessity that the whole fabric should be pulled down at once and the ground cleared for putting up a theoretical, experimental building in its place? All France was of a different opinion at the beginning of the year 1789. The instructions given to the representatives at the States-General, from every district in that kingdom, were full of projects for reforming that government, without the remotest hint of any design to destroy it. Had such a design been so much as insinuated then, I believe there would have been but one voice, and that voice would have rejected it with scorn and horror. Men have sometimes been led by degrees, and sometimes hurried, into things they would never have allowed the remotest approach to if they could have seen the whole of them together. When those instructions were given there was no question that abuses existed and that they demanded reform, nor is there now. In the interval between the instructions and the Revolution things changed their shape; and in consequence of that change, the true question now is whether those who would have reformed or those who have destroyed are in the right.

To hear some men speak of the late monarchy of France, you would imagine they were talking about Persia bleeding under the ferocious sword of Nadir Shah, or at least describing the barbarous anarchic despotism of Turkey, where the finest countries in the most genial climates in the world are wasted by peace more than any countries have been harried by war, where the arts are unknown, where manufactures languish, where science is extinguished, where agriculture decays, where the human race itself melts away and perishes under the observer's eye. Was this the case of France? I have no way of settling the question except by reference to facts, and the facts do not support the resemblance. Along with much evil there is some good in monarchy itself; and the French monarchy must have received some correction of its evil from religion, from laws, from manners and from opinion, which made it a despotism rather in appearance than in reality — though by no means a free, and therefore by no means a good, constitution.

Among the standards by which the effects of a government on any country are to be estimated, I must count the state of its population as not the least reliable. No country in which population flourishes and is progressively improving can be under a very mischievous government. About sixty years ago the intendants of the généralités of France made, among other matters, a report on the population of their several districts. I have not the books by me, which are very voluminous, nor do I know where to get them — I am obliged to speak from memory, and therefore the less positively — but I think the population of France was estimated by them, even at that period, at twenty-two million souls. At the end of the last century it had generally been reckoned at eighteen. On either of these estimates France was not ill-peopled. M. Necker, whose authority for his own time is at least equal to that of the intendants for theirs, reckons the people of France in the year 1780, and on apparently sure principles, at twenty-four million six hundred and seventy thousand. But was this the probable ultimate limit under the old establishment? Dr Price is of the opinion that the growth of population in France was by no means at its height in that year. I certainly defer to Dr Price's authority a good deal more in these calculations than I do in his general politics. This gentleman, taking his ground on M. Necker's data, is very confident that since the date of that minister's calculation the French population has increased rapidly — so rapidly that in the year 1789 he will not agree to rate the people of that kingdom at less than thirty millions. After making a large deduction from Dr Price's sanguine calculation, and I think a large one ought to be made, I have no doubt the population of France did increase considerably during this later period. But suppose it increased by no more than enough to bring the twenty-four million six hundred and seventy thousand up to twenty-five millions: still a population of twenty-five millions, and increasing, on a space of about twenty-seven thousand square leagues, is immense. It is, for instance, a good deal more than the proportional population of this island, or even than that of England, the best-peopled part of the United Kingdom.

It is not universally true that France is a fertile country. Considerable tracts of it are barren and labour under other natural disadvantages. In those parts of that territory where things are more favourable, so far as I can discover, the numbers of the people correspond to the indulgence of nature. The généralité of Lille — this, I admit, is the strongest example — about ten years ago contained seven hundred and thirty-four thousand six hundred souls on an extent of four hundred and four and a half leagues, which is one thousand seven hundred and seventy-two inhabitants to each square league. The average for the rest of France is about nine hundred inhabitants to the same measure.

Footnote: De l'Administration des Finances de la France, by M. Necker, volume 1, page 288.

I do not attribute this population to the deposed government, because I do not like to compliment the contrivances of men with what is largely due to the bounty of Providence. But that decried government cannot have obstructed, and most probably favoured, the operation of those causes — whatever they were, whether of nature in the soil or of habits of industry among the people — which have produced so large a number of the species throughout that whole kingdom, and in some particular places such prodigies of population. I will never suppose that the fabric of a state which experience shows to contain a principle favourable to the increase of mankind, however latent that principle may be, is the worst of all political institutions.

The wealth of a country is another and no contemptible standard by which we may judge whether a government is on the whole protective or destructive. France far exceeds England in the multitude of her people, but I take her comparative wealth to be much inferior to ours, less equal in its distribution and less ready in its circulation. I believe the difference in the form of the two governments to be among the causes of this advantage on England's side. I speak of England, not of the whole British dominions, which if compared with those of France would to some degree weaken the comparative rate of wealth on our side. But wealth that will not bear comparison with the riches of England may still amount to a very respectable degree of prosperity. M. Necker's book, published in 1785, contains an accurate and interesting collection of facts about public economy and political arithmetic, and his reflections on the subject are generally wise and liberal. In that work he gives an idea of the state of France very remote from the portrait of a country whose government was a perfect grievance, an absolute evil, admitting no cure but the violent and uncertain remedy of a total revolution. He states that from the year 1726 to the year 1784 there was coined at the mint of France, in gold and silver, to the amount of about one hundred million pounds sterling.

Footnote: De l'Administration des Finances de la France, by M. Necker.

Footnote: Volume 3, chapters 8 and 9.

It is impossible that M. Necker should be mistaken about the amount of bullion coined at the mint. That is a matter of official record. This able financier's reasonings about the quantity of gold and silver that remained in circulation when he wrote in 1785 — about four years before the deposition and imprisonment of the French king — are not equally certain; but they rest on grounds so apparently solid that it is not easy to refuse a considerable degree of assent to his calculation. He puts the numéraire, or what we call specie, then actually existing in France at about eighty-eight millions of the same English money. A great accumulation of wealth for one country, large as that country is! So far was M. Necker from thinking this influx of wealth likely to cease when he wrote in 1785, that he assumes a future annual increase of two per cent on the money brought into France during the periods he computed from.

Some adequate cause must originally have brought all the money coined at its mint into that kingdom, and some cause equally effective must have kept at home, or drawn back into its bosom, such a vast flood of treasure as M. Necker calculates to remain for domestic circulation. Make any reasonable deductions from M. Necker's computation, and the remainder must still amount to an immense sum. Causes powerful enough to acquire and to retain like this are not to be found in discouraged industry, insecure property and a positively destructive government. Indeed, when I consider the face of the kingdom of France, the multitude and wealth of her cities, the useful magnificence of her spacious highroads and bridges, the opportunity of her artificial canals and navigations opening the conveniences of sea communication through a solid continent of such immense extent; when I turn my eyes to the stupendous works of her ports and harbours and to her whole naval apparatus, whether for war or for trade; when I bring before my view the number of her fortifications, built with such bold and masterly skill and made and maintained at such prodigious cost, presenting an armed front and an impenetrable barrier to her enemies on every side; when I recall how very small a part of that extensive region is uncultivated, and to what complete perfection the growing of many of the best productions of the earth has been brought in France; when I reflect on the excellence of her manufactures and fabrics, second to none but ours and in some particulars not second; when I contemplate the grand foundations of charity, public and private; when I survey the state of all the arts that beautify and polish life; when I count up the men she has bled to extend her fame in war, her able statesmen, the multitude of her profound lawyers and theologians, her philosophers, her critics, her historians and antiquaries, her poets and her orators, sacred and profane — I see in all this something that awes and commands the imagination, that checks the mind on the brink of hasty and indiscriminate censure, and that demands we should examine very seriously what and how great the latent vices are that could authorise us to level so spacious a fabric with the ground all at once. I do not recognise in this view of things the despotism of Turkey. Nor do I discern the character of a government that has been on the whole so oppressive, or so corrupt, or so negligent, as to be utterly unfit for all reformation. I must think such a government well deserved to have its excellences heightened, its faults corrected and its capacities improved into a British constitution.

Whoever has examined the proceedings of that deposed government over several years back cannot fail to have noticed, amid the inconstancy and fluctuation natural to courts, an earnest effort towards the prosperity and improvement of the country. He must admit that it had long been employed in removing some abusive practices and usages that had prevailed in the state, and in considerably correcting many others — and that even the unlimited power of the sovereign over the persons of his subjects, inconsistent as it undoubtedly was with law and liberty, had been growing more moderate in its exercise every day. So far from refusing itself to reformation, that government was open, with a blameworthy degree of readiness, to all sorts of projects and projectors on the subject. Rather too much countenance was given to the spirit of innovation, which was soon turned against those who fostered it and ended in their ruin. It is only cold and no very flattering justice to that fallen monarchy to say that for many years it offended more by levity and want of judgment in several of its schemes than by any lack of diligence or public spirit. To compare the government of France over the last fifteen or sixteen years with wise and well-constituted establishments of that or any other period is not to act fairly. But if it is compared with any of the former reigns, in point of prodigality in spending money or of rigour in the exercise of power, I believe candid judges will give little credit to the good intentions of those who dwell perpetually on the gifts to favourites, or the expenses of the court, or the horrors of the Bastille, in the reign of Louis the Sixteenth.

Footnote: The world is obliged to M. de Calonne for the pains he has taken to refute the scandalous exaggerations about some of the royal expenses, and to expose the false account given of pensions, for the wicked purpose of provoking the populace to crimes of every kind.

Whether the system, if it deserves such a name, now built on the ruins of that ancient monarchy will be able to give a better account of the population and wealth of the country it has taken under its care is very doubtful. Instead of improving by the change, I expect that a long series of years must be counted before it can recover in any degree from the effects of this philosophic Revolution, and before the nation can be put back on its former footing. If Dr Price should think fit, a few years from now, to favour us with an estimate of the population of France, he will hardly be able to make up his tale of thirty million souls as computed in 1789, or the Assembly's computation of twenty-six millions for that year, or even M. Necker's twenty-five millions in 1780. I hear that there are considerable emigrations from France, and that many, quitting that voluptuous climate and that seductive Circean liberty, have taken refuge in the frozen regions and under the British despotism of Canada.

With coin disappearing as it now is, nobody would think it the same country in which the present minister of finance was able to discover eighty million pounds sterling in specie. From its general aspect one would conclude it had been for some time past under the special direction of the learned academicians of Laputa and Balnibarbi. The population of Paris has already declined so far that M. Necker stated to the National Assembly that the provision to be made for its subsistence was a fifth less than what had formerly been found necessary. It is said, and I have never heard it contradicted, that a hundred thousand people are out of work in that city, though it has become the seat of the imprisoned court and of the National Assembly. Nothing, I am credibly informed, can exceed the shocking and disgusting spectacle of beggary displayed in that capital. Indeed the votes of the National Assembly leave no doubt of the fact. They have lately appointed a standing committee on begging. They are contriving at once a vigorous police on this subject and, for the first time, the imposition of a tax to maintain the poor, for whose present relief great sums appear on the face of the public accounts for the year. Meanwhile the leaders of the legislative clubs and coffee houses are intoxicated with admiration at their own wisdom and ability. They speak with the most sovereign contempt of the rest of the world. They tell the people, to comfort them in the rags they have clothed them in, that they are a nation of philosophers; and sometimes by all the arts of quackish parade, by show and tumult and bustle, and sometimes by alarms of plots and invasions, they try to drown the cries of poverty and to divert the observer's eyes from the ruin and wretchedness of the state. A brave people will certainly prefer liberty accompanied by a virtuous poverty to a depraved and wealthy servitude. But before the price of comfort and wealth is paid, one ought to be pretty sure that it is real liberty being purchased, and that she is to be had at no other price. I shall always, however, regard that liberty as very equivocal in her appearance which has not wisdom and justice for her companions, and does not lead prosperity and plenty in her train.

Footnote: See Gulliver's Travels for the idea of countries governed by philosophers.

Footnote: M. de Calonne states the falling off of the population of Paris as far more considerable, and it may be so, since the date of M. Necker's calculation.

When I sent this book to the press I had some doubt about the nature and extent of the last item in the accounts above, which appears only under a general head with no detail. Since then I have seen M. de Calonne's work. I must count it a great loss to me that I did not have that advantage earlier. M. de Calonne thinks this item is on account of general subsistence; but as he cannot understand how a loss of more than £1,661,000 sterling could be sustained on the difference between the price and the sale of grain, he seems to attribute this enormous head of charge to secret expenses of the Revolution. I cannot say anything positive on that subject. The reader is able to judge, from the aggregate of these immense charges, of the state and condition of France and of the system of public economy adopted in that nation. These items of account produced no inquiry and no discussion in the National Assembly.

The advocates of this Revolution, not content with exaggerating the vices of their ancient government, strike at the fame of their country itself by painting almost everything that could have attracted the attention of strangers — I mean their nobility and their clergy — as objects of horror. If this were only a libel there would not be much in it. But it has practical consequences. Had your nobility and gentry, who formed the great body of your landed men and the whole of your military officers, resembled those of Germany at the period when the Hanseatic towns were forced to confederate against the nobles in defence of their property; had they been like the Orsini and the Vitelli in Italy, who used to sally out of their fortified dens to rob the trader and the traveller; had they been like the Mamelukes in Egypt or the Nayars on the coast of Malabar — then I do admit that too critical an inquiry into the means of freeing the world from such a nuisance might not be advisable. The statues of Equity and Mercy might be veiled for a moment. The tenderest minds, confounded by the dreadful emergency in which morality submits to the suspension of its own rules in favour of its own principles, might turn aside while fraud and violence accomplished the destruction of a pretended nobility that disgraced human nature while it persecuted it. The persons most abhorrent from blood and treason and arbitrary confiscation might remain silent spectators of this civil war between the vices.

But did the privileged nobility who met at Versailles in 1789 under the king's summons, or their constituents, deserve to be looked on as the Nayars or Mamelukes of this age, or as the Orsini and Vitelli of former times? If I had asked the question then, I should have passed for a madman. What have they done since, that they should be driven into exile, that their persons should be hunted about, mangled and tortured, their families dispersed, their houses laid in ashes; that their order should be abolished and its very memory extinguished if possible, by ordering them to change the names they were commonly known by? Read their instructions to their representatives. They breathe the spirit of liberty as warmly, and recommend reformation as strongly, as any other order. Their privileges in the matter of taxation were surrendered voluntarily, just as the king from the beginning surrendered all pretence of a right of taxation. On the question of a free constitution there was only one opinion in France. The absolute monarchy was at an end. It breathed its last without a groan, without a struggle, without a convulsion. All the struggle, all the dissension, arose afterwards, over preferring a despotic democracy to a government of reciprocal control. The victorious party's triumph was a triumph over the principles of a British constitution.

I have noticed the affectation that has prevailed in Paris for many years past, to a degree that is perfectly childish, of idolising the memory of your Henry the Fourth. If anything could put a man out of humour with that ornament of the kingly character, it would be this overdone style of insidious panegyric. The people who have worked this engine the hardest are those who have ended their panegyrics by dethroning his successor and descendant — a man at least as good-natured as Henry the Fourth, altogether as fond of his people, and who has done infinitely more to correct the ancient vices of the state than that great monarch did, or than we can be sure he ever meant to do. It is well for his panegyrists that they do not have him to deal with. For Henry of Navarre was a resolute, active and politic prince. He did possess great humanity and mildness, but a humanity and mildness that never stood in the way of his interests. He never sought to be loved without first putting himself in a position to be feared. He used soft language with determined conduct. He asserted and maintained his authority wholesale, and dealt out his acts of concession only in retail. He spent the income of his prerogative nobly, but he took care not to break into the capital — never abandoning for a moment any of the claims he made under the fundamental laws, and never sparing to shed the blood of those who opposed him, often in the field and sometimes on the scaffold. Because he knew how to make his virtues respected by the ungrateful, he has earned the praises of men whom, if they had lived in his time, he would have shut up in the Bastille and brought to punishment along with the regicides he hanged after he had starved Paris into surrender.

If these panegyrists are in earnest in their admiration of Henry the Fourth, they must remember that they cannot think more highly of him than he thought of the nobility of France, whose virtue, honour, courage, patriotism and loyalty were his constant theme.

But the nobility of France have degenerated since the days of Henry the Fourth. This is possible, but it is more than I can believe to be true to any great degree. I do not pretend to know France as accurately as some others; but I have tried all my life to make myself acquainted with human nature, or I should be unfit to take even my humble part in the service of mankind. In that study I could not pass over a vast portion of our nature as it appeared modified in a country only twenty-four miles from the shore of this island. On my best observation, compared with my best inquiries, I found your nobility for the greater part composed of men of high spirit and a delicate sense of honour, both as individuals and with regard to their whole body, over which they kept a censorial eye beyond what is common in other countries. They were tolerably well bred; very obliging, humane and hospitable; frank and open in conversation; with a good military tone, and reasonably tinctured with literature, particularly the authors in their own language. Many had claims far above this description. I speak of those one generally met with.

As to their behaviour towards the lower classes, they seemed to me to conduct themselves with good nature, and with something nearer to familiarity than is generally practised with us in the dealings between the higher and lower ranks of life. To strike anyone, even in the most abject condition, was in a manner unknown and would be highly disgraceful. Instances of other ill treatment of the humble part of the community were rare; and as to attacks on the property or personal liberty of the commons, I never heard of any at all from them — nor, while the laws were in force under the ancient government, would such tyranny in subjects have been permitted. As landowners I had no fault to find with their conduct, though I found much to blame and much I could wish changed in many of the old tenures. Where they let their land for rent, I could not discover that their agreements with their farmers were oppressive; nor, when they were in partnership with the farmer, as was often the case, have I ever heard that they took the lion's share. The proportions did not seem inequitable. There may have been exceptions, but they were certainly exceptions only. I have no reason to believe that in these respects the landed nobility of France were worse than the landed gentry of this country, and they were certainly in no respect more vexatious than the landholders of their own nation who were not noble. In the cities the nobility had no kind of power; in the country very little. You know, Sir, that much of the civil government, and the policing of the most essential parts of it, was not in the hands of the nobility that presents itself first to our thoughts. The revenue, whose system and collection were the most grievous parts of the French government, was not administered by the men of the sword, nor were they answerable for the vices of its principle, or for the vexations in its management where any such existed.

Denying, as I am well warranted in doing, that the nobility had any considerable share in the oppression of the people in the cases where real oppression existed, I am ready to admit that they were not without considerable faults and errors. A foolish imitation of the worst part of the manners of England, which damaged their natural character without putting in its place what they perhaps meant to copy, has certainly made them worse than they used to be. Habitual dissoluteness of manners, continued beyond the pardonable period of life, was more common among them than it is with us; and it reigned with less hope of remedy, though possibly with somewhat less mischief, by being covered with more outward decorum. They gave too much countenance to that licentious philosophy which has helped to bring on their ruin. There was another and more fatal error among them. Those of the commons who came up to or exceeded many of the nobility in wealth were not fully admitted to the rank and estimation that wealth, in reason and good policy, ought to confer in every country, though I do not think equally with the estimation given to other nobility. The two kinds of aristocracy were kept too punctiliously apart — less so, however, than in Germany and some other nations.

This separation, as I have already taken the liberty of suggesting to you, I take to be one principal cause of the destruction of the old nobility. The military in particular was too exclusively reserved for men of family. But after all, this was an error of opinion, which a conflicting opinion would have rectified. A permanent assembly, in which the commons had their share of power, would soon have abolished whatever was too invidious and insulting in these distinctions; and even the faults in the morals of the nobility would probably have been corrected by the greater variety of occupation and pursuit that a constitution by orders would have given rise to.

All this violent outcry against the nobility I take to be a mere work of art. To be honoured and even privileged by the laws, opinions and inveterate usages of one's country, growing out of the prejudice of ages, has nothing in it to provoke horror and indignation in any man. Even to be too tenacious of those privileges is not absolutely a crime. The strong struggle in every individual to keep possession of what he has found to belong to him and to distinguish him is one of the securities against injustice and despotism implanted in our nature. It works as an instinct to secure property and to keep communities in a settled state. What is there to shock anyone in this? Nobility is a graceful ornament to the civil order. It is the Corinthian capital of polished society. "Omnes boni nobilitati semper favemus" — all good men always favour the nobility — was the saying of a wise and good man. It is indeed one sign of a liberal and benevolent mind to incline towards it with some sort of partial leaning. A man feels no ennobling principle in his own heart who wishes to level all the artificial institutions that have been adopted to give a body to opinion and permanence to fleeting esteem. It is a sour, malignant, envious disposition, with no taste for the reality of virtue or for any image or representation of it, that sees with joy the unmerited fall of what had long flourished in splendour and honour. I do not like to see anything destroyed, any void produced in society, any ruin on the face of the land. It was therefore with no disappointment or dissatisfaction that my inquiries and observations failed to show me any incorrigible vices in the nobility of France, or any abuse that could not be removed by a reform well short of abolition. Your nobility did not deserve punishment; but to degrade is to punish.

It was with the same satisfaction that I found the result of my inquiry about your clergy was not dissimilar. It is no soothing news to my ears that great bodies of men are incurably corrupt. I do not listen with much credulity to anyone speaking evil of those they are about to plunder. I rather suspect that vices are invented or exaggerated when profit is expected from punishing them. An enemy is a bad witness; a robber is a worse. There were undoubtedly vices and abuses in that order, and there must be. It was an old establishment, and not frequently revised. But I saw no crimes in the individuals that merited confiscation of their property, nor those cruel insults and degradations and that unnatural persecution which have been substituted for regulation that would have improved them.

If there had been any just cause for this new religious persecution, the atheistic libellers who act as trumpeters to rouse the populace to plunder do not love anybody so much as to refrain from dwelling with satisfaction on the vices of the existing clergy. They have not done so. They find themselves obliged to rake through the histories of former ages — which they have ransacked with a malignant and profligate industry — for every instance of oppression and persecution committed by that body or in its favour, in order to justify their own persecutions and their own cruelties on very iniquitous, because very illogical, principles of retaliation. After destroying all other genealogies and family distinctions, they invent a sort of pedigree of crimes. It is not very just to chastise men for the offences of their natural ancestors; but to take the fiction of ancestry in a corporate succession as a ground for punishing men who have no relation to the guilty acts except in names and general descriptions is a refinement in injustice belonging to the philosophy of this enlightened age. The Assembly punishes men, many and perhaps most of whom abhor the violent conduct of clergymen in former times as much as their present persecutors can, and who would be as loud and as strong in saying so if they were not well aware of the purposes all this declamation is being used for.

Corporate bodies are immortal for the good of their members but not for their punishment. Nations themselves are such corporations. We in England might as well think of waging inexpiable war on all Frenchmen for the evils they have brought on us in the various periods of our mutual hostilities. You might on your side think yourselves justified in falling on all Englishmen for the unparalleled calamities brought on the people of France by the unjust invasions of our Henrys and our Edwards. Indeed we should be mutually justified in this exterminating war on one another quite as much as you are in the unprovoked persecution of your present countrymen for the conduct of men of the same name in other times.

We do not draw the moral lessons from history that we might. On the contrary, without care it may be used to corrupt our minds and destroy our happiness. In history a great volume is unrolled for our instruction, drawing the materials of future wisdom out of the past errors and infirmities of mankind. Perverted, it may serve as an arsenal furnishing offensive and defensive weapons for parties in church and state, supplying the means of keeping alive or reviving dissensions and animosities, and adding fuel to civil fury. History consists for the greater part of the miseries brought on the world by pride, ambition, avarice, revenge, lust, sedition, hypocrisy, ungoverned zeal and all the train of disorderly appetites, which shake the public with the same

	        "troublous storms that toss
	The private state, and render life unsweet."

These vices are the causes of those storms. Religion, morals, laws, prerogatives, privileges, liberties, rights of men, are the pretexts. The pretexts are always found in some specious appearance of a real good. Would you secure men from tyranny and sedition by rooting out of the mind the principles these fraudulent pretexts appeal to? If you did, you would root out everything valuable in the human breast. As these are the pretexts, so the ordinary actors and instruments in great public evils are kings, priests, magistrates, senates, parliaments, national assemblies, judges and captains. You would not cure the evil by resolving that there should be no more monarchs, nor ministers of state, nor ministers of the Gospel — no interpreters of law, no general officers, no public councils. You might change the names; the things in some shape must remain. A certain quantum of power must always exist in the community, in some hands and under some name.
