Wise men will apply their remedies to vices, not to names — to the causes of evil, which are permanent, and not to the occasional organs by which they act and the transitory forms in which they appear. Otherwise you will be wise historically and a fool in practice. Two ages seldom have the same fashion in their pretexts and the same modes of mischief. Wickedness is a little more inventive than that. While you are discussing the fashion, the fashion has gone by. The very same vice takes on a new body. The spirit transmigrates; and far from losing its principle of life by the change of appearance, it is renewed in its new organs with the fresh vigour of a youthful activity. It walks abroad, it continues its ravages, while you are gibbeting the carcass or demolishing the tomb. You are terrifying yourselves with ghosts and apparitions while your house is the haunt of robbers. So it is with all those who, attending only to the shell and husk of history, think they are waging war on intolerance, pride and cruelty, while under colour of abhorring the bad principles of antiquated parties they are authorising and feeding the same odious vices in different factions, and perhaps in worse ones.

Your citizens of Paris formerly lent themselves as ready instruments to slaughter the followers of Calvin, at the infamous massacre of St Bartholomew. What should we say to anyone who could think of retaliating on the Parisians of today for the abominations and horrors of that time? They are indeed brought to abhor that massacre. Ferocious as they are, it is not difficult to make them dislike it, because the politicians and fashionable teachers have no interest in giving their passions exactly the same direction. Still, they find it in their interest to keep the same savage dispositions alive. It was only the other day that they had this very massacre acted on the stage for the amusement of the descendants of those who committed it. In this tragic farce they brought on the Cardinal of Lorraine in his robes of office, ordering general slaughter. Was this spectacle meant to make the Parisians abhor persecution and loathe the shedding of blood? No: it was to teach them to persecute their own pastors. It was to rouse them, by raising a disgust and horror of their clergy, to an eagerness for hunting down an order which, if it ought to exist at all, ought to exist not only in safety but in reverence. It was to stimulate their cannibal appetites — which one would think had been gorged sufficiently — with variety and seasoning, and to make them quick for new murders and massacres if it should suit the purpose of the Guises of the day. An Assembly in which a multitude of priests and prelates sat was obliged to endure this indignity at its door. The author was not sent to the galleys, nor the players to the house of correction. Not long after this performance those same players came before the Assembly to claim the rites of the very religion they had dared to expose, and to show their prostituted faces in the senate, while the Archbishop of Paris, whose office was known to his people only by his prayers and blessings and his wealth only by his alms, is forced to abandon his house and fly from his flock as from ravening wolves, because, if you please, in the sixteenth century the Cardinal of Lorraine was a rebel and a murderer.

Footnote: This is on a supposition of the truth of this story; but he was not in France at the time. One name serves as well as another.

Such is the effect of the perversion of history by men who for the same nefarious purposes have perverted every other part of learning. But those who will stand on that elevation of reason which places centuries under our eye and brings things to the true point of comparison, which obscures little names and effaces the colours of little parties, and to which nothing can rise but the spirit and moral quality of human actions — those men will say to the teachers of the Palais Royal: the Cardinal of Lorraine was the murderer of the sixteenth century; you have the glory of being the murderers in the eighteenth; and this is the only difference between you. But history in the nineteenth century, better understood and better employed, will, I trust, teach a civilised posterity to abhor the misdeeds of both these barbarous ages. It will teach future priests and magistrates not to retaliate on the speculative and inactive atheists of future times for the enormities committed by the present practical zealots and furious fanatics of that wretched error, which in its quiescent state is more than punished whenever it is embraced. It will teach posterity not to make war on either religion or philosophy for the abuse the hypocrites of both have made of the two most valuable blessings conferred on us by the bounty of the universal Patron, who eminently favours and protects the race of man in all things.

If your clergy, or any clergy, should show themselves vicious beyond the fair bounds allowed to human infirmity, and beyond those professional faults that can hardly be separated from professional virtues, then, though their vices can never countenance the exercise of oppression, I do admit they would naturally have the effect of much abating our indignation against tyrants who exceed measure and justice in punishing them. I can allow in clergymen of every division some tenaciousness of their own opinion, some overflow of zeal to propagate it, some partiality to their own state and office, some attachment to the interest of their own body, some preference for those who listen to their doctrines with docility over those who scorn and deride them. I allow all this, because I am a man who has to deal with men, and who would not, through a violence of toleration, run into the greatest intolerance of all. I must bear with infirmities until they fester into crimes.

Undoubtedly the natural progress of the passions from frailty into vice ought to be prevented by a watchful eye and a firm hand. But is it true that the body of your clergy had passed those limits of a just allowance? From the general style of your recent publications of all sorts, one would be led to believe that your clergy in France were a sort of monsters, a horrible composition of superstition, ignorance, sloth, fraud, avarice and tyranny. But is this true? Is it true that the lapse of time, the ceasing of conflicting interests, and the woeful experience of the evils that come of party rage have had no sort of influence in gradually improving their minds? Is it true that they were daily making fresh invasions on the civil power, troubling the domestic quiet of their country, and making the operations of its government feeble and precarious? Is it true that the clergy of our times have pressed the laity down with an iron hand and were everywhere lighting the fires of a savage persecution? Did they try by every fraud to increase their estates? Did they habitually exceed the due demands on estates that were their own? Or, rigidly screwing right up into wrong, did they turn a legal claim into a vexatious extortion? Not possessing power, were they filled with the vices of those who envy it? Were they inflamed with a violent, litigious spirit of controversy? Goaded on by the ambition of intellectual sovereignty, were they ready to fly in the face of all magistracy, to fire churches, to massacre the priests of other descriptions, to pull down altars, and to make their way over the ruins of overthrown governments to an empire of doctrine — sometimes flattering, sometimes forcing the consciences of men out of the jurisdiction of public institutions into submission to their personal authority, beginning with a claim of liberty and ending with an abuse of power?

These, or some of these, were the vices charged, and not wholly without foundation, against several of the churchmen of former times, who belonged to the two great parties that then divided and distracted Europe.

If in France there was, as in other countries there visibly is, a great abatement rather than any increase of these vices, then instead of loading the present clergy with the crimes of other men and the odious character of other times, in common equity they ought to be praised, encouraged and supported for departing from a spirit that disgraced their predecessors, and for taking on a temper of mind and manners more suited to their sacred function.

When my occasions took me to France towards the close of the late reign, the clergy in all their forms engaged a considerable part of my curiosity. So far from finding the complaints and discontents against that body which some publications had given me reason to expect — except from one set of men, not then very numerous though very active — I noticed little or no public or private uneasiness on their account. On further examination I found the clergy in general to be persons of moderate minds and decorous manners; and I include the seculars and the regulars of both sexes. I did not have the good fortune to know a great many of the parish clergy, but in general I received a perfectly good account of their morals and of their attention to their duties. With some of the higher clergy I had a personal acquaintance, and of the rest of that class very good means of information. They were almost all of them persons of noble birth. They resembled others of their own rank, and where there was any difference it was in their favour. They were more fully educated than the military nobility, so as by no means to disgrace their profession by ignorance or by unfitness for the exercise of their authority. They seemed to me, beyond the clerical character, liberal and open, with the hearts of gentlemen and men of honour, neither insolent nor servile in their manners and conduct. They seemed to me rather a superior class — a set of men among whom you would not be surprised to find a Fénelon. I saw among the clergy in Paris men of great learning and candour, many of a description not to be met with anywhere else, and I had reason to believe that this description was not confined to Paris. What I found in other places I know was accidental, and therefore to be presumed a fair sample. I spent a few days in a provincial town where, in the bishop's absence, I passed my evenings with three clergymen, his vicars-general, men who would have done honour to any church. They were all well informed; two of them of deep, general and extensive erudition, ancient and modern, Eastern and Western, and particularly in their own profession. They had a wider knowledge of our English divines than I expected, and they entered into the genius of those writers with a critical accuracy. One of these gentlemen has since died: the Abbé Morangis. I pay this tribute without reluctance to the memory of that noble, reverend, learned and excellent person, and I would do the same as cheerfully to the merits of the others, who I believe are still living, if I did not fear to hurt men I am unable to help.

Some of these clergymen of rank deserve general respect on every ground. They deserve gratitude from me and from many English people. If this letter should ever come into their hands, I hope they will believe that there are people of our nation who feel for their unmerited fall, and for the cruel confiscation of their fortunes, with no common feeling. What I say of them is a testimony, so far as one feeble voice can go, that I owe to truth. Wherever the question of this unnatural persecution is concerned, I will pay it. No one shall prevent me from being just and grateful. The time is fitted for the duty; and it is particularly becoming to show our justice and gratitude when men who have deserved well of us and of mankind are labouring under popular obloquy and the persecutions of oppressive power.

Before your Revolution you had about a hundred and twenty bishops. A few of them were men of eminent sanctity and charity without limit. When we talk of the heroic we are of course talking of rare virtue. I believe instances of eminent depravity may be as rare among them as instances of transcendent goodness. Examples of avarice and of licentiousness may be picked out, I do not question it, by those who delight in the sort of investigation that leads to such discoveries. A man as old as I am will not be astonished that several men in every description do not lead that perfect life of self-denial with regard to wealth or pleasure which everyone wishes for, which some expect, and which nobody exacts more rigorously than those who are most attentive to their own interests or most indulgent to their own passions. When I was in France, I am certain that the number of vicious prelates was not great. Certain individuals among them, not distinguished for the regularity of their lives, made some amends for lacking the severe virtues by possessing the liberal ones, and were endowed with qualities that made them useful in church and state. I am told that with few exceptions Louis the Sixteenth was more attentive to character in his promotions to that rank than his immediate predecessor, and I believe it may be true, since some spirit of reform has prevailed throughout the whole reign. But the present ruling power has shown a disposition only to plunder the church. It has punished all prelates, which is to favour the vicious, at least in point of reputation. It has made a degrading establishment of pensioners, to which no man of liberal ideas or liberal condition will destine his children. It must settle into the lowest classes of the people. As the lower clergy with you are not numerous enough for their duties, as those duties are beyond measure minute and toilsome, and as you have left no middle class of clergy at their ease, in future nothing of science or erudition can exist in the Gallican Church. To complete the project, without the least attention to the rights of patrons, the Assembly has provided for an elective clergy in future — an arrangement that will drive out of the clerical profession every man of sobriety and everyone who can claim independence in his function or his conduct, and that will throw the whole direction of the public mind into the hands of a set of licentious, bold, crafty, factious, flattering wretches, of such a condition and such habits of life as will make their contemptible pensions, beside which an exciseman's stipend is lucrative and honourable, an object of low and illiberal intrigue. Those officers whom they still call bishops are to be elected to a comparatively mean provision, by the same arts — that is, electioneering arts — by men of every religious tenet known or inventable. The new lawgivers have settled nothing whatever about their qualifications, in doctrine or in morals, any more than they have done for the subordinate clergy; nor does anything appear to stop both the higher and the lower from practising or preaching, at their discretion, any mode of religion or irreligion they please. I do not yet see what jurisdiction the bishops are to have over their subordinates, or whether they are to have any jurisdiction at all.

In short, Sir, it seems to me that this new ecclesiastical establishment is intended only to be temporary, and preparatory to the utter abolition of the Christian religion under any of its forms, whenever men's minds have been prepared for this last stroke against it by the accomplishment of the plan for bringing its ministers into universal contempt. Anyone who will not believe that the philosophical fanatics who guide these matters have long entertained such a design is utterly ignorant of their character and their proceedings. These enthusiasts do not scruple to avow their opinion that a state can subsist better without any religion than with one, and that they are able to supply the place of any good there may be in it by a project of their own — namely, by a sort of education they have imagined, founded on a knowledge of the physical wants of men and carried progressively up to an enlightened self-interest which, when well understood, they tell us, will identify itself with a larger and public interest. The scheme of this education has long been known. Lately they distinguish it, having acquired an entirely new nomenclature of technical terms, by the name of a Civic Education.

I hope their partisans in England — to whom I attribute very inconsiderate conduct rather than the ultimate object of this detestable design — will succeed neither in plundering the clergy nor in introducing a principle of popular election to our bishoprics and parish livings. In the present condition of the world this would be the last corruption of the church, the utter ruin of the clerical character, and the most dangerous shock the state ever received through a misunderstood arrangement of religion. I know well enough that the bishoprics and livings, under royal and seigniorial patronage as they now are in England and as they lately were in France, are sometimes acquired by unworthy methods; but the other mode of ecclesiastical canvassing subjects them infinitely more surely and more generally to all the evil arts of low ambition, which, operating on and through greater numbers, will produce mischief in proportion.

Those of you who have robbed the clergy think you will easily reconcile your conduct to all Protestant nations, because the clergy you have plundered, degraded and given over to mockery and scorn are Roman Catholic — that is, of their own supposed persuasion. I have no doubt that some miserable bigots will be found here as elsewhere, who hate sects and parties different from their own more than they love the substance of religion, and who are angrier with those who differ from them over particular plans and systems than they are displeased with men who attack the foundation of our common hope. Such men will write and speak on the subject in the manner to be expected from their temper and character. Burnet says that when he was in France in the year 1683, "the method which carried over the men of the finest parts to Popery was this: they brought themselves to doubt of the whole Christian religion; when that was once done, it seemed a more indifferent thing of what side or form they continued outwardly." If that was then the ecclesiastical policy of France, they have since had far too much reason to repent of it. They preferred atheism to a form of religion that did not agree with their ideas. They succeeded in destroying that form, and atheism has succeeded in destroying them. I can readily believe Burnet's story, because I have seen too much of a similar spirit among ourselves — and a little of it is much too much. The humour, however, is not general.

The teachers who reformed our religion in England bore no sort of resemblance to your present reforming doctors in Paris. Perhaps they were, like the men they opposed, rather more under the influence of party spirit than one could wish; but they were the most sincere believers, men of the most fervent and exalted piety, ready to die — as some of them did die — like true heroes in defence of their particular ideas of Christianity, and they would have died with equal fortitude and more cheerfully for that stock of general truth whose branches they contended for with their blood. Those men would have disowned with horror the wretches who claim fellowship with them on no other ground than having pillaged the people they carried on controversies with, and having despised the common religion for whose purity the reformers exerted themselves with a zeal that unmistakably declared their highest reverence for the substance of the system they wanted to reform. Many of their descendants have kept the same zeal, but, being less engaged in conflict, with more moderation. They do not forget that justice and mercy are substantial parts of religion. Impious men do not recommend themselves to their communion by iniquity and cruelty towards any description of their fellow creatures.

We hear these new teachers continually boasting of their spirit of toleration. That men should tolerate all opinions when they think none of any value is a matter of small merit. Equal neglect is not impartial kindness. The species of benevolence that arises out of contempt is not true charity. There are plenty of men in England who tolerate in the true spirit of toleration. They think the dogmas of religion are all of importance, though in different degrees, and that among them there is, as among all things of value, a just ground of preference. They favour, therefore, and they tolerate. They tolerate not because they despise opinions but because they respect justice. They would reverently and affectionately protect all religions, because they love and venerate the great principle they all agree on and the great object they are all directed to. They begin to see more and more plainly that we all have a common cause against a common enemy. They will not be so misled by the spirit of faction as to fail to distinguish what is done in favour of their own subdivision from acts of hostility which, aimed through some particular description of men, are aimed at the whole body that they themselves are included in under another name. It is impossible for me to say what the character of every description of men among us may be. But I speak for the greater part; and for them I must tell you that sacrilege is no part of their doctrine of good works; and that, so far from inviting you into their fellowship on such a title, if your professors are admitted to their communion they must carefully conceal their doctrine that it is lawful to proscribe innocent men, and they must make restitution of all stolen goods whatsoever. Until then they are none of ours.

You may suppose that we do not approve your confiscating the revenues of bishops and deans and chapters and parish clergy who possess independent estates in land, because we have the same sort of establishment in England. That objection, you will say, cannot apply to the confiscation of the goods of monks and nuns and the abolition of their orders. It is true that this particular part of your general confiscation does not touch England as a precedent in point; but the reason applies, and it goes a long way. The Long Parliament confiscated the lands of deans and chapters in England on the same ideas on which your Assembly has put the lands of the monastic orders up for sale. But the danger lies in the principle of injustice and not in the description of persons it is first exercised on. I see in a country very near us a course of policy pursued that sets justice, the common concern of mankind, at defiance. With the National Assembly of France, possession is nothing, law and usage are nothing. I see the National Assembly openly condemning the doctrine of prescription, which one of the greatest of their own lawyers tells us, with great truth, is part of the law of nature. He tells us that fixing its limits positively, and securing it from invasion, were among the reasons why civil society itself was instituted. Once prescription is shaken, no kind of property is secure as soon as it becomes an object large enough to tempt the cupidity of needy power. I see a practice perfectly answering to their contempt for this great fundamental part of natural law. I see the confiscators beginning with bishops and chapters and monasteries, but I do not see them ending there. I see the princes of the blood, who by the oldest usages of that kingdom held large landed estates, deprived of their possessions with hardly the compliment of a debate, and reduced, in place of their stable independent property, to the hope of some precarious charitable pension at the pleasure of an Assembly which will of course pay little regard to the rights of pensioners at pleasure when it despises those of legal proprietors. Flushed with the insolence of their first inglorious victories, and pressed by the distresses their own lust of unhallowed gain has caused, disappointed but not discouraged, they have at last ventured to subvert all property of every description throughout the whole extent of a great kingdom. They have compelled all men, in all transactions of commerce, in the disposal of land, in civil dealing and through the whole intercourse of life, to accept as perfect payment and good and lawful tender the symbols of their speculations on a projected sale of their plunder. What vestiges of liberty or property have they left? The tenant right of a cabbage garden, a year's interest in a hovel, the goodwill of an alehouse or a baker's shop, the very shadow of a constructive property, are treated more ceremoniously in our Parliament than the oldest and most valuable landed possessions in the hands of the most respectable persons are with you, or than the whole body of the moneyed and commercial interest of your country. We hold the legislative authority in high regard; but we have never dreamt that Parliaments had any right whatever to violate property, to overrule prescription, or to force a currency of their own invention into the place of one that is real and recognised by the law of nations. But you, who began by refusing to submit to the most moderate restraints, have ended by establishing an unheard-of despotism. I find that the ground your confiscators go on is this: that their proceedings could not indeed be supported in a court of justice, but that the rules of prescription cannot bind a legislative assembly. So this legislative assembly of a free nation sits not for the security but for the destruction of property — and not of property only, but of every rule and maxim that can give it stability, and of the very instruments that alone can give it circulation.

Footnote: Domat.

Footnote: Speech of M. Camus, published by order of the National Assembly.

When the Anabaptists of Münster in the sixteenth century had filled Germany with confusion by their system of levelling and their wild opinions about property, what country in Europe did the progress of their fury not give just cause for alarm? Of all things, wisdom is the most terrified by epidemic fanaticism, because of all enemies it is the one she is least able to find any kind of resource against. We cannot be ignorant of the spirit of atheistical fanaticism inspired by a multitude of writings scattered about with incredible industry and expense, and by sermons delivered in all the streets and public places of Paris. These writings and sermons have filled the populace with a black and savage ferocity of mind that overrides in them the common feelings of nature as well as all sentiments of morality and religion — so much so that these wretches are brought to bear with sullen patience the intolerable distresses caused them by the violent convulsions and transfers that have been made in property. The spirit of proselytism attends this spirit of fanaticism. They have societies to intrigue and correspond at home and abroad for the propagation of their tenets. The republic of Berne, one of the happiest, most prosperous and best-governed countries on earth, is one of the great objects they aim at destroying. I am told they have to some extent succeeded in sowing the seeds of discontent there. They are busy throughout Germany. Spain and Italy have not been left untried. England is not left out of the comprehensive scheme of their malignant charity; and in England we find men who stretch out their arms to them, who recommend their example from more than one pulpit, and who choose, at more than one periodical meeting, to correspond publicly with them, to applaud them and to hold them up for imitation — who receive from them tokens of brotherhood, and standards consecrated in the middle of their rites and mysteries, and who propose leagues of perpetual friendship to them at the very time when the power to which our constitution has exclusively delegated the treaty-making capacity of this kingdom may find it expedient to make war on them.

Footnote: Whether the following description is strictly true I do not know; but it is what the publishers would have pass for true, in order to encourage others. In a letter from Toul, printed in one of their papers, is this passage about the people of that district: "Dans la Révolution actuelle, ils ont résisté à toutes les séductions du bigotisme, aux persécutions et aux tracasseries des ennemis de la Révolution. Oubliant leurs plus grands intérêts pour rendre hommage aux vues d'ordre général qui ont déterminé l'Assemblée Nationale, ils voient, sans se plaindre, supprimer cette foule d'établissemens ecclésiastiques par lesquels ils subsistoient; et même, en perdant leur siège épiscopal, la seule de toutes ces ressources qui pouvoit, ou plutôt qui devoit, en toute équité, leur être conservée, condamnés à la plus effrayante misère sans avoir été ni pu être entendus, ils ne murmurent point; ils restent fidèles aux principes du plus pur patriotisme; ils sont encore prêts à verser leur sang pour le maintien de la constitution, qui va réduire leur ville à la plus déplorable nullité." In English: in the present Revolution they have resisted all the seductions of bigotry, and all the persecutions and harassments of the enemies of the Revolution. Forgetting their own greatest interests in order to pay homage to the views of general order that have guided the National Assembly, they watch without complaining while that crowd of church establishments is suppressed which was what they lived by; and even in losing their bishop's seat, the one resource of them all that could have been — or rather that in all equity ought to have been — preserved to them, condemned to the most frightful poverty without having been heard, or having been able to be heard, they do not murmur. They remain faithful to the principles of the purest patriotism; they are still ready to shed their blood to maintain the constitution that is about to reduce their town to the most deplorable nothingness. These people are not supposed to have endured those sufferings and injustices in a struggle for liberty, for the same account states truly that they have always been free. Their patience under beggary and ruin, and their enduring the most flagrant and admitted injustice without remonstrance, can, if it is strictly true, be nothing but the effect of this dire fanaticism. A great multitude all over France is in the same condition and the same temper.

Footnote: See the proceedings of the confederation at Nantes.

It is not the confiscation of our church property, following this example in France, that I dread, though I think that would be no trifling evil. The great source of my anxiety is that it should ever be considered in England to be the policy of a state to look for a resource in confiscations of any kind, or that any one description of citizens should come to regard any of the others as their proper prey. Nations are wading deeper and deeper into an ocean of boundless debt. Public debts, which at first were a security to governments by giving many people an interest in the public peace, are likely in their excess to become the means of overthrowing them. If governments provide for these debts by heavy taxes, they perish by becoming odious to the people. If they do not provide for them, they will be undone by the efforts of the most dangerous of all parties: I mean an extensive, discontented moneyed interest, injured and not destroyed. The men who make up this interest look for their security first to the good faith of government and second to its power. If they find the old governments worn out and effete, their springs slack, and not vigorous enough for their purposes, they may look for new ones possessed of more energy — and this energy will be derived not from any acquisition of resources but from a contempt of justice. Revolutions are favourable to confiscation, and it is impossible to know under what obnoxious names the next confiscations will be authorised. I am sure that the principles now predominant in France extend to a great many persons, and descriptions of persons, in every country, who think their harmless idleness is their security. This kind of innocence in proprietors can be argued into uselessness, and uselessness into an unfitness for their estates. Many parts of Europe are in open disorder. In many others there is a hollow murmuring underground; a confused movement is felt that threatens a general earthquake in the political world. Already confederacies and correspondences of the most extraordinary kind are forming in several countries. In such a state of things we ought to be on our guard. In all changes, if changes there must be, the circumstance that will do most to blunt the edge of their mischief and to promote whatever good may be in them is that they should find us with our minds tenacious of justice and tender of property.

Footnote: "Si plures sunt ii quibus improbe datum est, quam illi quibus injuste ademptum est, idcirco plus etiam valent? Non enim numero hæc judicantur, sed pondere. Quam autem habet æquitatem, ut agrum multis annis, aut etiam sæculis ante possessum, qui nullum habuit habeat, qui autem habuit amittat? Ac, propter hoc injuriæ genus, Lacedæmonii Lysandrum Ephorum expulerunt; Agin regem (quod nunquam antea apud eos acciderat) necaverunt; exque eo tempore tantæ discordiæ secutæ sunt, ut et tyranni exsisterent, et optimates exterminarentur, et preclarissime constituta respublica dilaberetur. Nec vero solum ipsa cecidit, sed etiam reliquam Græciam evertit contagionibus malorum, quæ a Lacedæmoniis profectæ manarunt latius." In English: if those to whom property has been wrongly given are more numerous than those from whom it was unjustly taken, does that make them weigh more? These matters are judged not by number but by weight. And what fairness is there in a man who has never owned any land taking possession of land held for many years or even for centuries, and the man who did own it losing it? It was for this kind of injustice that the Spartans expelled Lysander the ephor and put King Agis to death, a thing that had never happened among them before; and from that time such discords followed that tyrants arose, the leading men were driven out, and a state most admirably constituted fell to pieces. Nor did it merely fall itself; it overturned the rest of Greece as well by the contagion of the evils which, starting from the Spartans, spread more widely. After speaking of the conduct of Aratus of Sicyon, the model of true patriots, which was in a very different spirit, he says: "Sic par est agere cum civibus; non (ut bis jam vidimus) hastam in foro ponere et bona civium voci subjicere præconis. At ille Græcus (id quod fuit sapientis et præstantis viri) omnibus consulendum esse putavit: eaque est summa ratio et sapientia boni civis, commoda civium non divellere, sed omnes eadem æquitate continere." In English: this is the right way to deal with one's fellow citizens — not, as we have now twice seen, to set up the spear in the forum and put the goods of citizens under the auctioneer's voice. But that Greek, as became a wise and outstanding man, thought that the good of all must be consulted; and this is the highest reason and wisdom in a good citizen, not to tear the interests of the citizens apart but to hold them all together within the same equity. — Cicero, On Duties, book 2.

Footnote: See two books entitled "Einige Originalschriften des Illuminatenordens" and "System und Folgen des Illuminatenordens". Munich, 1787.

But it will be argued that this confiscation in France ought not to alarm other nations. They say it is not made out of wanton rapacity, that it is a great measure of national policy adopted to remove an extensive, inveterate, superstitious mischief. It is with the greatest difficulty that I am able to separate policy from justice. Justice is itself the great standing policy of civil society, and any notable departure from it, in any circumstances, lies under the suspicion of being no policy at all.

When men are encouraged into a certain mode of life by the existing laws and protected in it as a lawful occupation; when they have adapted all their ideas and all their habits to it; when the law has long made adherence to its rules a ground of reputation and departure from them a ground of disgrace and even of penalty — then I am sure it is unjust for a legislature by an arbitrary act to do sudden violence to their minds and their feelings, forcibly to degrade them from their state and condition, and to stigmatise with shame and infamy that character and those customs which had before been made the measure of their happiness and honour. If to this is added expulsion from their homes and confiscation of all their goods, I am not sagacious enough to see how this despotic sport made of the feelings, consciences, prejudices and properties of men can be distinguished from the rankest tyranny.

If the injustice of the course pursued in France is clear, the policy of the measure — that is, the public benefit expected from it — ought to be at least as evident and at least as important. A man who acts under the influence of no passion, and who has nothing in view in his projects but the public good, will immediately be struck by a great difference between what policy would dictate at the original introduction of such institutions and what it dictates on a question of abolishing them entirely, when they have cast their roots wide and deep and when, by long habit, things more valuable than themselves have become adapted to them and in a manner interwoven with them, so that the one cannot be destroyed without notably damaging the other. He might be embarrassed if the case really were as the sophisters represent it in their paltry style of debating. But in this, as in most questions of state, there is a middle. There is something else besides the bare alternative of absolute destruction or unreformed existence. Spartam nactus es; hanc exorna — Sparta is what has fallen to you, so make the best of it. This is in my opinion a rule of profound sense, and it ought never to be out of the mind of an honest reformer. I cannot conceive how any man can have brought himself to such a pitch of presumption as to regard his country as nothing but a carte blanche on which he may scribble whatever he pleases. A man full of warm, speculative benevolence may wish his society were constituted otherwise than he finds it; but a good patriot and a true politician always considers how he can make the most of the existing materials of his country. A disposition to preserve and an ability to improve, taken together, would be my standard of a statesman. Everything else is vulgar in the conception and perilous in the execution.

There are moments in the fortunes of states when particular men are called on to make improvements by great mental exertion. In those moments, even when they seem to enjoy the confidence of their prince and their country and to be invested with full authority, they do not always have apt instruments to hand. A politician who wants to do great things looks for a power — what our workmen call a purchase; and if he finds that power, in politics as in mechanics, he cannot be at a loss how to apply it. In the monastic institutions, in my opinion, was to be found a great power for the machinery of political benevolence. There were revenues under a public direction; there were men wholly set apart and dedicated to public purposes, with no ties and no principles but public ones — men who could not possibly convert the estate of the community into a private fortune, men closed to self-interest whose avarice is on behalf of some community, men to whom personal poverty is honour and implicit obedience stands in the place of freedom. It is vain for a man to look to the possibility of making such things when he wants them. The winds blow as they please. These institutions are the products of enthusiasm; they are the instruments of wisdom. Wisdom cannot create materials; they are the gifts of nature or of chance, and her pride is in the use of them. The perennial existence of corporate bodies, and their fortunes, are things particularly suited to a man who takes long views — who meditates designs that take time to fashion and are meant to last when they are accomplished. He does not deserve to rank high, or even to be mentioned in the order of great statesmen, who, having obtained the command and direction of such a power as existed in the wealth, the discipline and the habits of the corporations you have rashly destroyed, can find no way of turning it to the great and lasting benefit of his country. On a view of this subject a thousand uses suggest themselves to a contriving mind. To destroy any power growing wild from the rank productive force of the human mind is almost equivalent, in the moral world, to destroying the apparently active properties of bodies in the material one. It would be like trying to destroy — if it were within our competence to destroy — the expansive force of fixed air in nitre, or the power of steam, or of electricity, or of magnetism. These energies always existed in nature and were always discernible. Some of them seemed useless, some noxious, some no better than a plaything for children, until contemplative ability, combining with practical skill, tamed their wild nature, subdued them to use, and made them at once the most powerful and the most tractable agents in the service of the great views and designs of men. Did fifty thousand persons, whose mental and bodily labour you might direct, and so many hundred thousand a year of a revenue that was neither lazy nor superstitious, seem too big for your abilities to wield? Had you no way of using the men except by converting monks into pensioners? Had you no way of turning the revenue to account except through the improvident resource of a spendthrift sale? If you were so destitute of mental funds, the proceeding follows in its natural course. Your politicians do not understand their trade, and so they sell their tools.

But the institutions savour of superstition in their very principle, and they nourish it by a permanent and standing influence. This I do not mean to dispute; but it ought not to hinder you from drawing out of superstition itself whatever resources it may furnish for the public advantage. You draw benefits from many dispositions and many passions of the human mind that are of quite as doubtful a colour, to the moral eye, as superstition is. It was your business to correct and moderate whatever was noxious in this passion, as in all the passions. But is superstition the greatest of all possible vices? In its possible excess I think it becomes a very great evil. It is, however, a moral subject, and of course admits of every degree and every modification. Superstition is the religion of feeble minds, and they must be tolerated in an admixture of it, in some trifling or some enthusiastic shape or other, or else you will deprive weak minds of a resource that the strongest have found necessary. The body of all true religion consists, to be sure, in obedience to the will of the Sovereign of the world, in a confidence in His declarations, and in imitation of His perfections. The rest is our own. It may be prejudicial to that great end, or it may be a help to it. Wise men, who as such are not admirers — not admirers, at least, of the munera terræ, the gifts of the earth — are not violently attached to these things, nor do they violently hate them. Wisdom is not the severest corrector of folly. It is the rival follies that wage such unrelenting war on one another, and make such cruel use of their advantages whenever they can engage the immoderate common run of people on the one side or the other in their quarrels. Prudence would stay neutral; but if, in the contest between fond attachment and fierce antipathy over things whose nature was never meant to produce such heat, a prudent man were obliged to choose which errors and excesses of enthusiasm he would condemn and which he would bear with, perhaps he would think the superstition that builds more tolerable than the one that demolishes; the one that adorns a country than the one that deforms it; the one that endows than the one that plunders; the one that disposes men to mistaken beneficence than the one that stimulates them to real injustice; the one that leads a man to refuse himself lawful pleasures than the one that snatches from others the scanty subsistence of their self-denial. Such, I think, is very nearly the state of the question between the ancient founders of monkish superstition and the superstition of the pretended philosophers of the hour.

For the present I put off all consideration of the supposed public profit of the sale, which I take to be perfectly delusive in any case. I shall consider it here only as a transfer of property, and I shall trouble you with a few thoughts on the policy of that transfer.

In every prosperous community something more is produced than goes to the immediate support of the producer. This surplus forms the income of the landed capitalist. It will be spent by a proprietor who does not labour. But this idleness is itself the spring of labour, this repose the spur of industry. The state's only concern is that the capital taken in rent from the land should be returned to the industry it came from, and that its spending should do the least possible damage to the morals of those who spend it and of the people it is returned to.

In every view of receipt, of expenditure and of personal employment, a sober legislator would carefully compare the possessor he is being urged to expel with the stranger proposed to fill his place. Before we incur the inconveniences that must attend all violent revolutions in property through extensive confiscation, we ought to have some rational assurance that the purchasers of the confiscated property will be to a considerable degree more industrious, more virtuous, more sober, less disposed to extort an unreasonable proportion of the labourer's gains or to consume on themselves a larger share than is fit for one individual — or else that they will be qualified to dispense the surplus in a steadier and more equal manner, so as to answer the purposes of a politic expenditure, than the old possessors, call those possessors bishops or canons or commendatory abbots or monks or what you please. The monks are lazy. Be it so. Suppose them employed in nothing but singing in the choir. They are as usefully employed as those who neither sing nor say — as usefully, even, as those who sing on the stage. They are as usefully employed as if they worked from dawn to dark at the innumerable servile, degrading, unseemly, unmanly and often most unwholesome and pestilent occupations that the social economy inevitably dooms so many wretches to. If it were not generally pernicious to disturb the natural course of things and to impede in any degree the great wheel of circulation that is turned by the strangely directed labour of these unhappy people, I should be infinitely more inclined to rescue them by force from their miserable industry than to disturb the tranquil repose of monastic quiet by violence. Humanity, and perhaps policy, might justify me better in the first than in the second. It is a subject I have often reflected on, and never reflected on without feeling. I am sure that nothing except the necessity of submitting to the yoke of luxury and the despotism of fashion, which will distribute the surplus product of the soil in their own imperious way, can justify tolerating such trades and employments in a well-regulated state. But for the purpose of that distribution, it seems to me that the idle expenses of monks are quite as well directed as the idle expenses of us lay loiterers.

Where the advantages of the possession and of the project are equal, there is no motive for a change. But in the present case they are perhaps not equal, and the difference is in favour of the possession. It does not appear to me that the expenses of the men you are going to expel in fact take a course so directly and generally tending to corrupt and degrade and make miserable those they pass through as the expenses of the favourites you are intruding into their houses. Why should the spending of a great landed property, which is a dispersal of the surplus product of the soil, appear intolerable to you or to me when it takes its course through the building up of vast libraries, which are the history of the strength and weakness of the human mind; through great collections of ancient records and medals and coins, which attest and explain laws and customs; through paintings and statues that by imitating nature seem to extend the limits of creation; through grand monuments of the dead, which carry on the regards and connections of life beyond the grave; through collections of the specimens of nature, which become a representative assembly of all the classes and families of the world, and which by their arrangement make science easier and by exciting curiosity open the way into it? If by great permanent establishments all these objects of expense are better secured from the inconstant sport of personal caprice and personal extravagance, are they worse than if the same tastes prevailed in scattered individuals? Does not the sweat of the mason and the carpenter, who toil in order to share the sweat of the peasant, flow as pleasantly and as healthily in constructing and repairing the majestic buildings of religion as in the painted booths and sordid sties of vice and luxury? As honourably and as profitably in repairing the sacred works that grow hoary with innumerable years as on the momentary receptacles of passing voluptuousness — in opera houses and brothels and gaming houses and clubhouses and obelisks in the Champ de Mars? Is the surplus product of the olive and the vine worse employed in the frugal maintenance of persons whom the fictions of a pious imagination raise to dignity by placing them in the service of God, than in pampering the innumerable multitude who are degraded by being made useless domestics, subservient to the pride of man? Is the decoration of temples an expenditure less worthy of a wise man than ribbons and laces and national cockades and little private houses and late suppers and all the innumerable fopperies and follies that wealth sports away the burden of its superfluity in?

We tolerate even these — not out of love for them, but for fear of worse. We tolerate them because property and liberty require that toleration up to a point. But why proscribe the other and surely, from every point of view, the more laudable use of estates? Why, through the violation of all property and an outrage on every principle of liberty, forcibly carry them from the better use to the worse?

This comparison between the new individuals and the old bodies is made on the supposition that no reform could be made in the latter. But in a question of reformation I always consider corporate bodies, whether single persons or many, to be much more susceptible than private citizens ever can be, or perhaps ought to be, of a public direction by the power of the state in the use of their property and in the regulation of their members' modes and habits of life; and this seems to me a very material consideration for anyone undertaking something that deserves the name of a political enterprise. So much for the estates of the monasteries.

As to the estates held by bishops and canons and commendatory abbots, I cannot find out any reason why some landed estates may not be held otherwise than by inheritance. Can any philosophic spoiler undertake to demonstrate the positive or the comparative evil of having a certain, and a large, portion of landed property pass in succession through persons whose title to it is, always in theory and often in fact, an eminent degree of piety, morals and learning — a property which by its destination, in their turn and on the score of merit, gives renewal and support to the noblest families and to the lowest the means of dignity and elevation; a property whose tenure is the performance of a duty, whatever value you choose to set on that duty, and whose proprietors' character demands at least an outward decorum and gravity of manners; men who are to exercise a generous but temperate hospitality, who are to consider part of their income as a trust for charity, and who, even when they fail in that trust, when they slide out of their character and degenerate into a mere ordinary secular nobleman or gentleman, are in no respect worse than those who may succeed them in their forfeited possessions? Is it better that estates should be held by men who have no duty than by men who have one? By men whose character and destination point them at virtues, than by men who have no rule and no direction in spending their estates but their own will and appetite? Nor are these estates held altogether in the character or with the evils supposed to be inherent in mortmain. They pass from hand to hand in a more rapid circulation than any other. No excess is good, and therefore too great a proportion of landed property may be held officially for life; but it does not seem to me materially injurious to any commonwealth that there should be some estates that stand a chance of being acquired by means other than the previous acquisition of money.

Section Eleven: The New Constitution, and How It Was Built

This letter has grown to a great length, though it is short indeed compared with the infinite extent of the subject. Various other calls have from time to time drawn my mind away from it. I was not sorry to give myself leisure to see whether I might not find reasons in the proceedings of the National Assembly to change or qualify some of my first sentiments. Everything has confirmed me more strongly in my first opinions. It was my original purpose to survey the principles of the National Assembly with regard to the great fundamental establishments, and to compare the whole of what you have put in the place of what you have destroyed with the several members of our British constitution. But this plan is of greater extent than I first reckoned, and I find you have little wish to take advantage of any examples. For the present I must content myself with some remarks on your establishments, reserving what I meant to say about the spirit of our British monarchy, aristocracy and democracy as they exist in practice for another time.

I have surveyed what has been done by the governing power in France. I have certainly spoken of it freely. Men whose principle it is to despise the ancient, permanent sense of mankind and to set up a scheme of society on new principles must naturally expect that those of us who think better of the judgment of the human race than of theirs will regard both them and their devices as men and schemes on trial. They must take it for granted that we attend closely to their reason but not at all to their authority. They do not have one of the great influencing prejudices of mankind in their favour. They avow their hostility to opinion. Of course, then, they must expect no support from that influence, which they have deposed from the seat of its jurisdiction along with every other authority.

I can never regard this Assembly as anything but a voluntary association of men who have used circumstances to seize the power of the state. They do not have the sanction and authority of the character they first met under. They have assumed another of a very different nature, and have completely altered and inverted all the relations they originally stood in. They do not hold the authority they exercise under any constitutional law of the state. They have departed from the instructions of the people who sent them — and those instructions, since the Assembly did not act by virtue of any ancient usage or settled law, were the sole source of their authority. The most considerable of their acts have not been carried by great majorities; and in this sort of near division, which carries only the constructive authority of the whole, strangers will consider the reasons as well as the resolutions.

If they had set up this new experimental government as a necessary substitute for an expelled tyranny, mankind would anticipate the time of prescription, which through long usage mellows into legality governments that were violent at their beginning. Everyone whose affections lead them towards the conservation of civil order would recognise the child as legitimate even in its cradle, having been produced from those principles of compelling expediency to which all just governments owe their birth and by which they justify their continuance. But men will be late and reluctant in giving any sort of countenance to the operations of a power that has derived its birth from no law and no necessity, and that on the contrary has had its origin in the vices and sinister practices by which the social union is often disturbed and sometimes destroyed. This Assembly has hardly a year's prescription. We have their own word for it that they have made a revolution. To make a revolution is a measure which primâ fronte, on the face of it, requires an apology. To make a revolution is to subvert the ancient state of one's country, and no common reasons are called for to justify so violent a proceeding. The sense of mankind authorises us to inquire into the manner in which new power was acquired, and to criticise the use made of it, with less awe and reverence than is usually granted to a settled and recognised authority.

In obtaining and securing their power, the Assembly proceeds on principles that are the exact opposite of those which appear to direct them in the use of it. An observation on this difference will let us into the true spirit of their conduct. Everything they have done, or continue to do, in order to obtain and keep their power, is done by the most common arts. They proceed exactly as their ancestors in ambition have done before them. Trace them through all their artifices and frauds and violences, and you will find nothing at all that is new. They follow precedents and examples with the punctilious exactness of a pleader. They never depart an inch from the authentic formulas of tyranny and usurpation. But in all the regulations that touch the public good the spirit has been the very reverse of this. There they commit the whole to the mercy of untried speculations; they abandon the dearest interests of the public to loose theories that none of them would choose to trust the slightest of his own private concerns to. They make this difference because in their desire to obtain and secure power they are thoroughly in earnest, and there they travel by the beaten road. The public interests, about which they have no real anxiety, they abandon wholly to chance — and I say to chance, because there is nothing in experience to prove that their schemes tend to any benefit.

We must always look with a pity not unmixed with respect on the errors of men who are timid and doubtful of themselves on points where the happiness of mankind is concerned. But in these gentlemen there is nothing of the tender parental anxiety that shrinks from cutting up the infant for the sake of an experiment. In the vastness of their promises and the confidence of their predictions they far outdo all the boasting of quacks. The arrogance of their pretensions in a manner provokes and challenges us to inquire into their foundation.

I am convinced that there are men of considerable parts among the popular leaders in the National Assembly. Some of them display eloquence in their speeches and their writings. That cannot exist without powerful and cultivated talents. But eloquence may exist without a proportionate degree of wisdom. When I speak of ability I am obliged to distinguish. What they have done to support their system speaks of no ordinary men. In the system itself, taken as the scheme of a republic constructed to procure the prosperity and security of the citizen and to promote the strength and grandeur of the state, I confess I am unable to find anything that shows, in a single instance, the work of a comprehensive and disposing mind, or even the provisions of a common prudence. Everywhere their purpose seems to have been to evade and slip aside from difficulty. It has been the glory of the great masters in every art to confront difficulty and overcome it, and, having overcome the first difficulty, to turn it into an instrument for new conquests over new difficulties — so enabling themselves to extend the empire of their science and even to push forward the landmarks of the human understanding itself beyond the reach of their original thoughts. Difficulty is a severe instructor, set over us by the supreme ordinance of a parental Guardian and Legislator, who knows us better than we know ourselves, and who loves us better too. Pater ipse colendi haud facilem esse viam voluit — the Father himself willed that the way of cultivation should not be easy. He who wrestles with us strengthens our nerves and sharpens our skill. Our antagonist is our helper. This friendly conflict with difficulty obliges us to become intimately acquainted with our object and compels us to consider it in all its relations. It will not let us be superficial. It is the want of nerve in the understanding for such a task, it is the degenerate fondness for tricky short cuts and little deceptive facilities, that has created governments with arbitrary powers in so many parts of the world. It created the late arbitrary monarchy of France. It has created the arbitrary republic of Paris. With such men, defects in wisdom are to be supplied by fullness of force. They gain nothing by it. Beginning their labours on a principle of sloth, they have the common fortune of slothful men. The difficulties they had eluded rather than escaped meet them again on their way; they multiply and thicken on them; they are entangled, through a labyrinth of confused detail, in an industry without limit and without direction; and in the end the whole of their work turns out feeble, vicious and insecure.

It is this inability to wrestle with difficulty that has obliged the arbitrary Assembly of France to begin their schemes of reform with abolition and total destruction. But is it in destroying and pulling down that skill is displayed? Your mob can do that at least as well as your assemblies. The shallowest understanding and the rudest hand are more than equal to that task. Rage and frenzy will pull down more in half an hour than prudence, deliberation and foresight can build up in a hundred years. The errors and defects of old establishments are visible and palpable. It calls for little ability to point them out; and where absolute power is given, a single word is all it takes to abolish the vice and the establishment together. The same lazy but restless disposition, which loves sloth and hates quiet, directs these politicians when they come to the work of supplying the place of what they have destroyed. To make everything the reverse of what they have seen is quite as easy as to destroy. No difficulties arise in what has never been tried. Criticism is almost baffled in discovering the defects of what has not existed; and eager enthusiasm and cheating hope have the whole wide field of imagination to range in with little or no opposition.

Footnote: A leading member of the Assembly, M. Rabaut de St Étienne, has expressed the principle of all their proceedings as clearly as possible; nothing could be simpler: "Tous les établissemens en France couronnent le malheur du peuple: pour le rendre heureux, il faut le renouveler, changer ses idées, changer ses loix, changer ses mœurs, ... changer les hommes, changer les choses, changer ses mots, ... tout détruire; oui, tout détruire; puisque tout est à recréer." In English: every establishment in France crowns the misery of the people; to make them happy it must be made new — change their ideas, change their laws, change their manners, change the men, change the things, change the words, destroy everything; yes, destroy everything, since everything must be created again. This gentleman was chosen president of an assembly that does not sit at the Quinze-Vingts or the Petites Maisons — the Paris hospital for the blind and the madhouse — and that is composed of persons giving themselves out to be rational beings; but neither his ideas nor his language nor his conduct differ in the smallest degree from the speeches, opinions and actions of those, inside the Assembly and outside it, who direct the machine now at work in France.

To preserve and to reform at the same time is quite another thing. When the useful parts of an old establishment are kept, and whatever is added has to be fitted to what is retained, then a vigorous mind, steady and persevering attention, various powers of comparison and combination, and the resources of an understanding fruitful in expedients must all be exercised — and exercised in a continued conflict with the combined force of opposite vices: with the obstinacy that rejects all improvement and the levity that is fatigued and disgusted with everything it possesses. But you may object: "A process of this kind is slow. It is not fit for an Assembly that glories in performing the work of ages in a few months. Such a way of reforming might possibly take many years." Without question it might, and it ought. It is one of the excellences of a method that has time among its assistants that its operation is slow and in some cases almost imperceptible. If circumspection and caution are a part of wisdom when we work only on inanimate matter, surely they become a part of duty as well when the subject of our demolition and construction is not brick and timber but sentient beings, multitudes of whom may be made miserable by a sudden alteration of their state and condition and habits. But it seems to be the prevailing opinion in Paris that an unfeeling heart and an undoubting confidence are the only qualifications for a perfect legislator. My ideas of that high office are very different. The true lawgiver ought to have a heart full of sensibility. He ought to love and respect his kind, and to fear himself. His temperament may be allowed to catch his ultimate object at an intuitive glance, but his movements towards it ought to be deliberate. Political arrangement, being a work for social ends, can only be wrought by social means. There mind must conspire with mind. Time is required to produce that union of minds which alone can produce all the good we aim at. Our patience will achieve more than our force. If I might venture to appeal to something so much out of fashion in Paris — I mean experience — I should tell you that in my course I have known, and according to my measure have worked alongside, great men, and I have never yet seen a plan that was not improved by the observations of people much inferior in understanding to the man who took the lead in the business. By a slow but well-sustained progress the effect of each step is watched; the success or failure of the first gives us light for the second; and so, from light to light, we are conducted safely through the whole series. We see that the parts of the system do not clash. The evils latent in the most promising contrivances are provided for as they arise. One advantage is sacrificed to another as little as possible. We compensate, we reconcile, we balance. We are enabled to unite into a consistent whole the various anomalies and contending principles that are found in the minds and the affairs of men. From this arises not an excellence in simplicity but one far superior, an excellence in composition. Where the great interests of mankind are concerned through a long succession of generations, that succession ought to be admitted to some share in the councils that are to affect it so deeply. If justice requires this, the work itself requires the help of more minds than one age can furnish. It is from this view of things that the best legislators have often been satisfied with establishing some sure, solid, ruling principle in government — a power like the one some of the philosophers have called a plastic nature — and, having fixed the principle, have afterwards left it to its own operation.

To proceed in this manner, that is, with a presiding principle and a prolific energy, is to my mind the criterion of profound wisdom. What your politicians take for the marks of a bold, hardy genius are only proofs of a deplorable want of ability. By their violent haste and their defiance of the process of nature, they are delivered over blindly to every projector and adventurer, to every alchemist and quack. They despair of turning anything common to account. Diet is nothing in their system of remedies. The worst of it is that this despair of curing common disorders by regular methods arises not only from a defect of comprehension but, I fear, from some malignity of disposition. Your legislators seem to have taken their opinions of all professions, ranks and offices from the declamations and buffooneries of satirists, who would themselves be astonished if they were held to the letter of their own descriptions. By listening only to these, your leaders regard everything only on the side of its vices and faults, and view those vices and faults under every colour of exaggeration. It is undoubtedly true, though it may seem paradoxical, that in general those who are habitually employed in finding and displaying faults are unqualified for the work of reformation, because their minds are not only unfurnished with patterns of the fair and the good, but by habit they come to take no delight in contemplating such things. By hating vices too much they come to love men too little. It is therefore no wonder that they should be disinclined and unable to serve them. From this arises the constitutional disposition of some of your guides to pull everything to pieces. At this malicious game they display the whole of their quadrimanous activity. As for the rest: the paradoxes of eloquent writers, brought out purely as a sport of fancy to try their talents, rouse attention and excite surprise, are taken up by these gentlemen not in the spirit of the original authors, as a means of cultivating their taste and improving their style — these paradoxes become with them serious grounds of action on which they proceed to regulate the most important concerns of the state. Cicero describes Cato comically as trying to act in the commonwealth on the school paradoxes that exercised the wits of junior students of Stoic philosophy. If that was true of Cato, these gentlemen copy him in the manner of certain people who lived about his time — pede nudo Catonem, playing Cato by going barefoot. Mr Hume told me that he had the secret of Rousseau's principles of composition from Rousseau himself. That acute though eccentric observer had perceived that to strike and interest the public, the marvellous must be produced; that the marvellous of the heathen mythology had long since lost its effect; that the giants, magicians, fairies and heroes of romance which succeeded it had exhausted the portion of credulity belonging to their age; and that nothing was now left to a writer but the species of the marvellous which could still be produced, and with as great an effect as ever, though in another way — that is, the marvellous in life, in manners, in characters and in extraordinary situations, giving rise to new and unlooked-for strokes in politics and morals. I believe that if Rousseau were alive and in one of his lucid intervals, he would be shocked at the practical frenzy of his pupils, who are servile imitators in their paradoxes and reveal an implicit faith even in their unbelief.

Men who undertake considerable things, even in a regular way, ought to give us grounds for presuming ability. But the physician of the state who is not satisfied with curing disorders and undertakes to regenerate constitutions ought to display uncommon powers. Some very unusual appearances of wisdom ought to show themselves on the face of the designs of men who appeal to no practice and copy no model. Have any such appeared? I shall take a view — and for the subject it will be a very short one — of what the Assembly has done with regard, first, to the constitution of the legislature; next to that of the executive power; then to that of the judicature; afterwards to the model of the army; and I shall conclude with the system of finance: to see whether we can discover in any part of their schemes the portentous ability that might justify these bold undertakers in the superiority they assume over mankind.

It is in the model of the sovereign and presiding part of this new republic that we should expect their grand display. Here they had to prove their title to their proud demands. For the plan itself at large, and for the reasons it is grounded on, I refer to the journals of the Assembly of 29 September 1789 and to the later proceedings that have made any alterations in it. So far as I can see light in a matter that is somewhat confused, the system remains substantially as it was originally framed. My few remarks will concern its spirit, its tendency, and its fitness for framing a popular commonwealth — which is what they profess theirs to be — suited to the ends for which any commonwealth, and particularly such a commonwealth, is made. At the same time I mean to consider whether it is consistent with itself and with its own principles.

Old establishments are tried by their effects. If the people are happy, united, wealthy and powerful, we presume the rest. We conclude that to be good from which good is derived. In old establishments various correctives have been found for their departures from theory. Indeed they are the results of various necessities and expediences. They are not often constructed after any theory; theories are rather drawn out of them. In them we often see the end best obtained where the means do not seem perfectly reconcilable to what we imagine was the original scheme. The means taught by experience may be better suited to political ends than those contrived in the original project. They react in turn on the primitive constitution, and sometimes improve the very design they seem to have departed from. I think all this might be curiously illustrated in the British constitution. At worst, the errors and deviations of every kind in the reckoning are found and computed, and the ship proceeds on her course. That is the case with old establishments; but in a new and merely theoretical system, every contrivance is expected to show on the face of it that it answers its ends, especially when the projectors are in no way embarrassed by any effort to fit the new building to an old one, either in the walls or in the foundations.

The French builders, clearing away as mere rubbish everything they found, and, like their ornamental gardeners, forming everything into an exact level, propose to rest the whole local and general legislature on three bases of three different kinds — one geometrical, one arithmetical and the third financial. The first they call the basis of territory, the second the basis of population, and the third the basis of contribution. To accomplish the first of these purposes they divide the area of their country into eighty-three pieces, regularly square, of eighteen leagues by eighteen. These large divisions are called Departments. These they portion out, still by square measurement, into seventeen hundred and twenty districts called Communes. These again they subdivide, still by square measurement, into smaller districts called Cantons, making 6,400 in all.

At first view this geometrical basis of theirs presents nothing much to admire or to blame. It calls for no great legislative talents. Nothing more than an accurate land surveyor with his chain and sight and theodolite is needed for a plan like this. In the old divisions of the country, various accidents from time to time, and the ebb and flow of various properties and jurisdictions, settled the boundaries. Those boundaries were undoubtedly not made on any fixed system. They were subject to some inconveniences, but they were inconveniences for which use had found remedies and habit had supplied accommodation and patience. In this new pavement of square within square, and this organisation and semi-organisation made on the system of Empedocles and Buffon rather than on any political principle, it is impossible that innumerable local inconveniences, which men are not used to, should not arise. But I pass over these, because specifying them requires an accurate knowledge of the country which I do not possess.

When these state surveyors came to look over their work of measurement, they soon found that in politics the most fallacious of all things was geometrical demonstration. They then had recourse to another basis, or rather buttress, to support the building that was tottering on that false foundation. It was evident that the goodness of the soil, the number of the people, their wealth and the largeness of their contribution made such infinite variations between square and square as to make measurement a ridiculous standard of power in the commonwealth, and equality in geometry the most unequal of all measures in the distribution of men. However, they could not give it up; but dividing their political and civil representation into three parts, they allotted one of those parts to square measurement, without a single fact or calculation to establish whether this territorial proportion of representation was fairly assigned, or ought on any principle really to be a third. Having given geometry this portion, a third for her dowry, out of compliment, I suppose, to that sublime science, they left the other two to be scrambled for between the other parts, population and contribution.

When they came to provide for population, they were not able to proceed quite so smoothly as they had done in the field of their geometry. Here their arithmetic came up against their juridical metaphysics. Had they stuck to their metaphysical principles, the arithmetical process would have been simple indeed. Men, with them, are strictly equal and are entitled to equal rights in their own government. On this system each head would have its vote, and every man would vote directly for the person who was to represent him in the legislature. "But softly — by regular degrees, not yet." This metaphysical principle, to which law, custom, usage, policy and reason were all to yield, is to yield itself to their pleasure. There must be many degrees, and some stages, before the representative can come into contact with his constituent. Indeed, as we shall soon see, these two persons are to have no communion with each other at all. First, the voters in the Canton, who compose what they call primary assemblies, are to have a qualification. What! A qualification on the indefeasible rights of men? Yes, but it shall be a very small qualification. Our injustice shall be very little oppressive: only the local valuation of three days' labour paid to the public. Why, this is not much, I readily admit, for anything but the utter subversion of your equalising principle.
