As a qualification it might as well be dropped, for it answers none of the purposes qualifications are established for; and on your own ideas it excludes from a vote the very man whose natural equality most needs protection and defence — I mean the man who has nothing but his natural equality to guard him. You order him to buy the right which you had told him nature gave him free at his birth, and of which no authority on earth could lawfully deprive him. As against the man who cannot come up to your market, a tyrannous aristocracy is established at the very outset, and by you who pretend to be its sworn enemy.

The gradation goes on. These primary assemblies of the Canton elect deputies to the Commune, one for every two hundred qualified inhabitants. Here is the first medium interposed between the primary elector and the representative legislator, and here a new turnpike is set up for taxing the rights of men with a second qualification: for nobody can be elected to the Commune who does not pay the value of ten days' labour. Nor have we finished yet. There is still another gradation. These Communes, chosen by the Canton, elect to the Department; and the deputies of the Department choose their deputies to the National Assembly. Here is a third barrier of senseless qualification. Every deputy to the National Assembly must pay, in direct contribution, the value of a mark of silver. Of all these qualifying barriers we must think alike: that they are impotent to secure independence and strong only to destroy the rights of men.

Footnote: The Assembly, in carrying out their committee's plan, made some alterations. They have struck out one stage in these gradations, which removes part of the objection; but the main objection — namely, that in their scheme the first constituent voter has no connection with the representative legislator — remains in all its force. There are other alterations, some possibly for the better and some certainly for the worse; but to the author the merit or demerit of these smaller alterations seems of no moment, where the scheme itself is fundamentally vicious and absurd.

In all this process, which in its fundamental elements professes to consider only population on a principle of natural right, there is a manifest attention to property — which, however just and reasonable it may be on other schemes, is on theirs perfectly indefensible.

When they come to their third basis, that of Contribution, we find that they have lost sight of the rights of men still more completely. This last basis rests entirely on property. A principle totally different from the equality of men, and utterly irreconcilable with it, is thereby admitted; but no sooner is this principle admitted than, as usual, it is subverted — and it is not subverted, as we shall see presently, in order to bring the inequality of riches nearer to the level of nature. The additional share in the third portion of representation, a portion reserved exclusively for the higher contribution, is made to regard only the district and not the individuals in it who pay. It is easy to see, from the course of their reasonings, how much they were embarrassed by their contradictory ideas of the rights of men and the privileges of riches. The Committee of Constitution as good as admit that the two are wholly irreconcilable. "The relation with regard to the contributions is without doubt null," they say, "when the question is about the balance of political rights as between individual and individual, without which personal equality would be destroyed and an aristocracy of the rich would be established. But this inconvenience entirely disappears when the proportional relation of the contribution is considered only in the great masses and is solely between province and province; in that case it serves only to form a just reciprocal proportion between the cities, without affecting the personal rights of the citizens."

Here the principle of contribution, taken between man and man, is condemned as null and destructive of equality — and as pernicious too, because it leads to establishing an aristocracy of the rich. Yet it must not be abandoned. And the way of getting round the difficulty is to establish the inequality between department and department, leaving all the individuals in each department on an exact par. Observe that this parity between individuals had already been destroyed when the qualifications within the departments were settled; nor does it seem a matter of great importance whether the equality of men is injured in masses or individually. An individual is not of the same importance in a mass represented by a few as in a mass represented by many. It would be too much to tell a man jealous of his equality that the elector who votes for three members has the same franchise as the one who votes for ten.

Now take it from the other point of view, and let us suppose their principle of representation according to contribution — that is, according to riches — to be well imagined, and a necessary basis for their republic. In this third basis they assume that riches ought to be respected, and that justice and policy require that riches should entitle men in some way or other to a larger share in the administration of public affairs. It is now to be seen how the Assembly provides for the pre-eminence, or even the security, of the rich, by conferring on their district, in virtue of their wealth, that larger measure of power which is denied to them personally. I readily admit — indeed I should lay it down as a fundamental principle — that in a republican government with a democratic basis the rich do require an additional security beyond what they need in monarchies. They are subject to envy, and through envy to oppression. On the present scheme it is impossible to guess what advantage they get from the aristocratic preference on which the unequal representation of the masses is founded. The rich cannot feel it either as a support to their dignity or as a security to their fortune, for the aristocratic mass is generated from purely democratic principles, and the prevalence given to it in the general representation has no sort of reference to, or connection with, the persons on account of whose property this superiority of the mass is established. If the contrivers of this scheme meant any sort of favour to the rich in consequence of their contribution, they ought to have conferred the privilege either on the individual rich or on some class formed of rich persons, as historians represent Servius Tullius to have done in the early constitution of Rome; because the contest between the rich and the poor is not a struggle between corporation and corporation but a contest between men and men — a competition not between districts but between descriptions of people. The scheme would answer its purpose better if it were inverted: if the votes of the masses were made equal and the votes within each mass were proportioned to property.

Let us suppose one man in a district — it is an easy supposition — to contribute as much as a hundred of his neighbours. Against them he has only one vote. If there were only one representative for the mass, his poor neighbours would outvote him a hundred to one for that single representative. Bad enough. But amends are to be made to him. How? The district, in virtue of his wealth, is to choose, say, ten members instead of one; that is to say, by paying a very large contribution he has the happiness of being outvoted a hundred to one by the poor for ten representatives instead of being outvoted in exactly the same proportion for a single member. In truth, instead of benefiting from this larger quantity of representation, the rich man is subjected to an additional hardship. The increase of representation within his province sets up nine more persons, and as many more than nine as there may be democratic candidates, to cabal and intrigue and flatter the people at his expense and to his oppression. By this means an interest is held out to multitudes of the lower sort in obtaining a salary of eighteen livres a day — to them a vast object — besides the pleasure of living in Paris and their share in the government of the kingdom. The more the objects of ambition are multiplied and made democratic, in just that proportion the rich are endangered.

So it must go between the poor and the rich in the province deemed aristocratic, which in its internal relations is the very reverse of that character. In its external relations, that is in its relation to the other provinces, I cannot see how the unequal representation given to masses on account of wealth becomes the means of preserving the equipoise and the tranquillity of the commonwealth. For if it is one of the objects to secure the weak from being crushed by the strong, as in all society it undoubtedly is, how are the smaller and poorer of these masses to be saved from the tyranny of the wealthier? Is it by giving the wealthy further and more systematic means of oppressing them? When we come to a balance of representation between corporate bodies, provincial interests and rivalries and jealousies are quite as likely to arise among them as among individuals, and their divisions are likely to produce a much hotter spirit of dissension and something coming much nearer to a war.

I see that these aristocratic masses are formed on what is called the principle of direct contribution. Nothing could be a more unequal standard than this. Indirect contribution, which arises from duties on consumption, is in truth a better standard, and follows and discovers wealth more naturally than direct contribution does. It is difficult, indeed, to fix a standard of local preference on account of the one, or the other, or both, because some provinces may pay more of either or of both on account of causes that are not intrinsic but arise from the very districts over which they have gained a preference in consequence of their apparent contribution. If the masses were independent sovereign bodies who were to provide for a federal treasury by separate contingents, and if the revenue did not, as it does, include many impositions running through the whole, which affect men individually and not corporately and which by their nature confound all territorial limits, then something might be said for the basis of contribution founded on masses. But of all things, this representation measured by contribution is the most difficult to settle on principles of equity in a country that regards its districts as members of a whole. For a great city such as Bordeaux or Paris appears to pay a vast body of duties, almost out of all assignable proportion to other places, and its mass is reckoned accordingly. But are these cities the true contributors in that proportion? No. The consumers of the goods imported into Bordeaux, who are scattered all over France, pay the import duties of Bordeaux. The produce of the vintage in Guienne and Languedoc gives that city the means of a contribution that grows out of an export trade. The landholders who spend their estates in Paris, and are thereby the creators of that city, contribute for Paris out of the provinces their revenues arise in. Very nearly the same arguments apply to the representative share given on account of direct contribution, because direct contribution must be assessed on wealth, real or presumed, and that local wealth will itself arise from causes that are not local and which therefore in equity ought not to produce a local preference.

It is very remarkable that in this fundamental regulation, which settles the representation of the mass on direct contribution, they have not yet settled how that direct contribution is to be laid or how apportioned. Perhaps there is some hidden policy in this strange procedure, tending to keep the present Assembly in being. However, until they do settle it they can have no certain constitution. It must depend in the end on the system of taxation, and must vary with every variation in that system. As they have contrived matters, their taxation does not depend so much on their constitution as their constitution on their taxation. This must introduce great confusion among the masses, just as the variable qualification for votes within the district must, if ever real contested elections take place, cause endless internal controversy.

Comparing the three bases together, not on their political reason but on the ideas the Assembly works from, and testing its consistency with itself, we cannot help observing that the principle the committee call the basis of population does not begin to operate from the same point as the two other principles, called the bases of territory and of contribution, which are both aristocratic in nature. The consequence is that where all three begin to operate together, the working of the first on the two others produces the most absurd inequality. Every canton contains four square leagues and is reckoned to contain on average 4,000 inhabitants, or 680 voters in the primary assemblies, which vary in numbers with the population of the canton and send one deputy to the commune for every 200 voters. Nine cantons make a commune.

Now let us take a canton containing a seaport town of trade or a great manufacturing town. Let us suppose the population of this canton to be 12,700 inhabitants, or 2,193 voters, forming three primary assemblies and sending ten deputies to the commune.

Set against this one canton two others of the remaining eight in the same commune. We may suppose these to have their fair population of 4,000 inhabitants and 680 voters each, or 8,000 inhabitants and 1,360 voters between them. These will form only two primary assemblies and send only six deputies to the commune.

When the assembly of the commune comes to vote on the basis of territory, which is the first principle admitted to operate in that assembly, the single canton, which has half the territory of the other two, will have ten voices to six in electing three deputies to the assembly of the department — deputies chosen on the express ground of representing territory. Striking as this inequality is, it will be aggravated still further if we suppose, as we fairly may, that the several other cantons of the commune fall proportionally short of the average population by as much as the principal canton exceeds it.

Now for the basis of contribution, which is also a principle admitted to operate first in the assembly of the commune. Let us again take one canton such as the one described above. If the whole of the direct contributions paid by a great trading or manufacturing town is divided equally among the inhabitants, each individual will be found to pay much more than an individual living in the country on the same average. The whole paid by the inhabitants of the first will be more than the whole paid by the inhabitants of the second — we may fairly assume a third more. Then the 12,700 inhabitants, or 2,193 voters, of the canton will pay as much as 19,050 inhabitants, or 3,289 voters, of the other cantons, which is nearly the estimated proportion of inhabitants and voters of five other cantons. Now the 2,193 voters will, as I said before, send only ten deputies to the assembly; the 3,289 voters will send sixteen. So for an equal share in the contribution of the whole commune there will be a difference of sixteen voices to ten in voting for deputies who are to be chosen on the principle of representing the general contribution of the whole commune.

By the same mode of computation we shall find that 15,875 inhabitants, or 2,741 voters, of the other cantons, who pay one sixth less towards the contribution of the whole commune, will have three voices more than the 12,700 inhabitants, or 2,193 voters, of the one canton.

Such is the fantastical and unjust inequality between mass and mass in this curious apportionment of the rights of representation arising out of territory and contribution. The qualifications these confer are in truth negative qualifications, which give a right in inverse proportion to the possession of them.

In this whole contrivance of the three bases, consider it in whatever light you please, I do not see a variety of objects reconciled in one consistent whole, but several contradictory principles reluctantly and irreconcilably brought together and held together by your philosophers, like wild beasts shut up in a cage to claw and bite each other to their mutual destruction.

I am afraid I have gone too far into their way of considering the formation of a constitution. They have much metaphysics, but bad; much geometry, but bad; much proportional arithmetic, but false. Yet even if it were all as exact as metaphysics and geometry and arithmetic ought to be, and if their schemes were perfectly consistent in all their parts, it would only make a fairer and more sightly vision. It is remarkable that in a great arrangement of mankind not one reference whatever is to be found to anything moral or anything political — nothing that relates to the concerns, the actions, the passions, the interests of men. Hominem non sapiunt — they have no savour of the human being.

You see that I am considering this constitution only as electoral, leading by steps to the National Assembly. I do not go into the internal government of the departments and their genealogy through the communes and cantons. In the original plan these local governments are to be composed as nearly as possible in the same manner and on the same principles as the elective assemblies. Each of them is a body perfectly compact and rounded in itself.

You cannot fail to see that this scheme has a direct and immediate tendency to sever France into a variety of republics and to make them totally independent of each other, with no direct constitutional means of coherence, connection or subordination except what may come from their acquiescence in the decisions of the general congress of ambassadors from each independent republic. That is what the National Assembly is in reality; and such governments, I admit, do exist in the world, though in forms infinitely better suited to the local and habitual circumstances of their people. But such associations, which are hardly bodies politic at all, have generally been the effect of necessity and not of choice; and I believe the present French power is the very first body of citizens who, having obtained full authority to do what they pleased with their country, have chosen to dismember it in this barbarous manner.

It is impossible not to observe that in the spirit of this geometrical distribution and arithmetical arrangement these pretended citizens treat France exactly like a conquered country. Acting as conquerors, they have imitated the policy of the harshest of that harsh breed. The policy of such barbarous victors, who despise a subdued people and insult their feelings, has always been to destroy every vestige of the ancient country so far as they could — in religion, in government, in laws and in manners; to confound all territorial limits; to produce a general poverty; to put their properties up to auction; to crush their princes, nobles and pontiffs; to lay low everything that had lifted its head above the level, or that could serve to combine or rally the disbanded people in their distresses under the standard of old opinion. They have made France free in the way that those sincere friends of the rights of mankind, the Romans, freed Greece and Macedon and other nations. They destroyed the bonds of their union under colour of providing for the independence of each of their cities.

When the members who compose these new bodies of cantons and communes and departments — arrangements deliberately produced through the medium of confusion — begin to act, they will find themselves in great measure strangers to one another. The electors and the elected throughout, especially in the rural cantons, will frequently be without any civil habits or connections, or any of that natural discipline which is the soul of a true republic. Magistrates and collectors of revenue are now no longer acquainted with their districts, nor bishops with their dioceses, nor parish priests with their parishes. These new colonies of the rights of men bear a strong resemblance to the sort of military colonies Tacitus remarked on in the declining policy of Rome. In better and wiser days the Romans, whatever course they took with foreign nations, were careful to make the elements of a methodical subordination and settlement grow up together, and even to lay the foundations of discipline within the military. But when all the good arts had fallen into ruin, they proceeded, as your Assembly does, on the equality of men, and with as little judgment and as little care for the things that make a republic tolerable or durable. In this as in almost every instance your new commonwealth is born and bred and fed in the corruptions that mark degenerate and worn-out republics. Your child comes into the world with the symptoms of death on it; the facies Hippocratica forms the character of its face and the prognosis of its fate.

Footnote: "Non, ut olim, universæ legiones deducebantur, cum tribunis, et centurionibus, et sui cujusque ordinis militibus, ut consensu et caritate rempublicam efficerent; sed ignoti inter se, diversis manipulis, sine rectore, sine affectibus mutuis, quasi ex alio genere mortalium repente in unum collecti, numerus magis quam colonia." In English: whole legions were no longer settled together, as they once were, with their tribunes and centurions and the soldiers of each rank, so as to make a commonwealth by common consent and mutual affection; instead they were strangers to one another, drawn from different companies, with no leader and no mutual attachments, as though suddenly collected into one place out of some other race of men — a number rather than a colony. — Tacitus, Annals, book 14, section 27. All this will apply still better to the unconnected, rotating, biennial national assemblies in this absurd and senseless constitution.

The legislators who framed the ancient republics knew that their business was too arduous to be accomplished with no better apparatus than the metaphysics of an undergraduate and the mathematics and arithmetic of an exciseman. They had to deal with men, and they were obliged to study human nature. They had to deal with citizens, and they were obliged to study the effects of the habits communicated by the circumstances of civil life. They were aware that the operation of this second nature on the first produced a new combination, and that from this arose many diversities among men according to their birth, their education, their professions, the periods of their lives, their residence in town or country, their several ways of acquiring and settling property, and the quality of that property itself — all of which made them, as it were, so many different species of animal. From this they thought themselves obliged to sort their citizens into such classes, and to place them in such situations in the state, as their particular habits qualified them to fill, and to allot them such appropriate privileges as would secure them what their specific occasions required, and would furnish each description with the force to protect it in the conflict caused by the diversity of interests that must exist and must contend in every complex society. For the legislator would have been ashamed that the coarse husbandman should know well how to sort and use his sheep and horses and oxen, and should have common sense enough not to abstract and equalise them all into animals without providing each kind with its appropriate food and care and employment — while he, the economist and disposer and shepherd of his own kindred, subliming himself into an airy metaphysician, was determined to know nothing of his flocks except as men in general. It is for this reason that Montesquieu observed, very justly, that the great legislators of antiquity made their greatest display of power in their classification of the citizens, and even soared above themselves there. It is here that your modern legislators have gone deep into the negative series and sunk below even their own nothing. Where the first sort of legislators attended to the different kinds of citizens and combined them into one commonwealth, the others, the metaphysical and alchemical legislators, have taken exactly the contrary course. They have tried to confound all sorts of citizens, as far as they could, into one homogeneous mass, and then they divided this amalgam of theirs into a number of incoherent republics. They reduce men to loose counters, merely for the sake of simple counting, and not to figures, whose power is to come from their place in the table. The elements of their own metaphysics might have taught them better lessons. The roll of their own table of categories might have informed them that there was something else in the intellectual world besides substance and quantity. They might learn from the catechism of metaphysics that there are eight more heads in every complex deliberation which they have never thought of — though these, of all the ten, are the ones on which the skill of man can operate at all.

Footnote: Qualitas, Relatio, Actio, Passio, Ubi, Quando, Situs, Habitus. (Quality, relation, action, passion, place, time, position, state.)

So far from following that able arrangement of some of the old republican legislators, which follows the moral conditions and propensities of men with a solicitous accuracy, they have levelled and crushed together all the orders they found even under the coarse, unartificial arrangement of the monarchy — a form of government in which the classing of the citizens is not of so much importance as it is in a republic. It is true, however, that every such classification, if properly ordered, is good in all forms of government, and forms a strong barrier against the excesses of despotism as well as being the necessary means of giving effect and permanence to a republic. For want of something of this kind, if the present project of a republic should fail, all securities for a moderated freedom fail along with it, and all the indirect restraints that mitigate despotism are removed — so much so that if monarchy should ever regain an entire ascendancy in France, under this or any other dynasty, it will probably be the most completely arbitrary power that has ever appeared on earth, unless it is voluntarily tempered at the outset by the wise and virtuous counsels of the prince. This is to play a most desperate game.

They even declare that the confusion attending all such proceedings is one of their objects, and they hope to secure their constitution by a terror of the return of the evils that attended the making of it. "By this," they say, "its destruction will become difficult for authority, which cannot break it up without the entire disorganisation of the whole state." They presume that if this authority ever came to the same degree of power that they have acquired, it would make a more moderate and chastened use of it, and would piously tremble at entirely disorganising the state in the savage manner they have done themselves. They expect from the virtues of a returning despotism the security that is to be enjoyed by the offspring of their own popular vices.

I wish, Sir, that you and my readers would read M. de Calonne's work on this subject attentively. It is not only eloquent but an able and instructive performance. I confine myself to what he says about the constitution of the new state and the condition of the revenue. As for this minister's disputes with his rivals, I do not wish to pronounce on them. As little do I mean to hazard any opinion about his ways and means, financial or political, for taking his country out of its present disgraceful and deplorable condition of servitude, anarchy, bankruptcy and beggary. I cannot speculate quite so sanguinely as he does; but he is a Frenchman, and has a closer duty in these matters and better means of judging them than I can have. I hope that the formal avowal he refers to, made by one of the principal leaders in the Assembly, about the tendency of their scheme to bring France not only from a monarchy to a republic but from a republic to a mere confederacy, will be very particularly attended to. It adds new force to my observations; and indeed M. de Calonne's work supplies my deficiencies with many new and striking arguments on most of the subjects of this letter.

Footnote: See l'État de la France, page 363.

It is this determination to break their country into separate republics that has driven them into the greatest number of their difficulties and contradictions. If it were not for this, all the questions of exact equality, and these balances of individual rights and population and contribution that can never be settled, would be wholly useless. Representation, though drawn from the parts, would be a duty that regarded the whole equally. Each deputy to the Assembly would be the representative of France and of all its descriptions of people — of the many and the few, of the rich and the poor, of the great districts and the small. All these districts would themselves be subordinate to some standing authority existing independently of them, an authority in which their representation and everything belonging to it originated and to which it was directed. This standing, unalterable, fundamental government would make that territory truly and properly a whole, and it is the only thing that could. With us, when we elect popular representatives, we send them to a council in which each man individually is a subject, submitted to a government complete in all its ordinary functions. With you the elective Assembly is the sovereign and the sole sovereign, and all the members are therefore integral parts of that sole sovereignty. With us it is totally different. With us the representative, separated from the other parts, can have no action and no existence. The government is the point of reference for the several members and districts of our representation. This is the centre of our unity. This government of reference is a trustee for the whole and not for the parts. So is the other branch of our public council: I mean the House of Lords. With us the king and the Lords are separate and joint securities for the equality of each district, each province, each city. When did you ever hear in Great Britain of a province suffering from the inequality of its representation? Of a district that had no representation at all? Not only do our monarchy and our peerage secure the equality on which our unity depends, but so does the spirit of the House of Commons itself. The very inequality of representation that is so foolishly complained of is perhaps the very thing that prevents us from thinking or acting as members for districts. Cornwall elects as many members as all Scotland. But is Cornwall better taken care of than Scotland? Few people trouble their heads about any of your bases, outside a few giddy clubs. Most of those who wish for any change on any plausible grounds desire it on different ideas altogether.

Your new constitution is the very reverse of ours in its principle, and I am astonished that anyone could dream of holding up anything done in it as an example for Great Britain. With you there is little, or rather no, connection between the last representative and the first constituent. The member who goes to the National Assembly is not chosen by the people, nor accountable to them. There are three elections before he is chosen; two sets of magistracy come between him and the primary assembly, so as to make him, as I have said, the ambassador of a state and not the representative of the people within a state. This changes the whole spirit of the election; nor can any corrective your constitution-mongers have devised make him anything other than what he is. The very attempt to do it would inevitably introduce a confusion more horrid, if possible, than the present one. There is no way of making a connection between the original constituent and the representative except by the roundabout means that would lead the candidate to apply in the first instance to the primary electors, so that by their authoritative instructions — and perhaps something more — those primary electors might force the two succeeding bodies of electors to make a choice agreeable to their wishes. But this would plainly subvert the whole scheme. It would be to plunge them back into that tumult and confusion of popular election which their interposed gradations of election are meant to avoid, and in the end to risk the whole fortune of the state with those who have the least knowledge of it and the least interest in it. This is a perpetual dilemma into which they are thrown by the vicious, weak and contradictory principles they have chosen. Unless the people break up and level this gradation, it is plain that they do not substantially elect to the Assembly at all; indeed they elect as little in appearance as in reality.

What is it that we all look for in an election? To answer its real purposes you must first possess the means of knowing whether your man is fit, and then you must retain some hold on him by personal obligation or dependence. To what end are these primary electors complimented, or rather mocked, with a choice? They can never know anything about the qualities of the man who is to serve them, nor has he any obligation whatever to them. Of all the powers unfit to be delegated by those who have any real means of judging, the most peculiarly unfit is one that relates to a personal choice. In case of abuse, that body of primary electors can never call the representative to account for his conduct. He is too far removed from them in the chain of representation. If he behaves badly at the end of his two years' lease, it does not concern him for two years more. By the new French constitution the best and wisest representatives go into this Limbus Patrum equally with the worst. Their bottoms are presumed foul, and they must go into dock to be refitted. Every man who has served in an Assembly is ineligible for two years after. Just as these magistrates begin to learn their trade, like chimney sweeps, they are disqualified from exercising it. Superficial, new, petulant acquisition, and interrupted, dronish, broken, ill recollection, are to be the destined character of all your future governors. Your constitution has too much jealousy in it to have much sense. You consider the representative's breach of trust so exclusively that you do not regard the question of his fitness to execute it at all.

This purgatorial interval is not unfavourable to a faithless representative, who may be as good a canvasser as he was a bad governor. In this time he may intrigue his way into a superiority over the wisest and most virtuous. Since in the end all the members of this elective constitution are equally fugitive and exist only for the election, the people to whom he is to be responsible when he asks for a renewal of his trust may no longer be the same people who chose him. To call all the secondary electors of the commune to account is ridiculous, impracticable and unjust: they may themselves have been deceived in their choice, as the third set of electors, those of the department, may be in theirs. In your elections responsibility cannot exist.

Finding no sort of principle of coherence with one another in the nature and constitution of the several new republics of France, I considered what cement the legislators had provided for them out of any extraneous materials. I take no notice of their confederations, their spectacles, their civic feasts and their enthusiasm; they are nothing but mere tricks. But tracing their policy through their actions, I think I can make out the arrangements by which they propose to hold these republics together. The first is the confiscation, with the compulsory paper currency attached to it; the second is the supreme power of the city of Paris; the third is the general army of the state. I shall reserve what I have to say about the last until I come to consider the army as a head by itself.

As to the operation of the first, the confiscation and the paper currency, merely as a cement, I cannot deny that these two, each depending on the other, may for some time make a sort of cement — provided their madness and folly in managing it, and in tempering the parts together, does not produce a repulsion at the very outset. But allowing the scheme some coherence and some duration, it appears to me that if after a while the confiscation is not found sufficient to support the paper coinage, as I am morally certain it will not be, then instead of cementing it will add infinitely to the dissociation and distraction and confusion of these confederate republics, both in relation to each other and in the several parts within each of them. But if the confiscation should succeed so far as to sink the paper currency, the cement is gone with the circulation. Meanwhile its binding force will be very uncertain, and it will tighten or slacken with every variation in the credit of the paper.

Only one thing is certain in this scheme, an effect that is seemingly collateral but is, I have no doubt, direct in the minds of the men who conduct this business: its effect in producing an oligarchy in every one of the republics. A paper circulation not founded on any real money deposited or pledged, already amounting to forty-four millions of English money, and forcibly substituted for the coin of the kingdom, thereby becoming the substance of its revenue as well as the medium of all its commercial and civil intercourse, must put whatever power and authority and influence is left, in whatever form it may take, into the hands of the managers and conductors of that circulation.

In England we feel the influence of the Bank, though it is only the centre of a voluntary dealing. A man knows little indeed of the influence of money on mankind who does not see the force of the management of a moneyed concern so much more extensive than any of ours, and by its nature so much more dependent on its managers. But this is not merely a money concern. There is another member of the system inseparably connected with this money management. It consists in the means of drawing out portions of the confiscated lands for sale at discretion, and carrying on a process of continual transmutation of paper into land and land into paper. When we follow this process in its effects, we may conceive something of the intensity of the force with which this system must operate. By this means the spirit of money-jobbing and speculation goes into the mass of land itself and incorporates with it. By this kind of operation that species of property becomes, as it were, volatilised; it takes on an unnatural and monstrous activity, and thereby throws into the hands of the several managers, principal and subordinate, Parisian and provincial, all the representative of money and perhaps a full tenth part of all the land in France — land which has now acquired the worst and most pernicious part of the evil of a paper circulation, the greatest possible uncertainty in its value. They have reversed Latona's kindness to the landed property of Delos. They have sent theirs to be blown about like the light fragments of a wreck, oras et littora circum, round the shores and the coasts.

The new dealers, being all habitual adventurers with no fixed habits or local attachments, will buy in order to job out again, as the market in paper or in money or in land offers an advantage. For though a holy bishop thinks agriculture will derive great advantages from the "enlightened" usurers who are to buy the church confiscations, I, who am not a good farmer but an old one, beg leave with great humility to tell his late Lordship that usury is no tutor of agriculture; and if the word "enlightened" is to be understood according to the new dictionary, as it always is in your new schools, I cannot conceive how a man's not believing in God can teach him to cultivate the earth with the least additional skill or encouragement. "Diis immortalibus sero" — I sow for the immortal gods — said an old Roman as he held one handle of the plough while Death held the other. Though you were to add all the directors of the two Academies to the directors of the Caisse d'Escompte in the commission, an old experienced peasant is worth them all. I have got more information on a curious and interesting branch of husbandry in one short conversation with an old Carthusian monk than I have derived from all the bank directors I have ever talked with. There is no cause for alarm, however, at money dealers meddling with rural economy. These gentlemen are too wise in their generation. At first, perhaps, their tender and susceptible imaginations may be captivated with the innocent and unprofitable delights of a pastoral life; but in a little while they will find that agriculture is a trade much more laborious and much less lucrative than the one they had left. After delivering its panegyric, they will turn their backs on it like their great precursor and prototype. Like him, they may begin by singing "Beatus ille" — happy the man — but what will be the end?

	Hæc ubi locutus fœnerator Alphius,
	  Jam jam futurus rusticus,
	Omnem relegit Idibus pecuniam,
	  Quærit Calendis ponere.
	(When Alphius the moneylender had said all this, on the very point
	of becoming a countryman, he called in all his money on the Ides
	— and on the first of the month was looking to put it out again.)

They will cultivate the Caisse d'Église, under this prelate's sacred auspices, with much more profit than its vineyards and its cornfields. They will employ their talents according to their habits and their interests. They will not follow the plough while they can direct treasuries and govern provinces.

Your legislators, new in everything, are the very first to have founded a commonwealth on gaming and infused that spirit into it as its vital breath. The great object of these politics is to metamorphose France from a great kingdom into one great gaming table; to turn its inhabitants into a nation of gamblers; to make speculation as extensive as life and mix it with all life's concerns; and to divert the whole of the hopes and fears of the people out of their usual channels into the impulses and passions and superstitions of men who live on chances. They loudly proclaim their opinion that this present system of a republic cannot possibly exist without this kind of gaming fund, and that the very thread of its life is spun out of the staple of these speculations. The old gaming in the funds was mischievous enough, undoubtedly, but it was mischievous only to individuals. Even at its greatest extent, in the Mississippi and the South Sea, it affected comparatively few; and where it extends further, as in lotteries, the spirit has only a single object. But where the law, which in most circumstances forbids gaming and in none countenances it, is itself debauched so as to reverse its own nature and policy and expressly to force the subject to this destructive table, by bringing the spirit and symbols of gaming into the smallest matters and engaging everybody in it and in everything — there a more dreadful epidemic disease of that kind is spread than has yet appeared in the world. With you a man can neither earn nor buy his dinner without a speculation. What he receives in the morning will not have the same value at night. What he is compelled to take in payment of an old debt will not be received as the same when he comes to pay a debt he has contracted himself; nor will it be the same when by prompt payment he could avoid contracting any debt at all. Industry must wither away. Economy must be driven out of your country. Careful provision will have no existence. Who will labour without knowing the amount of his pay? Who will study to increase what nobody can estimate? Who will accumulate when he does not know the value of what he saves? Abstract it from its uses in gaming, and to accumulate your paper wealth would be not the providence of a man but the disordered instinct of a jackdaw.

The truly melancholy part of the policy of systematically making a nation of gamblers is this: that though all are forced to play, few can understand the game, and fewer still are in a condition to make use of that knowledge. The many must be the dupes of the few who conduct the machine of these speculations. What effect it must have on the country people is visible. The townsman can calculate from day to day; the countryman cannot. When the peasant first brings his corn to market, the magistrate in the town obliges him to take the assignat at par; when he goes to the shop with this money, he finds it seven per cent the worse for crossing the street. He will not readily come back to that market. The townspeople will be inflamed; they will force the country people to bring their corn. Resistance will begin, and the murders of Paris and St Denis may be repeated all over France.

What does the empty compliment paid to the country signify, in giving it perhaps more than its share in the theory of your representation? Where have you placed the real power over moneyed and landed circulation? Where have you placed the means of raising and lowering the value of every man's freehold? Those whose operations can take ten per cent from the possessions of every man in France, or add ten per cent to them, must be the masters of every man in France. The whole of the power obtained by this Revolution will settle in the towns, among the burghers and the moneyed directors who lead them. The landed gentleman, the yeoman and the peasant have none of them the habits or inclinations or experience that could lead them to any share in this, the sole source of power and influence now left in France. The very nature of country life, the very nature of landed property, in all the occupations and all the pleasures they afford, make combination and arrangement — the sole way of procuring and exerting influence — in a manner impossible among country people. Combine them with all the art and all the industry you can, and they are always dissolving back into individuality. Anything in the nature of incorporation is almost impracticable among them. Hope, fear, alarm, jealousy, the story of a day that does its business and dies — all the things that are the reins and spurs by which leaders check or urge the minds of followers — are not easily employed, or hardly at all, among scattered people. They assemble, they arm, they act with the utmost difficulty and at the greatest expense. Their efforts, if they can ever be begun, cannot be sustained. They cannot proceed systematically. If the country gentlemen attempt an influence through the mere income of their property, what is that against the influence of men who have ten times their income to sell, and who can ruin their property by bringing their plunder to meet it at market? If the landed man wants to mortgage, he lowers the value of his land and raises the value of assignats. He increases the power of his enemy by the very means he must use to contend with him. The country gentleman, therefore, and the officer by sea and land, and the man of liberal views and habits attached to no profession, will be as completely excluded from the government of his country as if he had been proscribed by law. It is obvious that in the towns all the things that conspire against the country gentleman combine in favour of the money manager and director. In towns, combination is natural. The habits of burghers, their occupations, their amusements, their business, their idleness, continually bring them into contact with one another. Their virtues and their vices are sociable; they are always in garrison; and they come embodied and half-disciplined into the hands of men who mean to form them for civil or military action.

All these considerations leave no doubt in my mind that if this monster of a constitution can last, France will be wholly governed by the agitators in corporations, by societies in the towns formed of directors in assignats and trustees for the sale of church lands, attorneys, agents, money-jobbers, speculators and adventurers, composing an ignoble oligarchy founded on the destruction of the crown, the church, the nobility and the people. Here end all the deceitful dreams and visions of the equality and rights of men. In "the Serbonian bog" of this base oligarchy they are all absorbed, sunk and lost for ever.

Though human eyes cannot trace them, one would be tempted to think that some great offences in France must cry to heaven, which has thought fit to punish her with subjection to a vile and inglorious domination, in which no comfort or compensation is to be found even in the false splendours that play about other tyrannies and keep mankind from feeling themselves dishonoured while they are oppressed. I confess I am touched with a sorrow mixed with some indignation at the conduct of a few men, once of great rank and still of great character, who, deluded by specious names, have engaged in a business too deep for the line of their understanding to fathom — who have lent their fair reputation and the authority of their high-sounding names to the designs of men they could not possibly have known, and have thereby made their very virtues work towards the ruin of their country.

So much for the first cementing principle.

The second material of cement for their new republic is the superiority of the city of Paris, and this, I admit, is strongly connected with the other cementing principle of paper circulation and confiscation. It is in this part of the project that we must look for the cause of the destruction of all the old boundaries of provinces and jurisdictions, ecclesiastical and secular, and the dissolution of all ancient combinations of things, as well as the formation of so many small unconnected republics. The power of the city of Paris is plainly one great spring of all their politics. It is through the power of Paris, now become the centre and focus of jobbing, that the leaders of this faction direct, or rather command, the whole legislative and the whole executive government. Everything must therefore be done that can confirm the authority of that city over the other republics. Paris is compact; she has an enormous strength, wholly out of proportion to the force of any of the square republics, and this strength is collected and condensed within a narrow compass. Paris has a natural and easy connection between its parts, which no scheme of a geometrical constitution will affect; nor does it much matter whether its proportion of representation is greater or smaller, since it has the whole draught of fishes in its net. The other divisions of the kingdom, being hacked and torn to pieces and separated from all their habitual means and even principles of union, cannot for some time at least confederate against her. Nothing was to be left in all the subordinate members but weakness, disconnection and confusion. To confirm this part of the plan, the Assembly has lately resolved that no two of their republics shall have the same commander-in-chief.

To anyone who takes a view of the whole, the strength of Paris, formed in this way, will appear a system of general weakness. It is boasted that the geometrical policy has been adopted so that all local ideas should be sunk, and that the people should no longer be Gascons, Picards, Bretons, Normans, but Frenchmen, with one country, one heart and one Assembly. But instead of being all Frenchmen, the greater likelihood is that the inhabitants of that region will shortly have no country at all. No man was ever attached by a sense of pride, partiality or real affection to a description of square measurement. He will never glory in belonging to square number 71, or to any other badge ticket. We begin our public affections in our families. No cold relation is a zealous citizen. We pass on to our neighbourhoods and our habitual provincial connections. These are inns and resting places. Such divisions of our country as have been formed by habit, and not by a sudden jerk of authority, were so many little images of the great country, in which the heart found something it could fill. Love of the whole is not extinguished by this subordinate partiality. Perhaps it is a sort of elementary training for those higher and larger regards by which alone men come to be affected, as with a concern of their own, in the prosperity of a kingdom as extensive as France. In that general territory itself, as in the old name of provinces, the citizens are interested by old prejudices and unreasoned habits, and not on account of the geometrical properties of its figure. The power and pre-eminence of Paris does certainly press down and hold these republics together as long as it lasts; but for the reasons I have already given you, I do not think it can last very long.

Passing from the civil creating and civil cementing principles of this constitution to the National Assembly, which is to appear and act as sovereign, we see a body constituted with every possible power and no possible external control. We see a body without fundamental laws, without established maxims, without respected rules of proceeding, which nothing can hold firm to any system whatever. Their idea of their own powers is always taken at the utmost stretch of legislative competence, and their precedents for common cases are taken from the exceptions of the most urgent necessity. The future Assembly is to be in most respects like the present one; but by the manner of the new elections and the tendency of the new circulations it will be purged of the small degree of internal control that exists in a minority originally chosen from various interests and preserving something of their spirit. If it is possible, the next Assembly must be worse than the present. The present one, by destroying and altering everything, will leave its successors apparently nothing popular to do. They will be roused by rivalry and example to the boldest and most absurd enterprises. To suppose such an Assembly sitting in perfect quiet is ridiculous.

Your all-sufficient legislators, in their hurry to do everything at once, have forgotten one thing that seems essential, and which I believe no projector of a republic has ever omitted before, in theory or in practice. They have forgotten to constitute a senate, or something of that nature and character. Never before this time was a body politic heard of that consisted of one legislative and active assembly and its executive officers, without such a council — without something for foreign states to connect themselves with, something the people could look up to in the ordinary detail of government, something to give a bias and a steadiness and to preserve something like consistency in the proceedings of state. Kings generally have such a body as a council. A monarchy may exist without one, but it seems to be of the very essence of a republican government. It holds a sort of middle place between the supreme power exercised by the people or immediately delegated from them, and the mere executive. There are no traces of this in your constitution; and in providing nothing of the kind your Solons and Numas have shown a sovereign incapacity, as much as in anything else.

Let us now turn our eyes to what they have done towards forming an executive power. For this they have chosen a degraded king. This first executive officer of theirs is to be a machine, without any sort of deliberative discretion in any single act of his function. At best he is only a channel for conveying to the National Assembly such matter as that body may need to know. If he had been made the exclusive channel, the power would not have been without its importance, though infinitely perilous to anyone who chose to exercise it. But public intelligence and statements of fact may reach the Assembly with equal authenticity by any other conveyance. As a means of giving direction to measures by the statement of an authorised reporter, therefore, this office of intelligence is nothing at all.

Consider the French scheme of an executive officer in its two natural divisions, civil and political. In the first it must be observed that under the new constitution the higher parts of judicature, in either of its lines, are not in the king. The king of France is not the fountain of justice. Neither the original nor the appellate judges are of his nomination. He neither proposes the candidates nor has a veto on the choice. He is not even the public prosecutor. He serves only as a notary, to authenticate the choice of judges made in the several districts. Through his officers he is to carry out their sentence. When we look into the true nature of his authority, he appears to be nothing more than a chief of bailiffs, sergeants-at-mace, catchpolls, jailers and hangmen. It is impossible to place anything called royalty in a more degrading light. It would have been a thousand times better for the dignity of this unhappy prince if he had had nothing at all to do with the administration of justice, deprived as he is of everything venerable and everything consoling in that function, with no power of originating any process and no power of suspension, mitigation or pardon. Everything in justice that is vile and odious is thrown on him. It was not for nothing that the Assembly took such pains to remove the stigma from certain offices, when they were resolved to place the man who had lately been their king in a situation only one degree above the executioner's, and in an office of nearly the same quality. It is not in nature that the king of the French, situated as he now is, can respect himself or be respected by others.

View this new executive officer on the side of his political capacity, as he acts under the orders of the National Assembly. To execute laws is a royal office; to execute orders is not to be a king. However, a political executive magistracy, even a merely political one, is a great trust. It is a trust that has much depending on its faithful and diligent performance, both in the person presiding over it and in all his subordinates. The means of performing this duty ought to be given by regulation, and the dispositions towards it ought to be infused by the circumstances attending the trust. It ought to be surrounded with dignity, authority and consideration, and it ought to lead to glory. The office of execution is an office of exertion. It is not from impotence that we are to expect the tasks of power. What sort of person is a king to command executive service who has no means whatever of rewarding it — not with a permanent office, not with a grant of land, no, not with a pension of fifty pounds a year, not with the vainest and most trivial title? In France the king is no more the fountain of honour than he is the fountain of justice. All rewards, all distinctions, are in other hands. Those who serve the king can be moved by no natural motive but fear — fear of everything except their master. His functions of internal coercion are as odious as those he exercises in the department of justice. If relief is to be given to any municipality, the Assembly gives it. If troops are to be sent to reduce a municipality to obedience to the Assembly, the king is to execute the order; and on every occasion he is to be spattered over with the blood of his people. He has no veto, yet his name and authority are used to enforce every harsh decree. Indeed, he must join in the butchery of anyone who attempts to free him from his imprisonment, or who shows the slightest attachment to his person or to his ancient authority.

Executive magistracy ought to be constituted in such a way that those who compose it are disposed to love and venerate the people they are bound to obey. A deliberate neglect, or, what is worse, a literal but perverse and malignant obedience, must be the ruin of the wisest counsels. The law will attempt in vain to anticipate or to follow such studied neglects and fraudulent attentions. To make men act zealously is not within the competence of law. Kings, even such as are truly kings, may and ought to bear with the freedom of subjects who are obnoxious to them. They may also, without derogating from themselves, bear even the authority of such persons if it promotes their service. Louis the Thirteenth mortally hated Cardinal Richelieu, but his support of that minister against his rivals was the source of all the glory of his reign and the solid foundation of his throne itself. Louis the Fourteenth, when he came to the throne, did not love Cardinal Mazarin, but he kept him in power for his own interests. When he was old he detested Louvois, but for years, while Louvois faithfully served his greatness, he endured the man. When George the Second took Mr Pitt, who certainly was not agreeable to him, into his councils, he did nothing that could humble a wise sovereign. But these ministers, who were chosen by affairs and not by affections, acted in the name of kings and in trust for them, not as their avowed constitutional and ostensible masters. I think it impossible that any king, once he has recovered from his first terrors, can cordially infuse vivacity and vigour into measures that he knows to be dictated by men whom he must be persuaded are in the highest degree ill disposed towards his person. Will any ministers who serve such a king — or whatever he is to be called — with even a decent appearance of respect cordially obey the orders of men whom only the other day they had committed to the Bastille in his name? Will they obey the orders of men whom they believed they were treating with leniency while they were exercising despotic justice on them, and for whom they thought they had provided an asylum in a prison? If you expect such obedience, then among your other innovations and regenerations you ought to make a revolution in nature and provide a new constitution for the human mind; otherwise your supreme government cannot harmonise with its executive system. There are cases in which we cannot make do with names and abstractions. You may call half a dozen leading individuals whom we have reason to fear and hate the nation. It makes no difference except to make us fear and hate them the more. If it had been thought justifiable and expedient to make such a revolution by such means and through such persons as you have made yours, it would have been wiser to have completed the business of the fifth and sixth of October. The new executive officer would then owe his position to men who were his creators as well as his masters; and he might be bound, by interest, by the fellowship of crime, and, if crimes could have virtues, by gratitude, to serve those who had promoted him to a place of great profit and great sensual indulgence — and of something more besides, for more he must have received from men who certainly would not have limited a creature they had aggrandised as they have limited a submitting antagonist.

A king in the present one's circumstances, if he is so totally stupefied by his misfortunes as to think it not the necessity but the privilege and prize of life to eat and sleep with no regard to glory, can never be fit for the office. If he feels as men commonly feel, he must be aware that an office so circumstanced is one in which he can win no fame and no reputation. He has no generous interest to rouse him to action. At best his conduct will be passive and defensive. To inferior people such an office might be a matter of honour. But to be raised to it and to descend to it are different things, and suggest different sentiments. Does he really name the ministers? Then they will have a sympathy with him. Are they forced on him? Then the whole business between them and the nominal king will be mutual counteraction. In every other country the office of minister of state is of the highest dignity. In France it is full of peril and incapable of glory. Rivals they will still have in their nothingness, as long as shallow ambition exists in the world or the desire of a miserable salary is an incentive to short-sighted avarice. Your constitution enables those competitors of the ministers to attack them in their vital parts, while the ministers have no means of repelling the charges except in the degrading character of culprits. The ministers of state in France are the only persons in that country who are incapable of a share in the national councils. What ministers! What councils! What a nation! But they are responsible. Responsibility is a poor sort of service to get. The elevation of mind derived from fear will never make a nation glorious. Responsibility prevents crimes. It makes all attempts against the laws dangerous. But as a principle of active and zealous service, none but idiots could think of it. Is the conduct of a war to be trusted to a man who may abhor its very principle — who with every step he takes to make it succeed confirms the power of those by whom he is oppressed? Will foreign states seriously treat with a man who has no prerogative of peace or war, not so much as a single vote, by himself or his ministers or anyone he can possibly influence? A state of contempt is no state for a prince: better be rid of him at once.

I know it will be said that these ill humours in the court and the executive government will last only through this generation, and that the king has been brought to declare that the dauphin shall be educated in conformity with his situation. If he is made to conform to his situation, he will have no education at all. His training must be worse even than that of an arbitrary monarch. If he reads — and whether he reads or not — some good or evil genius will tell him that his ancestors were kings. From that moment his object must be to assert himself and to avenge his parents. This, you will say, is not his duty. That may be; but it is nature; and while you pit nature against you, you are unwise to trust to duty. In this futile scheme of politics, the state nurses in its bosom for the present a source of weakness, perplexity, counteraction, inefficiency and decay, and it prepares the means of its own final ruin. In short, I see nothing in the executive force — I cannot call it authority — that has even an appearance of vigour, or the smallest degree of just correspondence or symmetry or friendly relation with the supreme power, either as it now exists or as it is planned for the future government.

You have settled, by an economy as perverted as the policy, two establishments of government, one real and one fictitious, both maintained at a vast expense — but the fictitious one at the greater, I think. Such a machine as the second is not worth the grease of its wheels. The expense is exorbitant, and neither the show nor the use deserves a tenth part of the charge. Oh, but I am not doing justice to the talents of the legislators; I am not making the allowance I ought for necessity. Their scheme of executive force was not their own choice. This pageant must be kept up. The people would not consent to part with it. Right; I understand you. In spite of your grand theories, to which you would have heaven and earth bend, you do know how to conform yourselves to the nature and circumstances of things. But when you were obliged to conform this far to circumstances, you ought to have carried your submission further and made what you were obliged to take a proper instrument, useful to its end. That was in your power. For instance, among many others, it was in your power to leave your king the right of peace and war. What! Leave the executive magistrate the most dangerous of all prerogatives? I know none more dangerous, nor any that more needs to be trusted to him. I do not say that this prerogative ought to be trusted to your king unless he enjoyed other auxiliary trusts along with it, which he does not now hold. But if he did possess them, hazardous as they undoubtedly are, advantages would arise from such a constitution more than compensating the risk. There is no other way of keeping the several potentates of Europe from intriguing separately and personally with the members of your Assembly, from meddling in all your concerns, and from fomenting in the heart of your country the most pernicious of all factions — factions in the interest and under the direction of foreign powers. From that worst of evils, thank God, we are still free. Your skill, if you had any, would be well employed in finding indirect correctives and controls on this perilous trust. If you did not like the ones we have chosen in England, your leaders might have exerted their abilities in contriving better. If it were necessary to illustrate the consequences of such an executive government as yours in the management of great affairs, I should refer you to M. de Montmorin's recent reports to the National Assembly and to all the other proceedings relating to the differences between Great Britain and Spain. It would be treating your understanding with disrespect to point them out to you.

Footnote: In reality three, if we count the provincial republican establishments.

I hear that the persons who are called ministers have signified an intention of resigning their places. I am rather astonished that they did not resign long ago. Not for the universe would I have stood in the position they have occupied for the last twelvemonth. They wished well to the Revolution, I take it for granted. However that may be, placed as they were on an eminence — though an eminence of humiliation — they could not help being the first to see collectively, and to feel each in his own department, the evils that Revolution has produced. In every step they took, or refrained from taking, they must have felt the degraded situation of their country and their own utter incapacity to serve it. They are in a species of subordinate servitude in which no men before them were ever seen. Having the confidence neither of their sovereign, on whom they were forced, nor of the Assembly, which forced them on him, all the noble functions of their office are performed by committees of the Assembly, without any regard whatever to their personal or their official authority. They are to execute, without power; to be responsible, without discretion; to deliberate, without choice. In their bewildered situation under two sovereigns, over neither of whom they have any influence, they must act in such a way as, whatever they may intend, to betray sometimes the one and sometimes the other, and always to betray themselves. Such has been their situation, and such must be the situation of those who succeed them. I have much respect and many good wishes for M. Necker. I am obliged to him for attentions. When his enemies had driven him from Versailles, I thought his exile a subject for the most serious congratulation. Sed multæ urbes et publica vota vicerunt — but many cities and the public prayers prevailed. He is now sitting on the ruins of the finances and of the monarchy of France.

A great deal more might be observed on the strange constitution of the executive part of the new government; but fatigue must set bounds to the discussion of subjects which in themselves have hardly any limits.

I am able to perceive as little genius and talent in the plan of judicature formed by the National Assembly. Following their invariable course, the framers of your constitution have begun by utterly abolishing the parlements. These venerable bodies, like the rest of the old government, needed reform, even if no change had been made in the monarchy. They needed several more alterations to adapt them to the system of a free constitution. But they had particular features in their constitution, and not a few of them, that deserved the approval of wise men. They possessed one fundamental excellence: they were independent. The most doubtful circumstance attending their office, that it was purchasable, actually contributed to this independence of character. They held for life. Indeed they may be said to have held by inheritance. Appointed by the monarch, they were regarded as nearly out of his power. The most determined exertions of that authority against them only showed their radical independence. They formed permanent bodies politic, constituted to resist arbitrary innovation; and from that corporate constitution, and from most of their forms, they were well calculated to give both certainty and stability to the laws. They had been a safe asylum securing those laws through all the revolutions of humour and opinion. They had saved that sacred deposit of the country through the reigns of arbitrary princes and the struggles of arbitrary factions. They kept alive the memory and the record of the constitution. They were the great security for private property, which might be said, at a time when personal liberty had no existence, to be in fact as well guarded in France as in any other country. Whatever is supreme in a state ought as far as possible to have its judicial authority so constituted as not only not to depend on it but in some sort to balance it. It ought to give a security to its justice against its power. It ought to make its judicature, as it were, something exterior to the state.

Those parlements had furnished, not the best certainly, but some considerable corrective to the excesses and vices of the monarchy. Such an independent judicature was ten times more necessary when a democracy became the absolute power of the country. In that constitution, elective, temporary, local judges such as you have contrived, exercising their dependent functions in a narrow society, must be the worst of all tribunals. It will be vain to look in them for any appearance of justice towards strangers, towards the obnoxious rich, towards the minority of defeated parties, towards everyone who supported unsuccessful candidates at the election. It will be impossible to keep the new tribunals clear of the worst spirit of faction. We know by experience that all contrivances by ballot are vain and childish as a way of preventing inclinations from being discovered. Where they best answer the purposes of concealment, they answer by producing suspicion, and that is a still more mischievous cause of partiality.

If the parlements had been preserved instead of being dissolved at such ruinous cost to the nation, they might have served in this new commonwealth — not precisely the same purposes, I do not mean an exact parallel, but near the same purposes as the court and senate of the Areopagus served in Athens: that is, as one of the balances and correctives to the evils of a light and unjust democracy. Everyone knows that this tribunal was the great stay of that state; everyone knows with what care it was upheld and with what religious awe it was consecrated. The parlements were not wholly free from faction, I admit; but that evil was external and accidental, and not so much a vice of their constitution itself as it must be in your new contrivance of six-yearly elective judicatures. Several English people commend the abolition of the old tribunals, supposing that they determined everything by bribery and corruption. But they have stood the test of both monarchic and republican scrutiny. The court was well disposed to prove corruption on those bodies when they were dissolved in 1771; the men who have dissolved them again would have done the same if they could; but since both inquisitions failed, I conclude that gross pecuniary corruption must have been rather rare among them.

It would have been prudent, along with the parlements, to preserve their ancient power of registering all the decrees of the National Assembly, and at least of remonstrating against them, as they did with the decrees passed in the time of the monarchy. It would be a means of squaring the occasional decrees of a democracy to some principles of general jurisprudence. The vice of the ancient democracies, and one cause of their ruin, was that they ruled, as you do, by occasional decrees, psephismata. This practice soon broke in on the tenor and consistency of the laws; it lowered the people's respect for them, and in the end destroyed them altogether.

Your vesting the power of remonstrance, which in the time of the monarchy belonged to the Parlement of Paris, in your principal executive officer — whom, in spite of common sense, you persist in calling king — is the height of absurdity. You ought never to allow remonstrance from the man who is to execute. This is to understand neither counsel nor execution, neither authority nor obedience. The person you call king ought not to have this power, or else he ought to have more.

Your present arrangement is strictly judicial. Instead of imitating your monarchy and seating your judges on a bench of independence, your object is to reduce them to the blindest obedience. Having changed all things, you have invented new principles of order. You first appoint judges, who I suppose are to decide according to law, and then you let them know that at some time or other you intend to give them some law to decide by. Whatever studies they have made, if they have made any, are to be useless to them. But to make up for those studies, they are to be sworn to obey all the rules and orders and instructions they receive from time to time from the National Assembly. If they submit to these, they leave the subject no ground of law at all. They become complete and most dangerous instruments in the hands of the governing power, which, in the middle of a case or in prospect of one, may wholly change the rule of decision. If these orders of the National Assembly come to be contrary to the will of the people who locally choose those judges, such confusion must follow as is terrible to think of. For the judges owe their place to the local authority, and the commands they are sworn to obey come from men who have no share in appointing them. Meanwhile they have the example of the court of the Châtelet to encourage and guide them in the exercise of their functions. That court is to try criminals sent to it by the National Assembly, or brought before it by other means of accusation. They sit under a guard to save their own lives. They do not know by what law they judge, nor under what authority they act, nor by what tenure they hold. It is thought that they are sometimes obliged to condemn at the peril of their lives. This is perhaps not certain, and cannot be ascertained; but when they acquit, we know that they have seen the men they discharge hanged at the door of their court, with perfect impunity to those who did it.

The Assembly does indeed promise that they will form a body of law that shall be short, simple, clear and so forth. That is, by their short laws they will leave much to the discretion of the judge, while they have exploded the authority of all the learning that could make judicial discretion — a perilous thing at best — deserve the name of a sound discretion.

It is curious to observe that the administrative bodies are carefully exempted from the jurisdiction of these new tribunals. That is, the very persons who ought to be most entirely subject to the laws are exempted from their power.
