Those who execute public financial trusts ought of all men to be held most strictly to their duty. One would have thought that it must have been among your earliest cares, unless you meant those administrative bodies to be real, sovereign, independent states, to form an awe-inspiring tribunal, like your late parlements or like our King's Bench, where all corporate officers might obtain protection in the legal exercise of their functions and would meet with coercion if they trespassed against their legal duty. But the reason for the exemption is plain. These administrative bodies are the great instruments of the present leaders in their progress through democracy to oligarchy. They must therefore be put above the law. It will be said that the legal tribunals you have made are unfit to coerce them. Undoubtedly they are. They are unfit for any rational purpose. It will be said too that the administrative bodies will be accountable to the general Assembly. This, I fear, is talking without much consideration of the nature of that Assembly or of these corporations. However, to be subject to the pleasure of that Assembly is not to be subject to law, either for protection or for constraint.

This establishment of judges still wants something to complete it. It is to be crowned by a new tribunal. This is to be a grand state judicature, and it is to judge crimes committed against the nation — that is, against the power of the Assembly. It seems as if they had something in view of the nature of the high court of justice erected in England during the great usurpation. As they have not yet finished this part of the scheme, it is impossible to form a direct judgment on it. However, unless great care is taken to form it in a spirit very different from the one that has guided them in their proceedings on state offences, this tribunal, subservient to their inquisition the Committee of Research, will extinguish the last sparks of liberty in France and settle the most dreadful and arbitrary tyranny ever known in any nation. If they wish to give this tribunal any appearance of liberty and justice, they must not remove cases relating to their own members from it, or send such cases to it, at their pleasure. They must also move the seat of that tribunal out of the republic of Paris.

Footnote: For further explanation of all these judicatures and of the Committee of Research, see M. de Calonne's work.

Has more wisdom been displayed in the constitution of your army than can be discovered in your plan of judicature? Arranging this part ably is the more difficult, and requires the greater skill and attention, not only as a great concern in itself but because it is the third cementing principle in the new body of republics that you call the French nation. Truly it is not easy to guess what that army may become in the end. You have voted a very large one, and on good pay, at least fully equal to your apparent means of paying it. But what is the principle of its discipline? Or whom is it to obey? You have got the wolf by the ears, and I wish you joy of the happy position you have chosen to place yourselves in, and in which you are so well circumstanced for a free deliberation about that army, or about anything else.

The minister and secretary of state for the War Department is M. de La Tour du Pin. This gentleman, like his colleagues in the administration, is a most zealous supporter of the Revolution and a sanguine admirer of the new constitution that originated in it. His statement of the facts about the military of France is important, not only from his official and personal authority but because it displays very clearly the actual condition of the army in France, and because it throws light on the principles the Assembly proceeds by in administering this critical object. It may enable us to judge how far it would be expedient in this country to imitate the martial policy of France.

On the fourth of last June M. de La Tour du Pin comes to give an account of the state of his department as it exists under the auspices of the National Assembly. No man knows it so well; no man can express it better. Addressing the National Assembly, he says:

"His Majesty has sent me this day to inform you of the multiplied disorders of which every day he receives the most distressing news. The army [le corps militaire] threatens to fall into the most turbulent anarchy. Entire regiments have dared to violate at once the respect due to the laws, to the king, to the order established by your decrees, and to the oaths they have taken with the most awful solemnity. Compelled by my duty to inform you of these excesses, my heart bleeds when I consider who it is that has committed them. Those against whom I have no power to withhold the most grievous complaints are part of that very soldiery which until this day has been so full of honour and loyalty, and among whom I have lived for fifty years as comrade and friend.

"What incomprehensible spirit of delirium and delusion has led them astray all at once? While you are working tirelessly to establish uniformity in the empire and to mould the whole into one coherent and consistent body; while the French are being taught by you at once the respect the laws owe to the rights of man and the respect the citizens owe to the laws — the administration of the army presents nothing but disturbance and confusion. In more than one corps I see the bonds of discipline slackened or broken; the most unheard-of pretensions avowed directly and without any disguise; the ordinances without force; the commanders without authority; the military chest and the colours carried off; the authority of the king himself [risum teneatis — try not to laugh] proudly defied; the officers despised, degraded, threatened, driven away, and some of them prisoners in the middle of their own corps, dragging out a precarious life in an atmosphere of disgust and humiliation. To fill up the measure of all these horrors, the commandants of garrisons have had their throats cut before the eyes and almost in the arms of their own soldiers.

"These evils are great, but they are not the worst consequences that such military insurrections may produce. Sooner or later they may threaten the nation itself. The nature of things requires that the army should never act except as an instrument. The moment it sets itself up as a deliberative body and acts on its own resolutions, the government, be it what it may, will immediately degenerate into a military democracy — a species of political monster that has always ended by devouring those who produced it.

"After all this, who can fail to be alarmed at the irregular consultations and turbulent committees formed in some regiments by the common soldiers and non-commissioned officers, without the knowledge of their superiors or even in contempt of their authority? — although the presence and concurrence of those superiors could give no authority to such monstrous democratic assemblies [comices]."

There is no need to add much to this finished picture — finished so far as its canvas allows, but not, I think, taking in the whole nature and complexity of the disorders of this military democracy, which, as the war minister truly and wisely observes, must be the real constitution of the state wherever it exists, by whatever formal name it goes. For although he informs the Assembly that the greater part of the army has not thrown off its obedience but is still attached to its duty, travellers who have seen the corps whose conduct is best observe in them the absence of mutiny rather than the existence of discipline.

I cannot help pausing here a moment to reflect on the expressions of surprise this minister lets fall about the excesses he relates. To him the departure of the troops from their ancient principles of loyalty and honour seems quite inconceivable. Surely the men he is addressing know the causes of it only too well. They know the doctrines they have preached, the decrees they have passed, the practices they have countenanced. The soldiers remember the sixth of October. They remember the French guards. They have not forgotten the taking of the king's castles in Paris and at Marseilles. That the governors of both places were murdered with impunity is a fact that has not slipped out of their minds. They do not abandon the principles of the equality of men, laid down so ostentatiously and so laboriously. They cannot shut their eyes to the degradation of the whole nobility of France and the suppression of the very idea of a gentleman. The total abolition of titles and distinctions is not lost on them. Yet M. du Pin is astonished at their disloyalty, when the doctors of the Assembly have taught them at the same time the respect due to the laws. It is easy to judge which of the two sorts of lesson men with arms in their hands are likely to learn. As for the authority of the king, we may gather from the minister himself, if any argument on that head were not quite superfluous, that it counts for no more with these troops than it does with everybody else. "The king," he says, "has repeated his orders over and over again to put a stop to these excesses; but in so terrible a crisis your" — the Assembly's — "concurrence has become indispensably necessary to prevent the evils that threaten the state. You unite to the force of the legislative power that of opinion, which is still more important." To be sure, the army can have no opinion of the power or authority of the king. Perhaps by this time the soldier has learned that the Assembly itself does not enjoy a much greater degree of liberty than that royal figure.

We are now to see what has been proposed in this emergency, one of the greatest that can happen in a state. The minister asks the Assembly to array itself in all its terrors and to call forth all its majesty. He wishes the grave and severe principles they have announced to give vigour to the king's proclamation. After this we should have looked for courts civil and martial, the breaking of some regiments, the decimating of others, and all the terrible means that necessity has employed in such cases to arrest the progress of the most terrible of all evils. In particular one might expect a serious inquiry into the murder of commandants before their soldiers' eyes. Not one word of all this, or of anything like it. After being told that the soldiery trampled on the decrees of the Assembly promulgated by the king, the Assembly passes new decrees and authorises the king to make new proclamations. After the secretary at war had stated that the regiments had paid no regard to oaths prêtés avec la plus imposante solennité, sworn with the most imposing solemnity, they propose — what? More oaths. They renew decrees and proclamations as fast as they find them insufficient, and they multiply oaths in proportion as they weaken the sanctions of religion in men's minds. I hope that handy abridgements of the excellent sermons of Voltaire, D'Alembert, Diderot and Helvétius on the immortality of the soul, on a particular superintending providence, and on a future state of rewards and punishments are being sent down to the soldiers along with their civic oaths. Of this I have no doubt, since I understand that a certain kind of reading makes no small part of their military exercises, and that they are quite as well supplied with the ammunition of pamphlets as with cartridges.

To prevent the mischiefs arising from conspiracies, irregular consultations, seditious committees and monstrous democratic assemblies [comitia, comices] among the soldiers, and all the disorders arising from idleness, luxury, dissipation and insubordination, I believe the most astonishing means have been used that ever occurred to men, even among all the inventions of this prolific age. It is nothing less than this: the king has promulgated in circular letters to all the regiments his direct authority and encouragement for the several corps to join the clubs and confederations in the several municipalities, and to mix with them in their feasts and civic entertainments. This jolly discipline, it seems, is to soften the ferocity of their minds, to reconcile them to their bottle companions of other descriptions, and to merge particular conspiracies in more general associations. That this remedy would be pleasing to the soldiers, as M. de La Tour du Pin describes them, I can readily believe — and that, mutinous as they may be in other respects, they will dutifully submit themselves to these royal proclamations. But I should question whether all this civic swearing and clubbing and feasting would dispose them any more than they are disposed at present to obey their officers, or teach them to submit better to the austere rules of military discipline. It will make them admirable citizens in the French mode, but not quite such good soldiers in any mode. A doubt might well arise whether the conversation at these good tables would fit them a great deal better for the character of mere instruments, which this veteran officer and statesman justly observes that the nature of things always requires an army to be.

Footnote: "Comme sa Majesté a reconnu, non un système d'associations particulières, mais une réunion de volontés de tous les François pour la liberté et la prospérité communes, ainsi que pour le maintien de l'ordre publique, il a pensé qu'il convenoit que chaque régiment prît part à ces fêtes civiques pour multiplier les rapports, et resserrer les liens d'union entre les citoyens et les troupes." In English: as His Majesty has recognised in this not a system of particular associations but a union of the wills of all Frenchmen for the common liberty and prosperity, and likewise for the maintenance of public order, he has thought it fitting that each regiment should take part in these civic festivals, so as to multiply the contacts and tighten the bonds of union between the citizens and the troops. I insert the words authorising the troops to feast with the popular confederacies, in case I should not be believed.

As to how likely this improvement of discipline is, by the free conversation of the soldiers with the municipal festive societies, thus officially encouraged by royal authority and sanction, we may judge from the state of the municipalities themselves as the war minister gives it to us in this very speech. He has good hopes of the success of his efforts to restore order for the present, from the good disposition of certain regiments; but he finds something cloudy about the future. As to preventing the return of confusion, "for that," he says, "the administration cannot answer to you, as long as they see the municipalities claiming an authority over the troops which your own institutions have reserved wholly to the monarch. You have fixed the limits of the military authority and of the municipal authority. You have confined the action you have allowed the second over the first to the right of requisition; but never did the letter or the spirit of your decrees authorise the commons in these municipalities to break the officers, to try them, to give orders to the soldiers, to drive them from the posts committed to their guard, to stop them on marches ordered by the king, or, in a word, to enslave the troops to the caprice of every city or even market town they have to pass through."

Such is the character and disposition of the municipal society that is to reclaim the soldiery, bring them back to the true principles of military subordination, and render them machines in the hands of the supreme power of the country. Such are the disorders of the French troops. Such is their cure. As the army is, so is the navy. The municipalities override the orders of the Assembly, and the seamen in their turn override the orders of the municipalities. From my heart I pity the condition of a respectable servant of the public like this war minister, obliged in his old age to pledge the Assembly in their civic cups and to enter with a grey head into all the fantastic vagaries of these juvenile politicians. Such schemes are not like propositions coming from a man of fifty years' wear and tear among mankind. They seem rather what is to be expected from those grand compounders in politics who take the short road to their degrees in the state and have a certain inward fanatical assurance and illumination on all subjects — on the credit of which one of their doctors has thought fit, with great applause and greater success, to caution the Assembly not to attend to old men, or to anyone who values himself on his experience. I suppose all the ministers of state must qualify and take this test, wholly abjuring the errors and heresies of experience and observation. Every man has his own taste; but I think that if I could not attain to the wisdom of age, I would at least preserve something of its stiff and peremptory dignity. These gentlemen deal in regeneration; but at no price would I yield my rigid fibres to be regenerated by them, or begin, in my grand climacteric, to squall in their new accents, or to stammer in my second cradle the elementary sounds of their barbarous metaphysics. Si isti mihi largiantur ut repuerascam, et in eorum cunis vagiam, valde recusem! — if they were to grant me the favour of becoming a child again, and squalling in their cradle, I should firmly refuse.

Footnote: This war minister has since left the school and resigned his office.

The feebleness of any part of the puerile and pedantic system they call a constitution cannot be laid open without revealing the utter insufficiency and mischief of every other part it comes into contact with, or that bears the remotest relation to it. You cannot propose a remedy for the incompetence of the crown without displaying the debility of the Assembly. You cannot deliberate on the confusion of the army of the state without disclosing the worse disorders of the armed municipalities. The military lays open the civil, and the civil betrays the military anarchy. I wish everybody would read M. de La Tour du Pin's eloquent speech — for such it is — carefully. He attributes the salvation of the municipalities to the good behaviour of some of the troops. These troops are to preserve the well-disposed part of the municipalities, which is admitted to be the weakest, from the pillage of the worst-disposed part, which is the strongest. But the municipalities claim a sovereignty and will command the troops that are necessary for their protection. Indeed they must either command them or court them. The municipalities, by the necessity of their situation and by the republican powers they have obtained, must be, in relation to the military, either masters or servants or confederates, or each of these in turn, or they must make a jumble of all of them together according to circumstances. What government is there to coerce the army but the municipality, or the municipality but the army? To preserve concord where authority is extinguished, at the hazard of all consequences, the Assembly tries to cure the diseases by the diseases themselves, and hopes to preserve itself from a purely military democracy by giving that democracy a debauched interest in the municipal one.

If the soldiers once come to mix for any length of time in the municipal clubs and cabals and confederacies, an elective affinity will draw them to the lowest and most desperate part of them. Their habits and affections and sympathies will go with them. The military conspiracies that are to be remedied by civic confederacies; the rebellious municipalities that are to be made obedient by furnishing them with the means of seducing the very armies of the state that are meant to keep them in order — all these chimeras of a monstrous and portentous policy must aggravate the confusion they arose from. There must be blood. The want of common judgment shown in the construction of all their descriptions of forces, and in all their kinds of civil and judicial authority, will make it flow. Disorders may be quieted at one time and in one part. They will break out in others, because the evil is radical and intrinsic. All these schemes of mixing mutinous soldiers with seditious citizens must weaken the military connection between soldiers and their officers more and more, as well as adding a military and mutinous audacity to turbulent artisans and peasants. To secure a real army, the officer must be first and last in the eye of the soldier — first and last in his attention, his observance and his esteem. There are to be officers, it seems, whose chief qualification must be temper and patience. They are to manage their troops by electioneering arts. They must carry themselves as candidates and not as commanders. But since power may occasionally come into their hands by such means, the authority by which they are nominated becomes of high importance.

What you may do in the end does not appear, nor does it much matter, while the strange and contradictory relation between your army and all the parts of your republic, and the puzzled relation of those parts to each other and to the whole, remain as they are. You seem to have given the provisional nomination of the officers in the first instance to the king, with a reserved approval by the National Assembly. Men who have an interest to pursue are extremely sagacious in discovering the true seat of power. They must soon perceive that those who can veto indefinitely are the ones who really appoint. The officers must therefore look to their intrigues in the Assembly as the only certain road to promotion. Yet by your new constitution they must still begin their solicitations at court. This double negotiation for military rank seems to me a contrivance as well adapted as if it had been studied for the purpose to promote faction in the Assembly itself over this vast military patronage — and then to poison the corps of officers with factions of a kind still more dangerous to the safety of government, on whatever footing it may be placed, and destructive in the end to the efficiency of the army itself. Those officers who lose the promotions the crown intended for them must become part of a faction opposed to the Assembly that rejected their claims, and must nourish discontent against the ruling powers in the heart of the army. Those officers, on the other hand, who carry their point through an interest in the Assembly, and feel themselves at best only second in the goodwill of the crown though first in that of the Assembly, must slight an authority that could neither advance nor retard their promotion. If, to avoid these evils, you will have no rule for command or promotion but seniority, you will have an army of formality; and at the same time it will become more independent and more of a military republic. It is not they but the king who is the machine. A king is not to be deposed by halves. If he is not everything in the command of an army, he is nothing. What is the effect of a power placed nominally at the head of an army that is to that army no object of gratitude and no object of fear? Such a cipher is not fit to administer the most delicate of all objects, the supreme command of military men. They must be constrained — and their inclinations lead them to what their necessities require — by a real, vigorous, effective, decided, personal authority. The authority of the Assembly itself suffers by passing through such a debilitating channel as they have chosen. The army will not long look up to an Assembly acting through the organ of false show and palpable imposition. They will not seriously yield obedience to a prisoner. They will either despise a pageant or pity a captive king. This relation of your army to the crown will, unless I am greatly mistaken, become a serious dilemma in your politics.

It is also to be considered whether an Assembly like yours, even supposing it possessed a different sort of organ for its orders to pass through, is fit to promote the obedience and discipline of an army. It is known that armies have hitherto yielded a very precarious and uncertain obedience to any senate or popular authority, and they will yield it least of all to an Assembly that is to last only two years. The officers must lose the characteristic disposition of military men entirely if they can see the dominion of pleaders with perfect submission and due admiration — especially when they find they have a new court to pay to an endless succession of those pleaders, whose military policy and genius for command, if they have any, must be as uncertain as their tenure is brief. In the weakness of one kind of authority and the fluctuation of all, the officers of an army will remain mutinous and full of faction for some time, until some popular general who understands the art of conciliating the soldiery and possesses the true spirit of command draws the eyes of all men on himself. Armies will obey him on his own personal account. There is no other way of securing military obedience in this state of things. But the moment that happens, the man who really commands the army is your master — the master of your king, which is a small thing, the master of your Assembly, the master of your whole republic.

How did the Assembly come by its present power over the army? Chiefly, to be sure, by debauching the soldiers away from their officers. They have begun with a most terrible operation. They have touched the central point about which the particles that compose armies are at rest. They have destroyed the principle of obedience in the great, essential, critical link between the officer and the soldier, just where the chain of military subordination begins and on which the whole of that system depends. The soldier is told that he is a citizen and has the rights of man and citizen. The right of a man, he is told, is to be his own governor and to be ruled only by those to whom he delegates that self-government. It is very natural that he should think he ought most of all to have his choice where he is to yield the greatest degree of obedience. In all probability, therefore, he will do systematically what he does at present occasionally: that is, he will exercise at least a veto in the choice of his officers. At present the officers are known at best to be only permitted, and to serve on good behaviour. In fact there have been many instances of their being cashiered by their own corps. Here is a second veto on the king's choice, a veto at least as effective as the Assembly's. The soldiers know already that it has been a question not ill received in the National Assembly whether they ought not to have the direct choice of their officers, or of some proportion of them. When such matters are under deliberation, it is no extravagant supposition that they will incline to the opinion most favourable to their own claims. They will not bear to be reckoned the army of an imprisoned king while another army in the same country, which they are also to feast and confederate with, is to be considered the free army of a free constitution. They will cast their eyes on the other and more permanent army: I mean the municipal one. That corps, they know very well, does actually elect its own officers. They may not be able to see the grounds of the distinction on which they are not to elect a Marquis de La Fayette — or whatever his new name is — of their own. If this election of a commander-in-chief is part of the rights of men, why not part of theirs? They see elective justices of the peace, elective judges, elective parish priests, elective bishops, elective municipalities, and an elective commander of the Parisian army. Why should they alone be excluded? Are the brave troops of France the only men in that nation who are not fit judges of military merit and of the qualifications a commander-in-chief needs? Are they paid by the state, and do they therefore lose the rights of men? They are part of that nation themselves and contribute to that pay. And is not the king paid, and the National Assembly, and all who elect the National Assembly? Instead of seeing all these forfeit their rights by receiving a salary, they see that in every one of these cases a salary is given for exercising those rights. All your resolutions, all your proceedings, all your debates, all the works of your doctors in religion and politics have been industriously put into their hands; and you expect them to apply to their own case just as much of your doctrines and examples as suits your pleasure.

Everything depends on the army in a government like yours, for you have industriously destroyed all the opinions and prejudices, and so far as in you lay all the instincts, that support government. Therefore the moment any difference arises between your National Assembly and any part of the nation, you must have recourse to force. Nothing else is left to you — or rather, you have left nothing else to yourselves. You see from the report of your war minister that the distribution of the army is made largely with a view to internal coercion. You must rule by an army; and you have infused into that army by which you rule, as well as into the whole body of the nation, principles which after a time must disable you from making the use of it that you intend. The king is to call out troops to act against his people, when the world has been told, and the assertion is still ringing in our ears, that troops ought not to fire on citizens. The colonies claim an independent constitution and a free trade for themselves. They must be constrained by troops. In what chapter of your code of the rights of men can they read that it is part of the rights of men to have their commerce monopolised and restrained for the benefit of others? As the colonists rise against you, the people they hold enslaved rise against them. Troops again — massacre, torture, hanging! These are your rights of men! These are the fruits of metaphysical declarations wantonly made and shamefully retracted! It was only the other day that the farmers of land in one of your provinces refused to pay certain sorts of rent to the lord of the soil. In consequence you decree that the country people shall pay all rents and dues except those you have abolished as grievances; and if they refuse, then you order the king to march troops against them. You lay down metaphysical propositions that imply universal consequences, and then you try to limit logic by despotism. The leaders of the present system tell the people of their rights as men to take fortresses, to murder guards, to seize on kings without the least appearance of authority even from the Assembly, while that Assembly was sitting as the sovereign legislative body in the name of the nation; and yet these leaders presume to order out the very troops that took part in those disorders, to coerce men who judge on the principles and follow the examples their own approval has guaranteed.

Footnote: Courrier François, 30 July 1790. Assemblée Nationale, number 210.

The leaders teach the people to abhor and reject all feudalism as the barbarism of tyranny, and then tell them afterwards how much of that barbarous tyranny they are to bear with patience. As prodigal as they are of light about grievances, the people find them extremely sparing about redress. They know that certain quit-rents and personal duties, which you have allowed them to buy out but for which you have provided no money, are as nothing beside the burdens you have made no provision for at all. They know that almost the whole system of landed property is feudal in its origin — that it is the distribution of the possessions of the original proprietors, made by a barbarous conqueror to his barbarous instruments — and that the most grievous effects of the conquest are the land-rents of every kind, as beyond question they are.

The peasants are in all probability the descendants of those ancient proprietors, Romans or Gauls. But if their titles fail in any degree on the principles of antiquaries and lawyers, they retreat into the citadel of the rights of men. There they find that men are equal, and that the earth, the kind and equal mother of all, ought not to be monopolised to feed the pride and luxury of men who by nature are no better than themselves, and who, if they do not labour for their bread, are worse. They find that by the laws of nature the occupier and cultivator of the soil is the true proprietor; that there is no prescription against nature; that the agreements made with the landlords during the time of slavery, where there are any, are only the effect of duress and force; and that when the people re-entered into the rights of men, those agreements were made as void as everything else that had been settled under the prevalence of the old feudal and aristocratic tyranny. They will tell you they see no difference between an idler in a hat with a national cockade and an idler in a cowl or a rochet. If you ground the title to rents on succession and prescription, they tell you, out of M. Camus's speech published by the National Assembly for their information, that things ill begun cannot avail themselves of prescription; that the title of those lords was vicious in its origin; and that force is at least as bad as fraud. As for the title by succession, they will tell you that the succession of those who have cultivated the soil is the true pedigree of property, and not rotten parchments and silly entails; that the lords have enjoyed their usurpation too long; and that if they allow these lay monks any charitable pension, the monks ought to be thankful for the bounty of the true proprietor, who is so generous towards a false claimant to his goods.

When the peasants give you back that coin of sophistic reason on which you have stamped your own image and superscription, you cry it down as base money and tell them you will pay in future with French guards and dragoons and hussars. To chastise them you hold up the second-hand authority of a king who is only the instrument of destroying, with no power of protecting either the people or his own person. Through him, it seems, you will make yourselves obeyed. They answer: "You have taught us that there are no gentlemen; and which of your principles teaches us to bow to kings we have not elected? We know without your teaching that lands were given for the support of feudal dignities, feudal titles and feudal offices. When you took down the cause as a grievance, why should the more grievous effect remain? Since there are now no hereditary honours and no distinguished families, why are we taxed to maintain what you tell us ought not to exist? You have sent our old aristocratic landlords down to us in no other character and with no other title than that of collectors under your authority. Have you tried to make these rent-gatherers of yours respectable to us? No. You have sent them to us with their arms reversed, their shields broken, their devices defaced — so displumed, degraded and metamorphosed, such unfeathered two-legged things, that we no longer know them. They are strangers to us. They do not even go by the names of our ancient lords. Physically they may be the same men, though we are not quite sure even of that on your new philosophical doctrines of personal identity. In every other respect they are totally changed. We do not see why we have not as good a right to refuse them their rents as you have to abolish all their honours and titles and distinctions. We never commissioned you to do that, and it is one instance among many of your assuming an undelegated power. We see the burghers of Paris, through their clubs and mobs and national guards, directing you at their pleasure, and laying down as law to you what is then handed down as law to us under your authority. Through you these burghers dispose of the lives and fortunes of us all. Why should you not attend as much to the wishes of the labouring husbandman about our rent, which affects us in the most serious manner, as you do to the demands of these insolent burghers about distinctions and titles of honour, which affect neither them nor us at all? But we find you pay more regard to their fancies than to our necessities. Is it among the rights of man to pay tribute to his equals? Before this measure of yours we might have thought we were not perfectly equal; we might have kept up some old, habitual, unmeaning prepossession in favour of those landlords. But we cannot conceive that you could have made the law that degrades them with any other view than of destroying all respect for them. You have forbidden us to treat them with any of the old formalities of respect; and now you send troops to sabre and bayonet us into a submission to fear and force which you would not let us yield to the mild authority of opinion."

The ground of some of these arguments is horrid and ridiculous to all rational ears; but to the politicians of metaphysics, who have opened schools for sophistry and made establishments for anarchy, it is solid and conclusive. It is obvious that, on a mere consideration of the right, the leaders in the Assembly would not have scrupled in the least to abolish the rents along with the titles and family arms. It would only be following up the principle of their reasonings and completing the analogy of their conduct. But they had newly possessed themselves of a great body of landed property by confiscation. They had this commodity on the market, and the market would have been wholly destroyed if they had allowed the husbandmen to riot in the speculations they had so freely intoxicated themselves with. The only security any one description of property enjoys comes from the interest their rapacity has in some other kind. They have left nothing but their own arbitrary pleasure to determine what property is to be protected and what subverted.

Neither have they left any principle by which any of their municipalities can be bound to obedience, or even conscientiously obliged not to separate from the whole, to become independent, or to attach itself to some other state. The people of Lyons, it seems, have lately refused to pay taxes. Why should they not? What lawful authority is left to exact them? The king imposed some of them. The old States, organised by orders, settled the older ones. They may say to the Assembly: "Who are you, who are not our kings, nor the States we elected, nor sitting on the principles we elected you on? And who are we, that when we see the gabelles you ordered to be paid shaken off altogether, and see the act of disobedience afterwards ratified by yourselves — who are we, that we are not to judge what taxes we ought or ought not to pay, and are not to make use of the same powers whose validity you have approved in others?" To this the answer is, "We will send troops." The last argument of kings is always the first with your Assembly. This military aid may serve for a time, while the impression of the increase of pay lasts and the vanity of being umpires in every dispute is flattered. But this weapon will snap short and prove unfaithful to the hand that uses it. The Assembly keeps a school where, systematically and with unremitting perseverance, they teach principles and make regulations destructive of all spirit of subordination, civil and military — and then they expect to hold an anarchic people in obedience by means of an anarchic army.

The municipal army, which by their new policy is to balance this national army, considered in itself alone, is of a much simpler constitution and in every respect less objectionable. It is a mere democratic body, unconnected with the crown or the kingdom, armed and trained and officered at the pleasure of the districts the several corps belong to; and the personal service of the individuals who compose it, or the fine paid instead of personal service, is directed by the same authority. Nothing could be more uniform. If, however, it is considered in any relation to the crown, to the National Assembly, to the public tribunals or to the other army, or with a view to any coherence or connection between its parts, it seems a monster, and can hardly fail to end its perplexed movements in some great national calamity. It is a worse preservative of a general constitution than the systasis of Crete, or the confederation of Poland, or any other ill-devised corrective yet imagined among the necessities produced by an ill-constructed system of government.

Footnote: I see from M. Necker's account that the national guards of Paris have received about £145,000 sterling out of the public treasure, over and above the money levied within their own city. Whether this is an actual payment for the nine months of their existence or an estimate of their yearly charge, I cannot clearly make out. It is of no great importance, since they may certainly take whatever they please.

Section Twelve: The Finances, and the Paper Money

Having finished my few remarks on the constitution of the supreme power, the executive, the judicature and the military, and on the reciprocal relation of all these establishments, I shall say something about the ability your legislators have shown with regard to the revenue.

In their proceedings on this object, if it is possible, still fewer traces appear of political judgment or financial resource. When the States met, the great object seemed to be to improve the system of revenue, to enlarge its collection, to cleanse it of oppression and vexation, and to establish it on the most solid footing. Great expectations were entertained on that head throughout Europe. It was by this grand arrangement that France was to stand or fall; and this became, very properly in my opinion, the test by which the skill and patriotism of the men who ruled in that Assembly would be tried. The revenue of the state is the state. In effect, everything depends on it, whether for support or for reform. The dignity of every occupation depends wholly on the quantity and the kind of virtue that can be exerted in it. Since all great qualities of the mind that operate in public, and are not merely suffering and passive, require force for their display — I had almost said for their unequivocal existence — the revenue, which is the spring of all power, becomes in its administration the sphere of every active virtue. Public virtue, being magnificent and splendid in its nature, instituted for great things and occupied with great concerns, requires abundant scope and room, and cannot spread and grow under confinement in straitened, narrow and sordid circumstances. It is through the revenue alone that the body politic can act in its true genius and character; and it will therefore display just as much of its collective virtue, and as much of the virtue that characterises the men who move it and are as it were its life and guiding principle, as it possesses a just revenue. For it is from this source that not only magnanimity and liberality and beneficence and fortitude and providence and the tutelary protection of all good arts derive their food and the growth of their organs, but continence and self-denial and labour and vigilance and frugality and whatever else there is in which the mind shows itself above the appetite are nowhere more in their proper element than in the provision and distribution of the public wealth. It is therefore not without reason that the science of speculative and practical finance, which must call so many auxiliary branches of knowledge to its aid, stands high in the estimation not only of ordinary people but of the wisest and best of men. And as this science has grown along with the progress of its object, the prosperity and improvement of nations has generally increased with the increase of their revenues; and both will continue to grow and flourish as long as the balance between what is left to strengthen the efforts of individuals and what is collected for the common efforts of the state keeps a due reciprocal proportion and a close correspondence and communication. And perhaps it may be owing to the greatness of revenues, and to the urgency of state necessities, that old abuses in the constitution of finances are discovered and their true nature and rational theory comes to be more perfectly understood — so much so that a smaller revenue might have been more distressing in one period than a far greater one is found to be in another, the proportionate wealth remaining the same. In this state of things the French Assembly found something in their revenues to preserve, secure and administer wisely, as well as to abrogate and alter. Though their proud assumptions might justify the severest tests, in trying their abilities on their financial proceedings I shall only consider what is the plain, obvious duty of a common finance minister, and try them on that, and not on models of ideal perfection.

The objects of a financier, then, are to secure an ample revenue; to impose it with judgment and equality; to employ it economically; and, when necessity obliges him to use credit, to secure its foundations in that instance and for ever by the clearness and candour of his proceedings, the exactness of his calculations and the solidity of his funds. Under these heads we may take a short and distinct view of the merits and abilities of the men in the National Assembly who have taken the management of this arduous concern on themselves.

Far from any increase of revenue in their hands, I find from a report by M. Vernier of the Committee of Finances, dated the second of last August, that the amount of the national revenue, compared with what it produced before the Revolution, was diminished by two hundred millions, or eight millions sterling, of the annual income — considerably more than a third of the whole.

If this is the result of great ability, surely ability was never displayed in a more distinguished manner or with a more powerful effect. No common folly, no vulgar incapacity, no ordinary official negligence, no official crime even, no corruption, no peculation, hardly any direct hostility that we have seen in the modern world could in so short a time have made so complete an overthrow of the finances and, with them, of the strength of a great kingdom. Cedo quî vestram rempublicam tantam amisistis tam cito? — tell me, how did you lose so great a commonwealth so quickly?

As soon as the Assembly met, the sophisters and declaimers began by decrying the ancient constitution of the revenue in many of its most essential branches, such as the public monopoly of salt. They charged it, as truly as unwisely, with being ill contrived, oppressive and partial. They were not content to use this description in speeches introducing some plan of reform; they declared it in a solemn resolution or public sentence, as though judicially passed on it, and circulated this throughout the nation. At the same time as they passed the decree, they ordered with equal gravity that this same absurd, oppressive and partial tax should go on being paid until they could find a revenue to replace it. The consequence was inevitable. The provinces that had always been exempt from this salt monopoly, some of them charged with other contributions perhaps equivalent to it, were wholly disinclined to bear any part of the burden that an equal distribution was to relieve the others of. As for the Assembly, occupied as it was with declaring and violating the rights of men and with its arrangements for general confusion, it had neither the leisure nor the capacity to contrive, nor the authority to enforce, any plan of any kind for replacing the tax, or equalising it, or compensating the provinces, or for leading their minds towards any scheme of accommodation with the other districts that were to be relieved. The people of the salt provinces, impatient under taxes condemned by the very authority that had ordered them to be paid, very soon found their patience exhausted. They thought themselves as skilful at demolishing as the Assembly could be. They relieved themselves by throwing off the whole burden. Encouraged by this example, each district, or part of a district, judging its own grievance by its own feeling and its own remedy by its own opinion, did as it pleased about the other taxes.

We are next to see how they have conducted themselves in contriving equal taxes, proportioned to the means of the citizens and least likely to lie heavily on the active capital employed in generating the private wealth from which the public fortune must be derived. By allowing the several districts, and several individuals within each district, to judge what part of the old revenue they might withhold, a new inequality of the most oppressive kind was introduced instead of better principles of equality. Payments were regulated by dispositions. The parts of the kingdom that were most submissive, most orderly or most affectionate towards the commonwealth bore the whole burden of the state. Nothing turns out to be so oppressive and unjust as a feeble government. To fill up all the deficiencies in the old taxes, and the new deficiencies of every kind that were to be expected, what was left to a state without authority? The National Assembly called for a voluntary benevolence — for a quarter of the income of all the citizens, to be assessed on the honour of the people who were to pay it. They obtained rather more than could rationally have been calculated on, but far indeed from what answered their real necessities, and much less their fond expectations. Rational people could have hoped for little from this tax of theirs in the disguise of a benevolence — a weak, ineffective and unequal tax, a tax by which luxury and avarice and selfishness were screened and the load thrown on productive capital, on integrity, generosity and public spirit, a tax laid by regulation upon virtue. At length the mask is thrown off, and they are now trying, with little success, to exact their benevolence by force.

This benevolence, the rickety offspring of weakness, was to be supported by another resource, the twin brother of the same prolific imbecility. The patriotic donations were to make good the failure of the patriotic contribution. John Doe was to become security for Richard Roe. By this scheme they took things of great price from the giver, comparatively of small value to the receiver; they ruined several trades; they pillaged the crown of its ornaments, the churches of their plate, and the people of their personal decorations. The invention of these juvenile pretenders to liberty was really nothing but a servile imitation of one of the poorest resources of a doting despotism. They took an old, huge, full-bottomed periwig out of the wardrobe of the antiquated frippery of Louis the Fourteenth to cover the premature baldness of the National Assembly. They produced this old-fashioned formal folly, though it had been so abundantly exposed in the Memoirs of the Duke de Saint-Simon — as if reasonable men needed any argument to show its mischief and insufficiency. A device of the same kind was tried within my memory by Louis the Fifteenth, but it never answered at any time. However, the necessities of ruinous wars were some excuse for desperate projects. The deliberations of calamity are rarely wise. But here there was a season for arrangement and foresight. It was in a time of profound peace, enjoyed for five years and promising to last much longer, that they had recourse to this desperate trifling. They were bound to lose more reputation by sporting with these toys and playthings of finance in their serious situation, filling half their journals as these have done, than could possibly be made up by the poor temporary supply they yielded. It seemed as if the men who adopted such projects were wholly ignorant of their circumstances, or wholly unequal to their necessities. Whatever virtue there may be in these devices, it is obvious that neither the patriotic gifts nor the patriotic contribution can ever be resorted to again. The resources of public folly are soon exhausted. Indeed the whole of their scheme of revenue is to make, by any artifice, an appearance of a full reservoir for the hour, while at the same time they cut off the springs and living fountains of perennial supply. The account M. Necker furnished not long ago was without question meant to be favourable. He gives a flattering view of the means of getting through the year, but he expresses, as it is natural he should, some apprehension for the year to follow. On this last forecast, instead of going into the grounds of the apprehension in order to prevent the predicted evil by proper foresight, M. Necker receives a sort of friendly reprimand from the President of the Assembly.

As to their other schemes of taxation, it is impossible to say anything about them with certainty, since they have not yet come into operation; but nobody is so sanguine as to imagine that they will fill up any perceptible part of the wide gaping breach their incapacity has made in their revenues. At present the state of their treasury sinks lower every day in cash and swells higher every day in fictitious representation. Now that so little is to be found within or without but paper — the representative not of wealth but of want, the creature not of credit but of power — they imagine that our flourishing condition in England is owing to bank paper, and not the bank paper to the flourishing condition of our commerce, to the solidity of our credit, and to the total exclusion of any idea of power from any part of the transaction. They forget that in England not one shilling of paper money of any description is received except by choice; that the whole has had its origin in cash actually deposited; and that it is convertible at pleasure, in an instant and without the smallest loss, into cash again. Our paper is of value in commerce because in law it is of none. It is powerful on the Exchange because in Westminster Hall it is impotent. In payment of a debt of twenty shillings a creditor may refuse all the paper of the Bank of England. Nor is there among us a single public security, of any quality or nature whatever, that is enforced by authority. In fact it might easily be shown that our paper wealth, instead of lessening the real coin, tends to increase it; that instead of being a substitute for money it only makes its entry, its exit and its circulation easier; and that it is the symbol of prosperity and not the badge of distress. A scarcity of cash and an exuberance of paper was never a subject of complaint in this nation.

Well, but a lessening of prodigal expense, and the economy introduced by the virtuous and sapient Assembly, makes amends for the losses sustained in the receipt of revenue. In this at least they have fulfilled the duty of a financier. Have those who say so looked at the expenses of the National Assembly itself? Of the municipalities? Of the city of Paris? Of the increased pay of the two armies? Of the new police? Of the new judicatures? Have they even carefully compared the present pension list with the old one? These politicians have been cruel, not economical. Comparing the expenses of the former prodigal government in relation to the revenues of that time with the expenses of this new system as against the state of its new treasury, I believe the present will be found beyond all comparison more costly.

Footnote: The reader will observe that I have touched only lightly, as my plan required nothing more, on the condition of the French finances in relation to the demands on them. If I had intended to do otherwise, the materials in my hands for such a task are not altogether complete. On this subject I refer the reader to M. de Calonne's work and to the tremendous display he has made of the havoc and devastation in the public estate and in all the affairs of France, caused by the presumptuous good intentions of ignorance and incapacity. Such causes will always produce such effects. Looking over that account with a fairly strict eye, and deducting, with perhaps too much rigour, everything that may be put down to a financier out of office who might be supposed by his enemies to want to make the most of his case, I believe it will be found that mankind was never at any time furnished with a more salutary lesson of caution against the daring spirit of innovators than the one supplied at the expense of France.

It remains only to consider the proofs of financial ability furnished by the present French managers when they have to raise supplies on credit. Here I am a little at a stand, for credit, properly speaking, they have none. The credit of the ancient government was not the best, indeed; but they could always command money on some terms, not only at home but from most of the countries of Europe where a surplus capital had accumulated, and the credit of that government was improving daily. The establishment of a system of liberty would of course be supposed to give it new strength — and so it would actually have done, if a system of liberty had been established. What offers has their government of pretended liberty had from Holland, from Hamburg, from Switzerland, from Genoa, from England, to deal in their paper? Why should these nations of commerce and economy enter into any money dealings with a people who attempt to reverse the very nature of things, among whom they see the debtor prescribing at the point of the bayonet the medium of his own solvency to his creditor, discharging one of his engagements with another, turning his very penury into his resource, and paying his interest with his rags?

Their fanatical confidence in the omnipotence of church plunder has led these philosophers to overlook all care of the public estate, just as the dream of the philosopher's stone leads dupes, under the more plausible delusion of the hermetic art, to neglect all rational means of improving their fortunes. With these philosophic financiers, this universal medicine made of church mummy is to cure all the evils of the state. These gentlemen perhaps do not believe much in the miracles of piety, but it cannot be questioned that they have an undoubting faith in the prodigies of sacrilege. Is there a debt that presses them? Issue assignats. Are compensations to be made, or a maintenance decreed, to men they have robbed of their freehold in their office or expelled from their profession? Assignats. Is a fleet to be fitted out? Assignats. If sixteen millions sterling of these assignats forced on the people leave the wants of the state as urgent as ever, then issue, says one, thirty millions sterling of assignats — issue eighty millions more of assignats, says another. The only difference between their financial factions is over the greater or lesser quantity of assignats to be imposed on the public sufferance. They are all professors of assignats. Even those whose natural good sense and knowledge of commerce, not yet obliterated by philosophy, furnish decisive arguments against this delusion, conclude their arguments by proposing an issue of assignats. I suppose they must talk of assignats, since no other language would be understood. All experience of their inefficacy does not discourage them in the least. Are the old assignats depreciated at market? What is the remedy? Issue new assignats. Mais si maladia opiniatria non vult se garire, quid illi facere? Assignare; postea assignare; ensuita assignare — but if the obstinate disease will not be cured, what is he to do? Assignate; then assignate; and after that assignate. The word is altered by a trifle. The Latin of your present doctors may be better than that of your old comedy; their wisdom and the variety of their resources are the same. They have no more notes in their song than the cuckoo — though, far from the softness of that harbinger of summer and plenty, their voice is as harsh and as ominous as the raven's.

Who but the most desperate adventurers in philosophy and finance could ever have thought of destroying the settled revenue of the state, the sole security for the public credit, in the hope of rebuilding it with the materials of confiscated property? If, however, an excessive zeal for the state has led a pious and venerable prelate — a father of the Church by anticipation — to pillage his own order, and, for the good of the church and the people, to take on himself the place of grand financier of confiscation and comptroller-general of sacrilege, then in my opinion he and his coadjutors were bound to show by their subsequent conduct that they knew something of the office they had assumed. Once they had resolved to appropriate a certain portion of the landed property of their conquered country to the fisc, it was their business to make their bank a real fund of credit, so far as such a bank was capable of becoming one.

Footnote: La Bruyère, of Bossuet.

To establish a current circulating credit on any land-bank, in any circumstances whatever, has so far proved difficult at the very least. The attempt has commonly ended in bankruptcy. But when the Assembly had been led, through a contempt of moral principles, into a defiance of economic ones, it might at least have been expected that nothing would be left undone on their part to lessen this difficulty and to prevent any aggravation of this bankruptcy. It might be expected that, to make your land-bank tolerable, every means would be adopted that could display openness and candour in the statement of the security, and everything that could help the recovery of the debt. To take things at their most favourable, your condition was that of a man with a large landed estate which he wished to dispose of in order to discharge a debt and supply certain services. Not being able to sell it at once, you wished to mortgage. What would a man of fair intentions and ordinary clear understanding do in such circumstances? Ought he not first to ascertain the gross value of the estate, the charges of managing and disposing of it, and the encumbrances of all kinds, perpetual and temporary, that affect it — and then, striking a net surplus, calculate the just value of the security? When that surplus, which is the creditor's only security, had been clearly ascertained and properly vested in the hands of trustees, he would then indicate the parcels to be sold and the time and conditions of sale. After that he would allow the public creditor, if he chose, to subscribe his stock into this new fund — or he might receive proposals for an assignat from people who would advance money to buy this species of security. That would be to proceed like men of business, methodically and rationally, and on the only principles of public and private credit that exist. The dealer would then know exactly what he was buying, and the only doubt that could hang on his mind would be the dread of a resumption of the spoil, which might one day be made, perhaps with the addition of punishment, from the sacrilegious grip of the execrable wretches who could become purchasers at the auction of their innocent fellow citizens.

An open and exact statement of the clear value of the property, and of the time and circumstances and place of sale, was necessary in order to efface as far as possible the stigma that has always been branded on every kind of land-bank. It became necessary on another principle too: that is, on account of a pledge of faith previously given on the subject, so that their future fidelity in a slippery business might be established by their adherence to their first engagement. When they had finally decided on a state resource from church booty, they came to a solemn resolution on the subject on the fourteenth of April 1790, and pledged themselves to their country "that, in the statement of the public charges for each year, there should be brought to account a sum sufficient to defray the expenses of the Roman Catholic and Apostolic religion, the support of the ministers at the altars, the relief of the poor, and the pensions of the clergy, secular and regular, of the one sex and of the other, in order that the estates and goods which are at the disposal of the nation may be disengaged of all charges, and employed by the representatives, or the legislative body, to the great and most pressing exigencies of the state." On the same day they further engaged that the sum necessary for the year 1791 should be determined forthwith.

In this resolution they admit that it is their duty to show distinctly the expense of the objects above, which by other resolutions they had already engaged should come first in the order of provision. They admit that they ought to show the estate clear and disengaged of all charges, and that they should show it immediately. Have they done this immediately, or at any time? Have they ever furnished a rent roll of the immovable estate, or given in an inventory of the movable effects, which they confiscate to their assignats? How they can fulfil their engagement to hold out to the public service "an estate disengaged of all charges" without authenticating either the value of the estate or the amount of the charges, I leave to their English admirers to explain. Instantly on this assurance, and before taking a single step towards making it good, they issue sixteen millions sterling of their paper on the credit of so handsome a declaration. This was manly. Who, after such a masterly stroke, can doubt their abilities in finance? But then, before any further emission of these financial indulgences, they took care at least to make good their original promise. If any such estimate, either of the value of the estate or of the amount of the encumbrances, has been made, it has escaped me. I have never heard of it.

At length they have spoken out, and have made a full discovery of their abominable fraud in holding out the church lands as a security for any debts or any service whatever. They rob only to enable themselves to cheat; but in a very short time they defeat the ends of both the robbery and the fraud by making out accounts for other purposes, which blow up their whole apparatus of force and deception. I am obliged to M. de Calonne for his reference to the document that proves this extraordinary fact; it had somehow escaped me. Indeed it was not necessary to make out my assertion about the breach of faith on the declaration of the fourteenth of April 1790. It now appears from a report of their own committee that the charge of keeping up the reduced ecclesiastical establishments, and the other expenses attending religion, and maintaining the religious of both sexes who are retained or pensioned, and the other accompanying expenses of the same nature which they have brought on themselves by this convulsion in property, exceeds the income of the estates acquired by it by the enormous sum of two millions sterling a year — besides a debt of seven millions and upwards. These are the calculating powers of imposture! This is the finance of philosophy! This is the result of all the delusions held out to engage a miserable people in rebellion, murder and sacrilege, and to make them prompt and zealous instruments in the ruin of their country! Never in any case did a state enrich itself by confiscating from its citizens. This new experiment has succeeded like all the rest. Every honest mind, every true lover of liberty and humanity, must rejoice to find that injustice is not always good policy, nor rapine the highroad to riches. I add with pleasure, in a note, the able and spirited observations of M. de Calonne on this subject.

Footnote: "Ce n'est point à l'assemblée entière que je m'adresse ici; je ne parle qu'à ceux qui l'égarent, en lui cachant sous des gazes séduisantes le but où ils l'entraînent. C'est à eux que je dis: Votre objet, vous n'en disconviendrez pas, c'est d'ôter tout espoir au clergé, et de consommer sa ruine; c'est-là, en ne vous soupçonnant d'aucune combinaison de cupidité, d'aucun regard sur le jeu des effets publics, c'est-là ce qu'on doit croire que vous avez en vue dans la terrible opération que vous proposez; c'est ce qui doit en être le fruit. Mais le peuple que vous y intéressez, quel avantage peut-il y trouver? En vous servant sans cesse de lui, que faites-vous pour lui? Rien, absolument rien; et, au contraire, vous faites ce qui ne conduit qu'à l'accabler de nouvelles charges. Vous avez rejeté, à son préjudice, une offre de 400 millions, dont l'acceptation pouvoit devenir un moyen de soulagement en sa faveur; et à cette ressource, aussi profitable que légitime, vous avez substitué une injustice ruineuse, qui, de votre propre aveu, charge le trésor public, et par conséquent le peuple, d'un surcroît de dépense annuelle de 50 millions au moins, et d'un remboursement de 150 millions. Malheureux peuple! voilà ce que vous vaut en dernier résultat l'expropriation de l'Église, et la dureté des décrets taxateurs du traitement des ministres d'une religion bienfaisante; et désormais ils seront à votre charge: leurs charités soulageoient les pauvres; et vous allez être imposés pour subvenir à leur entretien!" In English: it is not the whole assembly I am addressing here; I speak only to the men who are leading it astray, hiding from it under seductive veils the goal they are dragging it towards. To them I say: your object, you will not deny it, is to take away all hope from the clergy and to complete their ruin. That — without suspecting you of any calculation of greed, or any eye to the play of the public funds — is what we must believe you have in view in the terrible operation you propose, and that is what its fruit must be. But the people you are engaging in it: what advantage can they find in it? Making constant use of them as you do, what do you do for them? Nothing, absolutely nothing; on the contrary, you do what can only end in loading them with new burdens. To their prejudice you have rejected an offer of 400 millions, the acceptance of which might have become a means of relief in their favour; and for that resource, as profitable as it was legitimate, you have substituted a ruinous injustice which, by your own admission, charges the public treasury, and consequently the people, with an additional annual expense of at least 50 millions and a repayment of 150 millions. Unhappy people! This is what the expropriation of the church comes to for you in the end, and the harshness of the decrees fixing the salaries of the ministers of a beneficent religion; and from now on they will be a charge on you. Their charity used to relieve the poor, and you are going to be taxed to keep them. — De l'État de la France, page 81. See also page 92 and the pages following.

To persuade the world of the bottomless resource of ecclesiastical confiscation, the Assembly has proceeded to other confiscations of estates in offices, which could not be done with any ordinary colour of justice without being compensated out of this grand confiscation of landed property. On this fund, which was to show a surplus disengaged of all charges, they have thrown a new charge: namely, the compensation of the whole body of the disbanded judicature and of all suppressed offices and estates — a charge I cannot ascertain, but which unquestionably amounts to many millions of French money. Another of the new charges is an annuity of four hundred and eighty thousand pounds sterling, to be paid, if they choose to keep faith, by daily payments, for the interest of the first assignats. Have they ever given themselves the trouble of stating fairly the expense of managing the church lands in the hands of the municipalities, to whose care and skill and diligence, and that of their legion of unknown under-agents, they have chosen to commit the charge of the forfeited estates — the consequence of which had been so ably pointed out by the Bishop of Nancy?

But it is unnecessary to dwell on these obvious heads of encumbrance. Have they made out any clear statement of the grand encumbrance of all, by which I mean the whole of the general and municipal establishments of every sort, and compared it with the regular income from revenue? Every deficiency in these becomes a charge on the confiscated estate before the creditor can plant his cabbages on a single acre of church property. There is no other prop but this confiscation to keep the whole state from tumbling to the ground. In this situation they have deliberately covered with a thick fog everything they ought to have taken pains to clear; and then, blindfolded themselves, like bulls that shut their eyes when they charge, they drive their slaves at the point of the bayonet — blindfolded, indeed, no worse than their lords — to take their fictions for currency and to swallow paper pills at thirty-four millions sterling a dose. Then they proudly lay in their claim to future credit, after the failure of all their past engagements, and at a time when it is clear, if anything in such a matter can be clear, that the surplus estates will never answer even the first of their mortgages — I mean that of the four hundred millions, or sixteen millions sterling, of assignats. In all this procedure I can discern neither the solid sense of plain dealing nor the subtle dexterity of ingenious fraud. The objections within the Assembly to pulling up the floodgates for this inundation of fraud go unanswered; but they are thoroughly refuted by a hundred thousand financiers in the street. These are the numbers the metaphysical arithmeticians compute by. These are the grand calculations on which a philosophical public credit is founded in France. They cannot raise supplies, but they can raise mobs. Let them rejoice in the applause of the club at Dundee for their wisdom and patriotism in applying the plunder of the citizens to the service of the state. I hear of no address on this subject from the directors of the Bank of England — though their approval would carry a little more weight in the scale of credit than the club at Dundee's. But to do the club justice, I believe the gentlemen who compose it are wiser than they appear: that they will be less liberal with their money than with their addresses, and that they would not give a dog's ear of their most rumpled and ragged Scotch paper for twenty of your fairest assignats.

Early this year the Assembly issued paper to the amount of sixteen millions sterling. What must be the state into which the Assembly has brought your affairs, when the relief afforded by so vast a supply has been hardly perceptible? This paper also suffered an almost immediate depreciation of five per cent, which in a little while came to about seven. The effect of these assignats on the receipt of revenue is remarkable. M. Necker found that the collectors of revenue, who received in coin, paid the treasury in assignats. The collectors made seven per cent by receiving in money and accounting in depreciated paper. It was not very difficult to foresee that this must be inevitable. It was no less embarrassing for that. M. Necker was obliged, for a considerable part of it in the London market I believe, to buy gold and silver for the mint, which came to about twelve thousand pounds above the value of the commodity gained. That minister was of the opinion that, whatever secret nutritive virtue assignats might have, the state could not live on them alone — that some real silver was necessary, particularly to satisfy men who, having iron in their hands, were not likely to distinguish themselves for patience when they perceived that an increase of pay held out to them in real money was to be fraudulently drawn back again in depreciated paper. In this very natural distress the minister applied to the Assembly to order the collectors to pay in specie what they had received in specie. It could not escape him that if the Treasury paid three per cent for the use of a currency that came back to it seven per cent worse than the minister issued it, such dealing could not greatly tend to enrich the public. The Assembly took no notice of his recommendation. They were in this dilemma: if they went on receiving the assignats, cash must become a stranger to their Treasury; if the Treasury refused those paper amulets, or discountenanced them in any degree, they would destroy the credit of their sole resource. They seem, then, to have made their choice, and to have given their paper some sort of credit by taking it themselves; while at the same time, in their speeches, they made a kind of swaggering declaration, something rather beyond legislative competence I think — that there is no difference in value between metallic money and their assignats. This was a good, stout, proof article of faith, pronounced under an anathema by the venerable fathers of this philosophic synod. Credat who will — certainly not Judæus Apella.

A noble indignation rises in the minds of your popular leaders on hearing the magic lantern of their financial show compared with the fraudulent exhibitions of Mr Law. They cannot bear to hear the sands of his Mississippi compared with the rock of the church on which they build their system. Pray let them suppress this glorious spirit until they show the world what piece of solid ground there is for their assignats which they have not already preoccupied with other charges. They do injustice to that great mother fraud in comparing it with their degenerate imitation. It is not true that Law built solely on a speculation about the Mississippi. He added the East India trade; he added the African trade; he added the farms of all the farmed revenue of France. All these together unquestionably could not support the structure that the public enthusiasm, not he, chose to build on those bases. But by comparison these were generous delusions. They supposed, and they aimed at, an increase of the commerce of France. They opened the whole range of the two hemispheres to it. They did not think of feeding France out of her own substance. A grand imagination found something captivating in this flight of commerce. It was enough to dazzle the eye of an eagle. It was not made to entice the nose of a mole, nuzzling and burying itself in its mother earth, as yours is. Men had not then shrunk from their natural dimensions through a degrading and sordid philosophy, fitted for low and vulgar deceptions. Above all, remember that in imposing on the imagination, the managers of that system paid a compliment to the freedom of men. In their fraud there was no admixture of force. That was reserved for our own time, to quench the little glimmerings of reason that might break in on the solid darkness of this enlightened age.

On recollection, I have said nothing of one scheme of finance that might be urged in favour of these gentlemen's abilities, and which has been introduced with great pomp though not yet finally adopted in the National Assembly. It comes with something solid in aid of the credit of the paper circulation, and much has been said of its usefulness and its elegance. I mean the project for coining the bells of the suppressed churches into money. This is their alchemy. There are some follies that baffle argument, that go beyond ridicule, and that excite no feeling in us but disgust; and therefore I say no more about it.

It is as little worth remarking any further on all their drawing and redrawing, on their circulation for putting off the evil day, on the play between the Treasury and the Caisse d'Escompte, and on all these old exploded contrivances of mercantile fraud now exalted into policy of state.
