The revenue will not be trifled with. Prattle about the rights of men will not be accepted in payment for a biscuit or a pound of gunpowder. Here, then, the metaphysicians come down from their airy speculations and faithfully follow examples. What examples? The examples of bankrupts. But defeated, baffled, disgraced, with their breath and their strength and their inventions and their fancies all deserting them, their confidence still holds its ground. In the manifest failure of their abilities they take credit for their benevolence. When the revenue disappears in their hands, they have the presumption, in some of their recent proceedings, to value themselves on the relief given to the people. They did not relieve the people. If they had any such intentions, why did they order the obnoxious taxes to be paid? The people relieved themselves, in spite of the Assembly.

But waiving all discussion about which party can claim the merit of this fallacious relief, has there in fact been any relief to the people in any form? M. Bailly, one of the grand agents of paper circulation, lets you into the nature of this relief. His speech to the National Assembly contained a high and laboured panegyric on the inhabitants of Paris for the constancy and unbroken resolution with which they have borne their distress and misery. A fine picture of public happiness! What! Great courage and unconquerable firmness of mind, to endure benefits and to sustain redress? One would think from this learned lord mayor's speech that the Parisians had spent the last twelvemonth suffering the straits of some dreadful blockade — that Henry the Fourth had been stopping up the approaches to their supply and Sully thundering with his artillery at the gates of Paris — when in reality they are besieged by no enemies but their own madness and folly, their own credulity and perverseness. But M. Bailly will sooner thaw the eternal ice of his Atlantic regions than restore the central heat to Paris while it remains "smitten with the cold, dry, petrific mace" of a false and unfeeling philosophy. Some time after this speech, on the thirteenth of last August, the same magistrate, giving an account of his administration at the bar of the same Assembly, expresses himself as follows: "In the month of July 1789" — the period of everlasting commemoration — "the finances of the city of Paris were yet in good order; the expenditure was counterbalanced by the receipts, and she had at that time a million (forty thousand pounds sterling) in the bank. The expenses she has been forced to incur subsequent to the Revolution amount to 2,500,000 livres. From these expenses, and from the great falling off in the produce of the free gifts, there has arisen not only a momentary but a total want of money." This is the Paris on whose nourishment such immense sums, drawn from the vitals of all France, have been spent in the course of the last year. As long as Paris stands in the place of ancient Rome, so long will she be maintained by the subject provinces. It is an evil inevitably attending the dominion of sovereign democratic republics. As happened at Rome, it may outlive the republican domination that gave rise to it. In that case despotism itself must submit to the vices of popularity. Rome under her emperors united the evils of both systems, and this unnatural combination was one great cause of her ruin.

To tell the people that they are relieved by the dilapidation of their public estate is a cruel and insolent imposition. Before valuing themselves on the relief given to the people by the destruction of their revenue, statesmen ought first to have attended carefully to the solution of this problem: whether it is more advantageous to the people to pay considerably and gain in proportion, or to gain little or nothing and be unburdened of all contribution. My mind is made up to decide in favour of the first. Experience is with me, and I believe the best opinions are too. To keep a balance between the subject's power of acquisition and the demands he has to answer on the part of the state is the fundamental part of a true politician's skill. The means of acquisition come first in time and in arrangement. Good order is the foundation of all good things. To be able to acquire, the people must be tractable and obedient without being servile. The magistrate must have his reverence and the laws their authority. The body of the people must not have the principles of natural subordination artificially rooted out of their minds. They must respect the property they cannot share. They must labour to obtain what labour can obtain; and when they find, as they commonly do, that the success is not proportioned to the effort, they must be taught where their consolation lies, in the final proportions of eternal justice. Whoever deprives them of that consolation deadens their industry and strikes at the root of all acquisition as of all conservation. The man who does this is the cruel oppressor and the merciless enemy of the poor and wretched — at the same time as, by his wicked speculations, he exposes the fruits of successful industry and the accumulations of fortune to the plunder of the negligent, the disappointed and the unprosperous.

Too many professional financiers are apt to see nothing in revenue but banks and circulations and annuities on lives and tontines and perpetual rents and all the small wares of the shop. In a settled order of the state these things are not to be slighted, nor is skill in them to be held of trivial value. They are good, but only good when they assume the effects of that settled order and are built on it. But when men think that these beggarly contrivances can supply a resource against the evils that result from breaking up the foundations of public order, and from causing or allowing the principles of property to be subverted, they will leave, in the ruin of their country, a melancholy and lasting monument to the effect of preposterous politics and presumptuous, short-sighted, narrow-minded wisdom.

The effects of the incapacity shown by the popular leaders in all the great members of the commonwealth are to be covered with the "all-atoning name" of liberty. In some people I do see great liberty; in many, if not in most, I see an oppressive, degrading servitude. But what is liberty without wisdom and without virtue? It is the greatest of all possible evils, for it is folly and vice and madness without tuition or restraint. Those who know what virtuous liberty is cannot bear to see it disgraced by incapable heads on the strength of their having high-sounding words in their mouths. Grand, swelling sentiments of liberty I am sure I do not despise. They warm the heart; they enlarge and liberalise our minds; they animate our courage in a time of conflict. Old as I am, I read the fine raptures of Lucan and Corneille with pleasure. Nor do I wholly condemn the little arts and devices of popularity. They make it easier to carry many points of moment; they keep the people together; they refresh the mind in its exertions; and they spread an occasional gaiety over the severe brow of moral freedom. Every politician ought to sacrifice to the Graces and to join compliance with reason. But in such an undertaking as the one in France, all these subsidiary sentiments and artifices are of little use. To make a government requires no great prudence. Settle the seat of power, teach obedience, and the work is done. To give freedom is easier still. There is no need to guide; you need only let go the rein. But to form a free government — that is, to temper these opposite elements of liberty and restraint together into one consistent work — requires much thought, deep reflection, and a sagacious, powerful, combining mind. This I do not find in the men who take the lead in the National Assembly. Perhaps they are not so miserably deficient as they appear. I rather believe they are not. It would put them below the common level of human understanding. But when leaders choose to make themselves bidders at an auction of popularity, their talents will be of no service in constructing the state. They will become flatterers instead of legislators, the instruments and not the guides of the people. If any of them should happen to propose a scheme of liberty soberly limited and defined with proper qualifications, he will immediately be outbid by competitors producing something more splendidly popular. Suspicions will be raised about his fidelity to his cause. Moderation will be stigmatised as the virtue of cowards, and compromise as the prudence of traitors — until, in the hope of preserving the credit that might let him temper and moderate on some occasions, the popular leader is obliged to become active in propagating doctrines and establishing powers that will afterwards defeat any sober purpose he might ultimately have aimed at.

Section Thirteen: What I Wish for My Own Country

But am I so unreasonable as to see nothing at all deserving praise in the tireless labours of this Assembly? I do not deny that among an infinite number of acts of violence and folly, some good may have been done. Men who destroy everything will certainly remove some grievance. Men who make everything new stand a chance of establishing something beneficial. But to give them credit for what they have done by virtue of the authority they have usurped, or to excuse them for the crimes by which that authority was acquired, it would have to appear that the same things could not have been accomplished without producing such a revolution. Most assuredly they could; because almost every one of their regulations that is not highly equivocal was contained either in the king's concession, voluntarily made at the meeting of the States, or in the concurring instructions to the orders. Some usages have been abolished on just grounds; but they were such that if they had stood exactly as they were to all eternity, they would have detracted very little from the happiness and prosperity of any state. The improvements of the National Assembly are superficial; their errors are fundamental.

Whatever they are, I would rather my countrymen recommended the example of the British constitution to our neighbours than took models from them for improving our own. In our constitution we have an invaluable treasure. We are not, I think, without some causes for apprehension and complaint; but we owe these not to our constitution but to our own conduct. I think our happy situation is owing to our constitution — but owing to the whole of it, and not to any part of it singly; owing in great measure to what we have left standing through our several reviews and reformations, as well as to what we have altered or added. Our people will find employment enough for a truly patriotic, free and independent spirit in guarding what they possess from violation. I would not rule out alteration either; but even when I changed, it should be in order to preserve. I should be led to my remedy by a great grievance. In whatever I did, I should follow the example of our ancestors. I would make the repair as nearly as possible in the style of the building. A politic caution, a guarded circumspection, a timidity of principle rather than of temperament, were among the ruling principles of our forefathers in their most decided conduct. Not being illuminated with the light of which the gentlemen of France tell us they have so abundant a share, they acted under a strong sense of the ignorance and fallibility of mankind. He who had made them fallible rewarded them for attending to their own nature in their conduct. Let us imitate their caution if we wish to deserve their fortune or to keep what they left us. Let us add, if we please, but let us preserve what they have left; and, standing on the firm ground of the British constitution, let us be content to admire the aeronauts of France rather than attempt to follow them in their desperate flights.

I have told you my sentiments candidly. I do not think they are likely to alter yours. I do not know that they ought to. You are young; you cannot guide the fortune of your country, but must follow it. Hereafter, though, they may be of some use to you, in some future form your commonwealth may take. In its present form it can hardly remain; but before its final settlement it may be obliged to pass, as one of our poets says, "through great varieties of untried being", and to be purified by fire and blood in all its transmigrations.

I have little to recommend my opinions but long observation and much impartiality. They come from a man who has been no tool of power and no flatterer of greatness, and who in his last acts does not wish to belie the tenor of his life. They come from a man almost the whole of whose public exertion has been a struggle for the liberty of others; in whose breast no anger lasting or vehement has ever been kindled except by what he considered to be tyranny; and who has snatched the hours he has spent on your affairs from his share in the efforts good men are making to discredit wealthy oppression, persuading himself as he did so that he was not departing from his usual office. They come from a man who desires honours and distinctions and salaries very little, and expects them not at all; who has no contempt for fame and no fear of obloquy; who avoids contention, though he will hazard an opinion; from a man who wishes to preserve consistency, but who would preserve it by varying his means in order to secure the unity of his end — and who, when the balance of the vessel he sails in may be endangered by overloading on one side, is anxious to carry the small weight of his reasons over to the side that may keep it in trim.
